Completed means the initial curated cross-reference pass: the source verse and every listed target passage were read in the pinned Saints-Translation English records, and each retained pair has a specific explanation. It does not mean every possible biblical connection has been exhausted, or independent scholarly approval.
Reference edition: Saints Holy Bible 70-book edition, pinned Saints-Translation English records. Greek-based source numbering is retained.

Jeremiah 1:1
The word of God that came upon Jeremiah son of Hilkiah, one of the priests, who lived in Anathoth in the land of Benjamin,
  Jeremiah 11:21-23 — historical connection through Anathoth: Jeremiah is introduced as a priest living in Anathoth. The later passage names men of Anathoth who demand that the prophet stop speaking in the Lord's name, connecting his hometown with opposition to his commission.

Jeremiah 1:2
which became God’s word to him in the days of Josiah son of Amos, king of Judah, in the thirteenth year of his reign.
  2 Chronicles 34:3 — regnal chronology of Josiah's reforms and Jeremiah's calling: Jeremiah dates the beginning of God's word to Josiah's thirteenth year. Chronicles dates Josiah's beginning to cleanse Judah and Jerusalem to his twelfth year, providing adjacent regnal chronology without identifying the calling and the purge as the same event.

Jeremiah 1:3
It also came in the days of Jehoiakim son of Josiah, king of Judah, until the eleventh year of Zedekiah son of Josiah, king of Judah, until Jerusalem’s captivity in the fifth month.
  2 Kings 25:2-11 — historical counterpart to Jerusalem's captivity: Jeremiah's heading ends with Zedekiah's eleventh year and Jerusalem's captivity in the fifth month. Kings records the siege lasting to Sedekias's eleventh year and the fifth-month destruction and removal of Jerusalem's population; the royal name forms differ.

Jeremiah 1:5
Before I formed you in the belly, I know you; before you came out of the womb, I have consecrated you. I have appointed you a prophet to nations.
  Isaiah 49:5-6 — parallel formation associated with the womb and commission toward nations: Jeremiah is known and consecrated before birth and appointed a prophet to nations. Isaiah describes one formed from the belly as God's servant and appointed a light for nations, sharing vocation associated with formation and an international scope without identifying the two speakers.

Jeremiah 1:6
I said, O Master Lord! Behold, I do not know how to speak, because I am young.
  Exodus 4:10-12 — parallel objection about inability to speak when called: Jeremiah protests that he does not know how to speak because he is young. Moses similarly pleads weakness of voice and slowness of tongue; the grounds differ, but both objections arise when God commissions a speaker.

Jeremiah 1:7
The Lord said to me, Do not say, I am young, because you will go to everyone to whom I send you, and you will speak according to everything I command you.
  Deuteronomy 18:18 — parallel prophetic obligation to speak God's commands: God tells Jeremiah to go to those to whom he is sent and speak everything commanded. Deuteronomy describes a prophet into whose mouth God puts His word and who speaks all God commands; this compares the commission rather than asserting an exclusive identification.

Jeremiah 1:8
Do not fear them, because I am with you to rescue you, says the Lord.
  Acts 18:9-10 — parallel assurance of divine presence against fear: Jeremiah receives the command not to fear because God is with him to rescue him. The Lord likewise tells Paul not to fear or cease speaking because He is with him, applying the assurance to a different threatened ministry.

Jeremiah 1:9
The Lord stretched out His hand toward me and touched my mouth. The Lord said to me, Behold, I have put My words into your mouth.
  Isaiah 51:16 — shared image of God placing words in a mouth: The Lord touches Jeremiah's mouth and declares that He has put His words there. Isaiah also promises to put divine words in the addressee's mouth; the touching action belongs specifically to Jeremiah's account.

Jeremiah 1:10
Behold, today I have appointed you over nations and kingdoms, to uproot, demolish, destroy, build and plant.
  Jeremiah 18:7-10 — parallel authority to announce destruction and restoration of nations: The commission names uprooting, demolition, destruction, building and planting over nations and kingdoms. Jeremiah 18 explains God's speech about destroying a nation or kingdom and rebuilding and planting it, adding the conditions of repentance and disobedience.

Jeremiah 1:12
The Lord said to me, You have seen well, because I am watchful over My words to perform them.
  Isaiah 55:10-11 — parallel certainty that God's spoken word will be performed: God says He watches over His words to perform them. Isaiah compares the effective divine word to rain that produces growth and says it will not return before God's will is accomplished; it supports the performance promise without explaining the nut-wood vision.

Jeremiah 1:13
The Lord’s word came to me a second time, saying, What do you see? I said, A cauldron heated from beneath, its face away from the north.
  Ezekiel 24:3-6 — parallel cauldron imagery in judgment upon Jerusalem: Jeremiah sees a cauldron heated from beneath, interpreted in the following verses through northern calamity. Ezekiel's cauldron parable explicitly addresses the city of bloodshed; both employ a heated cooking vessel for judgment, while Ezekiel's contents and rust are distinct details.

Jeremiah 1:14
The Lord said to me, From the north calamities will be kindled upon all who inhabit the land.
  Jeremiah 6:1 — parallel announcement of northern calamity: The explanation of Jeremiah's vision says calamities will be kindled from the north upon the land's inhabitants. Jeremiah 6 again announces evils looking out from the north and great crushing, repeating the direction and destructive threat.

Jeremiah 1:15
Because behold, I am summoning all the kingdoms from the north of the land, says the Lord. They will come and each set his throne at the forecourts of Jerusalem’s gates, against all her surrounding walls and against all Judah’s cities.
  Jeremiah 6:22-23 — parallel gathering of northern forces against Zion: God summons northern kingdoms to set their thrones against Jerusalem's gates, walls and Judah's cities. Jeremiah 6 describes northern peoples and nations armed and drawn up for war against Zion, giving a corresponding military description without reproducing the thrones detail.

Jeremiah 1:16
I will speak to them with judgment concerning all their evil, because they abandoned Me, sacrificed to foreign gods and bowed down to the works of their hands.
  Isaiah 2:8 — parallel condemnation of worshiping handmade objects: Jeremiah explains judgment by abandonment of God, sacrifice to foreign gods and bowing to human handiwork. Isaiah likewise condemns a land filled with abominations whose people bow before what their fingers made.

Jeremiah 1:17
But you, gird your waist, rise and tell them everything I command you. Do not fear them or be terrified before them, because I am with you to rescue you, says the Lord.
  Ezekiel 2:6-7 — parallel fearless delivery of God's words despite hostile hearers: Jeremiah must rise, speak everything commanded and not fear his opponents. Ezekiel similarly receives the command not to fear hostile faces or words and to deliver God's words to the embittering house.

Jeremiah 1:18
Behold, on this day I have made you like a fortified city and like a strong bronze wall against all Judah’s kings, its rulers and the people of the land,
  Ezekiel 3:8-9 — parallel strengthening of a prophet against opposition: Jeremiah is made like a fortified city and bronze wall against Judah's authorities and people. Ezekiel's face and contention are strengthened beyond rock against opponents; the comparison concerns divinely strengthened resistance, not identical material imagery.

Jeremiah 1:19
and they will fight you, but they will certainly not prevail against you, because I am with you to rescue you, said the Lord.
  Jeremiah 15:20-21 — repeated promise that opponents will not prevail against the prophet: The Lord promises that those fighting Jeremiah will not prevail because He is present to rescue him. Jeremiah 15 repeats the fight, failure to prevail and saving presence, then specifies rescue from evil and pestilent people.

Jeremiah 2:2
And He said, Thus says the Lord: I remembered the mercy of your youth and the love of your completion, when you followed the Holy One of Israel, says the Lord.
  Hosea 11:1-2 — parallel recollection of Israel's early relationship with God: Jeremiah recalls the mercy of Israel's youth and the love of its completion. Hosea likewise looks back to Israel's infancy and God's love before describing departure and Baal worship; Jeremiah's supplied wording is not replaced with a marriage-in-the-wilderness formulation.

Jeremiah 2:3
Israel is holy. To the Lord he is the first of His produce. All who devour him will offend; calamities will come upon them, declares the Lord.
  Exodus 19:5-6 — parallel designation of Israel as holy to God: Jeremiah calls Israel holy to the Lord. Exodus describes covenant-keeping Israel as God's treasured people and a holy nation, providing a covenant counterpart to that sacred status.
  James 1:18 — shared firstfruits imagery for people belonging to God: Jeremiah calls Israel the first of the Lord's produce. James calls those born through the word of truth a kind of firstfruits of God's creatures; the shared agricultural imagery is clear, while the target does not repeat Jeremiah's warning against devouring Israel.

Jeremiah 2:5
Thus says the Lord: What offense did your fathers find in Me, that they withdrew far from Me, followed futile things and became futile?
  Romans 1:21-23 — parallel futility resulting from departure into false worship: Israel's fathers followed futile things and became futile. Romans similarly describes people who fail to glorify God, become futile in thought and exchange divine glory for images, connecting distorted worship with the worshipers' own futility.

Jeremiah 2:6
They did not say, Where is the Lord who brought us up from Egypt’s land, who guided us in the wilderness, in a boundless and impassable land, a waterless and fruitless land, a land through which nothing passed and where no human being lived?
  Deuteronomy 8:14-16 — parallel remembrance of deliverance and guidance through a waterless wilderness: Jeremiah indicts those who fail to ask after the God who brought them from Egypt through an impassable, waterless wilderness. Deuteronomy warns against forgetting that same deliverance and guidance through a fearful wilderness where there was no water.

Jeremiah 2:7
I brought you into Carmel to eat its fruits and good things, but you entered and polluted My land and made My inheritance an abomination.
  Ezekiel 36:17-19 — parallel defilement of the land by Israel's conduct: Jeremiah contrasts God's gift of fruitful land with the people's pollution of His inheritance. Ezekiel likewise says Israel defiled its land through its way, idols and uncleannesses and describes the resulting judgment and dispersal.

Jeremiah 2:8
The priests did not say, Where is the Lord? Those holding to the law did not know Me; the shepherds acted ungodly toward Me; the prophets prophesied by Baal and followed what is useless.
  Micah 3:9-11 — parallel indictment of corrupt civil and religious leadership: Jeremiah indicts several leadership groups, including law handlers who do not know God and prophets who speak by Baal. Micah similarly indicts leaders, priests and prophets, specifying distorted justice and paid religious service rather than Jeremiah's particular Baal charge.

Jeremiah 2:9
Therefore I will still enter judgment with you, says the Lord, and with your children’s children I will enter judgment.
  Exodus 20:5-6 — related intergenerational judgment in a context of idolatry: Jeremiah announces judgment with the present people and their children's children after describing false worship. Exodus warns that the jealous God repays the sins of fathers upon later generations among those who hate Him; the connection does not assert that descendants are judged independently of their own conduct.

Jeremiah 2:11
Will nations exchange their gods, though these are not gods? But My people exchanged their glory, from which they will not benefit.
  Psalms 105:19-21 — parallel exchange of glory for futile worship: Jeremiah says God's people exchanged their glory and will derive no benefit. The psalm specifies the exchange of their glory for the likeness of a grass-eating calf and the forgetting of their saving God.

Jeremiah 2:12
Heaven was astonished at this and shuddered exceedingly, says the Lord.
  Isaiah 1:2-4 — related heavenly witness to Israel's rejection of God: Jeremiah portrays heaven as astonished and shuddering at the people's exchange. Isaiah summons heaven and earth to hear the Lord's charge that His children rejected and abandoned Him; the cosmic response differs from the summons to witness.

Jeremiah 2:13
For My people did two evil things: they abandoned Me, a spring of water of life, and dug broken cisterns for themselves that will not be able to hold water.
  Jeremiah 17:13 — repeated description of abandoning the divine fountain of life: Jeremiah 2 names the Lord as a spring of water of life abandoned for broken cisterns. Jeremiah 17 again charges people with abandoning the Lord, the fountain of life, and attaches shame to that departure.
  John 4:10-14 — related living-water imagery with a contrasting human water source: Jeremiah contrasts the abandoned divine spring with cisterns unable to hold water. Jesus contrasts ordinary well water with His gift of living water becoming a spring toward eternal life; the target develops the water imagery without reproducing the accusation or broken cisterns.

Jeremiah 2:14
Is Israel a slave, or is he house-born? Why has he become spoil?
  Exodus 4:22-23 — contrast between Israel as God's son and Israel reduced to spoil: Jeremiah questions whether Israel is a slave or house-born servant in view of his becoming spoil. Exodus identifies Israel as God's firstborn son and demands his release for divine service, supplying a contrasting account of Israel's identity rather than a direct explanation of this later calamity.

Jeremiah 2:15
Lions roared over him and raised their voice. They made his land a wilderness, and his cities were demolished so that none inhabited them.
  Jeremiah 4:7 — parallel lion imagery for devastating invasion: Lions roar over Israel, making his land wilderness and cities uninhabited. Jeremiah 4 portrays a lion leaving its enclosure to destroy nations and make the land desolate and cities uninhabited, repeating the animal and the consequences.

Jeremiah 2:17
Has abandoning Me not brought these things upon you? says the Lord your God.
  Judges 2:11-15 — parallel causal link between forsaking God and enemy domination: Jeremiah asks whether abandoning God brought the calamities upon the people. Judges explicitly joins Israel's forsaking the Lord and serving other gods with being delivered to plunderers and surrounding enemies.

Jeremiah 2:18
Now what do you have to do with Egypt’s road, to drink Gihon’s water? And what do you have to do with the Assyrians’ road, to drink river waters?
  Isaiah 30:1-3 — parallel challenge to seeking security through Egypt: Jeremiah challenges the journey on Egypt's road to drink Gihon's water and also mentions the Assyrian road. Isaiah condemns going to Egypt for Pharaoh's help without consulting God; the shared Egyptward reliance does not require renaming Jeremiah's river.

Jeremiah 2:19
Your apostasy will discipline you, and your evil will rebuke you. Know and see that abandoning Me is bitter for you, says the Lord your God; and I took no pleasure in you, says the Lord your God.
  Proverbs 5:21-23 — parallel wrongdoing becoming the wrongdoer's own restraint or punishment: Jeremiah says apostasy will discipline the people and their evil will rebuke them. Proverbs says lawless deeds hunt a person and his own sins bind him, similarly depicting wrongdoing as returning upon its agent.

Jeremiah 2:20
For long ago you shattered your yoke and tore apart your bonds, and said, I will not be enslaved, but I will go upon every high hill and beneath every shady tree; there I will abandon myself to my prostitution.
  Deuteronomy 12:2-3 — cultic counterpart to worship on hills and under trees: Jeremiah describes prostitution upon every high hill and beneath every shady tree after breaking bonds. Deuteronomy commands the destruction of foreign worship places on mountains, hills and under leafy trees, identifying the repeated locations as cultic settings.

Jeremiah 2:21
But I planted you as a fruitful vine, wholly genuine. How have you changed into bitterness, you foreign vine?
  Isaiah 5:1-7 — parallel failure of a carefully planted divine vine: God planted a fruitful, genuine vine that became bitter and foreign. Isaiah's vineyard likewise receives careful planting but produces thorns instead of expected grapes; the images express failed cultivation while preserving each text's different bad outcome.

Jeremiah 2:22
Even if you wash with natron and multiply cleansing herb for yourself, you remain stained in your injustices before Me, says the Lord.
  Job 9:30-31 — related washing imagery that cannot establish cleanness before God: Jeremiah says natron and cleansing herb cannot remove the stain of injustice before God. Job imagines washing with snow and clean hands yet being dipped in filth by God; his complaint and Jeremiah's accusation differ, but both deny that the imagined washing secures cleanness.

Jeremiah 2:25
Turn your foot from a rough road and your throat from thirst. But she said, I will be strong, because she had loved strangers and went after them.
  Hosea 2:7-9 — parallel pursuit of other lovers despite warning or obstruction: Jeremiah's woman answers the warning about her road and thirst with I will be strong, and the text says she loved strangers and followed them. Hosea's mother likewise declares that she will follow lovers, whose pursuit is then obstructed; the eventual return in Hosea is not asserted of Jeremiah's speaker.

Jeremiah 2:27
They said to wood, You are my father, and to stone, You gave birth to me. They turned their backs toward Me, not their faces; and in the time of their calamities they will say, Rise and save us!
  Isaiah 44:17-20 — parallel exposure of religious claims made toward manufactured objects: Jeremiah describes wood called a father and stone credited with birth, while people turn their backs on God. Isaiah describes a wooden remainder made into a god and addressed in prayer; this comparison concerns manufactured objects and avoids assigning Jeremiah's later cry for rescue specifically to the wood or stone.

Jeremiah 2:28
Where are your gods that you made for yourself? Will they rise and save you in the time of your affliction? For your gods were as numerous as your cities, Judah, and according to the number of Jerusalem’s streets they sacrificed to Baal.
  Deuteronomy 32:37-39 — parallel challenge that false gods should rise and rescue their worshipers: Jeremiah asks where Judah's manufactured gods are and whether they can rise and save in affliction. Deuteronomy similarly asks where the trusted gods are, challenges them to rise and help, and contrasts them with God's unrivaled power.

Jeremiah 2:30
In vain I struck your children; you accepted no discipline. A sword devoured your prophets like a destroying lion, and you did not fear.
  Amos 4:9-11 — parallel failure to respond to repeated divine discipline: Jeremiah says blows upon the children were vain because discipline was not accepted. Amos repeatedly recounts blows, sword and destruction followed by failure to turn to God, matching the indictment of unresponsive recipients without equating every punishment.

Jeremiah 2:31
Hear the Lord’s word. Thus says the Lord: Have I become a wilderness to Israel, or a land left barren? Why did My people say, We will not be ruled, and we will come to Thee no more?
  Deuteronomy 32:15-18 — related indictment of rejecting the God who sustained Israel: God asks whether He became a wilderness or barren land to Israel while the people reject His rule. Deuteronomy describes the nourished and satisfied people forsaking their Maker and forgetting the God who nourishes them, supplying the corresponding contrast between divine provision and rejection.

Jeremiah 2:32
Will a bride forget her adornment, or a virgin her breastband? Yet My people forgot Me for days beyond counting.
  Isaiah 49:14-16 — contrast between the people's forgetfulness and God's refusal to forget: Jeremiah compares the people's long forgetfulness of God with the implausibility of a bride or virgin forgetting adornment. Isaiah answers Zion's complaint of being forgotten with God's refusal to forget her; the direction of the forgetting is reversed.

Jeremiah 2:34
Even on your hands was found the blood of innocent lives. I did not find them in break-ins, but at every oak.
  Exodus 22:1-2 — legal contrast distinguishing innocent blood from a fatal break-in: Jeremiah charges bloodshed against innocent lives and explicitly says they were not found in break-ins. Exodus excludes murder in the death of a thief found breaking in and then distinguishes a case after sunrise; this supplies a legal contrast without resolving the target's later pronouns or Jeremiah's oak detail.

Jeremiah 2:35
And you said, I am innocent; let His fury turn away from me. Behold, I enter judgment with you because you say, I did not sin.
  1 John 1:8-10 — parallel rejection of a claim to have no sin: Jeremiah announces judgment precisely because the people say they did not sin. First John likewise rejects claims of having no sin or not having sinned and contrasts them with confession and cleansing.

Jeremiah 2:36
Why did you show such contempt by repeating your ways? You will also be put to shame by Egypt, as you were put to shame by Assyria.
  Isaiah 30:3-5 — parallel shame resulting from reliance on Egypt: Jeremiah predicts that Egypt will put the people to shame as Assyria did. Isaiah likewise says Pharaoh's shelter and the hoped-for Egyptian assistance will become shame and reproach rather than benefit.

Jeremiah 2:37
For from here too you will go out with your hands on your head, because the Lord rejected your hope, and you will not prosper in it.
  2 Samuel 13:19 — shared gesture of departing with hands on the head: Jeremiah predicts departure with hands upon the head after the Lord rejects the people's hope. Tamar likewise leaves with her hands on her head and cries out; the visible gesture is shared, while her victimization must not be treated as the same moral situation as Judah's judgment.

Jeremiah 3:1
If a man sends his wife away, and she leaves him and becomes another man’s, surely she will not return to him again? Would not that woman be utterly polluted? And you prostituted yourself with many shepherds—and were you returning to Me? says the Lord.
  Deuteronomy 24:1-4 — legal background to the divorce and remarriage question: Jeremiah asks whether a dismissed wife who becomes another man's will return to her former husband, before confronting the people's many shepherds. Deuteronomy forbids the former husband from taking such a wife back after the intervening marriage, supplying the stated legal analogy rather than resolving all the prophetic question's implications.

Jeremiah 3:2
Lift your eyes straight ahead and see: where have you not been defiled? You sat for them on the roads like a solitary crow, and polluted the land with your prostitution and your evils.
  Ezekiel 16:24-26 — parallel roadside prostitution imagery for covenant infidelity: Jeremiah portrays the woman seated on roads and polluting the land through prostitution. Ezekiel's address to Jerusalem describes prostitution chambers in broad streets and at road heads, giving a particular counterpart to the public locations without reproducing the solitary-crow image.

Jeremiah 3:3
You had many shepherds as a stumbling block for yourself. You had a prostitute’s appearance; you were shameless toward everyone.
  Proverbs 7:10-13 — parallel prostitute appearance and shameless face: Jeremiah charges the people with a prostitute's appearance and shamelessness. Proverbs describes the seductress with a prostitute's appearance and a shameless face; Jeremiah applies such language collectively rather than narrating the same individual encounter.

Jeremiah 3:4
Did you not call Me a household, a father and the leader of your virginity?
  Malachi 2:10-11 — related appeal to divine fatherhood amid covenant infidelity: Jeremiah recalls calling God a father and leader of virginity amid accusations of faithlessness. Malachi asks whether all have one Father and then indicts covenant profanation and devotion to foreign gods, likewise exposing a gap between the paternal relationship and conduct.

Jeremiah 3:6
The Lord said to me in the days of King Josiah, You saw what the dwelling of Israel did to Me. They went upon every high mountain and beneath every wooded tree and prostituted themselves there.
  Hosea 4:12-13 — parallel prostitution imagery linked with hilltop and tree-shaded worship: Jeremiah describes Israel's prostitution on high mountains and beneath wooded trees. Hosea similarly joins a spirit of prostitution and departure from God with sacrifices on mountaintops, hills and beneath named shade trees.

Jeremiah 3:7
After she prostituted herself in all these ways, I said, Return to Me. But she did not return, and faithless Judah saw her faithlessness.
  Hosea 11:1-2 — parallel divine summons met by departure rather than return: God calls Israel to return, but she does not. Hosea likewise recalls God's summons followed by departure from His presence and sacrifice to Baalim; Jeremiah additionally says Judah observed Israel's faithlessness.

Jeremiah 3:8
I saw that, concerning all the things in which the dwelling of Israel was caught committing adultery, I sent her away and gave a certificate of divorce into her hands. Yet faithless Judah did not fear; she too went and prostituted herself.
  Isaiah 50:1 — related divorce-document imagery for separation caused by unfaithfulness: Jeremiah explicitly says God sent Israel away and gave her a divorce certificate. Isaiah asks about the mother's divorce document and attributes her dismissal to lawless acts; the rhetorical question and Jeremiah's explicit giving of a document are kept distinct.

Jeremiah 3:9
Her prostitution came to be regarded as nothing, and she committed adultery with wood and stone.
  Ezekiel 23:37-39 — parallel association of adultery imagery with idolatry: Jeremiah describes adultery with wood and stone. Ezekiel combines adultery with reflections, child offerings and the explicit mention of idols, placing false worship within the same language of marital unfaithfulness without replacing the target's unusual wording.

Jeremiah 3:10
In all these things faithless Judah did not return to Me with her whole heart, but in falsehood.
  Isaiah 29:13 — parallel contrast between outward religious approach and a distant heart: Judah's return is described as false rather than wholehearted. Isaiah similarly contrasts honoring God with lips and a heart far from Him, providing a precise counterpart to outward response lacking inward fidelity.

Jeremiah 3:11
The Lord said to me, Israel has justified himself in comparison with faithless Judah.
  Ezekiel 16:51-52 — parallel relative justification through another community's greater wrongdoing: Jeremiah says Israel is justified in comparison with faithless Judah. Ezekiel says Jerusalem's greater lawlessness justified her sisters, including Samaria; neither comparison declares the less guilty community absolutely innocent.

Jeremiah 3:12
Go and read these words toward the north: Return to Me, dwelling of Israel, says the Lord. I will not set My face firmly against you, because I am merciful, says the Lord, and I will not be angry with you for ever.
  Psalms 102:8-10 — parallel divine mercy and anger that does not last forever: God invites Israel to return because He is merciful and will not remain angry forever. The psalm likewise calls Him compassionate and merciful and says He does not maintain wrath forever.

Jeremiah 3:13
Only recognize your injustice: you acted ungodly toward the Lord your God and spread your ways to strangers beneath every wooded tree, but you did not obey My voice, says the Lord.
  1 John 1:9 — parallel acknowledgment of wrongdoing as the proper response: Jeremiah demands recognition of injustice, disobedience and departure toward strangers. First John describes confession of sins together with divine forgiveness and cleansing; the shared response is acknowledgment rather than a denial of wrongdoing.

Jeremiah 3:14
Return, children who have withdrawn, says the Lord, because I will rule over you. I will take you, one from a city and two from a clan, and bring you into Zion,
  Isaiah 11:11-12 — parallel gathering of a scattered remnant: God promises to take small numbers from cities and clans and bring them to Zion. Isaiah likewise promises recovery of a remaining remnant and the gathering of Israel's lost and Judah's scattered people, with a wider geographical enumeration rather than Jeremiah's numerical expressions.

Jeremiah 3:15
and I will give you shepherds according to My heart, and they will shepherd you, shepherding with knowledge.
  Ezekiel 34:23-24 — parallel divine provision of shepherds for the restored people: Jeremiah promises shepherds according to God's heart who shepherd with knowledge. Ezekiel promises one shepherd, God's servant David, over the people; the divine provision is common, while the plural shepherds and the single named shepherd are distinct.

Jeremiah 3:17
In those days and at that time they will call Jerusalem the Lord’s throne, and all the nations will gather to her. They will no longer follow the thoughts of their evil heart.
  Isaiah 2:2-3 — parallel future gathering of nations to Jerusalem: Jeremiah says Jerusalem will be called the Lord's throne and nations will gather there. Isaiah likewise describes nations coming to the Lord's mountain and receiving His way, with law and the Lord's word proceeding from Zion and Jerusalem.

Jeremiah 3:18
In those days the house of Judah will join the house of Israel, and together they will come from the northern land and from all the regions to the land I gave their fathers as an inheritance.
  Ezekiel 37:21-24 — parallel reunion of divided Israel in the inherited land: Jeremiah promises that Judah will join Israel and return together from northern and other regions. Ezekiel promises gathering into Israel's land and an end to division into two nations or kingdoms, giving a corresponding account of reunion.

Jeremiah 3:19
And I said, May it be, Lord! For I will place you among children and give you a chosen land, an inheritance of God Almighty of nations. I said, You will call Me Father and will not turn away from Me.
  Isaiah 63:16-18 — parallel acknowledgment of God as Father in relation to inheritance: Jeremiah anticipates the people calling God Father and receiving a chosen inheritance. Isaiah's prayer calls the Lord Father and appeals concerning the tribes of His inheritance and the holy mountain; its petition differs from Jeremiah's promise.

Jeremiah 3:20
But as a woman deals faithlessly with the one living with her, so the house of Israel dealt faithlessly with Me, says the Lord.
  Hosea 2:4-7 — parallel portrayal of the people's infidelity through a broken marriage: Jeremiah compares Israel's faithlessness with a woman's faithlessness toward her partner. Hosea likewise portrays the mother through prostitution and the repudiated wife-husband relationship, using marital language for the people's departure.

Jeremiah 3:21
A voice was heard from lips, the weeping and supplication of Israel’s children, because they acted unjustly in their ways and forgot their holy God.
  Joel 2:12-13 — parallel weeping associated with returning from wrongdoing: Jeremiah hears the weeping and supplication of Israel's children after their unjust ways and forgetting God. Joel calls for return to God with weeping, mourning and a torn heart, giving the corresponding summons to penitential sorrow.

Jeremiah 3:22
Return, returning children, and I will heal your fractures. Behold, we will be Thy servants, because Thou art the Lord our God.
  Hosea 14:2-5 — parallel summons to return followed by a promise of healing: Jeremiah calls the returning children back and promises to heal their fractures. Hosea calls Israel to return with words and later promises to heal their dwellings; the return-and-healing pattern is shared while fractures and dwellings remain different expressions.

Jeremiah 3:23
Truly the hills and the power of the mountains were false; but through the Lord our God is Israel’s salvation.
  Psalms 120:1-2 — related contrast between looking toward mountains and finding help in God: Jeremiah declares the hills and the mountains' power false and locates Israel's salvation in the Lord. The psalm looks toward the mountains, asks where help comes from and answers that it comes from the Lord; it does not itself call the mountains false.

Jeremiah 3:24
Shame consumed our fathers’ labors from our youth: their sheep and calves, their sons and daughters.
  Deuteronomy 28:31-33 — parallel loss of livestock, children and the fruit of labor: Jeremiah says shame consumed the fathers' labors, sheep, calves, sons and daughters. Deuteronomy's curse similarly describes livestock and children given away and an unknown nation consuming the people's labor; it supplies a concrete loss pattern without identifying Jeremiah's personified shame as that nation.

Jeremiah 3:25
We lay down in our shame, and our dishonor covered us, because we sinned before our God, we and our fathers, from our youth until this day, and did not obey the voice of the Lord our God.
  Ezra 9:6-7 — parallel corporate confession of longstanding sin and shame: Jeremiah's speakers lie in shame and confess that they and their fathers sinned from youth to the present. Ezra likewise confesses accumulated lawlessness from the fathers' days to the present and speaks of shame before God.

Jeremiah 4:1
If Israel returns, says the Lord, he will return to Me; if he removes his abominations from his mouth and shows reverence before Me,
  Hosea 14:2-4 — parallel summons to return and renounce false worship: Jeremiah conditions Israel's return on removing abominations from the mouth and reverence before God. Hosea similarly calls Israel to return with words and renounce calling human handiwork gods, joining verbal response with rejection of false worship.

Jeremiah 4:2
and swears, ‘The Lord lives,’ with truth and in judgment and righteousness, then nations will bless in her, and in Him they will praise God in Jerusalem.
  Zechariah 8:16-17 — parallel demand for truth, just judgment and truthful oaths: Jeremiah describes swearing that the Lord lives with truth, judgment and righteousness. Zechariah likewise commands truth and peaceful judgment while rejecting love of a false oath; Jeremiah additionally anticipates blessing among nations.

Jeremiah 4:3
For thus says the Lord to the men of Judah and those dwelling in Jerusalem: Break up fallow ground for yourselves, and do not sow among thorns.
  Hosea 10:12 — related cultivation imagery for a righteous response to God: Jeremiah commands breaking fallow ground and avoiding sowing among thorns. Hosea commands sowing in righteousness, reaping life's fruit and seeking the Lord; its supplied wording does not repeat the fallow-ground command, but both frame the needed response through cultivation.

Jeremiah 4:4
Be circumcised to your God, and circumcise your hardness of heart, men of Judah and those dwelling in Jerusalem, lest My fury come out like fire and burn, and there be no one to extinguish it because of the evil of your practices.
  Deuteronomy 10:16 — repeated command to circumcise hardness of heart: Jeremiah calls Judah and Jerusalem to circumcise their hardness of heart. Deuteronomy gives the same inward command and adds that they must no longer stiffen their neck, clarifying the obedience sought through the image.
  Romans 2:28-29 — related interpretation of circumcision as an inward matter: Jeremiah directs circumcision toward hardness of heart rather than merely a physical act. Romans likewise speaks of circumcision of the heart in Spirit and inward identity; its argument about who is a Jew is not explicitly stated in Jeremiah's warning.

Jeremiah 4:5
Announce in Judah, and let it be heard in Jerusalem; say: Signal with a trumpet over the land, and cry loudly! Say: Gather, and let us enter the walled cities!
  Joel 2:1 — parallel trumpet alarm addressed to Zion's region: Jeremiah commands a trumpet signal and loud warning in Judah and Jerusalem as people gather toward walled cities. Joel similarly commands the trumpet in Zion and proclamation on the holy mountain, explaining the alarm through the announced Lord's day.

Jeremiah 4:6
Take up and flee to Zion; hurry, do not stand still, for I am bringing evils from the north and great crushing.
  Jeremiah 6:1 — repeated warning of northern evils and great crushing: Jeremiah urges flight to Zion because evils and great crushing are coming from the north. Jeremiah 6 repeats the northern direction and great crushing while addressing Benjamin and calling for warning signals.

Jeremiah 4:7
A lion has gone up from his enclosure; destroying nations, he has set out and left his place to make the land a desolation, and cities will be demolished so that they are not inhabited.
  Jeremiah 2:15 — parallel lion imagery producing desolated land and empty cities: The departing lion destroys nations and leaves the land desolate and cities without inhabitants. Jeremiah 2 likewise joins roaring lions with wilderness and demolished, uninhabited cities, giving a close earlier form of the same destructive imagery.

Jeremiah 4:8
For these things, gird yourselves with sackcloth, beat yourselves in mourning and wail, because the Lord’s fury has not turned away from you.
  Joel 1:13-15 — parallel summons to sackcloth and lament under divine calamity: Jeremiah commands sackcloth, mourning and wailing because the Lord's fury has not turned away. Joel likewise summons ministers to lament and sleep in sackcloth, then announces the near day of the Lord; the addressees and immediate reasons differ.

Jeremiah 4:9
And it will be on that day, says the Lord: the king’s heart and the rulers’ heart will perish, and the priests will be beside themselves and the prophets astonished.
  Ezekiel 7:26-27 — parallel collapse of civil and religious leadership during judgment: Jeremiah describes the failure of kings and rulers and the astonishment of priests and prophets. Ezekiel likewise depicts the loss of priestly law and elders' counsel, a sought prophetic vision and a ruler clothed in devastation, showing institutions overwhelmed by judgment.

Jeremiah 4:10
And I said: O Master, Lord, surely Thou hast indeed deceived this people and Jerusalem, saying, ‘You will have peace,’ and behold, the sword has reached to their soul!
  Jeremiah 6:14 — parallel exposure of proclaimed peace contradicted by reality: Jeremiah protests that a promise of peace is contradicted by a sword reaching the people's soul. Jeremiah 6 likewise exposes the claim of peace by asking where peace is; that later charge against those treating the fracture does not settle every question about Jeremiah's address to God here.

Jeremiah 4:13
Behold, he will come up like a cloud, and his chariots like a storm; his horses are swifter than eagles. Woe to us, because we suffer misery!
  Habakkuk 1:6-8 — parallel rapid invading cavalry compared with an eagle: Jeremiah describes stormlike chariots and horses swifter than eagles. Habakkuk's Chaldean warriors likewise rush from afar with horsemen flying like an eagle, using the bird's speed to depict invasion while retaining distinct animal comparisons.

Jeremiah 4:14
Wash your heart clean of evil, Jerusalem, so that you may be saved. How long will the schemes of your troubles exist within you?
  Isaiah 1:16-17 — parallel command to wash away moral evil: Jeremiah orders Jerusalem to wash its heart of evil rather than retain harmful schemes. Isaiah likewise commands washing, removal of evils from souls and cessation of evil deeds, making the cleansing explicitly moral.

Jeremiah 4:15
For the voice of one announcing will come from Dan, and trouble will be heard from Mount Ephraim.
  Jeremiah 8:16 — repeated northern warning associated with Dan: Jeremiah anticipates an announcing voice from Dan and trouble from Ephraim. Jeremiah 8 again locates an alarming sound at Dan, this time the horses of an approaching destroyer; the source's announcement and the target's cavalry sound remain distinct.

Jeremiah 4:16
Remind the nations: Behold, they have come! Announce in Jerusalem: Bands come from a distant land and have raised their voice against the cities of Judah.
  Isaiah 5:26-29 — parallel threat from distant forces: Jeremiah announces bands from a distant land raising their voice against Judah's cities. Isaiah likewise describes a signal among distant nations and their rapid, armed arrival, giving the military counterpart to the distant bands.

Jeremiah 4:17
Like those guarding a field they came against her all around, because you neglected Me, says the Lord.
  Jeremiah 6:3 — parallel encirclement of Jerusalem: Jeremiah compares the forces around the city to those guarding a field. Jeremiah 6 depicts shepherds and flocks pitching tents against Zion all around, repeating the surrounding position through a different rural image.

Jeremiah 4:18
Your ways and your practices have done these things to you. This is your evil: it is bitter; it has reached to your heart.
  Jeremiah 2:19 — repeated explanation that the people's own evil brings bitter consequences: Jeremiah attributes what happened to the people's ways and practices and calls the result bitter. Jeremiah 2 similarly says apostasy and evil will discipline and rebuke them and describes abandonment of God as bitter.

Jeremiah 4:19
My belly, my belly, I am in pain, and the senses of my heart! My soul rages, my heart is torn; I will not be silent, because my soul heard the sound of a trumpet, a cry of war.
  Isaiah 21:2-4 — parallel bodily pain and a troubled heart amid an ominous message: Jeremiah speaks of pain in the belly, a torn heart and the heard cry of war. Isaiah's speaker likewise describes a harsh vision, bodily weakness, labor pains and a wandering, fearful heart; the particular vision and Jeremiah's trumpet report are distinct.

Jeremiah 4:20
It calls out misery and crushing, because the whole land has suffered misery; suddenly the tent has suffered misery, and my tent coverings have been torn apart.
  Jeremiah 10:20 — repeated lament over a ruined tent and torn coverings: Jeremiah laments sudden misery affecting the tent and torn coverings. Jeremiah 10 again describes the tent's misery and destruction and all its coverings torn, then adds the loss of sons and sheep.

Jeremiah 4:21
How long will I see people fleeing while hearing the sound of trumpets?
  Jeremiah 4:19 — same-chapter recurrence of the trumpet heard in distress: The question about how long flight and trumpet sounds will continue resumes the earlier voice's distress at a trumpet and cry of war. The explicit repeated sound connects the lamenting question with the preceding bodily anguish.

Jeremiah 4:22
For the leaders of My people did not know Me; they are foolish sons and not discerning. They are wise at doing evil, but did not learn to do well.
  Romans 16:19 — contrast between skill in evil and wisdom in goodness: Jeremiah calls the leaders of the people wise at evil but untrained in doing good. Paul desires the opposite orientation: wisdom about good and innocence about evil, furnishing a sharply reversed moral pattern.

Jeremiah 4:23
I looked upon the earth, and behold, nothing; and into heaven, and its lights were not there.
  Genesis 1:1-5 — literary contrast with the appearance of earth and light in creation: Jeremiah looks at earth and sees nothing, and finds heaven's lights absent. Genesis moves from the unseen, unformed earth and darkness to God's creation of light; the comparison reads Jeremiah's desolation against creation without substituting Genesis's wording for Jeremiah's.

Jeremiah 4:24
I saw the mountains, and they were trembling, and all the hills were being shaken.
  Nahum 1:5-6 — parallel trembling of mountains and hills before divine wrath: Jeremiah sees mountains trembling and hills shaken in a scene explained through the Lord's anger. Nahum likewise describes mountains and hills shaking before Him and asks who can resist His furious wrath.

Jeremiah 4:25
I looked, and behold, there was no human, and all the birds of heaven were terrified.
  Genesis 1:20-27 — contrast with creation's birds and humanity: Jeremiah's vision contains no human and birds of heaven in terror. Genesis describes the making and blessing of birds and the making of humanity; human absence and frightened birds invert the ordered, populated creation without saying Jeremiah's birds are absent.

Jeremiah 4:26
I saw, and behold, Carmel was a wilderness, and all the cities were burned with fire before the Lord, and they vanished before His furious anger.
  Isaiah 32:13-15 — parallel change of Carmel or Chermel into wilderness: Jeremiah sees Carmel become wilderness amid burned cities. Isaiah's supplied text likewise says Chermel will become wilderness after describing abandoned houses and a deserted city, providing the same direction of change with a different form of the place-name.

Jeremiah 4:27
Thus says the Lord: The whole land will be a wilderness, but I will certainly not make a complete end.
  Jeremiah 5:18 — repeated limit on destruction: no complete end: Jeremiah announces wilderness for the land but expressly excludes a complete end. Jeremiah 5 repeats God's promise not to bring the people to a complete end amid the invasion warnings.

Jeremiah 4:28
For these things let the earth mourn and let heaven above grow dark, because I have spoken and will not change My mind; I have set Myself in motion and will not turn back from it.
  Isaiah 14:24-27 — parallel declaration that God's announced purpose will not be reversed: Jeremiah explains mourning and darkened heaven by God's settled speech and refusal to turn back. Isaiah likewise says what God spoke and counseled will stand and asks who can turn back His hand, though that oracle specifically concerns Assyria.

Jeremiah 4:29
At the sound of a horseman and a drawn bow the whole countryside withdrew; they entered caves and hid in groves and climbed onto the rocks. Every city was abandoned; no human dwells in them.
  Isaiah 2:19-21 — parallel refuge in caves and rocks under overwhelming threat: Jeremiah describes flight into caves, groves and rocks at cavalry and bows. Isaiah likewise describes entry into rock clefts and holes before the Lord's terror; the refuges correspond while the stated immediate threats differ.

Jeremiah 4:30
And you, what will you do if you put on scarlet and adorn yourself with golden ornaments, and if you paint your eyes with antimony? Your adornment is in vain; your lovers have rejected you; they seek your soul.
  Ezekiel 23:40-45 — parallel painted eyes and ornaments within a condemned lovers image: Jeremiah describes scarlet, gold ornaments and eyes painted with antimony as futile because the lovers seek her life. Ezekiel similarly describes painted eyes, ornaments and approaching men within its adultery accusation; it subsequently announces punishment rather than repeating Jeremiah's exact rejection by lovers.

Jeremiah 4:31
For I heard a voice like a woman in labor, your groaning like one giving birth for the first time, the voice of the daughter of Zion. She will grow faint and let her hands fall: Woe to me, because my soul fails over those killed!
  Micah 4:9-10 — parallel portrayal of Zion's distress as childbirth: Jeremiah hears Zion groaning like a woman giving birth for the first time and failing over the killed. Micah likewise addresses Zion through labor pains and a woman giving birth, adding exile and promised rescue rather than Jeremiah's particular lament over deaths.

Jeremiah 5:1
Run through Jerusalem’s streets and see and learn, and search her squares: if you find a man, if there is one doing justice and seeking faithfulness, then I will be merciful to them, says the Lord.
  Genesis 18:26-32 — parallel mercy for a city conditional on finding righteous people: Jeremiah calls for a search for even one person doing justice and seeking faithfulness, with mercy promised if found. Genesis likewise presents sparing Sodom for righteous inhabitants, with the requested numbers descending to ten rather than Jeremiah's one.

Jeremiah 5:2
They say, ‘The Lord lives’; do they not therefore swear on falsehoods?
  Jeremiah 4:2 — contrast between truthful and false use of the same oath: Jeremiah 5 exposes falsehood beneath the oath that the Lord lives. Jeremiah 4 describes that same oath uttered with truth, judgment and righteousness, making the required quality of the oath explicit.

Jeremiah 5:3
Lord, Thine eyes are toward faithfulness. Thou didst scourge them, and they did not suffer; Thou broughtest them to an end, and they would not accept instruction. They hardened their faces beyond rock and would not return.
  Amos 4:9-11 — parallel resistance to correction despite repeated punishment: Jeremiah says the people refuse instruction and refuse to return even after scourging and devastation. Amos likewise recounts destructive blows and repeatedly says that the people still did not turn to God.

Jeremiah 5:4
And I said: Perhaps they are poor, because they had no ability, because they did not know the Lord’s way and God’s judgment.
  Jeremiah 5:5 — same-chapter contrast between poor and prosperous people in disobedience: The speaker first considers people who may be poor and lack ability and knowledge of God's way. He then proposes going to prosperous people who recognized that way and judgment, yet reports that they broke the yoke too; the shared failure cuts across the stated contrast in means and knowledge.

Jeremiah 5:5
I will go to the prosperous and speak to them, for they recognized the Lord’s way and God’s judgment. And behold, with one accord they broke the yoke and tore the bonds.
  Psalms 2:2-3 — parallel defiance expressed as tearing bonds and rejecting a yoke: The prosperous people together break the yoke and tear bonds despite recognizing God's way. The psalm likewise portrays kings and rulers opposing the Lord and His anointed by proposing to tear bonds and cast off the yoke; the groups are not identical.

Jeremiah 5:6
Therefore a lion from the woodland struck them, and a wolf destroyed them as far as the houses, and a leopard kept watch over their cities. All who leave them will be hunted, because they multiplied their impieties and grew strong in their turnings away.
  Hosea 13:7-9 — parallel predatory animals in the judgment of Israel: Jeremiah describes lion, wolf and leopard attacks because of multiplied impieties. Hosea likewise threatens Israel through panther, leopard, distressed bear and devouring forest lion cubs; the overlapping predators reinforce the judgment image without making the animal lists identical.

Jeremiah 5:7
For which of these things should I be merciful to you? Your sons abandoned Me and swore by those who are not gods. And I fed them fully, and they committed adultery and lodged in prostitutes’ houses.
  Deuteronomy 32:15-18 — parallel abandonment of God after being filled by His provision: Jeremiah contrasts feeding the people fully with their abandonment, false oaths and adultery. Deuteronomy likewise describes Jacob eating and being filled before forsaking the nourishing God and turning to foreign gods.

Jeremiah 5:8
They became horses mad for mares; each neighed after his neighbor’s wife.
  Exodus 20:17 — commandment counterpart to desire for a neighbor's wife: Jeremiah compares the people's desire for their neighbors' wives to horses mad for mares. Exodus explicitly prohibits coveting a neighbor's wife, supplying the command violated by the depicted desire.

Jeremiah 5:9
Shall I not visit for these things? says the Lord. Or will not My soul take vengeance on such a nation?
  Jeremiah 9:7-8 — repeated refrain of visitation and vengeance for wrongdoing: Jeremiah asks whether God will not visit and take vengeance after the accusations of adultery. Jeremiah 9 repeats the same two questions after deceitful speech and concealed enmity, applying the recurring judgment refrain to another form of wrong.

Jeremiah 5:10
Go up against her battlements and tear them down, but do not make a complete end. Leave her supports, because they belong to the Lord.
  Jeremiah 4:27 — parallel command or promise limiting total destruction: Jeremiah 5 commands the tearing down of battlements while forbidding a complete end and preserving supports belonging to the Lord. Jeremiah 4 similarly announces desolation while excluding a complete end; its general limit corresponds without turning the supports into vine branches.

Jeremiah 5:11
For the house of Israel and the house of Judah have utterly rejected Me, says the Lord.
  Jeremiah 3:8-10 — parallel indictment of both Israel and Judah: Jeremiah 5 says both houses utterly rejected God. Jeremiah 3 separately describes Israel's adultery and dismissal and Judah's fearless imitation and false return, supplying the two-house pattern behind the collective charge.

Jeremiah 5:12
They lied to their own Lord and said: These things are not so; evils will not come upon us, and we will not see sword or famine.
  Amos 9:10 — parallel denial that calamity will reach God's people: Jeremiah reports the denial that evils, sword or famine will come. Amos likewise condemns sinners among God's people who say evils will not approach them and expressly announces death by sword.

Jeremiah 5:13
Our prophets became wind, and the Lord’s word was not in them. Thus it will be for them.
  Ezekiel 13:3-8 — parallel prophecy lacking divine authorization: Jeremiah calls the prophets wind and says the Lord's word was not in them. Ezekiel similarly condemns prophets speaking from their own hearts, seeing false things and claiming the Lord spoke although He did not send them.

Jeremiah 5:14
Therefore thus says the Lord Almighty: Because you spoke this utterance, behold, I have made My words fire in your mouth, and this people wood, and it will devour them.
  Jeremiah 23:28-29 — repeated image of God's word as fire: God makes His words fire in Jeremiah's mouth and the people wood to be consumed. Jeremiah 23 again describes the divine words as blazing fire while contrasting them with false prophetic dreams; the wood metaphor is specific to Jeremiah 5.

Jeremiah 5:15
Behold, I am bringing a nation upon you from far away, house of Israel, says the Lord, a nation whose language’s sound you will not understand.
  Deuteronomy 28:49-50 — parallel distant invading nation with unintelligible speech: Jeremiah announces a nation from far away whose language Israel will not understand. Deuteronomy's curse likewise brings a distant nation whose speech is not understood, adding its merciless character.

Jeremiah 5:17
and they will devour your harvest and your bread, and devour your sons and daughters, and devour your sheep and calves, and devour your vineyards and fig orchards and olive groves; and with a sword they will thresh your fortified cities, in which you have trusted.
  Deuteronomy 28:51-52 — parallel consumption of produce and livestock and defeat of trusted fortifications: Jeremiah describes an invader consuming the harvest, animals and orchard produce and threshing fortified cities trusted by the people. Deuteronomy likewise describes cattle and produce consumed and the collapse of high fortified walls in which they trust.

Jeremiah 5:18
And it will be in those days, says the Lord your God: I will certainly not bring you to a complete end.
  Amos 9:8 — parallel judgment that stops short of completely removing Israel: Jeremiah promises that the invasion will not bring the people to a complete end. Amos similarly announces removal of the sinful kingdom but expressly says Jacob's house will not be completely removed.

Jeremiah 5:19
And when you say, ‘For what reason has the Lord our God done all these things to us?’ you will say to them: Because you served foreign gods in your land, so you will serve foreigners in a land not yours.
  Deuteronomy 28:47-48 — parallel transfer into enemy service after failure to serve God faithfully: Jeremiah explains foreign servitude by service to foreign gods in the people's own land. Deuteronomy likewise contrasts failure to serve the Lord properly with enforced service to enemies in deprivation, preserving the different descriptions of the original disobedience.

Jeremiah 5:21
Hear these things now, foolish and senseless people: they have eyes and do not see; they have ears and do not hear.
  Isaiah 6:9-10 — parallel unseeing eyes and unhearing ears among God's people: Jeremiah addresses a foolish people who have eyes but do not see and ears but do not hear. Isaiah's commission similarly describes hearing without understanding and seeing without perception, expanding the image through dull hearts and closed eyes.

Jeremiah 5:22
Will you not fear Me? says the Lord. Or will you not show reverence before Me, who appointed sand as the sea’s boundary, an eternal command it will not cross? It will be stirred and will not prevail, and its waves will roar, and it will not cross it.
  Job 38:8-11 — parallel divine establishment of a boundary the sea cannot cross: Jeremiah names sand as the sea's boundary and says roaring waves cannot cross God's command. In Job, God likewise sets the sea's boundaries with bars and gates and tells its waves they may go no farther; the boundary's imagery differs.

Jeremiah 5:23
But this people acquired an unheeding and disobedient heart, and they turned aside and went away.
  Jeremiah 7:24-26 — parallel disobedient hearts and departure from God's direction: Jeremiah 5 says the people acquired an unheeding, disobedient heart and turned away. Jeremiah 7 likewise joins failure to hear with following the evil heart's thoughts, moving backward and hardening the neck.

Jeremiah 5:24
And they did not say in their heart: Let us fear the Lord our God, who gives us early and late rain at the time of fulfillment of the harvest’s command, and He kept it for us.
  Deuteronomy 11:13-15 — parallel divine gift of seasonal early and late rains for harvest: Jeremiah indicts failure to fear the God who gives early and late rains and preserves the harvest's appointed fulfillment. Deuteronomy promises those seasonal rains and the gathering of grain, wine and oil in connection with faithful service.

Jeremiah 5:25
Your lawless acts turned these things aside, and your sins removed good things from you.
  Deuteronomy 11:16-17 — parallel loss of agricultural good through disobedience: Jeremiah explains the loss of the preceding gifts through lawless acts and sins. Deuteronomy likewise warns that turning to other gods will lead to closed heaven, withheld rain and failed produce.

Jeremiah 5:26
For impious people were found among My people, and they set traps to destroy men and were catching them.
  Psalms 139:5-6 — parallel wicked people laying traps for human victims: Jeremiah finds impious people setting traps to destroy and catch men. The psalm likewise asks rescue from unjust people who devise stumbling and hide traps and cords for the speaker's feet.

Jeremiah 5:27
Like a set trap full of birds, so their houses are full of deceit; therefore they became great and rich.
  Proverbs 1:13-19 — parallel enrichment of houses through predatory wrongdoing: Jeremiah compares houses full of deceit with a bird-filled trap and connects this with wealth. Proverbs portrays violent people seeking to fill their houses with spoils and later mentions nets for birds, placing acquisitive violence and trapping imagery within one warning.

Jeremiah 5:28
And they transgressed judgment; they did not judge an orphan’s case, and they were not judging a widow’s case.
  Isaiah 1:23 — parallel denial of justice to orphans and widows: Jeremiah charges failure to judge the orphan's and widow's cases. Isaiah similarly indicts rulers who do not judge for orphans or attend to widows' judgment, giving a close judicial counterpart.

Jeremiah 5:29
Shall I not visit for these things? says the Lord. Or will not My soul take vengeance on such a nation?
  Jeremiah 9:7-8 — repeated visitation-and-vengeance refrain following social wrongdoing: Jeremiah asks whether God will not visit and take vengeance after the account of predatory enrichment and failed justice. Jeremiah 9 repeats the two questions after deceit and concealed enmity, extending the same refrain to another social offense.

Jeremiah 5:31
The prophets prophesy unjust things, and the priests applauded with their hands, and My people loved it so. And what will you do after these things?
  2 Timothy 4:3-4 — parallel public preference for false religious speech: Jeremiah reports unjust prophecy, priestly applause and a people who love that arrangement. Second Timothy similarly predicts people preferring teachers suited to their desires and turning from truth to myths; it parallels the audience's complicity without identifying the later teachers with Jeremiah's prophets.

Jeremiah 6:1
Take courage, sons of Benjamin, from within Jerusalem, and signal with a trumpet in Tekoa, and raise a sign above Beth-haccherem, because evils have looked out from the north, and great crushing is taking place,
  Jeremiah 4:5-6 — repeated trumpet warning of northern calamity: Jeremiah 6 calls for a trumpet in Tekoa and a sign above Beth-haccherem because of northern evils and crushing. Jeremiah 4 likewise joins a trumpet alarm with northern evils and great crushing, while directing flight toward Zion.

Jeremiah 6:3
Shepherds and their flocks will come to her, and they will pitch tents against her all around, and each will shepherd with his own hand.
  Jeremiah 4:17 — parallel rural imagery for forces surrounding the city: The shepherds and their flocks pitch tents against Zion all around. Jeremiah 4 likewise compares the encircling forces to those guarding a field, giving a corresponding rural image of military encirclement.

Jeremiah 6:4
Prepare yourselves for war against her! Rise, and let us go up against her at noon! Woe to us, because the day has declined, because evening’s shadows are failing!
  Jeremiah 6:5 — same-chapter change from a proposed noon assault to a night assault: The call to attack at noon is interrupted by the complaint that daylight has declined. The following call proposes rising in the night to destroy the foundations, specifically continuing the attack plan despite the change of time.

Jeremiah 6:5
Rise, and let us go up in the night and destroy her foundations!
  Hosea 8:14 — parallel threat against city foundations: Jeremiah's attacking voices propose destroying the city's foundations. Hosea likewise links Judah's multiplied walled cities and Israel's forgotten Maker with divine fire that devours city foundations; Hosea does not specify Jeremiah's night attack.

Jeremiah 6:6
For thus says the Lord: Cut down her trees; pour out a force upon Jerusalem. O false city, all oppression is within her!
  Deuteronomy 20:19-20 — legal counterpart to cutting trees during a siege: Jeremiah commands cutting the city's trees and pouring a force upon Jerusalem. Deuteronomy discusses cutting trees to build siegeworks and restricts the practice to trees without edible fruit; Jeremiah does not identify the tree types, so the link does not presume a violation of that restriction.

Jeremiah 6:8
you will be instructed, Jerusalem, lest My soul depart from you, lest I make you an impassable land that will not be inhabited.
  Zephaniah 3:2-7 — parallel discipline opposed to the city's destruction: Jeremiah speaks of Jerusalem being instructed lest God's soul depart and it become uninhabitable. Zephaniah likewise indicts a city's refusal of discipline and reports a promise against destruction tied to fear of God and acceptance of discipline.

Jeremiah 6:9
For thus says the Lord: Glean, glean Israel’s remnants like a vine; turn back like the grape-gatherer to his basket.
  Isaiah 17:4-6 — related harvesting imagery for a diminished Israel: Jeremiah commands further gleaning of Israel's remnants as a vine is gleaned. Isaiah similarly portrays diminished Jacob through a gathered harvest, a remaining stalk and sparse olives; the source's further gleaning and the target's residual produce are related but distinct aspects of the image.

Jeremiah 6:10
To whom shall I speak and testify, and he will hear? Behold, their ears are uncircumcised, and they cannot hear. Behold, the Lord’s utterance became a reproach to them; they certainly will not wish to hear it.
  Acts 7:51 — parallel uncircumcised ears resistant to divine speech: Jeremiah says uncircumcised ears cannot hear and refuse the Lord's utterance. Acts likewise addresses people as uncircumcised in hearts and ears and accuses them of resisting the Holy Spirit, using the same bodily image for resistance.

Jeremiah 6:11
And I filled up My fury and held back and did not bring them to an end. I will pour it upon little children outside and upon a gathering of young men together, because man and woman will be seized, the elder with one full of days.
  Jeremiah 7:20 — repeated image of divine fury poured upon the land's inhabitants: Jeremiah 6 threatens an outpouring of fury across children, young people, men and women, and elders. Jeremiah 7 likewise announces wrath and fury poured upon the place, extending its list from people to livestock, trees and produce.

Jeremiah 6:12
And their houses will be transferred to others, fields and their wives together, because I will stretch out My hand against those dwelling in this land, says the Lord.
  Deuteronomy 28:30-33 — parallel dispossession of houses, fields and families: Jeremiah announces that houses, fields and wives will pass to others. Deuteronomy's curse similarly describes another man taking a wife, a builder not occupying his house and strangers consuming the land's produce and receiving the children.

Jeremiah 6:13
For from their small to their great, all have carried out lawless deeds; from priest to false prophet, all have done false things.
  Micah 3:9-11 — parallel corruption reaching priests and prophets: Jeremiah's charge of widespread lawless deeds expressly includes priests and false prophets. Micah likewise indicts leaders, priests and prophets for distorted justice and paid religious activity; its list supplies particular leadership examples rather than Jeremiah's small-to-great formula.

Jeremiah 6:14
And they treated My people’s fracture with contempt, saying, ‘Peace, peace’; and where is peace?
  Ezekiel 13:10 — parallel false proclamation of peace where there is none: Jeremiah condemns dismissive treatment of the people's fracture with the cry of peace and asks where peace is. Ezekiel explicitly describes prophets misleading the people by saying peace when there is no peace, then develops the image of a coated wall.

Jeremiah 6:15
They were put to shame because they failed, and even so they were not at all ashamed and did not recognize their dishonor. Therefore they will fall in their fall, and at the time of their visitation they will perish, said the Lord.
  Jeremiah 3:3 — recurring indictment of shamelessness: Jeremiah 6 says the people failed yet did not feel shame or recognize dishonor. Jeremiah 3 similarly calls the addressee shameless in its prostitution imagery; the later verse adds the threatened fall at visitation.

Jeremiah 6:16
Thus says the Lord: Stand upon the roads and see, and ask for the Lord’s eternal paths and see which is the good way, and walk in it, and you will find purification for your souls. And they said, ‘We will not go.’
  Jeremiah 18:15 — recurring ancient-road imagery amid refusal of God's way: Jeremiah 6 calls for the Lord's eternal paths and the good way, but receives refusal. Jeremiah 18 joins forgetting God with weakness in the people's ways, the age-old tracks, and stepping upon paths with no road for travel; both connect the road imagery with failed fidelity, without substituting rest for Jeremiah 6's purification.

Jeremiah 6:17
I have appointed watchers over you: Hear the trumpet’s sound! And they said, ‘We will not hear.’
  Ezekiel 33:2-5 — parallel ignored warning trumpet from an appointed watcher: Jeremiah reports God's appointment of watchers and the people's refusal to hear the trumpet. Ezekiel describes an appointed watchman sounding a trumpet and a hearer who fails to heed it and is overtaken by the sword.

Jeremiah 6:19
Hear, earth! Behold, I am bringing evils upon this people, the fruit of their turning away, because they did not attend to My words and rejected My law.
  Proverbs 1:29-31 — parallel rejection of instruction yielding the fruit of one's own way: Jeremiah calls the coming evils the fruit of turning away because the people rejected God's words and law. Wisdom's speech in Proverbs likewise joins rejected wisdom, counsel and reproof with eating the fruits of one's own way.

Jeremiah 6:20
Why do you bring Me frankincense from Sheba and cinnamon from a distant land? Your whole burnt offerings are not acceptable, and your sacrifices did not please Me.
  Isaiah 1:11-15 — parallel rejection of incense and sacrifices from a culpable people: Jeremiah says imported incense, burnt offerings and sacrifices do not please God. Isaiah likewise refuses burnt offerings and calls incense an abomination while exposing blood-filled hands; neither passage treats costly ritual gifts as a substitute for rectitude.

Jeremiah 6:22
Thus says the Lord: Behold, a people comes from the north, and nations will be roused from the earth’s extremity.
  Isaiah 5:26 — parallel arrival of peoples summoned from distant extremities: Jeremiah announces a people from the north and nations roused from the earth's extremity. Isaiah likewise describes a signal raised among distant nations and a whistle from the earth's end for their rapid arrival; it does not repeat the northern direction.

Jeremiah 6:23
They will grasp bow and spear; it is ruthless and will show no mercy. Its voice is like a surging sea; on horses and chariots it will draw up like fire for war against you, daughter of Zion.
  Isaiah 5:28-30 — parallel weapons and surging-sea sound within invasion accounts: Jeremiah depicts bows, spears, horses and chariots with a voice like a surging sea. Isaiah likewise describes bows, horses and chariot wheels and then a cry like a surging sea; this preserves the target's unresolved pronoun rather than forcing one actor onto every clause.

Jeremiah 6:24
We heard the report of them; our hands were paralyzed. Affliction seized us, labor pains like a woman giving birth.
  Jeremiah 4:31 — parallel disabling distress compared with childbirth: Jeremiah 6 describes paralyzed hands and labor pains after hearing the invasion report. Jeremiah 4 likewise depicts Zion fainting, letting her hands fall and groaning like a woman in labor over those killed.

Jeremiah 6:25
Do not go out into a field or walk on the roads, because the enemies’ sword dwells all around.
  Lamentations 1:20 — related danger from the sword outside: Jeremiah warns against fields and roads because the enemies' sword is all around. Lamentations similarly describes bereavement by the sword outside, likened to death in the house; the target's additional domestic comparison is not a promise of safety indoors.

Jeremiah 6:26
Daughter of My people, gird yourself with sackcloth; sprinkle yourself with ashes. Make mourning for yourself for a beloved one, a pitiful lament, because misery will suddenly come upon you.
  Amos 8:10 — parallel sackcloth and mourning for a beloved one: Jeremiah commands sackcloth, ashes and mourning for a beloved one because misery is coming. Amos likewise replaces celebrations with mourning, puts sackcloth on waists and describes mourning for a beloved one; neither supplied verse requires changing beloved to only son.

Jeremiah 6:27
I have made you a tester among tested peoples, and you will know Me when I test their way.
  Proverbs 17:3 — related metal-testing imagery for divine assessment: God declares that He has made the addressee a tester and speaks of testing the people's way, followed in context by metals and unsuccessful refining. Proverbs compares silver and gold tested in a furnace with hearts chosen before the Lord, giving a related assessment image while preserving its word chosen.

Jeremiah 6:28
All are unheeding, walking crookedly, bronze and iron; all are corrupted.
  Ezekiel 22:18-20 — parallel portrayal of Israel through base metals in a refining context: Jeremiah calls the unheeding people bronze and iron and says all are corrupted. Ezekiel likewise describes Israel's house as a mixture including copper and iron among silver, then gathers the mixture for melting in judgment.

Jeremiah 6:29
The bellows failed from the fire; the lead failed. In vain the silversmith works silver; their wickedness was not melted away.
  Ezekiel 24:11-13 — parallel heating that fails to remove moral impurity: Jeremiah's silversmith works vainly because the people's wickedness is not melted away. Ezekiel's heated cauldron likewise retains its abundant rust and is explained through uncleanness, sharing failed purification while using a different metal vessel image.

Jeremiah 6:30
Call them rejected silver, because the Lord rejected them.
  Isaiah 1:22 — parallel disapproved silver as an image of corruption: Jeremiah calls the people rejected silver because the Lord rejected them. Isaiah likewise tells Zion that her silver is unapproved, using failed metallic quality in an indictment of the city's condition.

Jeremiah 7:3
Thus says the Lord, Israel’s God: Correct your ways and your practices, and I will settle you in this place.
  Isaiah 1:16-20 — parallel correction of conduct linked with continued enjoyment of the land: Jeremiah calls for corrected ways and practices with a promise of settlement in the place. Isaiah similarly commands moral cleansing and learning good, then contrasts eating the land's good things through willing obedience with destruction through refusal.

Jeremiah 7:4
Do not put your confidence in yourselves on false words, because they will not benefit you at all when they say, ‘The Lord’s temple, the Lord’s temple it is.’
  Micah 3:11-12 — parallel rejection of confidence in sacred presence despite wrongdoing: Jeremiah warns against trusting the repeated temple claim. Micah likewise exposes leaders claiming the Lord is among them and no calamity can come, then announces ruin for Zion, Jerusalem and the mountain of the house.

Jeremiah 7:5
For if you truly correct your ways and practices and truly execute judgment between a man and his neighbor,
  Zechariah 8:16 — parallel requirement to judge truly between neighbors: Jeremiah makes genuine judgment between a person and a neighbor part of corrected conduct. Zechariah likewise commands each to speak truth to a neighbor and render peaceful judgment in the gates.

Jeremiah 7:6
and do not oppress a newcomer, orphan, or widow, and do not shed innocent blood in this place, and do not follow foreign gods to your own harm,
  Zechariah 7:9-10 — parallel prohibition of oppressing newcomers, orphans and widows: Jeremiah expressly prohibits oppression of the newcomer, orphan and widow. Zechariah names the same vulnerable groups, adds the poor and places their protection alongside righteous judgment and compassion.

Jeremiah 7:7
then I will settle you in this place, in the land I gave your fathers, from age to age.
  Deuteronomy 4:1 — parallel connection between obedience and inheriting the given land: Jeremiah promises continued settlement in the fathers' land after the concrete duties in the preceding verses. Deuteronomy likewise links hearing and doing God's ordinances with living, entering and inheriting the land given by the God of their fathers.

Jeremiah 7:8
But if you have trusted false words from which you will receive no benefit,
  Jeremiah 23:32 — parallel warning that false religious words bring no benefit: Jeremiah says the trusted false words will yield no benefit. Jeremiah 23 likewise condemns prophets' lies and false dreams and expressly says they bring the people no benefit, supplying a corresponding critique of misleading religious speech.

Jeremiah 7:9
and murder and commit adultery and steal and swear unjustly, and you burned incense to Baal and followed foreign gods whom you do not know, to your own harm,
  Exodus 20:13-16 — commandment background to the charges of adultery, theft and murder: Jeremiah's list includes murder, adultery and theft alongside unjust oaths and foreign worship. The supplied commandments explicitly forbid adultery, theft and murder and also false witness; the link identifies the shared prohibitions without equating every oath with courtroom testimony.

Jeremiah 7:10
and you came and stood before Me in the house over which My name has been called and said, ‘We have kept away, so as not to do all these abominations,’
  Isaiah 1:12-15 — parallel confrontation of culpable worshipers appearing before God: Jeremiah depicts people standing in God's named house and claiming to have kept away from abominations after the preceding accusations. Isaiah likewise confronts those appearing in His court while their hands are full of blood, challenging the supposed adequacy of their religious appearance.

Jeremiah 7:11
is My house, over which My name has been called there, a robbers’ cave before you? And behold, I have seen, says the Lord.
  Matthew 21:12-13 — reuse of the robbers-cave charge in Jesus' temple action: Jeremiah asks whether God's named house has become a robbers' cave before the people. Jesus repeats the robbers' cave charge after expelling buyers and sellers from the temple, joining it with the description of the house as a house of prayer.

Jeremiah 7:12
For go to My place in Shiloh, where I formerly made My name dwell, and see what I did to it because of the evil of My people Israel.
  Psalms 77:56-61 — historical counterpart to God's rejection of Shiloh: Jeremiah directs the people to Shiloh, where God formerly made His name dwell, as evidence of what Israel's evil brought about. The psalm explicitly recounts the people's infidelity and God's rejection of Shiloh's tent, His dwelling among humans.

Jeremiah 7:13
And now, because you did all these works, and I spoke to you and you did not hear Me, and I called you and you did not answer,
  Isaiah 65:11-12 — parallel ignored divine call and speech before judgment: Jeremiah says God spoke and called but the people did not hear or answer. Isaiah similarly explains judgment by God's call meeting disobedience and His speech meeting disregard, within an indictment of abandoned worship.

Jeremiah 7:14
