I will also do to this house over which My name has been called, in which you have trusted, and to the place I gave you and your fathers, as I did to Shiloh.
  Micah 3:12 — parallel threat to Jerusalem and its sacred site: Jeremiah threatens the house and place in which the people trusted with the fate of Shiloh. Micah likewise announces ruin for Jerusalem and the mountain of the house, showing that a sacred site is not exempt from judgment.

Jeremiah 7:15
And I will throw you away from My presence, as I threw away your brothers, all Ephraim’s offspring.
  2 Kings 17:18-23 — historical counterpart to Israel's removal from God's presence: Jeremiah threatens Judah with being thrown from God's presence as Ephraim's offspring were. Kings records God's removal of Israel from His presence and deportation to Assyria, while also noting Judah's disobedience.

Jeremiah 7:16
And you, do not pray for this people, and do not ask that they receive mercy, and do not pray or approach Me on their behalf, because I will not listen.
  Jeremiah 11:14 — repeated prohibition of intercession for the people: Jeremiah 7 forbids prayer and appeals for mercy because God will not listen. Jeremiah 11 repeats the prohibition of prayer and supplication and likewise says He will not listen in their time of affliction.

Jeremiah 7:18
Their sons gather wood, and their fathers burn a fire, and their women work dough to make cakes for heaven’s host; and they poured drink offerings to foreign gods to provoke Me to anger.
  Jeremiah 51:17-19 — parallel family participation in cakes and drink offerings for heavenly worship: Jeremiah 7 describes sons, fathers and women cooperating in cakes for heaven's host and drink offerings to foreign gods. Jeremiah 51 likewise describes cakes and libations to the queen of heaven with the husbands' involvement; host and queen are preserved as the texts' different designations.

Jeremiah 7:19
Is it Me they provoke to anger? says the Lord. Is it not themselves, so that their faces are put to shame?
  Job 35:6-8 — related distinction between divine offense and consequences for people: Jeremiah asks whether the provocations do not instead shame the perpetrators themselves. Elihu likewise contrasts what God receives with ungodliness and righteousness directed toward humans; his argument is a related reflection on consequences, not a denial of the wrath Jeremiah announces next.

Jeremiah 7:20
Therefore thus says the Lord: Behold, My wrath and fury are poured upon this place, upon the people and the livestock, upon every tree of their field and all the earth’s produce; and it will burn and not be extinguished.
  Deuteronomy 32:22 — parallel divine wrath burning the land and its produce: Jeremiah describes wrath poured upon people, animals, trees and produce, burning without being extinguished. Deuteronomy likewise describes fire kindled from God's wrath devouring the earth and its produce and reaching the mountains' foundations.

Jeremiah 7:21
Thus says the Lord: Gather your whole burnt offerings with your sacrifices, and eat meat!
  Hosea 8:13 — parallel sacrificial meat that does not secure God's acceptance: Jeremiah tells the people to gather burnt offerings with sacrifices and eat meat before contrasting ritual with obedience. Hosea explicitly says that even if they sacrifice and eat meat, the Lord will not accept them and will punish their sins.

Jeremiah 7:22
For I did not speak to your fathers or command them on the day I brought them up from Egypt’s land concerning whole burnt offerings and sacrifice.
  Amos 5:25 — related prophetic challenge concerning sacrifice at Israel's beginnings: Jeremiah denies commanding the fathers concerning burnt offerings and sacrifice on the day of the exodus. Amos asks whether Israel offered sacrifices to God during forty wilderness years; the link preserves the distinct time frames and the target's question rather than treating them as identical historical assertions.

Jeremiah 7:23
But this utterance I commanded them, saying: Hear My voice, and I will be your God and you will be My people; and walk in all My ways that I command you, so that it may be well with you.
  Leviticus 26:11-13 — parallel covenant formula of God and His people: Jeremiah links hearing God's voice and walking His ways with His being their God and their being His people. Leviticus likewise promises divine presence and that reciprocal formula in an obedience-conditioned blessing, recalling deliverance from Egypt.

Jeremiah 7:24
And they did not hear Me, and their ear did not attend, but they walked in the thoughts of their evil heart and went backward rather than forward.
  Psalms 80:12-14 — parallel refusal to hear followed by walking according to the heart: Jeremiah says the people ignored God and followed their evil heart's thoughts backward. The psalm likewise says Israel did not listen or attend and was sent away according to the practices of their hearts, contrasting this with walking in God's ways.

Jeremiah 7:25
From the day their fathers left Egypt’s land until this day, I sent out to you all My servants the prophets by day and at dawn, and I sent them.
  2 Chronicles 36:15-16 — parallel repeated early sending of prophets to an unresponsive people: Jeremiah describes the sending of God's servants the prophets by day and at dawn over Israel's history. Chronicles likewise describes God rising early and sending prophetic messengers whom the people scorned, without reproducing Jeremiah's exodus-to-present span.

Jeremiah 7:26
And they did not hear Me, and their ear did not attend, and they hardened their neck beyond their fathers.
  Acts 7:51-53 — parallel inherited resistance to divine instruction: Jeremiah indicts unhearing ears and a neck hardened beyond the fathers'. Acts similarly charges stiff-necked, uncircumcised hearers with resisting as their fathers did and failing to keep the received law; beyond and as are distinct comparisons.

Jeremiah 7:27–28
And you will say this word to them: This is the nation that did not hear the Lord’s voice or accept instruction; faithfulness failed from their mouth.
  Hosea 4:1-2 — parallel failure of truth and recognition of God among the people: The composite source unit describes a nation refusing God's voice and instruction, with faithfulness failing from its mouth. Hosea likewise charges the land with no truth or recognition of God and explicitly lists falsehood among its offenses, giving a concrete social counterpart to the failed speech.

Jeremiah 7:29
Shear your head and cast it away, and take up a lament on your lips, because the Lord rejected and thrust away the generation doing these things.
  Micah 1:16 — parallel hair cutting as mourning for national loss: Jeremiah commands shearing the head and taking up lament because God rejected the generation. Micah likewise commands shaving and shearing for children taken captive, using a corresponding mourning action with a specified loss.

Jeremiah 7:30
For Judah’s sons did evil before Me, says the Lord; they set their abominations in the house over which My name has been called, to defile it.
  2 Kings 21:4-7 — historical counterpart to abominations placed in God's named house: Jeremiah charges Judah with setting abominations in the house bearing God's name. Kings records Manasseh's altars and carved grove image in that house and repeatedly recalls God's placing His name there, giving a particular historical example of the profanation.

Jeremiah 7:31
And they built Topheth’s altar, which is in the ravine of the son of Hinnom, to burn up their sons and daughters in fire, which I did not command them and did not conceive in My heart.
  Deuteronomy 12:29-31 — legal condemnation corresponding to burning children for gods: Jeremiah condemns burning sons and daughters at Topheth as something God did not command. Deuteronomy forbids imitating the nations' worship and explicitly says they burn sons and daughters for their gods, acts the Lord hates.

Jeremiah 7:32
Therefore behold, days are coming, says the Lord, and they will no longer say Topheth’s Altar or Ravine of the Son of Hinnom, but Ravine of the Slain; and they will bury in Topheth because there is no space.
  2 Kings 23:10 — historical connection through Topheth and the Hinnom ravine: Jeremiah predicts that Topheth and the ravine of Hinnom's son will be renamed for the slain. Kings names Tapheth in the valley of Ennom's son when describing Josiah's defilement of the site and mentions children and fire; it provides the corresponding location with variant name forms, not an account of the predicted renaming.

Jeremiah 7:33
And this people’s dead will be food for heaven’s birds and the earth’s wild beasts, and there will be no one to drive them off.
  Deuteronomy 28:26 — repeated curse of corpses exposed to birds and beasts: Jeremiah says the people's dead will feed heaven's birds and earth's beasts with no one to drive them away. Deuteronomy's curse states the same combination of exposed dead, scavengers and absence of anyone to drive them off.

Jeremiah 7:34
And I will stop the sound of those rejoicing and the sound of those glad, the voice of bridegroom and the voice of bride, from Judah’s cities and Jerusalem’s thoroughfares, because the whole land will become a desolation.
  Jeremiah 16:9 — repeated removal of joy and wedding voices: Jeremiah 7 announces the ending of gladness and the voices of bridegroom and bride in Judah and Jerusalem. Jeremiah 16 repeats that specific list of silenced celebrations and adds that it will occur before the hearers' eyes and in their days.
  Jeremiah 40:10-11 — restoration reversing the silencing of wedding and joyful voices: Jeremiah 7 threatens the removal of joy and bridegroom and bride voices as the land becomes desolate. Jeremiah 40 promises that these same sounds will again be heard in the deserted cities and Jerusalem's surroundings, providing an explicit reversal through restoration.

Jeremiah 8:1
At that time, says the Lord, they will bring the bones of Judah’s kings and the bones of its rulers and the bones of the priests and the bones of the prophets and the bones of those dwelling in Jerusalem out of their tombs,
  2 Kings 23:16 — related removal of bones from tombs in an idolatry-judgment setting: Jeremiah threatens exposure of the bones of rulers, priests, prophets and Jerusalem's inhabitants. Kings likewise records bones taken from tombs during Josiah's judgment upon an idolatrous altar, but with a different agent, purpose and group rather than as fulfillment of Jeremiah's threat.

Jeremiah 8:2
and expose them to cool before the sun and moon and all the stars and all heaven’s host, which they loved and served and followed and clung to and bowed down to. They will not be mourned or buried, and they will be an example upon the earth’s surface,
  Deuteronomy 4:19 — prohibition underlying the charge of worshiping heaven's bodies: Jeremiah lists sun, moon, stars and heaven's host as objects the people loved, served and worshiped. Deuteronomy specifically warns against being led to bow to and serve the sun, moon, stars and heaven's adornment, identifying the forbidden worship behind the exposure of their bones.

Jeremiah 8:3
because they chose death rather than life, and for all the remnants left from that generation in every place where I drive them away.
  Deuteronomy 30:19-20 — contrast with the command to choose life: Jeremiah says they chose death rather than life in the account of judgment and dispersal. Deuteronomy sets the same alternatives before Israel and commands choosing life through love and obedience to God; Jeremiah describes the judged generation, whereas Deuteronomy exhorts those entering the land.

Jeremiah 8:4
For thus says the Lord: Does one who falls not rise? Or does one who turns away not return?
  Proverbs 24:16 — parallel expectation of rising after a fall: Jeremiah asks whether one who falls does not rise as an analogy preceding the charge of refusal to return. Proverbs likewise says the righteous fall seven times and rise, though Jeremiah's rhetorical question is not restricted to the proverb's righteous person.

Jeremiah 8:5
Why has this people of Mine turned away with a shameless turning away, and been held fast in their own choice, and been unwilling to return?
  Jeremiah 5:3 — repeated accusation of stubborn refusal to return: Jeremiah 8 describes the people held fast in their choice and unwilling to return. Jeremiah 5 likewise says their faces were hardened beyond rock and they would not return despite discipline, repeating the resistance through different images.

Jeremiah 8:6
Give ear now and hear: they will not speak thus; there is no person repenting of his evil, saying, ‘What have I done?’ The runner ceased from his course like a sweating horse in its neighing.
  Ezekiel 18:27-30 — contrast between absent repentance and actual turning from lawlessness: Jeremiah finds no one repenting or asking what he has done. Ezekiel describes a lawless person turning to judgment and righteousness and calls Israel to turn from impieties, presenting the response missing in Jeremiah without attempting to equate the horse image.

Jeremiah 8:7
Even the stork in heaven knew her time; turtledove and swallow, field sparrows, kept the times of their coming, but My people did not know the Lord’s judgments.
  Isaiah 1:3 — parallel comparison of knowledgeable animals with an unknowing people: Jeremiah contrasts birds knowing their seasons with the people's ignorance of the Lord's judgments. Isaiah likewise contrasts an ox and donkey knowing their keeper or manger with Israel's failure to know God, using different animal knowledge to expose the same human failure.

Jeremiah 8:9
The wise were ashamed and terrified and captured, because they rejected the Lord’s word. What wisdom is in them?
  Proverbs 1:7 — contrast between rejecting divine speech and the foundation of wisdom: Jeremiah questions what wisdom remains in those who rejected the Lord's word. Proverbs places wisdom and perception in fear and reverence toward God and calls the ungodly despisers of wisdom and discipline, showing why rejection of divine instruction undermines a claim to wisdom.

Jeremiah 8:10
Therefore I will give their wives to others and their fields to inheritors,
  Jeremiah 6:12 — repeated transfer of wives and fields to others: Jeremiah 8 announces wives given to others and fields to inheritors. Jeremiah 6 repeats the transfer of fields and wives along with houses and explicitly identifies it as God's action against the land's inhabitants.

Jeremiah 8:13
and they will gather their produce, says the Lord. There is no grape on the vines and no figs on the fig trees, and the leaves have fallen.
  Habakkuk 3:17-18 — parallel failure of vine and fig produce with a different response: Jeremiah announces no grapes on vines and no figs on fig trees, with fallen leaves. Habakkuk likewise anticipates fruitless figs and unproductive vines but answers with rejoicing in God, a response Jeremiah's agricultural description does not supply.

Jeremiah 8:14
Why are we sitting? Gather, and let us enter the fortified cities and be cast away, because God cast us away and made us drink gall-water, because we sinned before Him.
  Jeremiah 9:12-14 — repeated gall-water punishment explained by disobedience: Jeremiah 8 says God gave gall-water because the people sinned before Him. Jeremiah 9 likewise threatens gall-water after describing abandonment of God's law, refusal to hear and following the evil heart and idols.

Jeremiah 8:15
We gathered for peace, and there were no good things; for a time of healing, and behold, haste!
  Jeremiah 14:19 — parallel disappointed expectation of peace and healing: Jeremiah 8 reports gathering for peace without good things and for healing only to find haste. Jeremiah 14 repeats the expectation of peace and healing with no good things but turmoil; haste and turmoil are retained as different supplied outcomes.

Jeremiah 8:16
From Dan we will hear the shrill sound of his horses; at the sound of the neighing of his riding horses the whole earth shook. And he will come and devour the earth and its fullness, a city and those dwelling in it.
  Jeremiah 4:15 — repeated threatening sound from Dan: Jeremiah 8 places the alarming sound of invading horses at Dan. Jeremiah 4 similarly expects a voice announcing from Dan and trouble from Ephraim; the geographical alarm recurs without equating the announcer's voice with the horses themselves.

Jeremiah 8:17
Because behold, I am sending out among you deadly snakes that cannot be charmed, and they will bite you.
  Numbers 21:6-7 — parallel sending of deadly serpents among a sinful people: Jeremiah announces deadly snakes that cannot be charmed and will bite. Numbers likewise records the Lord sending deadly serpents that bite the people, followed by confession; the healing that follows there is not promised in Jeremiah's warning.

Jeremiah 8:19
Behold, the crying voice of the daughter of My people from a distant land: ‘Is the Lord not in Zion? Is a King not there?’ Why did they provoke Me with their carved things and foreign futile things?
  Micah 3:11-12 — related appeal to divine presence amid deserved calamity: Jeremiah hears the question whether the Lord and a king are in Zion and then gives God's charge concerning carved and futile things. Micah likewise exposes an appeal to the Lord's presence as supposed protection from calamity and announces Jerusalem's ruin; the latter's confident claim differs from Jeremiah's anguished question.

Jeremiah 8:21
At the crushing of the daughter of My people I was darkened; in bewilderment, pains like a woman giving birth overpowered me.
  Jeremiah 4:31 — parallel labor-pain image for distress over the people's destruction: Jeremiah 8 describes being overcome by labor-like pains at the crushing of the people's daughter. Jeremiah 4 likewise gives Zion a voice like a woman in labor and failing strength over those killed, linking the birth-pain imagery with the national disaster.

Jeremiah 8:22
Is there no resin in Gilead, or is there no physician there? Why did healing not rise for the daughter of My people?
  Jeremiah 26:11 — parallel Gilead resin and remedies failing to bring healing: Jeremiah 8 asks about resin and a physician in Gilead and the people's missing healing. Jeremiah 26 addresses Gilead and calls for resin for Egypt while declaring multiplied remedies vain and without benefit, repeating the named medicine and failed benefit for a different people.

Jeremiah 8:23
Who will give my head water and my eyes a fountain of tears, and I will weep day and night for this people of Mine, for the wounded of the daughter of My people?
  Lamentations 3:48-50 — parallel tears flowing from the eyes over the people's shattering: Jeremiah asks for a watery head and fountain-like eyes to weep day and night for the wounded people. Lamentations likewise describes the eye bringing down outpourings of water over the shattering of the people's daughter; the speaker then will not be silent because there is no recovery.

Jeremiah 9:1
Who would give me a remote station in the wilderness, and I will leave my people and go away from them? For all commit adultery, an assembly of those rejecting faithfulness.
  Psalms 54:7-9 — wilderness refuge from communal unfaithfulness: Jeremiah wishes for a remote wilderness station so that he can leave an unfaithful people. The psalmist likewise wishes to fly away, rest and lodge in the wilderness; the following city scene describes lawlessness and betrayal. Both express a desire for refuge from a corrupt social setting.

Jeremiah 9:2
And they stretched their tongue like a bow; falsehood and not faithfulness grew strong upon the earth, because they went from evils to evils and did not know Me.
  Psalms 63:4-5 — tongue and bow as weapons: Jeremiah compares the tongue to a stretched bow while describing falsehood. The psalm joins sharpened tongues to a stretched bow and secret shooting. Both portray hostile speech through weapon imagery; they do not identify the same particular attackers.

Jeremiah 9:3
Each of you, guard against his neighbor, and do not put trust in their brothers, because every brother will trip with the heel, and every friend will walk deceitfully.
  Micah 7:5-6 — distrust within intimate relationships: Jeremiah warns against trusting a neighbor or brother because betrayal is pervasive. Micah likewise warns against confidence in friends and describes enemies within one's household. The connection concerns the collapse of trust among people normally expected to be close.

Jeremiah 9:4
Each will mock his friend; they certainly will not speak truth. Their tongue has learned to speak false things; they did wrong and did not cease turning back.
  Psalms 11:3-4 — deceitful speech between neighbors: Jeremiah says friends mock one another and tongues have learned falsehood. The psalm describes empty speech to one's neighbor, deceitful lips and a divided heart. Both locate the failure of truthful speech within ordinary social relationships.

Jeremiah 9:5
Interest upon interest, deceit upon deceit: they were unwilling to know Me.
  Psalms 54:11-12 — interest-taking joined to deceit: Jeremiah places interest upon interest beside deceit upon deceit. The psalm's account of a lawless city likewise names interest-taking and deceit together in its squares. This connection follows the supplied interest-taking wording rather than replacing it with a different description of the people's dwelling.
  Hosea 4:1-2 — falsehood and refusal to know God: Jeremiah connects multiplying deceit with unwillingness to know the Lord. Hosea's indictment similarly joins the absence of recognition of God to falsehood and other offenses. The shared connection is between social wrongdoing and the failure to acknowledge God.

Jeremiah 9:6
Therefore thus says the Lord: Behold, I will put them through fire and test them, because I will act on account of the evil of the daughter of My people.
  Isaiah 1:25 — divine testing by fire: The Lord announces that He will put the people through fire and test them because of their evil. Isaiah likewise portrays His action against a disobedient people as burning to purity and removing the lawless. Both use fire for divine dealing with communal corruption.

Jeremiah 9:7
Their tongue is a wounding missile; the utterances of their mouth are deceitful. One speaks peaceful things to his neighbor, and within himself he holds enmity.
  Psalms 54:22 — gentle speech concealing injury: Jeremiah calls the tongue a wounding missile and contrasts peaceful words with inward enmity. The psalm says words became softer than oil yet are missiles. Both expose speech that sounds reassuring while functioning as a weapon.

Jeremiah 9:8
Shall I not visit for these things? says the Lord. Or will not My soul take vengeance on such a people?
  Jeremiah 5:9 — repeated divine question about vengeance: Both passages ask whether the Lord will visit these offenses and whether His soul will take vengeance on such a nation or people. The repeated rhetorical question links the new indictment of deceit to the earlier judgment refrain.

Jeremiah 9:9
Take up mourning for the mountains and a dirge for the wilderness paths, because they failed for lack of people. They did not hear the sound of possessions; from heaven’s birds to livestock, they were displaced and went away.
  Hosea 4:3 — land and animal life affected by judgment: Jeremiah calls for mourning over emptied wilderness paths as birds and livestock depart. Hosea similarly describes a mourning, diminishing land together with the loss of beasts and birds. Both extend the consequences of human wrongdoing to the inhabited landscape and its animals.

Jeremiah 9:10
And I will give Jerusalem over to deportation and as a dwelling of dragons, and make Judah’s cities disappear, so that they are not inhabited.
  Isaiah 34:13 — abandoned cities inhabited by strange creatures: Jeremiah describes Jerusalem becoming a dwelling of dragons and Judah's cities becoming uninhabited. Isaiah describes another ruined territory whose cities grow thorns and become a dwelling of sirens and ostriches. The parallel is the reversal from human habitation to desolation; the named creatures and territories differ.

Jeremiah 9:11
Who is the discerning person? Let him understand this; and the one to whom the word of the Lord’s mouth comes, let him announce it to you: for what reason did the land perish, kindled like a wilderness so that no one passes through it?
  Deuteronomy 29:23-24 — question about the cause of the land's ruin: Jeremiah asks a discerning hearer to explain why the land has perished and become impassable. Deuteronomy anticipates the nations asking why the Lord treated the land so severely and answers with covenant abandonment. Both frame the ruined land as a question requiring a covenantal explanation.

Jeremiah 9:12
And the Lord said to me: Because they abandoned My law that I set before them and did not hear My voice,
  Deuteronomy 29:24-26 — abandoned covenant law as the cause of judgment: The Lord explains the land's disaster by the people's abandonment of His law and refusal to hear Him. Deuteronomy likewise attributes the land's curses to forsaking the covenant and serving other gods. The passages connect judgment to prior obligations that the people rejected.

Jeremiah 9:13
but walked after the things pleasing to their evil heart and after the idols that their fathers taught them,
  Jeremiah 7:24 — repeated accusation of following the evil heart: Jeremiah 9 describes walking after what pleases the evil heart. Jeremiah 7 similarly says the people did not hear the Lord but walked in the thoughts of their evil heart. The recurrence identifies self-directed desire as a form of refusing divine instruction.

Jeremiah 9:14
therefore thus says the Lord, Israel’s God: Behold, I will feed them distresses and make them drink gall-water,
  Lamentations 3:15 — bitterness and gall as a diet of affliction: Jeremiah depicts the people's punishment as being fed distresses and made to drink gall-water. The lamenter says he was filled with bitterness and made drunk with gall. Both express suffering through bitter food and drink, with a communal subject in Jeremiah and an individual lamenting voice in Lamentations.

Jeremiah 9:15
and I will scatter them among nations that neither they nor their fathers knew, and send the sword upon them until I consume them by it.
  Leviticus 26:33 — covenant warning of scattering and the sword: Jeremiah announces scattering among unfamiliar nations and a sword sent after the people. Leviticus's covenant warning likewise combines scattering among nations with the consuming, advancing sword and a desolated homeland. The paired judgments provide a particularly close connection.

Jeremiah 9:16
Thus says the Lord: Call the women who lament, and let them come; send for the skilled women, and let them speak,
  Amos 5:16 — calling skilled mourners: Jeremiah commands that women skilled in lament be summoned. Amos's announcement of mourning in public places likewise calls people to mourning and to those skilled in wailing. Both portray lament as a communal response involving people practiced in it.

Jeremiah 9:17
and let them take up a dirge over you; let your eyes bring down tears, and let your eyelids flow with water.
  Lamentations 2:18-19 — command to pour out tears for Zion: Jeremiah calls for a dirge that brings water from eyes and eyelids. Lamentations addresses Zion's walls with the command to bring down torrents of tears and pour out the heart like water. Both make abundant weeping the fitting response to the people's catastrophe.

Jeremiah 9:18
For a voice of lament was heard in Zion: How we suffered misery and were greatly ashamed, because we abandoned the land and threw away our dwellings!
  Lamentations 1:1-3 — Zion's lament over displacement: Jeremiah hears Zion's lament over misery, shame and the loss of land and dwellings. Lamentations opens with ruined, weeping Jerusalem and deported Judea dwelling among the nations without rest. The passages share communal grief over the loss of a settled homeland.

Jeremiah 9:19
Hear God’s word now, women, and let your ears receive His mouth’s words; teach your daughters lament, and each woman her neighbor a dirge.
  Jeremiah 9:16-17 — same-chapter extension of the call to lament: After summoning skilled women to begin a dirge, the oracle tells women to teach lament to daughters and neighbors. The reference follows the specific movement from calling practiced mourners to spreading their lament through the community; both passages explicitly concern the women and the dirge.

Jeremiah 9:20
For death climbed through your windows and entered your land, to wipe out little children outside and young men from the squares.
  Lamentations 2:21 — death and devastation in public places: Jeremiah pictures death entering the land and wiping out children outside and young men in the squares. Lamentations describes child and elder lying at the way out, virgins and young men in captivity, and killing by sword and famine. Both portray catastrophe in the community's public spaces without assigning every listed fate to the same group.

Jeremiah 9:21
And people’s dead will become an example on the surface of your land’s plain, and like grass behind one reaping, and there will be no one gathering.
  Jeremiah 8:2 — exposed remains without gathering or burial: Jeremiah 9 depicts people's dead as an example upon the land's surface, left like cut grass without a gatherer. Jeremiah 8 uses the same earth-surface disgrace for exposed bones that will not be mourned or buried. The remains arise in different scenes, but both describe bodies deprived of ordinary care.

Jeremiah 9:22
Thus says the Lord: Let no wise man boast in his wisdom, let no strong man boast in his strength, and let no rich man boast in his wealth,
  1 Corinthians 1:26-29 — rejection of boasting in human advantage: Jeremiah forbids boasting in wisdom, strength and wealth. Paul points to the calling of people without worldly wisdom, power or noble birth and says no flesh may boast before God. Both reject human advantage as a sufficient ground for boasting; their lists are not identical.

Jeremiah 9:23
but let whoever boasts boast in this: understanding and knowing that I am the Lord, doing mercy and judgment and righteousness upon the earth, because in these is My will, says the Lord.
  1 Corinthians 1:30-31 — scriptural echo of boasting in the Lord: Jeremiah directs the boaster to understanding and knowing the Lord who acts in mercy, judgment and righteousness. Paul, after describing Christ as wisdom and righteousness from God, introduces as written the instruction to boast in the Lord. The shared redirection of boasting is explicit, without claiming identical wording throughout.

Jeremiah 9:24
Behold, days are coming, says the Lord, and I will visit all who have been circumcised in their foreskins:
  Romans 2:25-29 — outward circumcision does not avert judgment: Jeremiah announces visitation even upon those circumcised in the foreskin. Romans says circumcision benefits the law's practitioner but does not protect a transgressor, and distinguishes outward flesh from the heart. Both deny that the bodily sign by itself settles the person's standing before God.

Jeremiah 9:25
Egypt and Judea and Edom and the sons of Ammon and the sons of Moab and everyone who clips the hair around his face, those dwelling in the wilderness; because all the nations are uncircumcised in flesh, and all Israel’s house are uncircumcised in their hearts.
  Deuteronomy 10:16 — circumcision of the heart: Jeremiah calls Israel's house uncircumcised in heart despite the surrounding discussion of bodily circumcision. Deuteronomy commands circumcision of the hardness of the heart and an end to the stiffened neck. The connection uses the same bodily image for inward responsiveness.

Jeremiah 10:2
Thus says the Lord: Do not learn according to the nations’ ways, and do not fear heaven’s signs, because they fear them before their faces.
  Deuteronomy 4:19 — heavenly bodies and the practices of the nations: Jeremiah warns Israel not to learn the nations' ways or fear heaven's signs as they do. Deuteronomy warns against being led astray by the sun, moon, stars and heavenly array into serving them. Both distinguish Israel's response to the heavens from the religious practices associated with the nations.

Jeremiah 10:3
For the nations’ customs are futile: it is wood cut from the woodland, a carpenter’s work and a cast object.
  Isaiah 44:13-17 — wood transformed by craftsmen into an idol: Jeremiah reduces the nations' object of devotion to woodland timber and a carpenter's work. Isaiah follows wood from cutting and practical use to the remainder being fashioned into a god and worshipped. Both expose the manufactured origin of a supposedly divine object.

Jeremiah 10:4
They are adorned with silver and gold; with hammers and nails they fastened them, and they will not move.
  Isaiah 46:6-7 — precious metal and an immobile handmade object: Jeremiah describes silver and gold adornment and fastening the object so it does not move. Isaiah describes gold and silver given to a goldsmith and an object that remains where people set it. Both contrast costly workmanship with the object's dependence on human handling.

Jeremiah 10:5
They must be lifted and carried, because they will not take a step. Do not fear them, because they certainly will not do evil, and no good is in them.
  Isaiah 46:7 — idols must be carried and cannot save: Jeremiah says the objects must be lifted and carried because they cannot take a step, and have no power for good or evil. Isaiah likewise describes people carrying an object that never moves on its own and cannot answer or save. Both deny the carried image independent agency.

Jeremiah 10:9
It is worked silver; they will not walk. Applied silver will come from Tarshish, gold from Mophaz, and the goldsmiths’ hand; all are artisans’ works. They will dress them in blue and purple.
  Psalms 113:12-15 — metal idols made by human hands cannot walk: Jeremiah names worked silver, gold and artisans while stressing that the objects will not walk. The psalm describes the nations' silver-and-gold idols as human handiwork and says their feet will not walk. The parallel concerns material manufacture and the absence of living capability, not the particular trading places or clothing.

Jeremiah 10:11
This is what you will say to them: Gods who did not make heaven and earth, let them perish from the earth and from beneath this heaven.
  Psalms 95:4-5 — false gods contrasted with the maker of heaven: Jeremiah pronounces the passing away of gods that did not make heaven and earth. The psalm contrasts the nations' gods with the Lord who made the heavens. Creation marks the decisive difference in both passages.

Jeremiah 10:12
The Lord who made the earth by His strength, who set the inhabited world upright by His wisdom, also stretched out heaven by His understanding,
  Proverbs 3:19-20 — creation established by divine wisdom: Jeremiah joins the making of earth and stretching of heaven to the Lord's strength, wisdom and understanding. Proverbs likewise describes earth founded by wisdom and heaven prepared by good sense. Both attribute the ordered world to divine understanding.
  Jeremiah 28:15 — repeated creation proclamation: The two passages repeat the earth made by strength, the inhabited world established by wisdom and heaven stretched by understanding. This is a close internal recurrence in the edition's native Jeremiah numbering, linking the creator proclamation across the two oracles.

Jeremiah 10:13
and an abundance of water in heaven; and He brought clouds up from the earth’s extremity, made lightning for rain, and brought light out of His treasuries.
  Psalms 134:7 — clouds and lightning for rain with a wording distinction: Both passages speak of clouds brought from the earth's extremity and lightning made for rain. The supplied Jeremiah text then says light comes from the treasuries, while the psalm says winds. The shared weather clauses support the comparison without changing either final noun.
  Jeremiah 28:16 — repeated weather proclamation: Jeremiah 28 repeats the heavenly water, clouds from the earth's end, lightning for rain and light from divine treasuries. In this edition both Jeremiah passages say light. The parallel preserves that wording and follows the repeated account of the creator's activity.

Jeremiah 10:14
Every human became foolish, without knowledge; every goldsmith was put to shame at his carved things, because they cast false things; there is no spirit in them.
  Habakkuk 2:18-19 — cast images are false and breathless: Jeremiah calls the goldsmith's cast works false and says there is no spirit in them. Habakkuk likewise calls the cast object a false appearance and says the plated wood or stone has no breath at all. Both expose the lifelessness of the manufactured object despite its maker's confidence.

Jeremiah 10:15
They are futile, mocked works; at the time of their visitation they will perish.
  Jeremiah 28:18 — repeated verdict on futile works: Both passages call the images futile or vain, mocked works that will perish at the time of visitation. The repeated verdict directly follows the same criticism of their manufacture and absence of spirit.

Jeremiah 10:16
Jacob’s portion is not such as this, because the One who formed everything is himself his inheritance; Lord is His name.
  Jeremiah 28:19 — repeated identification of Jacob's inheritance: Both passages distinguish Jacob's portion from the lifeless works because the One who formed everything is Himself his inheritance and is named Lord. The connection retains the direction of inheritance stated in these texts.

Jeremiah 10:18
For thus says the Lord: Behold, I am tripping those dwelling in this land with affliction, so that your wound may be found.
  Jeremiah 37:12-14 — divine affliction pictured as a wound: Jeremiah 10 announces affliction that reveals the people's wound. Jeremiah 37 likewise has the Lord speak of a fracture and painful wound, then identify His blow as discipline for multiplied wrongdoing. Both describe divine judgment through injury imagery; no slinging action is supplied to Jeremiah 10.

Jeremiah 10:19
Woe for your crushing! Your wound is painful. And I said: Truly this is my wound, and it has seized me.
  Jeremiah 37:12-14 — painful wound and lament under discipline: The lament in Jeremiah 10 names a painful wound and acknowledges it as the speaker's own. Jeremiah 37 repeats the painful-wound language and explains the blow as discipline amid sin and abandonment by friends. The connection is the acknowledged injury of judgment, not an assertion that the voices are identical.

Jeremiah 10:20
My tent suffered misery and perished, and all my tent coverings were torn apart. My sons and my sheep are no more; there is no longer a place for my tent, a place for my coverings.
  Isaiah 54:2-3 — contrast between a ruined tent and an enlarged tent: Jeremiah's tent has perished, its coverings are torn and its sons are gone. Isaiah commands widening the tent and curtains because offspring will spread out and settle deserted cities. Both use dwelling and offspring imagery, with the restoration promise reversing the losses lamented in Jeremiah.

Jeremiah 10:21
For the shepherds acted foolishly and did not seek the Lord; therefore all the pasture did not perceive, and they were scattered.
  Ezekiel 34:2-6 — failed shepherds and a scattered flock: Jeremiah brings together foolish shepherds, failure to seek the Lord and scattering. Ezekiel indicts Israel's shepherds for feeding themselves rather than caring for sheep and explicitly describes the flock scattered without anyone seeking it. Both connect failed leadership with dispersal; Jeremiah's compressed pronouns are not expanded into a new subject.

Jeremiah 10:22
A sound of a report—behold, it comes! And a great shaking from the northern land, to lay Judah’s cities waste and make them a resting place for ostriches.
  Jeremiah 1:14-16 — calamity from the north against Judah's cities: The report of a great shaking from the northern land is directed toward the desolation of Judah's cities. Jeremiah's opening vision likewise locates calamity in the north and describes northern kingdoms arrayed against Jerusalem and Judah's cities. The direction, affected region and announced disaster correspond.

Jeremiah 10:23
I know, Lord, that a human’s way does not belong to him, nor will a man go and make his course straight.
  Proverbs 20:24 — human inability to direct one's own course: Jeremiah confesses that a person's way does not belong to him and that he cannot make his own course straight. Proverbs says human steps are directed by the Lord and asks how a mortal could understand his ways. Both qualify human control or understanding of one's path.

Jeremiah 10:24
Instruct us, Lord, but with judgment and not in fury, so that Thou dost not make us few.
  Psalms 6:2 — request for correction without consuming anger: Jeremiah asks the Lord to instruct with judgment rather than fury so that the people are not diminished. The psalm asks not to be rebuked in fury or disciplined in anger. Both seek a restrained form of divine correction rather than its devastating intensity.

Jeremiah 10:25
Pour Thy fury upon nations who do not know Thee and upon generations who did not call on Thy name, because they devoured Jacob and consumed him and made his pasture desolate.
  Psalms 78:6-7 — shared petition against those who devoured Jacob: Both prayers ask that divine fury be poured on nations that do not know the Lord or call on His name, because they devoured Jacob and desolated his place or pasture. The sequence of petition, description and reason makes the correspondence especially close.

Jeremiah 11:2
Hear the words of this covenant. And you will speak to the men of Judah and those dwelling in Jerusalem,
  Deuteronomy 31:11-13 — public hearing of covenant instruction: Jeremiah is to speak the covenant's words to Judah's men and Jerusalem's inhabitants. Deuteronomy orders public reading of the law before the gathered people so that they hear and learn to obey. Both make covenant instruction something delivered to the community in its hearing.

Jeremiah 11:3
and you will say to them: Thus says the Lord, Israel’s God: Cursed is the person who will not hear the words of this covenant,
  Deuteronomy 27:26 — curse upon failure to heed covenant words: Jeremiah pronounces a curse on the person who will not hear the covenant's words. Deuteronomy pronounces a curse on anyone who does not continue in all the law's words to do them. The shared curse attaches to rejection of the covenant's verbal obligations.

Jeremiah 11:4
which I commanded your fathers on the day I brought them up from Egypt’s land, from the iron furnace, saying: Hear My voice and do everything I command you, and you will be My people and I will be your God,
  Deuteronomy 4:20 — Egypt as an iron furnace: Jeremiah recalls the ancestors brought from Egypt, calling it the iron furnace, into a people-and-God relationship. Deuteronomy likewise names Egypt the iron furnace from which God brought Israel to be His people of inheritance. The unusual image and the exodus-to-belonging movement coincide.
  Leviticus 26:12 — covenant people-and-God formula: Jeremiah recalls the command to obey together with the promise that they will be His people and He their God. Leviticus states the same reciprocal relationship in the covenant promises. This connection concerns the explicit covenant formula rather than only a general mention of obedience.

Jeremiah 11:5
so that I may establish My oath that I swore to your fathers, to give them a land flowing with milk and honey, as on this day. And I answered and said: May it be, Lord.
  Exodus 3:8 — promise of a land flowing with milk and honey: Jeremiah connects the oath's establishment with the gift of a land flowing with milk and honey. Exodus presents the rescue from Egypt as movement into that same kind of good, spacious land. The earlier promised destination supplies the background to Jeremiah's acknowledgment of the land now given.
  Exodus 6:8 — land sworn to the ancestors and given as inheritance: Jeremiah speaks of establishing the oath sworn to the fathers by giving the land. Exodus names Abraham, Isaac and Jacob in the pledge to bring their descendants into the land and give it as inheritance. Both explicitly join ancestral pledge and the gift of land.

Jeremiah 11:6
And the Lord said to me: Read these words in Judah’s cities and outside Jerusalem, saying: Hear the words of this covenant and do them.
  Deuteronomy 31:11-13 — reading the law so that the people do it: The command to read the covenant words in Judah's cities and outside Jerusalem ends with hearing and doing them. Deuteronomy's public law reading likewise aims at hearing, learning and doing all its words. The shared purpose explains why proclamation is more than recitation.

Jeremiah 11:8
And they did not do them.
  Jeremiah 11:6 — same-chapter command and noncompliance: The short native unit says that they did not do them. The preceding command in 11:6 supplies the antecedent: the covenant words were to be heard and done. This reference makes the stated failure intelligible without reconstructing the absent native verse 11:7.

Jeremiah 11:10
They returned to the injustices of their earlier fathers, who would not listen to My words; and behold, they walk after foreign gods to serve them. And the house of Israel and the house of Judah shattered My covenant that I established with their fathers.
  Deuteronomy 29:24-26 — covenant broken through serving foreign gods: Jeremiah describes returning to ancestral wrongdoing, refusing to hear and serving foreign gods, thereby shattering the covenant. Deuteronomy likewise explains the land's judgment through forsaking the covenant and serving gods the people had not known. The correspondence connects religious abandonment with covenant breach.

Jeremiah 11:11
Therefore thus says the Lord: Behold, I am bringing evils upon this people from which they will not be able to escape; and they will cry to Me, and I will not listen to them.
  Proverbs 1:24-28 — refused instruction followed by an unanswered cry: Jeremiah says the people will cry during inescapable calamity but the Lord will not listen. Wisdom's speech in Proverbs similarly connects prior refusal of her calls and counsel with a later call that will not be heard amid affliction. The pattern corresponds while the literary speakers remain distinct.

Jeremiah 11:12
And Judah’s cities and those dwelling in Jerusalem will go and cry to the gods to whom they burn incense. Will those gods save them in the time of their evils?
  Jeremiah 2:27-28 — challenge to idols to save in affliction: Jeremiah 11 asks whether the gods receiving incense will save their worshippers in trouble. Jeremiah 2 likewise challenges the gods the people made to rise and save them when affliction comes. Both expose the failure of the alternative objects of worship at the moment help is needed.

Jeremiah 11:13
For your gods were according to the number of your cities, Judah; and according to the number of Jerusalem’s exits you set up altars to burn incense to Baal.
  Jeremiah 2:28 — repeated comparison of gods and altars to city numbers: Both passages say Judah's gods are as numerous as its cities and connect Jerusalem's streets or exits with sacrifice or incense to Baal. The repeated numerical comparison makes the proliferation of worship explicit; the differing location nouns are retained.

Jeremiah 11:14
And you, do not pray for this people or petition for them with supplication and prayer, because I will not listen when they call upon Me, in their time of affliction.
  Jeremiah 7:16 — repeated prohibition of intercession: In both passages the Lord tells Jeremiah not to pray or petition for this people because He will not listen. Jeremiah 11 specifies their time of affliction, while Jeremiah 7 also forbids approaching on their behalf. The shared command defines the prophet's restricted intercession.

Jeremiah 11:15
Why has the beloved woman committed an abomination in My house? Will prayers and holy meats remove your evils from you, or will you escape by these?
  Isaiah 1:11-15 — ritual and prayer do not remove persistent evil: Jeremiah asks whether prayers and holy meats can remove the beloved woman's evil committed in God's house. Isaiah rejects sacrifices and multiplied supplication when the people's hands are full of blood. Both deny that religious offerings and prayers neutralize ongoing wrongdoing.

Jeremiah 11:16
The Lord called your name a beautiful olive tree, well-shaded in appearance. At the sound of its pruning, fire was kindled upon it; great is the affliction upon you, and its branches became useless.
  Romans 11:17-22 — olive-tree judgment imagery: Jeremiah describes a once beautiful olive tree whose branches become useless under fiery affliction. Romans describes olive branches broken off because of unbelief and warns the remaining hearer not to boast. Both use damaged or removed olive branches to portray judgment within a relationship of belonging, without equating every detail of the images.

Jeremiah 11:17
And the Lord who planted you spoke evils against you in return for the evil of the house of Israel and the house of Judah, because they acted for themselves to provoke Me by burning incense to Baal.
  Jeremiah 2:21 — divine planting followed by corrupted response: Jeremiah 11 names the Lord as the planter who now speaks evil against Israel and Judah for provoking Him with Baal worship. Jeremiah 2 contrasts God's genuine, fruitful planting with the vine's turn to bitterness and foreign character. Both connect an initially good divine planting with a corrupted human response.

Jeremiah 11:19
But I, like a guileless lamb led to be slaughtered, did not know. Against me they devised an evil design, saying: Come, let us put wood into his bread and wipe him out from the land of the living, and his name will certainly be remembered no more.
  Isaiah 53:7-8 — lamb led to slaughter and removal from the living: Jeremiah compares himself to a guileless lamb led to slaughter while opponents seek to remove him from the land of the living. Isaiah compares the afflicted figure to a sheep led to slaughter and says his life is taken from the earth. The images and threatened removal correspond without identifying the two figures as the same person.

Jeremiah 11:20
Lord, who judges justly, who tests kidneys and hearts, may I see the vengeance from Thee upon them, because to Thee I revealed my claim of right.
  Psalms 7:9-12 — righteous judge who tests hearts and kidneys: Jeremiah appeals to the Lord who judges justly and tests kidneys and hearts while entrusting his claim to Him. The psalm asks for judgment according to innocence and names God as tester of hearts and kidneys and a righteous judge. Both ground a plea against wrongdoing in divine scrutiny.

Jeremiah 11:21
Therefore thus says the Lord concerning the men of Anathoth who seek my soul, who say: You must certainly not prophesy in the Lord’s name; otherwise you will die by our hands.
  Amos 7:12-16 — opposition forbidding a prophet to speak: Anathoth's men forbid Jeremiah to prophesy in the Lord's name and threaten death. Amaziah forbids Amos to prophesy at Bethel, despite Amos's account of the Lord's commission. Both concern attempts to silence a commissioned prophet; Amos's passage does not contain the same death threat.

Jeremiah 11:22
Behold, I will visit them; their young men will die by the sword, and their sons and daughters will end their lives in famine,
  Jeremiah 18:20-23 — judgment on those plotting against Jeremiah: The Lord announces sword for the young men and famine for the children of Jeremiah's opponents. Jeremiah 18 places the same family losses in the prophet's plea after describing plots against his life. The outcomes and persecution context correspond, while one passage is God's announcement and the other Jeremiah's petition.

Jeremiah 11:23
and no remnant of them will remain, because I will bring evils upon those dwelling in Anathoth in the year of their visitation.
  Jeremiah 11:21-22 — same-chapter identification and culmination of judgment: The announcement that no remnant of these opponents will remain completes the judgment on men who threatened Jeremiah and on their households. The earlier verses identify the threat and specify sword and famine. The reference ties the concluding visitation to its identified recipients and cause without enlarging 'of them' to every resident of Anathoth.

Jeremiah 12:1
Thou art righteous, Lord, as I make my defense before Thee; nevertheless I will speak judgments with Thee. Why does the impious person’s way prosper? Why did all who commit acts of rejection flourish?
  Job 21:7-13 — question about the prosperity of the ungodly: Jeremiah asks why the impious prosper and rejecters flourish while addressing the righteous Lord. Job likewise asks why the ungodly live long in wealth and describes flourishing households and children. Both put the visible success of wrongdoers into a questioning dialogue about justice.

Jeremiah 12:2
Thou didst plant them, and they took root; they produced children and bore fruit. Thou art near their mouth and far from their kidneys.
  Isaiah 29:13 — nearness in speech and distance within: Jeremiah says the Lord is near the people's mouth but far from their kidneys. Isaiah says the people honor Him with lips while their heart is far away. Both contrast outward religious speech with inward distance; each passage's own bodily wording is preserved.

Jeremiah 12:3
And Thou, Lord, knowest me; Thou hast tested my heart before Thee. Purify them for their day of slaughter.
  Psalms 138:1-3 — God's testing and knowledge of the speaker: Jeremiah appeals to the Lord's knowledge of him and testing of his heart. The psalm begins by saying that the Lord has tested and known the speaker and understood his movements and reasonings. Both ground the address in God's intimate knowledge rather than outward appearance.

Jeremiah 12:4
How long will the earth mourn and all the field’s grass dry up because of the evil of those dwelling in it? Livestock and birds vanished, because they said, ‘God will not see our ways.’
  Hosea 4:1-3 — wrongdoing brings mourning to land and animals: Jeremiah connects the mournful earth, dried grass and vanished livestock and birds with the inhabitants' evil. Hosea's indictment of the land's wrongdoing likewise leads to the land mourning and the decline of beasts and birds. Both explicitly join human evil to ecological devastation.

Jeremiah 12:6
For even your brothers and your father’s house rejected you, and they shouted; they gathered behind you. Do not trust them, because they will speak good things to you.
  Micah 7:5-6 — betrayal by one's own household: Jeremiah is warned that even brothers and his father's house reject him and that favorable words should not be trusted. Micah warns against trusting intimate associates and says a man's enemies belong to his own house. Both locate danger inside expected bonds of family confidence.

Jeremiah 12:7
I have abandoned My house, let go My inheritance, and given My beloved soul into her enemies’ hands.
  Psalms 77:59-62 — abandoned sanctuary and inheritance handed to enemies: The Lord says He has abandoned His house and inheritance and handed His beloved into enemy hands. The psalm describes rejection of Shiloh's tent, strength handed to captivity and the inheritance disregarded. Both portray the loss of divine protection in terms of dwelling, belonging and enemy control.

Jeremiah 12:8
My inheritance became to Me like a lion in woodland; she raised her voice against Me, therefore I hated her.
  Hosea 9:15 — divine rejection of a rebellious people: Jeremiah portrays the inheritance roaring against the Lord like a lion and states that He hated her. Hosea likewise connects the people's evil and disobedient rulers with divine hatred and expulsion from His house. The common point is rejection following rebellion; Hosea does not repeat the lion image.

Jeremiah 12:9
Is My inheritance to Me a hyena’s cave, or a cave around her? Go, gather all the field’s wild beasts, and let them come to eat her!
  Ezekiel 39:17-20 — summoning beasts to consume the judged: Jeremiah commands gathering the field's wild beasts to eat the threatened inheritance. Ezekiel summons birds and beasts to a divine sacrificial feast on the flesh of the defeated. Both use a summons to animal consumption as judgment imagery, but the victims and setting differ.

Jeremiah 12:10
Many shepherds ruined My vineyard, defiled My portion, and made My desirable portion an impassable wilderness.
  Isaiah 5:5-7 — God's vineyard reduced to desolation: Jeremiah says shepherds ruined the Lord's vineyard and turned His desirable portion into wilderness. Isaiah announces the beloved vineyard's removal of protection and abandonment, explicitly identifying it with Israel and Judah. Both depict the devastated people through a vineyard once valued by God.

Jeremiah 12:11
It was reduced to ruin and destruction; because of Me the whole land utterly vanished, because there is no man who lays it to heart.
  Isaiah 42:24-25 — devastation that people do not take to heart: Jeremiah describes the land's ruin and says no one lays it to heart. Isaiah likewise describes Israel handed to plunder, war and burning without understanding or taking it to soul. Both connect destructive judgment with a failure to comprehend its significance.

Jeremiah 12:12
Upon every passage through the wilderness those bringing misery came, because the Lord’s sword will devour from one end of the earth to the other; there is no peace for any flesh.
  Jeremiah 32:31-33 — the Lord's sword and devastation across the earth: Jeremiah 12 describes the Lord's sword consuming from one end of the earth to the other, leaving no flesh at peace. Jeremiah 32 describes His judgment with all flesh, the ungodly given to the sword and wounded people lying across the earth. The broad scope and divine judicial agency correspond.

Jeremiah 12:13
Sow wheat and harvest thorns! Their allotments will not benefit them. Be ashamed of your boasting, of reproach before the Lord.
  Hosea 8:7 — frustrated sowing and an unprofitable harvest: Jeremiah contrasts wheat sown with thorns reaped and denies benefit from the allotments. Hosea likewise describes sowing that yields a weak sheaf unable to make flour, with any produce taken by foreigners. Both use failed agricultural return in an oracle of judgment, without making their crop images identical.

Jeremiah 12:14
For thus says the Lord concerning all the evil neighbors who touch My inheritance that I apportioned to My people Israel: Behold, I am tearing them from their land, and I will cast Judah out from their midst.
  Jeremiah 18:7-10 — removal of nations under divine judgment: Jeremiah 12 announces tearing evil neighbors from their land alongside Judah's displacement. Jeremiah 18 describes the Lord's authority to remove and destroy a nation or kingdom, with its response affecting the announced outcome. The shared removal language places judgment beyond Judah alone.

Jeremiah 12:15
And after I cast them out, I will return and have mercy on them and settle each in his inheritance and each in his land.
  Deuteronomy 30:3-5 — mercy and resettlement after dispersion: Jeremiah promises renewed mercy and return to each inheritance after expulsion. Deuteronomy promises mercy, gathering from dispersion and return to ancestral land. The movement from removal to merciful resettlement corresponds, while Jeremiah's surrounding oracle also addresses neighboring peoples.

Jeremiah 12:16
And if they truly learn My people’s way, to swear by My name, ‘The Lord lives,’ as they taught My people to swear by Baal, then they will be built up in the midst of My people.
  Isaiah 56:6-7 — outsiders attached to the Lord and welcomed among His people: Jeremiah promises that neighbors who learn the people's way and swear by the Lord instead of Baal will be built up among His people. Isaiah welcomes foreigners attached to the Lord, serving Him, loving His name and holding His covenant. Both describe inclusion conditioned on attachment to the Lord rather than prior foreign identity.
  Zechariah 2:15 — nations becoming the Lord's people: Jeremiah speaks of nations learning the Lord's way and being built up among His people. Zechariah says many nations will take refuge with the Lord and become His people within Zion. Both explicitly envisage nations entering a relationship of belonging to the Lord.

Jeremiah 12:17
But if they do not return, I will remove that nation with removal and destruction.
  Jeremiah 18:7-10 — conditional judgment upon a nation's response: Jeremiah 12 makes removal and destruction depend on a nation's refusal to return. Jeremiah 18 likewise makes the announced destruction of a nation responsive to whether it turns from evil, and reverses promised good when it disobeys. The conditional structure, not only the vocabulary of destruction, connects the passages.

Jeremiah 13:1
Thus says the Lord: Go, acquire a linen waistcloth for yourself, and put it around your waist, and it will not pass through water.
  Jeremiah 13:11 — same-chapter interpretation of the waistcloth sign: The instruction to put a linen waistcloth around the prophet's waist receives its explicit interpretation when the Lord compares the clinging cloth to Israel and Judah made to cling to Him. The reference connects the particular commanded object and placement to its stated meaning.

Jeremiah 13:2
And I acquired the waistcloth according to the Lord’s word and put it around my waist.
  Jeremiah 13:1 — same-chapter enactment of a specific command: The prophet's acquisition and wearing of the waistcloth carries out the preceding command to acquire it and put it around his waist. The source itself says the action followed the Lord's word; the target supplies that particular word and its object.

Jeremiah 13:4
Take the waistcloth around your waist, and rise and go to the Euphrates, and hide it there in the rock’s opening.
  Jeremiah 13:7-10 — same-chapter concealment and interpretation of the ruined cloth: The command to hide the waistcloth at the Euphrates in a rock opening leads to its retrieval in a ruined, unusable state and the Lord's comparison with the people's fate. The connection follows the specific object through the sign's action and explicit interpretation.

Jeremiah 13:5
And I went and hid it at the Euphrates, as the Lord commanded me.
  Jeremiah 13:4 — same-chapter execution of the concealment command: The prophet says he went and hid the cloth at the Euphrates as commanded. The target identifies the command, the same river and the rock opening where the object was to be hidden. This connects the stated obedience to its precise instruction.

Jeremiah 13:6
And after many days the Lord said to me: Rise, go to the Euphrates, and take from there the waistcloth that I commanded you to hide there.
  Jeremiah 13:7 — same-chapter retrieval instruction and outcome: The Lord orders a return after many days to take the hidden cloth. The next unit records the return, digging and retrieval, revealing that the cloth is ruined. The particular retrieval links the command with the observed condition that gives the sign its force.

Jeremiah 13:7
And I went to the Euphrates River and dug and took the waistcloth from the place where I had buried it. And behold, it was ruined, so that it could certainly be used for nothing.
  Jeremiah 13:9-10 — same-chapter physical sign and its stated meaning: The retrieved waistcloth is ruined and useful for nothing. The Lord then applies that condition to Judah and Jerusalem's arrogance and disobedience, explicitly saying the people will be like this unusable cloth. This is the text's own interpretation of the sign.

Jeremiah 13:9
Thus says the Lord: Thus I will ruin Judah’s arrogance and Jerusalem’s arrogance,
  Proverbs 16:18 — arrogance brought to ruin: The Lord announces the ruin of Judah and Jerusalem's arrogance. Proverbs says insolence leads before ruin and evil-mindedness before a fall. The connection joins pride with its destructive outcome, while Jeremiah identifies a particular people and divine action.

Jeremiah 13:10
this great arrogance, those unwilling to obey My words and who went after foreign gods to serve them and bow down to them; and they will be like this waistcloth, which will be used for nothing.
  Jeremiah 13:7 — same-chapter comparison with the unusable waistcloth: The oracle says the disobedient, idol-serving people will be like the waistcloth useful for nothing. The earlier retrieval gives the concrete condition to which they are compared: the hidden cloth was ruined and unusable. The explicit comparison supports the reference without inventing a separate sign.

Jeremiah 13:11
For just as the waistcloth clings around a man’s waist, so I made Israel’s house and all Judah’s house cling to Myself, to become for Me a renowned people, a boast and a glory; and they did not listen to Me.
  Deuteronomy 26:18-19 — chosen people intended for renown and glory: Jeremiah says Israel and Judah were made to cling to the Lord as a renowned people, a boast and a glory, but did not listen. Deuteronomy links the people's chosen status and obedience with being renowned, a boast and glorious. The shared sequence of chosen belonging, intended honor and obedience is particularly close.

Jeremiah 13:12
And you will say to this people: Every wineskin will be filled with wine. And if they say to you, ‘Do we not surely know that every wineskin will be filled with wine?’
  Jeremiah 13:13-14 — same-chapter proverb explained as judgment: The saying that every wineskin will be filled with wine is answered by the Lord's announcement that the inhabitants and their leaders will be filled with intoxication and scattered. The target explicitly turns the familiar wineskin saying into a judgment on the people; no earthenware jar is substituted.

Jeremiah 13:13
then you will say to them: Thus says the Lord: Behold, I am filling those dwelling in this land, and their kings, David’s sons who sit on his throne, and the priests and prophets and Judah and all those dwelling in Jerusalem, with intoxication,
  Isaiah 51:17-21 — judgment portrayed as collective intoxication: Jeremiah announces filling the land's inhabitants, rulers, priests and prophets with intoxication. Isaiah addresses Jerusalem as drunk but not from wine after drinking the Lord's cup of anger. Both use intoxication to portray collective catastrophe, with different drinking-vessel imagery in their contexts.

Jeremiah 13:14
and I will scatter them, a man and his brother, their fathers and their sons together. I will not yearn for them, says the Lord, nor spare nor pity them so as to withhold their destruction.
  Ezekiel 5:10-12 — scattering families without sparing: Jeremiah announces scattering brothers, fathers and sons and declares that the Lord will not spare or pity them so as to withhold destruction. Ezekiel's oracle likewise combines familial catastrophe and scattering with the Lord's refusal to spare or have mercy. The shared pattern is severe communal judgment; the particular family injuries differ.

Jeremiah 13:15
Hear and give ear, and do not exalt yourselves, because the Lord has spoken.
  Proverbs 16:18 — warning against self-exaltation before ruin: Jeremiah tells the hearers not to exalt themselves because the Lord has spoken, within the preceding announcement of destruction. Proverbs warns that insolence goes before ruin and evil-mindedness before a fall. The wisdom saying reinforces the oracle's particular warning against pride in the face of judgment.

Jeremiah 13:16
Give glory to the Lord your God before darkness comes and before your feet stumble on dark mountains; you will wait for light, and there is death’s shadow, and they will be placed in darkness.
  John 12:35 — responding while light remains: Jeremiah urges giving God glory before darkness comes and feet stumble while people wait for light. Jesus urges walking while the light remains so that darkness does not overtake the hearers and leave them without direction. Both press for response before the loss of light makes the way uncertain.

Jeremiah 13:17
But if you do not hear, your soul will weep in secret because of arrogance, and your eyes will bring down tears, because the Lord’s flock was crushed.
  Lamentations 1:2 — tears over the community's devastation: Jeremiah warns that the addressed soul and eyes will weep because the Lord's flock was crushed. Lamentations pictures Jerusalem weeping at night with tears on her cheeks and no comforter. The shared grief concerns the devastated community; Jeremiah's wording is not changed into a statement about the prophet's own eyes.

Jeremiah 13:18
Say to the king and those exercising power: Humble yourselves and sit down, because your crown of glory was taken down from your head.
  Lamentations 5:16 — the fallen crown as loss of honor: Jeremiah tells the king and those in power to humble themselves because their crown of glory has been taken from their head. Lamentations says the crown fell from the people's head and connects the loss with sin. Both portray humbling and lost honor through the fallen crown, with different addressees.

Jeremiah 13:19
The southern cities were shut, and there was no one opening them. Judah was deported; a complete deportation was accomplished.
  2 Kings 25:21 — Judah's deportation from its land: Jeremiah declares Judah's deportation complete as the southern cities are shut. The Kings narrative concludes its account of executions at Deblatha by stating that Judah was deported from its land. The shared description of Judah's deportation connects the declaration with the narrative of exile without identifying a particular historical stage for Jeremiah's statement.

Jeremiah 13:20
Lift your eyes, Jerusalem, and see those coming from the north. Where is the flock given to you, the sheep of your glory?
  Jeremiah 23:1-2 — accountability for the Lord's lost flock: Jeremiah asks Jerusalem where the flock entrusted to her has gone as an enemy approaches from the north. Jeremiah 23 indicts shepherds who scattered and destroyed the Lord's sheep and failed to visit them. Both treat the people as a valued flock whose loss calls those responsible to account.

Jeremiah 13:21
What will you say when they visit you? And you taught them lessons against yourself for rule. Will not labor pains seize you like a woman giving birth?
  Jeremiah 6:22-24 — invasion likened to labor pains: Jeremiah asks whether labor pains will seize the addressed city like a woman giving birth when hostile rulers visit. Jeremiah 6 describes the northern attackers and the resulting affliction as labor pains like a woman giving birth. The same childbirth image portrays the distress caused by approaching domination.

Jeremiah 13:22
And if you say in your heart, ‘Why have these things happened to me?’ because of your great injustice your backside was uncovered, so that your heels were put on display.
  Nahum 3:5 — a city's wrongdoing answered with public exposure: Jeremiah explains the exposure of the city's backside and heels through her great injustice. Nahum announces the exposure of Nineveh's hind parts and the display of her shame to nations. Both use bodily exposure for public humiliation under judgment, without equating the two cities.

Jeremiah 13:23
If an Ethiopian changes his skin and a leopard her markings, then you too will be able to do well, having learned evil things.
  Matthew 7:17-20 — analogies for conduct that cannot produce its opposite: Jeremiah compares the difficulty of doing good after learning evil with changing skin or a leopard's markings. Jesus says a rotten tree cannot bear good fruit in His teaching about discerning false prophets. Both use an analogy of stable characteristics to expose the problem of expecting good conduct from an evil pattern.

Jeremiah 13:24
And I scattered them like brushwood carried by wind into a wilderness.
  Psalms 82:14-16 — wind-carried vegetation as dispersal in judgment: Jeremiah describes the people scattered like brushwood carried by wind into wilderness. The psalm asks God to make enemies like straw before wind and pursue them with a storm. Both use light vegetation blown away to depict people dispersed under divine judgment.

Jeremiah 13:25
This is your lot and portion for your disobeying Me, says the Lord, because you forgot Me and hoped in falsehoods.
  Jeremiah 2:32-35 — forgetting the Lord and facing judgment: Jeremiah assigns the people's portion to disobedience, forgetting the Lord and trusting falsehood. Jeremiah 2 likewise charges the people with forgetting Him and announces judgment against their claim of innocence. Both connect forgetting the Lord with wrongdoing and judgment; the explicit claim of innocence belongs to Jeremiah 2.

Jeremiah 13:26
And I will uncover your backside over your face, and your dishonor will be seen.
  Nahum 3:5 — closely parallel exposure of a city's dishonor: Jeremiah announces uncovering the city's backside over her face so that dishonor is seen. Nahum uses the same striking exposure over the face and shows the city's shame to nations and kingdoms. The literary parallel concerns the image of public disgrace, not an identification of Jerusalem with Nineveh.

Jeremiah 13:27
And your adultery and your neighing and the estrangement of your prostitution—on the hills and in the fields I have seen your abominations. Woe to you, Jerusalem, because you were not cleansed after Me! How much longer?
  Jeremiah 5:7-8 — adultery represented by neighing: Jeremiah 13 joins the city's adultery, neighing and prostitution to its abominations. Jeremiah 5 likewise describes adultery and prostitution, then compares the men to horses neighing after a neighbor's wife. The unusual equine image makes the connection more specific than a general accusation of sin.

Jeremiah 14:1
And the Lord’s word came to Jeremiah concerning the lack of rain:
  Amos 4:7-8 — lack of rain in an oracle of judgment: Jeremiah introduces the Lord's word concerning lack of rain. Amos describes the Lord withholding rainfall and cities searching unsuccessfully for sufficient water. The later details of Jeremiah's drought scene fit this particular prophetic treatment of restricted rain and resulting thirst.

Jeremiah 14:2
Judea mourned, and her gates were emptied and darkened upon the earth, and Jerusalem’s cry rose.
  Joel 1:10-12 — communal mourning when the land fails: Jeremiah describes mourning Judea, emptied gates and Jerusalem's cry in the lack of rain. Joel likewise calls for the earth to mourn as grain, wine, oil and trees fail and joy is shamed. Both connect public grief with the collapse of the land's provision, while their immediate disaster descriptions differ.

Jeremiah 14:3
Her magnates sent their younger ones for water; they came to wells and found no water and brought back their vessels empty.
  Amos 4:7-8 — unsuccessful search for water: Jeremiah's emissaries return from wells with empty vessels because they found no water. Amos describes people from several cities coming to one city for water but still not being filled. Both depict an organized search for water that fails to overcome the scarcity.

Jeremiah 14:4
And the land’s works failed because there was no rain; the farmers were ashamed and covered their heads.
  Joel 1:10-12 — failed produce and distressed farmers: Jeremiah says the land's works failed without rain and farmers were ashamed. Joel addresses farmers and estates amid dried grain, failed harvest and diminished trees. Both present agricultural failure together with the distress of those dependent on the fields.

Jeremiah 14:5
And deer in the field gave birth and abandoned their young, because there was no pasture.
  Joel 1:18 — animals deprived of pasture: Jeremiah describes deer abandoning their young because there is no pasture. Joel describes cattle weeping for lack of pasture and sheep disappearing. The connection concerns animals suffering the failure of their food supply; abandonment of young is specific to Jeremiah's image.

Jeremiah 14:6
Wild donkeys stood in wooded valleys; they drew in wind, their eyes failed, because there was no grass, because of a people of injustice.
  Joel 1:19-20 — animals suffering the loss of sustenance: Jeremiah's wild donkeys draw in wind and their eyes fail because there is no grass. Joel describes wilderness vegetation consumed and animals looking up because water outlets dried up. Both extend the land's failure to animals dependent on its sustenance, without making the causes or behaviors identical.

Jeremiah 14:7
If our sins stood against us, Lord, act for us for Thine own sake, because our sins before Thee are many, because we sinned against Thee.
  Psalms 78:8-9 — appeal for help for God's own sake despite sin: Jeremiah acknowledges many sins standing against the people but asks the Lord to act for His own sake. The psalm likewise asks for compassion, rescue and forgiveness for the glory of God's name. Both seek help for God's own sake while acknowledging wrongdoing; the psalm explicitly names His name.

Jeremiah 14:8
Israel’s patient expectation, Lord, Thou savest in a time of evils; why didst Thou become like a sojourner upon the earth and like a native turning aside to a lodging?
  Psalms 73:1-2 — prayer against apparent estrangement from God's people: Jeremiah addresses Israel's saving hope but asks why He seems like a sojourner or one turning aside to lodge. The psalm asks why God rejected His flock and urges remembrance of the congregation and Zion where He encamped. Both appeal from apparent withdrawal to the established relationship between God, people and dwelling.

Jeremiah 14:9
Wilt Thou be like a sleeping man, or like a man unable to save? Yet Thou art among us, Lord, and Thy name has been called over us; do not forget us.
  Psalms 43:24-27 — appeal to the apparently sleeping deliverer: Jeremiah asks whether the Lord is like a sleeping man or a man unable to save, then pleads that He not forget the people bearing His name. The psalm asks why the Lord sleeps and forgets affliction, then calls Him to arise and redeem for His name's sake. The images and sequence of the plea closely correspond.

Jeremiah 14:10
Thus says the Lord to this people: They loved to move their feet and did not restrain them, and God was not pleased with them; now He will remember their injustices.
  Hosea 8:13 — God now remembers the people's injustices: Jeremiah announces divine displeasure and says God will now remember their injustices. Hosea likewise says the Lord will not accept the people's sacrifice and will now remember their injustices and punish their sins. The repeated remembrance formula marks an impending accounting rather than acceptance.

Jeremiah 14:11
And the Lord said to me: Do not pray for good things for this people,
  Jeremiah 7:16 — repeated restriction of the prophet's prayer: The Lord tells Jeremiah not to pray for good things for this people. Jeremiah 7 similarly forbids prayer, requests for mercy and approaching on their behalf. Both restrict the prophet's intercession in the face of the people's persistent wrongdoing.

Jeremiah 14:12
