because if they fast, I will not listen to their supplication, and if they bring whole burnt offerings and sacrifices, I will not be pleased with them, because by sword and famine and death I will bring them to an end.
  Isaiah 1:11-15 — rejected offerings and supplication: Jeremiah says fasting will not secure a hearing and burnt offerings or sacrifices will not please the Lord. Isaiah likewise rejects the multitude of sacrifices and refuses to hear multiplied supplication from bloodstained hands. Both deny that religious activity itself averts judgment on an unrepentant people.

Jeremiah 14:13
And I said: O Lord, behold, their prophets prophesy and say, ‘You will not see sword, nor will famine be among you, because I will give truth and peace upon the land and in this place.’
  Jeremiah 23:16-17 — false assurances of peace: Jeremiah reports prophets promising freedom from sword and famine and peace in the land. Jeremiah 23 describes prophets speaking from their own hearts who promise peace and no evil to those rejecting the Lord's word. Both expose reassuring prophetic messages that contradict the announced judgment.

Jeremiah 14:14
And the Lord said to me: The prophets prophesy false things in My name; I did not send them or command them or speak to them, because they prophesy false visions, divinations, omens, and their heart’s choices to you.
  Ezekiel 13:6-9 — unsent prophets presenting false visions as divine speech: The Lord denies sending or speaking to Jeremiah's false prophets and names false visions and divinations. Ezekiel likewise describes vain visions and divinations presented as the Lord's word by people He did not send. Both distinguish the divine name claimed by the speakers from their actual lack of commission.

Jeremiah 14:15
Therefore thus says the Lord concerning the prophets prophesying false things in My name, though I did not send them, who say, ‘Sword and famine will not be upon this land’: They will die a diseased death, and the prophets will be consumed by famine.
  Jeremiah 35:15-17 — death announced for an unsent prophet: Jeremiah 14 announces death for prophets who falsely promise safety though the Lord did not send them. Jeremiah 35 provides the particular case of Hananiah, declared unsent, sentenced to die that year and then reported dead. The case parallels the judgment on false prophecy; it does not establish famine as Hananiah's cause of death.

Jeremiah 14:16
And the people to whom they prophesy will be thrown into Jerusalem’s thoroughfares because of sword and famine, and there will be no one burying them, and their wives and sons and daughters; and I will pour their evils upon them.
  Psalms 78:2-3 — Jerusalem's dead left without burial: Jeremiah describes the people and their families thrown into Jerusalem's thoroughfares through sword and famine, with no one burying them. The psalm likewise describes corpses and blood around Jerusalem with no one to bury the dead. The connection is the burial catastrophe; the psalm calls its dead God's servants, while Jeremiah describes recipients of false prophecy.

Jeremiah 14:17
And you will say this word to them: Bring tears down upon your eyes day and night, and let them not cease, because the daughter of My people was utterly crushed, with a very painful wound.
  Lamentations 2:18-19 — unceasing tears for the shattered daughter of the people: Jeremiah commands tears day and night without ceasing because the daughter of the people has been crushed. Lamentations similarly commands Zion's walls to bring down tears day and night without pause and to pour out the heart like water. The shared time, command and communal grief make the connection direct.

Jeremiah 14:18
If I go out to the plain, behold, those wounded by the sword; and if I enter the city, behold, the pain of famine! For priest and prophet went to a land they did not know.
  Lamentations 4:9 — sword and famine as paired forms of suffering: Jeremiah encounters the sword's wounded in the plain and famine's pain in the city. Lamentations compares those wounded by the sword with those wounded by famine. Both set these two forms of suffering together, although Lamentations also judges one condition better than the other.

Jeremiah 14:19
Hast Thou utterly rejected Judah, and has Thy soul departed from Zion? Why didst Thou strike us, and there is no healing for us? We waited for peace, and there were no good things; for a time of healing, and behold, turmoil!
  Jeremiah 8:15 — frustrated expectation of peace and healing: Both passages describe an expectation of peace with no good result and a time of healing answered by distress. Jeremiah 14 says turmoil and Jeremiah 8 says haste in the final clause. The repeated structure supports the reference while preserving that wording difference.

Jeremiah 14:20
We recognized, Lord, our sins and our fathers’ injustices, because we sinned before Thee.
  Psalms 105:6 — confession of the people's and fathers' wrongdoing: Jeremiah acknowledges the people's sins and their fathers' injustices before the Lord. The psalm likewise confesses sin with the fathers and acts of lawlessness and injustice. Both combine present confession with acknowledgment of the ancestral pattern.

Jeremiah 14:21
Abate for Thy name’s sake; do not destroy Thy throne of glory. Remember; do not shatter Thy covenant with us.
  Psalms 73:18-20 — appeal to God's name and covenant in catastrophe: Jeremiah asks for restraint for the Lord's name and remembrance of His covenant. The psalm likewise asks God to remember the reproach against His name, not forget the needy and look upon His covenant. Both appeal to the existing relationship as the ground for intervention.

Jeremiah 14:22
Is there among the nations’ idols one who brings rain? Can heaven give its fullness? Is it not Thou Thyself? And we will wait for Thee, because Thou madest all these things.
  Acts 14:15-17 — creator and giver of rain contrasted with worthless gods: Jeremiah denies that the nations' idols can bring rain and directs hope to the One who made all things. Acts calls hearers from worthless worship to the living maker of heaven and earth, whose witness includes rain and fruitful seasons. Both connect rain to the creator; Acts's immediate scene concerns a crowd attempting to worship the apostles as gods.

Jeremiah 15:1
And the Lord said to me: If Moses and Samuel stand before Me, My soul is not toward them. Send this people away, and let them go out.
  Exodus 32:11-14 — Moses as an effective intercessor contrasted with present refusal: Jeremiah says that even Moses standing before the Lord would not turn His disposition toward this people. Exodus records Moses pleading for Israel and the Lord being appeased concerning the announced calamity. The earlier successful intercession explains the force of naming Moses in the present refusal.
  1 Samuel 7:8-9 — Samuel as a heard intercessor contrasted with present refusal: Jeremiah names Samuel alongside Moses when declaring that even their standing before God would not secure acceptance. Samuel's narrative records the people asking him to keep crying for them and the Lord hearing him. That established intercessory role gives the contrast its particular significance.

Jeremiah 15:2
And if they say to you, ‘Where shall we go out?’ you will say to them: Thus says the Lord: As many as are for death, to death; as many for sword, to sword; as many for famine, to famine; as many for captivity, to captivity.
  Jeremiah 50:11 — repeated assignment to death, sword and deportation: Jeremiah 15 assigns people to death, sword, famine or captivity. Jeremiah 50 uses the same distribution formula for Egypt, naming death, deportation and sword. The repeated form and overlapping outcomes correspond; famine is not included in the target's list.

Jeremiah 15:3
And I will exact vengeance upon them in four kinds, says the Lord: the sword for slaughter, dogs for tearing apart, the earth’s wild beasts and heaven’s birds for eating and destruction.
  Ezekiel 14:21 — fourfold divine retribution with differing lists: Jeremiah announces four kinds of vengeance involving sword, dogs, wild beasts and birds. Ezekiel announces four evil retributions involving sword, famine, evil beasts and death. Both explicitly organize divine judgment in four kinds and share sword and beasts, but their fourfold lists are not treated as identical.

Jeremiah 15:4
And I will hand them over to distresses for all the earth’s kingdoms because of Manasseh son of Hezekiah, king of Judah, concerning all he did in Jerusalem.
  2 Kings 24:3-4 — Manasseh's acts in Jerusalem as grounds for Judah's judgment: Jeremiah attributes the people's distress among kingdoms to Manasseh son of Hezekiah and what he did in Jerusalem. Kings likewise connects Judah's removal with Manasseh's sins and the innocent blood with which he filled Jerusalem. The named king, location and stated causal connection coincide.

Jeremiah 15:5
Who will spare you, Jerusalem? Who will be afraid for you? Or who will turn back for your peace?
  Nahum 3:7 — rhetorical questions about an unpitied city: Jeremiah asks who will spare Jerusalem, be afraid for her or turn back for her peace. Nahum asks who will lament miserable Nineveh or provide consolation. Both frame the city's isolation under judgment through questions about the absence of compassionate response.

Jeremiah 15:6
You turned away from Me, says the Lord; you will go backward. And I will stretch out My hand and destroy you, and I will no longer let them go.
  Jeremiah 7:24 — turning away and going backward: Jeremiah 15 addresses a people who turned away from the Lord and will go backward. Jeremiah 7 similarly describes refusal to hear, following the evil heart and going backward instead of forward. Both use reversed movement to describe the people's response to the Lord.

Jeremiah 15:7
And I will scatter them in a dispersion; at My people’s gates they were bereaved; they destroyed My people because of their evils.
  Hosea 9:12-13 — bereavement as communal judgment: Jeremiah describes dispersion, bereavement and destruction among the people because of their evils. Hosea likewise announces bereavement and explicitly describes children reared and exposed to piercing. Both include bereavement within communal judgment, with the affected children named explicitly in Hosea.

Jeremiah 15:8
Their widows multiplied beyond the sea’s sand. I brought misery upon a young man’s mother at noon; suddenly I cast trembling and haste upon her.
  Lamentations 5:3 — multiplied widowhood amid national loss: Jeremiah describes widows multiplying and calamity falling suddenly on a young man's mother. Lamentations speaks for a community made fatherless whose mothers are like widows. The shared family loss connects the prophetic warning with the communal lament, without repeating Jeremiah's numerical comparison or timing.

Jeremiah 15:9
She who bore seven was emptied; her soul grew faint. Her sun set while the day was still at its middle; she was ashamed and reproached. Their remnants I will give to the sword before their enemies.
  Amos 8:9-10 — midday sunset and mourning: Jeremiah pictures a bereaved mother's sun setting while the day is still at its middle. Amos likewise announces the sun setting at midday and joins daytime darkness to mourning for a beloved one. Both use premature darkness to intensify the portrayal of catastrophic grief.

Jeremiah 15:10
Woe to me, mother! What sort of man did you give birth to—a man litigating and disputing with the whole earth? I benefited no one, nor did anyone benefit me; my strength failed among those cursing me.
  Jeremiah 20:14-18 — prophetic lament over being born into distress: Jeremiah laments to his mother that he was born to litigation, dispute and curses. Jeremiah 20 likewise curses the day and announcement of his birth and asks why he left the womb to see labor, pain and shame. The connection concerns the burden of the prophet's life; no lending or borrowing is substituted for the source's benefited wording.

Jeremiah 15:11
May it be, Master, as they are directed—if I did not stand before Thee in the time of their evils and their affliction, for good toward the enemy!
  Jeremiah 18:20 — standing before God for the good of opponents: Jeremiah 15 appeals to having stood before the Lord in a time of affliction for good toward the enemy. Jeremiah 18 similarly asks God to remember the prophet's standing before Him to speak good for people who plotted against his life. Both contrast intercession for others with hostility received from them.

Jeremiah 15:13
is your strength. And I will give your treasures as plunder, an exchange for all your sins and in all your borders.
  Jeremiah 20:5 — treasures handed over as plunder: Jeremiah 15 announces treasures given as plunder in connection with sins throughout the borders. Jeremiah 20 likewise announces the city's strength and Judah's royal treasures given into enemy hands and carried to Babylon. The connection concerns the explicit loss of resources; the preceding fragment about iron is not reconstructed.

Jeremiah 15:14
And I will enslave you all around to your enemies in the land you did not know, because a fire has been kindled from My fury; it will burn upon you.
  Deuteronomy 28:36 — exile to an unfamiliar land: Jeremiah announces enslavement to enemies in an unfamiliar land. Deuteronomy likewise warns that the people and rulers will be led to a nation neither they nor their fathers knew, where service follows. Both describe removal into foreign domination; the target explicitly names service to other gods.
  Deuteronomy 32:22 — fire kindled from divine wrath: Jeremiah's warning of enslavement adds that fire kindled from the Lord's fury will burn upon the people. Deuteronomy likewise describes fire kindled from His wrath and its consuming reach. The shared origin and burning action connect the two judgment images.

Jeremiah 15:15
Lord, remember me and visit me and vindicate me against those pursuing me; do not delay. Know how I received reproach concerning Thee
  Psalms 68:8-10 — reproach borne on God's account: Jeremiah asks for remembrance and vindication while saying he received reproach concerning the Lord. The psalmist likewise says he endured reproach on God's account and bears the reproaches directed against Him. Both place the speaker's persecution within his relationship to God.

Jeremiah 15:16
from those rejecting Thy words. Bring them to an end, and Thy word will become to me rejoicing and my heart’s joy, because Thy name has been called over me, Lord Almighty.
  Psalms 118:111 — God's word as the heart's rejoicing: Jeremiah says the Lord's word will become rejoicing and his heart's joy amid rejection and reproach. The psalmist calls God's testimonies the heart's rejoicing. The shared delight in divine speech supports the connection; Jeremiah's supplied unit does not say that the prophet ate the words.

Jeremiah 15:17
I did not sit in their gathering as they played, but I was reverent before Thy hand. I sat alone because I was filled with bitterness.
  Psalms 25:4-5 — refusal to sit with an improper assembly: Jeremiah says he did not sit in the others' playful gathering but sat alone in reverence and bitterness. The psalmist likewise denies sitting with an empty council or the ungodly assembly. Both describe deliberate separation from a gathering, while Jeremiah adds the particular cost of solitude.

Jeremiah 15:18
Why do those grieving me overpower me? My wound is firm; from where will I be healed? It indeed became to me like false water having no faithfulness.
  Job 6:15-18 — unreliable water as an image of suffering and disappointment: Jeremiah describes a persistent wound and compares the experience to false water without faithfulness. Job compares the failure of those nearest him to a failing winter torrent and melting snow, followed by abandonment. Both use unreliable-water imagery to express suffering and disappointment; Job explicitly identifies failed human support, while Jeremiah's referent remains less explicit.

Jeremiah 15:19
Therefore thus says the Lord: If you return, I will restore you, and you will stand before Me. If you bring out the honored from the unworthy, you will be as My mouth; and they will turn to you, and you will not turn to them.
  Jeremiah 1:9-10 — commission to serve as God's mouth: Jeremiah 15 promises that, upon return and the separation of honored from unworthy, the prophet will be as the Lord's mouth. Jeremiah's initial commission places the Lord's words in his mouth and appoints him over nations and kingdoms. The later conditional promise recalls the concrete speaking commission without removing its condition.

Jeremiah 15:20
And I will make you to this people like a fortified bronze wall, and they will fight against you but certainly will not prevail against you, because I am with you to save you
  Jeremiah 1:18-19 — repeated promise of a bronze wall and rescue: Both passages make Jeremiah a fortified or strong bronze wall against the people and rulers, say that opponents will fight but not prevail, and ground deliverance in the Lord's presence. The repeated image and assurances directly connect the renewed promise to his earlier commission.

Jeremiah 15:21
and to rescue you from the hand of evil people, and I will ransom you from the hand of pestilent people.
  Psalms 96:10 — rescue from the hand of wrongdoers: The Lord promises to rescue and ransom Jeremiah from evil and pestilent people's hands. The psalm says the Lord guards His holy ones' souls and rescues them from sinners' hands. Both express protection from hostile human power through the same hand-and-rescue image.

Jeremiah 16:1
And you must not take a wife, says the Lord, Israel’s God,
  1 Corinthians 7:26-28 — marriage counsel under distress: Jeremiah receives a specific command not to take a wife in the setting of the coming disaster described in the following verses. Paul likewise counsels not seeking a wife because of present distress, while explicitly saying marriage is not sin. The connection concerns marriage under crisis and preserves the different scope of command and counsel.

Jeremiah 16:2
and no son or daughter will be born to you in this place.
  Jeremiah 16:3-4 — same-chapter reason for the prophetic family sign: The statement that no son or daughter will be born to Jeremiah is followed by an oracle concerning the area's children and parents and their impending death. The adjoining passage explains why the prophet's own family life becomes a sign of that particular catastrophe.

Jeremiah 16:4
They will die a diseased death; they will not be mourned or buried. They will be an example upon the earth’s surface, and for the earth’s wild beasts and heaven’s birds; they will fall by the sword and be consumed by famine.
  Jeremiah 7:33 — dead people exposed to birds and beasts: Jeremiah 16 announces unburied and unmourned deaths, with people becoming an example upon the land and prey for beasts and birds. Jeremiah 7 likewise says the people's dead will feed heaven's birds and the earth's beasts with no one driving them off. Both depict the denial of protective burial through exposure to animal consumption.

Jeremiah 16:5
Thus says the Lord: Do not enter their gathering or go to beat yourself in mourning, and do not mourn them, because I have removed My peace from this people.
  Ezekiel 24:16-18 — prophet forbidden ordinary mourning as a sign: Jeremiah is told not to enter mourning or lament because the Lord has removed peace from the people. Ezekiel is likewise instructed not to perform ordinary mourning and then reports his wife's death and obedience. Both make restricted prophetic mourning part of a judgment sign, though their personal circumstances differ.

Jeremiah 16:6
They certainly will not mourn them or make cuts, and they will not shave themselves,
  Leviticus 19:28 — mourning incisions as a named practice: Jeremiah predicts that no mourning cuts will be made for the dead. Leviticus prohibits incisions in the body for a dead person. The law identifies the same funerary practice named in the oracle; Jeremiah's prediction of its absence is not turned into a new legal command.
  Deuteronomy 14:1 — baldness made for the dead: Jeremiah says the people will not shave themselves in mourning. Deuteronomy prohibits making a bald place between the eyes for the dead. Both name bodily hair removal in a funerary setting, with a more specific bodily location in the law and a prediction in the oracle.

Jeremiah 16:7
and bread certainly will not be broken in their mourning for consolation over one dead; they will not give him a cup for consolation over his father and mother.
  Ezekiel 24:21-23 — absence of consoling food and ordinary mourning: Jeremiah says bread will not be broken for consolation over the dead and no consoling cup will be given. Ezekiel's announcement of family loss likewise excludes eating the bread of men and ordinary mourning. The comparison concerns the interruption of funerary consolation; Ezekiel does not mention Jeremiah's cup.

Jeremiah 16:8
You must not enter a house of drinking to sit with them to eat and drink.
  Jeremiah 16:9 — same-chapter banquet prohibition and its explanation: The prophet is forbidden to sit with the people in a house of eating and drinking. The following because-clause explains that the Lord will stop joy, rejoicing and marriage voices from that place. The particular social prohibition functions as a sign of the announced end of communal celebration.

Jeremiah 16:9
Because thus says the Lord, Israel’s God: Behold, before your eyes and in your days I am stopping from this place the sound of joy and rejoicing, the voice of bridegroom and bride.
  Jeremiah 7:34 — repeated cessation of joy and marriage voices: Both oracles announce that the Lord will stop the sounds of joy and gladness and the voices of bridegroom and bride in the people's cities or place. Jeremiah 7 adds the desolation of the land, giving an earlier occurrence of the same judgment refrain.

Jeremiah 16:10
And when you announce all these utterances to this people and they say to you, ‘Why did the Lord speak all these evils against us? What is our injustice, and what is our sin that we sinned before the Lord our God?’
  Deuteronomy 29:23-25 — question about why the Lord brought disaster: Jeremiah anticipates the people's question about the evils announced against them and the wrongdoing behind them. Deuteronomy likewise anticipates questions about the Lord's anger against the land, answered by covenant abandonment and foreign worship. Both use a question-and-answer pattern to explain disaster through prior disobedience.

Jeremiah 16:11
you will say to them: Because your fathers abandoned Me, says the Lord, and went after foreign gods and served them and bowed down to them, and abandoned Me and did not keep My law,
  Deuteronomy 29:24-25 — ancestral covenant abandonment and foreign worship: Jeremiah explains the announced disaster through the fathers' abandonment of the Lord, service to foreign gods and failure to keep His law. Deuteronomy gives the corresponding explanation of forsaking the ancestral covenant and serving other gods. The relationship is the explicit cause assigned to judgment.

Jeremiah 16:12
and you acted more wickedly than your fathers, and behold, each of you follows what pleases your evil heart, so as not to obey Me,
  Jeremiah 7:25-26 — present disobedience exceeding the fathers' pattern: Jeremiah accuses the current people of doing worse than their fathers and following their own evil hearts rather than obeying. Jeremiah 7 likewise says repeated prophetic sending was answered by refusal to hear and a neck hardened beyond the fathers. Both emphasize continuation and intensification of the ancestral refusal.

Jeremiah 16:13
and I will cast you away from this land into the land neither you nor your fathers knew, and there you will serve other gods who will give you no mercy.
  Deuteronomy 28:36 — unfamiliar exile and service to other gods: Jeremiah announces removal to a land unknown to the people and fathers, where they will serve other gods without mercy. Deuteronomy warns of removal to a nation unknown to them and their fathers and service there to gods of wood and stone. The destination's unfamiliarity and the religious service are both explicit.

Jeremiah 16:14
Therefore behold, days are coming, says the Lord, and they will no longer say, ‘The Lord lives, who brought Israel’s sons up from Egypt’s land,’
  Jeremiah 23:7-8 — repeated replacement of the exodus oath formula: Jeremiah 16 says the coming oath will no longer refer simply to the Lord bringing Israel from Egypt; the following unit names the new return. Jeremiah 23 repeats that movement from the Egyptian exodus to gathering from northern and other lands. The full target preserves the replacement's positive half as necessary context.

Jeremiah 16:15
but, ‘The Lord lives, who brought Israel’s house up from the northern land and all the regions where they were driven.’ And I will restore them to their land that I gave their fathers.
  Jeremiah 23:8 — repeated gathering from northern and scattered lands: Both passages describe the Lord bringing Israel from the northern land and all the regions of dispersion and restoring the people to their land. The close wording links two statements of the promised return in this edition's native chapter order.
  Deuteronomy 30:3-5 — return from dispersion to ancestral land: Jeremiah promises return from every region of displacement to the land given to the fathers. Deuteronomy promises gathering from the nations and the ends of heaven and return to the land the fathers inherited. The passages share the geographical reversal from dispersion to ancestral possession.

Jeremiah 16:16
Behold, I am sending the many fishermen, says the Lord, and they will fish for them; and after these things I will send the many hunters, and they will hunt them on every mountain and hill and from the rocks’ openings.
  Amos 9:1-4 — judgment pursuit leaving no hiding place: Jeremiah announces fishermen and then hunters who pursue people on mountains, hills and in rock openings. Amos likewise describes divine pursuit of fugitives even on Carmel's summit, in sea depths or in captivity. Both portray an inescapable search in a judgment setting; Jeremiah's fishing is not treated as a mission to gather disciples.

Jeremiah 16:17
Because My eyes are upon all their ways, and their wrongdoings were not hidden before My eyes.
  Proverbs 15:3 — divine sight includes every way and wrongdoing: Jeremiah says the Lord's eyes are upon all the people's ways and their wrongs are not hidden. Proverbs says His eyes are in every place, observing both evil and good people. Both explicitly connect the reach of divine sight with moral conduct.

Jeremiah 16:18
And I will repay double their injustices and sins, for which they profaned My land with the corpses of their abominations and with their lawless acts, by which they offended against My inheritance.
  Isaiah 40:2 — double recompense for sins: Jeremiah announces repayment of double for the people's injustices and sins. Isaiah's consolation says Jerusalem has received double for her sins from the Lord's hand. The shared measure connects an announcement of recompense with a proclamation that suffering is fulfilled, without supplying an independent chronology between the oracles.

Jeremiah 16:19
Lord, my strength and help and refuge in a day of evils, nations will come to Thee from the earth’s extremity and say: How our fathers acquired false idols, and there is no benefit in them!
  Isaiah 45:20-22 — nations turning from useless idols to the saving Lord: Jeremiah anticipates nations coming from the earth's extremity and confessing that their inherited idols are false and useless. Isaiah gathers those saved from the nations, contrasts wooden images unable to save with the only saving God, and calls the earth's extremity to turn to Him. The movement from failed idols to the Lord corresponds.
  Psalms 85:8-10 — nations coming to acknowledge the unique God: Jeremiah says nations will come to the Lord and recognize the falsehood of inherited idols. The psalm likewise anticipates all nations coming to bow before and glorify Him because He alone is the great God. Both envision the nations' acknowledgment of divine uniqueness.

Jeremiah 16:20
Will a human make gods for himself? And these are not gods.
  Isaiah 44:9-11 — human manufacture cannot produce a useful god: Jeremiah asks whether a human will make gods and says the resulting objects are not gods. Isaiah calls those fashioning gods futile and their carved products useless. Both challenge the divine status or benefit claimed for things humans manufacture.

Jeremiah 16:21
Therefore behold, at this time I will show them My hand and make My power known to them, and they will know that My name is Lord.
  Exodus 6:6-7 — God's power makes His name known: Jeremiah announces that the Lord will show His hand and power so that people know His name is Lord. Exodus joins deliverance by an uplifted arm and great judgment to the promise that Israel will know He is the Lord their God. Both connect recognition of His name with an enacted display of His power.

Jeremiah 17:5
Cursed is the person who places his hope in a human and will rest the flesh of his arm upon him, and whose heart departs from the Lord;
  Psalms 145:3-5 — parallel warning against human reliance in place of hope in God: Jeremiah curses reliance on a human when the heart departs from the Lord; the psalm warns against trusting rulers and human beings who cannot save, then blesses hope in God. The contrast concerns the object of trust, not a ban on every human relationship.

Jeremiah 17:6
and he will be like a wild tamarisk in the wilderness; he will not see when good things come, and will dwell among salt-plants and in a wilderness, in a salt land that is not inhabited.
  Jeremiah 17:7-8 — same-chapter contrast between desolate and well-watered plants: The person relying on a human is compared to a wild tamarisk in an uninhabited salt wilderness. The following blessing compares the person trusting the Lord to a tree rooted beside water and continuing to bear fruit, forming the oracle's deliberate botanical contrast.

Jeremiah 17:7
And blessed is the person who has trusted in the Lord, and the Lord will be his hope;
  Psalms 145:5 — close blessing of the person whose hope is the Lord: Both explicitly pronounce blessing or happiness upon a person whose hope is in the Lord. The psalm further calls the God of Jacob that person's Helper.

Jeremiah 17:8
and he will be like a tree flourishing beside waters, and will send its roots toward moisture, and will not fear when heat comes. Wooded stems will be upon it; in a rainless year it will not fear or cease bearing fruit.
  Psalms 1:3 — close image of the fruitful tree beside water: Both portray a favored person as a tree beside water that continues to bear fruit. Jeremiah expressly addresses heat and a rainless year; the psalm adds an unfailing leaf and prosperity in the person's actions.

Jeremiah 17:9
The heart is deep beyond all things, and so is a human; and who will know him?
  Proverbs 20:5 — related depth of the human heart and difficulty of knowing it: Jeremiah calls the heart deep and asks who will know the human being; Proverbs compares counsel in a person's heart to deep water that an intelligent person draws out. The comparison preserves depth and discernment without importing a claim that Jeremiah calls the heart deceitful.

Jeremiah 17:10
I, the Lord, examine hearts and test kidneys, to give each according to his ways and the fruits of his practices.
  Psalms 7:10 — close divine testing of hearts and kidneys: Jeremiah names the Lord as examiner of hearts and tester of kidneys; the psalm addresses God with the same paired inward testing. Both place that examination in a setting of distinguishing righteous and wicked conduct.
  Romans 2:6 — close repayment according to each person's deeds: Jeremiah says the Lord gives each person according to his ways and the fruits of his practices; Romans says God repays each according to his works. This target supplies the explicit recompense clause, distinct from the heart-and-kidney imagery.

Jeremiah 17:11
A partridge called; it gathered what it did not bear. One makes his wealth without judgment; in the middle of his days they will abandon him, and at his last he will be foolish.
  Proverbs 13:11 — related adverse outcomes of unjustly acquired wealth: Jeremiah joins wealth made without judgment to abandonment in the middle of the possessor's days and final foolishness; Proverbs says property pursued with lawlessness diminishes. Proverbs does not reproduce the partridge image or Jeremiah's timing in the middle of the person's days.

Jeremiah 17:12
An exalted throne of glory is our sanctuary,
  Ezekiel 43:7 — related sanctuary as the place of God's throne: Jeremiah calls the sanctuary an exalted throne of glory. In Ezekiel's temple vision, God identifies the place of His throne and the footprints of His feet where His name will dwell among Israel.

Jeremiah 17:13
Israel’s patient expectation, Lord, let all who abandoned Thee be ashamed; let those who departed be written upon the earth, because they abandoned the Lord, the fountain of life.
  Jeremiah 2:13 — close accusation of abandoning the divine fountain of life: Both passages condemn abandoning the Lord under the image of life-giving water. Jeremiah 2 develops the contrast with broken cisterns, whereas Jeremiah 17 asks shame upon those abandoning Him.

Jeremiah 17:14
Heal me, Lord, and I will be healed; save me, and I will be saved, because Thou art my boast.
  Psalms 6:3-5 — parallel appeal to the Lord for healing and salvation: Jeremiah asks the Lord to heal and save him; the psalm likewise asks divine healing and rescue. Jeremiah grounds the appeal in the Lord as his boast, while the psalm appeals to mercy amid bodily and inward distress.

Jeremiah 17:15
Behold, they say to me: Where is the Lord’s word? Let it come!
  Isaiah 5:19 — close challenge that the announced divine action should arrive: Jeremiah reports people asking where the Lord's word is and demanding that it come. Isaiah reports a comparable challenge that God's action and the Holy One's counsel should approach so the challengers can see and know.

Jeremiah 17:16
But I did not grow weary following after Thee, and did not desire a human’s day; Thou knowest. The things going out through my lips are before Thee.
  Psalms 138:1-5 — related appeal to God's knowledge of the speaker: Jeremiah appeals to the Lord's knowledge and places what proceeds from his lips before Him. The psalm speaks of divine testing and knowledge of the speaker's movements and reasonings, then mentions the tongue and God's knowledge of all things; it does not explain Jeremiah's unusual phrase about a human's day.

Jeremiah 17:17
Do not become an estrangement to me, sparing me in an evil day.
  Psalms 70:9-12 — parallel plea against divine estrangement during danger: Jeremiah asks that the Lord not become an estrangement to him in an evil day. The psalm asks God not to forsake or remain far from the speaker while enemies plot against him.

Jeremiah 17:18
Let those pursuing me be ashamed, and may I not be ashamed; may they be terrified, and may I not be terrified. Bring an evil day upon them; crush them with double crushing.
  Psalms 34:4-8 — parallel appeal for shame and reversal upon pursuers: Both ask that those pursuing or seeking the speaker's life be shamed and overtaken by judgment. Jeremiah asks double crushing; the psalm develops reversal through the enemies' own hidden trap.

Jeremiah 17:19
Thus says the Lord: Go and stand in the gates of your people’s sons, through which Judah’s kings enter and leave, and in all Jerusalem’s gates,
  Nehemiah 13:19 — related use of Jerusalem's gates for Sabbath regulation: Jeremiah is sent to the gates to deliver the Sabbath warning that follows. Nehemiah later stations servants at Jerusalem's gates and closes them so that Sabbath burdens will not pass through, giving a concrete counterpart to the location of Jeremiah's warning.

Jeremiah 17:20
and you will say to them: Hear the Lord’s word, kings of Judah, all Judea and all Jerusalem, who enter these gates:
  Jeremiah 22:2 — close royal and public summons at the gates: Both summonses address Judah's king or kings together with the people entering the gates and call them to hear the Lord's word. Their surrounding instructions differ: Sabbath observance here and royal justice in chapter 22.

Jeremiah 17:21
Thus says the Lord: Guard your souls, and do not lift burdens on the Sabbath day or go out through Jerusalem’s gates,
  Nehemiah 13:15-19 — close prohibition of Sabbath burdens at Jerusalem's gates: Jeremiah forbids lifting Sabbath burdens and going out through Jerusalem's gates. Nehemiah describes burdens brought into Jerusalem on the Sabbath and orders the gates guarded against their passage; the directions of movement are not identical.

Jeremiah 17:22
and do not bring burdens out of your houses on the Sabbath day, and you must do no work; consecrate the Sabbath day, as I commanded your fathers. And they did not hear or incline their ear,
  Exodus 20:8-10 — explicit appeal to the inherited Sabbath command: Jeremiah commands the Sabbath's consecration and cessation from work, identifying this as what God commanded the fathers. Exodus supplies the command to keep the Sabbath holy and perform no work on it.

Jeremiah 17:23
and they hardened their neck beyond their fathers, so as not to hear Me or accept instruction.
  Nehemiah 9:16-17 — close hardened-neck refusal to hear inherited commandments: Jeremiah describes a neck hardened beyond the fathers and refusal to hear or accept instruction. The confession in Nehemiah likewise joins the fathers' stiffened necks with refusal to hear God's commandments.

Jeremiah 17:24
And if you truly listen to Me, says the Lord, not bringing burdens through this city’s gates on the Sabbath day and consecrating the Sabbath day by doing no work,
  Isaiah 58:13-14 — parallel conditional blessing attached to Sabbath observance: Jeremiah introduces blessing with the condition of listening, keeping the Sabbath holy and doing no work. Isaiah likewise conditions promised good upon honoring the Sabbath and refraining from one's own wishes and work; the promised benefits are expressed differently.

Jeremiah 17:25
then kings and rulers sitting on David’s throne will enter through this city’s gates, mounted on their chariots and horses, they and their rulers, Judah’s men and those dwelling in Jerusalem; and this city will be inhabited for ever.
  Jeremiah 22:4 — close promise of Davidic kings entering with chariots and horses: Both passages promise kings seated on David's throne entering through gates with chariots and horses if the preceding divine instruction is obeyed. Jeremiah 17 names the city's gates, while chapter 22 names the royal house's gates.

Jeremiah 17:26
And they will come from Judah’s cities, around Jerusalem, Benjamin’s land, the plain, the mountain and the south, bringing whole burnt offerings, sacrifice, incense offerings, grain offering and frankincense, bringing praise into the Lord’s house.
  Isaiah 56:7 — related offerings brought to the Lord's house: Jeremiah foresees worshippers from Judah and surrounding districts bringing offerings and praise into the Lord's house. Isaiah promises acceptable burnt offerings and sacrifices there for the foreigners described in its context; the groups are distinct, while the temple-and-offering connection is explicit.

Jeremiah 17:27
And if you do not listen to Me to consecrate the Sabbath day, not lifting burdens and not entering Jerusalem’s gates on the Sabbath day, I will kindle fire in her gates, and it will devour Jerusalem’s quarters and not be extinguished.
  2 Chronicles 36:19-21 — related warning and account of Jerusalem's burning: Jeremiah threatens unquenched fire consuming Jerusalem's quarters if Sabbath instructions are rejected. Chronicles records the burning of Jerusalem's citadels and the sanctuary, then relates the desolation to the land's Sabbaths and Jeremiah's word; it does not identify this individual warning as its sole referenced prediction.

Jeremiah 18:2
Rise and go down to the potter’s house, and there you will hear My words.
  Jeremiah 19:1-2 — related commands initiating pottery-based prophetic signs: God sends Jeremiah to the potter's house to hear His words; the following pottery sign begins with a command to acquire an earthenware jar and go to a specified place. The scenes use different actions and locations, while both divine instructions prepare a material sign involving pottery.

Jeremiah 18:3
And I went down to the potter’s house, and behold, he was doing work upon the stones.
  Sirach 38:29-30 — related observation of the potter at work: Jeremiah sees the potter working upon the stones. Sirach describes a potter at work turning a wheel with his feet and shaping clay with his arm; it supplies a craft parallel without equating the source's stones with an expressly named wheel.

Jeremiah 18:4
And the vessel he was making fell apart in his hands, and again he made it another vessel, as pleased him to make.
  Romans 9:21 — related potter's authority to shape a vessel: Jeremiah observes the potter remaking a vessel that fell apart according to what pleased him. Romans invokes the potter's authority over a lump of clay to make different vessels; it does not narrate the collapse and remaking seen by Jeremiah.

Jeremiah 18:6
Can I not shape you as this potter does, house of Israel? Behold, you are like the potter’s clay in My hands.
  Isaiah 64:7 — close divine shaping of a people pictured as clay: God tells Israel that they are clay in His hands like the potter's material. Isaiah's prayer likewise calls the people clay and the work of the Lord's hands, expressly addressing Him as Father.

Jeremiah 18:7
At last I will speak concerning a nation or kingdom, to remove them and destroy,
  Jeremiah 1:10 — close divine authority to remove and destroy nations: The Lord's announced action against a nation or kingdom recalls Jeremiah's commissioning over nations and kingdoms to uproot, demolish and destroy. Chapter 18 develops the conditional response to national conduct beyond the commissioning formula.

Jeremiah 18:8
and if that nation turns from all their evils, I will change My mind concerning the evils I planned to do to them.
  Jonah 3:8-10 — narrative counterpart to relenting when a nation turns from evil: Jeremiah states that when a nation turns from its evils God will change His mind about the planned calamity. Jonah records Nineveh's turning from evil and God's decision not to bring the calamity He had announced.

Jeremiah 18:9
And at last I will speak concerning a nation and kingdom, for it to be rebuilt and planted,
  Jeremiah 1:10 — close divine purpose to build and plant nations: The promised rebuilding and planting of a nation or kingdom repeats the constructive part of Jeremiah's commission to nations and kingdoms. The following verse in chapter 18 makes the nation's obedience consequential for that promise.

Jeremiah 18:10
and if they do evil things before Me by not hearing My voice, I will change My mind concerning the good things I spoke of doing for them.
  Ezekiel 33:13 — related reversal through subsequent wrongdoing: Jeremiah says a nation that does evil and refuses God's voice may lose the announced good. Ezekiel likewise says a person who trusts in his righteousness but commits lawlessness will die; the comparison distinguishes the national and individual settings.

Jeremiah 18:11
And now say to Judah’s men and those dwelling in Jerusalem: Behold, I am shaping evils against you and devising a design against you. Let each turn from his evil way now, and make your practices better.
  Jeremiah 7:3-5 — close summons to correct ways and practices: Both addresses to Judah and Jerusalem call for changed ways and improved practices. Chapter 18 sets the summons against announced evils, while the temple address promises continued settlement if the correction is real.

Jeremiah 18:12
And they said: We will be resolute, because we will follow our turnings away, and each will do what pleases his evil heart.
  Jeremiah 7:24 — close refusal expressed as following the evil heart: Jeremiah 18 reports the people's determination to follow their turnings away and what pleases each evil heart. Jeremiah 7 likewise describes refusal to listen and following the thoughts of their evil heart.

Jeremiah 18:13
Therefore thus says the Lord: Ask now among the nations: Who heard such horrifying things that virgin Israel committed so greatly?
  Jeremiah 2:10-12 — close appeal to the nations to expose Israel's extraordinary infidelity: Both challenge hearers to look among the nations for anything like Israel's conduct. Chapter 2 specifies exchanging their glory and heaven's astonishment; chapter 18 calls virgin Israel's actions horrifying.

Jeremiah 18:15
Because My people forgot Me, they burned incense in vain; and they will become weak in their ways, the age-old tracks, to step upon paths having no road for travel,
  Jeremiah 6:16 — related abandonment of the ancient paths: Jeremiah 18 combines forgotten allegiance with weakening in age-old tracks and stepping onto paths unfit for travel. Jeremiah 6 urges seeking the Lord's eternal paths and walking the good way, followed by the people's refusal.

Jeremiah 18:16
to make their land a ruin and an eternal hissing; all who pass through it will be astonished and shake their head.
  1 Kings 9:8-9 — close astonishment and hissing over a devastated place: Jeremiah predicts a ruined land that makes passersby astonished and shake their heads. The warning to Solomon similarly predicts astonishment and hissing at the Lord's house, explaining the calamity by abandonment of God.

Jeremiah 18:17
Like a scorching wind I will scatter them before their enemies; I will show them their day of destruction.
  Ezekiel 5:12 — related divine scattering expressed through wind imagery: Jeremiah compares the people's scattering before enemies to a scorching wind. Ezekiel says God will scatter a portion of Jerusalem's people to every wind; the passages share divine dispersion without making the winds' descriptions identical.

Jeremiah 18:18
And they said: Come, let us devise a design against Jeremiah, because law will not perish from a priest, counsel from the discerning, or word from a prophet. Come, let us strike him with the tongue, and we will hear all his words.
  Psalms 63:3-6 — close conspiracy using the tongue as a weapon: Jeremiah's opponents devise a plan and propose striking him with the tongue. The psalm describes an evildoers' conspiracy whose sharpened tongues are like a sword and who discuss concealed traps; it does not support reversing Jeremiah's final statement that they will hear his words.

Jeremiah 18:19
Listen to me, Lord, and listen to the voice of my claim of right.
  Psalms 16:1-2 — close plea that God hear the speaker's claim of right: Jeremiah asks the Lord to attend to the voice of his claim of right. The psalm similarly asks the Lord to hear righteousness and petition and to issue judgment from His presence.

Jeremiah 18:20
Is evil repaid for good things? For they spoke words together against my soul and hid their punishment for me. Remember my standing before Thee to speak good things for them, to turn Thy fury from them.
  Psalms 34:12-14 — close lament over evil repaid for good: Jeremiah asks whether evil should repay good and recalls interceding for his opponents. The psalm likewise laments repayment of evil for good and describes prayer, fasting and concern for those who troubled the speaker.

Jeremiah 18:21
Therefore give their sons to famine and gather them into the sword’s hands. Let their women become childless and widowed, their men killed by death, and their young men fallen by the sword in war.
  Psalms 108:9-13 — parallel imprecation involving widows and bereaved children: Jeremiah asks that the attackers' families suffer widowhood, childlessness and death. The psalm's imprecation likewise asks widowhood, orphanhood and destruction of children; the parallel concerns the petitions' content, not a general instruction to inflict these harms.

Jeremiah 18:22
Let there be a cry in their houses; Thou wilt bring robbers upon them suddenly, because they attempted a plan for my capture and hid traps against me.
  Psalms 34:7-8 — related judgment upon those concealing traps: Jeremiah asks for a cry in their houses and anticipates sudden robbers upon people who planned his capture and hid traps. The psalm describes an unjustly concealed trap and asks that the hidden snare catch those who set it.

Jeremiah 18:23
And Thou, Lord, knewest all their counsel against me for death. Do not acquit their injustices or erase their sins from before Thee. Let their weakness be before Thee; act among them in the time of Thy fury.
  Psalms 108:14-15 — parallel imprecation that sin remain before the Lord: Jeremiah asks that the opponents' injustices not be acquitted or their sins erased from before God. The psalm likewise asks that family lawlessness and sin remain remembered before the Lord rather than erased.

Jeremiah 19:1
Then the Lord said to me: Go and acquire a molded earthenware jar, and bring some of the elders of the people and of the elders of the priests.
  Ezekiel 4:1-3 — related material signs announcing Jerusalem's judgment: Jeremiah is commanded to obtain an earthenware jar, which the following oracle will use to depict the city's destruction. Ezekiel is commanded to take a brick, draw Jerusalem upon it and enact a siege, explicitly calling this a sign; the objects and enacted actions differ.

Jeremiah 19:2
And go out to the burial ground of the sons of their children, which is before the Charsith Gate, and read there all the words that I speak to you.
  Jeremiah 7:31-32 — related prophetic judgment at the Hinnom burial site: Jeremiah is directed to the burial ground for the proclamation whose later verse names the Son of Hinnom. The earlier oracle likewise names Hinnom and announces that the place associated with burning children will become a burial place of the slain; the Charsith Gate is named only here.

Jeremiah 19:3
And say to them: Hear the Lord’s word, kings of Judah, men of Judah, inhabitants of Jerusalem, and those entering these gates. Thus says the Lord, Israel’s God: Behold, I am bringing evils upon this place, so that both ears of everyone hearing them will ring.
  1 Samuel 3:11 — close announcement that both ears will ring at divine judgment: Both divine announcements say the coming action will make both ears of everyone hearing it ring or resound. The judgments concern different settings: Judah and Jerusalem here, and Eli's house in Samuel's surrounding oracle.
  2 Kings 21:12 — close warning of ear-ringing calamity upon Jerusalem and Judah: The warning in Kings explicitly names Jerusalem and Judah and says both ears of everyone hearing will ring. It supplies a particularly close counterpart to this announcement, beyond the earlier expression in Samuel concerning another household.

Jeremiah 19:4
Because they abandoned Me and alienated this place, burning incense in it to foreign gods whom neither they nor their fathers knew; and Judah’s kings filled this place with innocent blood,
  2 Kings 21:16 — historical counterpart to filling Jerusalem with innocent blood: Jeremiah accuses Judah's kings of filling the place with innocent blood. Kings specifically recounts Manasseh's pouring out innocent blood until Jerusalem was filled from end to end, alongside his leading Judah into sin.

Jeremiah 19:5
and built high places for Baal to burn their sons in fire, things I did not command or speak of or conceive in My heart.
  Jeremiah 7:31 — close rejection of child-burning never commanded by God: Both condemn burning children and expressly deny that God commanded or conceived it in His heart. Chapter 19 names Baal's high places, while chapter 7 identifies Topheth's altar in the Hinnom ravine.

Jeremiah 19:6
Therefore behold, days are coming, says the Lord, and this place will no longer be called Collapse or the Burial Ground of the Son of Hinnom, but the Burial Ground of Slaughter.
  Jeremiah 7:32 — close renaming of Hinnom as a place of slaughter: Both announce coming days when the former name associated with Hinnom will give way to a name identifying the slaughtered. The supplied names differ in wording: Burial Ground of Slaughter here and Ravine of the Slain in chapter 7.

Jeremiah 19:7
And I will slaughter Judah’s counsel and Jerusalem’s counsel in this place, and cast them down by the sword before their enemies and into the hands of those seeking their lives. I will give their dead as food to the birds of the heavens and the beasts of the earth.
  Jeremiah 7:33 — close judgment of corpses becoming food for birds and beasts: Both predict that the people's dead will feed the birds of heaven and beasts of the earth. Chapter 19 places this after military defeat and the collapse of Judah's and Jerusalem's counsel.

Jeremiah 19:8
And I will make this city a ruin and a hissing. Everyone passing through it will look gloomy and hiss over its whole wound.
  Jeremiah 18:16 — close desolation and dismayed passersby: Both portray a devastated place drawing a dismayed response from everyone passing through. Chapter 19 specifies the city and its wound; chapter 18 speaks of the land, astonishment and shaking heads.

Jeremiah 19:9
And they will eat the flesh of their sons and daughters, and each will eat his neighbor’s flesh in the encirclement and siege with which their enemies will besiege them.
  Deuteronomy 28:53-57 — close siege calamity of eating one's children: Jeremiah's prediction that besieged people will eat their sons and daughters directly parallels the covenant warning about consuming children under enemy-imposed distress. Jeremiah additionally names the flesh of one's neighbor.
  Lamentations 4:9-10 — lamented counterpart to children becoming food during calamity: Jeremiah announces children being eaten during siege. Lamentations describes famine and compassionate women boiling their children as food at the people's shattering, providing a retrospective lament alongside the covenant warning.

Jeremiah 19:10
And smash the jar before the eyes of the men going out with you,
  Psalms 2:9 — related breaking of pottery as an image of judgment: Jeremiah is commanded to smash the jar publicly, and the next verse interprets the act as judgment on the people and city. The psalm likewise pictures the crushing of nations as a potter's vessel; it does not depict Jeremiah's enacted sign.

Jeremiah 19:11
and say: Thus says the Lord: This is how I will smash this people and this city, as an earthenware vessel is smashed that cannot be healed again.
  Isaiah 30:14 — close image of irreparable destruction through shattered earthenware: Jeremiah explains that people and city will be smashed like a vessel that cannot be healed again. Isaiah similarly compares judgment to an earthen vessel broken into pieces too small for useful recovery.

Jeremiah 19:13
And Jerusalem’s houses and the houses of Judah’s kings will be like the collapsing place of uncleannesses, in all the houses where they burned incense upon their roofs to all the host of the heavens and poured drink offerings to foreign gods.
  Zephaniah 1:4-5 — close condemnation of rooftop worship of heaven's host: Jeremiah identifies houses where incense was burned on rooftops to heaven's host and drink offerings were poured to foreign gods. Zephaniah's judgment upon Judah and Jerusalem likewise singles out people worshipping heaven's army on rooftops.

Jeremiah 19:14
And Jeremiah came from Collapse, where the Lord had sent him to prophesy, stood in the courtyard of the Lord’s house, and said to all the people:
  Jeremiah 33:2 — close prophetic proclamation in the temple courtyard: Jeremiah stands in the courtyard of the Lord's house to speak to the people after the pottery sign. The oracle in chapter 33 explicitly commands him to stand in that courtyard and deliver the Lord's words to Judeans coming to worship.

Jeremiah 19:15
Thus says the Lord: Behold, I am bringing upon this city, all its towns, and its villages all the evils I spoke against it, because they hardened their necks so as not to listen to My words.
  Jeremiah 7:26 — close hardened-neck refusal to hear God's words: Both connect stubbornly hardened necks with refusal to hear the divine message. Chapter 19 announces the threatened evils upon the city and its dependent settlements as the consequence.

Jeremiah 20:2
And he struck him and put him into the trap, which was at the gate of the separated upper house that was in the Lord’s house.
  Acts 16:22-24 — related beating and physical confinement of proclaimed messengers: Pashhur strikes Jeremiah and places him in a trap after hearing his prophecy. Acts records Paul and Silas beaten and placed in prison with their feet in stocks; the authorities, setting and named restraint differ.

Jeremiah 20:4
Because thus says the Lord: Behold, I am giving you to migration with all your friends. They will fall by their enemies’ sword, and your eyes will see. I will give you and all Judah into the hands of Babylon’s king, and they will deport them and cut them down with swords.
  2 Chronicles 36:17-20 — related warning and account of Judah's defeat and Babylonian captivity: Jeremiah announces death by enemy swords and deportation of Judah into Babylon's king's hands. Chronicles recounts the Chaldean king's killings and deportation of survivors to Babylon; it does not identify the fate of every friend of Pashhur.

Jeremiah 20:5
And I will give all this city’s strength, all its labors, and all the treasures of Judah’s king into his enemies’ hands, and they will bring them to Babylon.
  2 Chronicles 36:18 — close transfer of royal treasures to Babylon: Jeremiah announces that the city's strength, labors and royal treasures will be carried to Babylon. Chronicles records the transfer of temple vessels and treasures of the king and magnates there, supplying a concrete historical counterpart to the treasure clause.

Jeremiah 20:6
And you and all who dwell in your house will go into captivity. In Babylon you will die and there be buried—you and all your friends to whom you prophesied false things.
  Jeremiah 36:21-23 — related Babylonian doom associated with unauthorized prophecy: Pashhur and those to whom he prophesied falsehood are told they will die in Babylon. The later oracle names Ahab and Zedekiah as condemned through Babylon's king and links their fate with an oracle God did not command; the condemned people and manner of death differ.

Jeremiah 20:7
Thou didst deceive me, Lord, and I was deceived. Thou tookest hold and didst prevail. I became a laughingstock; all day I continued being mocked.
  Psalms 68:8-13 — related reproach endured in relation to God: Jeremiah addresses the Lord about being overpowered and becoming a continually mocked laughingstock. The psalm describes reproach endured on God's account and public mockery; it does not reproduce Jeremiah's charge that God deceived him.

Jeremiah 20:8
Because I will laugh with my bitter word; I will call upon rejection and misery, because the Lord’s word became a reproach and a derision to me all day long.
  Psalms 68:10 — related reproach incurred through association with God: Jeremiah says the Lord's word became reproach and derision to him. The psalm says reproaches directed at God fell upon the speaker; this comparison concerns transferred reproach and does not paraphrase Jeremiah's unusual opening as a cry against violence.

Jeremiah 20:9
And I said: I will certainly not name the Lord’s name or speak in His name again. And it became like a burning fire blazing in my bones; I am weakened on every side and cannot bear it.
  Amos 3:8 — related inability to withhold prophecy after divine speech: Jeremiah describes his failed resolve to stop speaking in the Lord's name. Amos asks who will not prophesy when the Lord has spoken, expressing the compelling force of divine speech without Jeremiah's bodily fire image.
  Psalms 38:2-4 — close movement from attempted silence to inward fire and speech: Jeremiah's attempted silence becomes unbearable burning in his bones. The psalm moves from guarding the mouth and remaining silent to a hot heart, kindled fire and speech; its stated concern about avoiding sin differs from Jeremiah's resolve to abandon prophecy.

Jeremiah 20:10
Because I heard the reproach of many gathering all around: Rise against him, and let us rise against him, all you men who are his friends! Watch his purpose, to see whether he will be deceived and we can prevail against him and take our revenge on him.
  Psalms 30:14 — close report of surrounding reproach and conspiracy against the speaker: Both speakers hear the reproach of many around them and describe people gathering or acting together against them. Jeremiah additionally records supposed friends watching for deception so they may take revenge.

Jeremiah 20:11
And the Lord is with me like a strong fighter. Therefore they pursued and could not understand; they were greatly shamed, because they did not understand their dishonors, which will never be forgotten.
  Psalms 34:1-3 — parallel divine combat on behalf of a threatened speaker: Jeremiah declares that the Lord is with him like a strong fighter. The psalm petitions the Lord to make war on the speaker's attackers, take weapons and block his pursuers; one declares confidence while the other asks for such intervention.

Jeremiah 20:12
Lord, testing righteous things, understanding kidneys and hearts: May I see the vengeance from Thee among them, because to Thee I disclosed my defenses.
  Jeremiah 11:20 — close appeal to God's inward knowledge for vindication: Both appeals ask to see God's vengeance upon opponents because the speaker disclosed his defense or claim of right to Him. Jeremiah 20 says God understands kidneys and hearts, while chapter 11 says He tests them; the appeals preserve that verbal distinction within their close correspondence.

Jeremiah 20:13
Sing to the Lord; praise Him, because He rescued a poor person’s life from the hand of those doing evil.
  Psalms 34:10 — close praise for rescue of the poor from hostile hands: Jeremiah commands praise because the Lord rescued a poor person's life from evildoers' hands. The psalm celebrates the Lord's rescue of the poor and needy from stronger people and plunderers.

Jeremiah 20:14
Accursed be the day on which I was born! Let the day my mother bore me not be a day to pray for!
  Job 3:1-4 — close curse upon the day of one's birth: Jeremiah curses the day his mother bore him. Job likewise curses his day and wishes the day of his birth to perish and become dark, expressing a closely related birth lament.

Jeremiah 20:15
Accursed be the man who brought my father good news, saying, A boy has been born to you!—rejoicing.
  Job 3:3 — related reversal of the announcement of a male child's birth: Jeremiah curses the man who joyfully announced a boy's birth to his father. Job curses the night in which a male child was announced; the target of the curse is the time in Job, not the announcing person.

Jeremiah 20:16
Let that man be like the cities the Lord overthrew in fury without changing His mind. Let him hear a cry in the morning and a battle shout at noon,
  Genesis 19:24-25 — related comparison with cities overthrown by the Lord: Jeremiah wishes the birth-announcer to become like cities God overthrew in fury. Genesis supplies the explicit account of the Lord overthrowing Sodom and Gomorrah; Jeremiah's individual verse leaves the cities unnamed.

Jeremiah 20:17
because he did not kill me in my mother’s womb, so that my mother would have become my tomb and the womb one of everlasting conception.
  Job 3:10-16 — close wish that life had ended in the womb: Jeremiah wishes he had been killed in his mother's womb so that it became his tomb. Job asks why he did not die in the womb and compares the wished-for fate to miscarriage or infants who never saw light; Jeremiah's everlasting-conception image is distinctive.

Jeremiah 20:18
Why did I come out from the womb to see labors and pains, and have my days continue in shame?
  Job 3:20-23 — parallel question about life given for suffering: Jeremiah asks why he emerged from the womb to experience labor, pain and shame. Job asks why light and life are given to people in bitterness and pain who long for death.

Jeremiah 21:1
The word that came from the Lord to Jeremiah when King Zedekiah sent to him Pashhur son of Malchiah and Zephaniah son of Maaseiah the priest, saying:
  Jeremiah 44:3 — close account of Zedekiah sending Zephaniah to Jeremiah: Both reports name King Zedekiah and the priest Zephaniah son of Maaseiah in a delegation to Jeremiah. The other messenger differs: Pashhur here and Jehucal in chapter 44.

Jeremiah 21:2
Ask the Lord about us, because Babylon’s king has come against us, whether the Lord will act according to all His wonders, so that he goes away from us.
  Jeremiah 44:7-10 — related royal inquiry about relief from the Babylonian siege: Zedekiah's delegation asks whether divine wonders will make Babylon's king withdraw. The later reply to a king who sent to seek the Lord rules out lasting Chaldean withdrawal and announces the city's burning.

Jeremiah 21:4
Thus says the Lord: Behold, I am turning the weapons of war with which you fight the Chaldeans who have shut you in outside the wall into this city,
  Jeremiah 44:10 — related futility of armed resistance to the announced capture: Jeremiah 21 announces the turning of Judah's weapons into the besieged city. Chapter 44 says even surviving wounded Chaldeans would rise to burn the city; both undermine military confidence without describing identical actions upon the weapons.

Jeremiah 21:5
and I Myself will fight you with outstretched hand and mighty arm, with fury, anger, and great indignation.
  Isaiah 63:10 — close declaration that God Himself fights His disobedient people: Jeremiah says God Himself will fight the city with anger and might. Isaiah recalls the people's disobedience and says God turned hostile and Himself fought them, reversing the preceding account of His saving action.

Jeremiah 21:6
And I will strike all this city’s inhabitants, the people and the livestock, with a great death, and they will die.
  Ezekiel 14:19 — close divine judgment by death upon people and livestock: Jeremiah announces a great death striking the city's human and animal inhabitants. Ezekiel similarly describes God sending death upon a land in wrath to destroy human beings and livestock.

Jeremiah 21:7
And afterward—thus says the Lord—I will give Zedekiah king of Judah, his servants, and the people remaining in this city after death, famine, and sword into the hands of their enemies who seek their lives. They will cut them down with the sword’s edge. I will not spare them or have compassion on them.
  Ezekiel 5:11-12 — related unspared judgment through death, famine and sword: Jeremiah names survivors of death, famine and sword being handed to enemies without divine sparing. Ezekiel's Jerusalem oracle likewise denies sparing and names death, famine and sword, while apportioning its judgments differently.

Jeremiah 21:8
And say to this people: Thus says the Lord: Behold, I have set before your face the way of life and the way of death.
  Deuteronomy 30:15-19 — close setting of life and death before the hearers: Both addresses explicitly set life and death before their audience. Deuteronomy frames the choice through covenant obedience; Jeremiah immediately applies it to remaining in the city or going out to the Chaldeans.

Jeremiah 21:9
Whoever stays in this city will die by sword and famine; whoever goes out to join the Chaldeans who have shut you in will live, and his life will be spoil, and he will live.
  Jeremiah 45:2 — close repeated promise of life through going out to the Chaldeans: Both announcements contrast death by sword and famine for those staying in the city with life for those going out to the Chaldeans. The life described as spoil here is called gain in chapter 45.

Jeremiah 21:10
Because I have fixed My face upon this city for evils and not for good things. It will be handed into Babylon’s king’s hands, and he will burn it down with fire.
  Jeremiah 41:2 — close prediction of capture and burning by Babylon's king: Both announce that the city will be handed over to Babylon's king and burned with fire. Jeremiah 21 expressly states God's intention of evils rather than good for the city.

Jeremiah 21:11
House of Judah’s king, hear the Lord’s word.
  Jeremiah 22:2 — close summons to Judah's royal house to hear: The brief address to the house of Judah's king is expanded in chapter 22's address to the king seated on David's throne, his household and people. Both introduce instructions about royal justice, not merely an unrelated prophetic speech formula.

Jeremiah 21:12
House of David, thus says the Lord: Judge a judgment in the morning, set things straight, and rescue the plundered from the hand of the one wronging him, so that My anger is not kindled like fire and burns with no one to quench it.
  Psalms 71:1-4 — parallel royal duty to judge justly and rescue the vulnerable: The house of David is commanded to set judgment straight and rescue the plundered. The psalm for Solomon asks righteous royal judgment and describes the king saving the children of the needy and humbling the false accuser.

Jeremiah 21:13
Behold, I am against you who dwell in the valley of Sor, the level land, you who say, Who will frighten us? Or who will enter our dwelling?
  Obadiah 1:3-4 — related proud confidence that a dwelling cannot be overcome: Jeremiah confronts inhabitants who ask who could frighten them or enter their dwelling. Obadiah confronts Idumea's confidence in a lofty dwelling with God's promise to bring it down; the source's valley setting is distinct from Obadiah's height imagery.

Jeremiah 21:14
And I will kindle a fire in her woodland, and it will consume everything around her.
  Isaiah 10:16-18 — related divine judgment pictured as consuming woodland fire: Jeremiah announces fire consuming the city's woodland and everything around it. Isaiah likewise describes a divinely sent burning that consumes woodland, forests and more, in its surrounding judgment upon arrogant power.

Jeremiah 22:2
and say: Hear the Lord’s word, king of Judah who sits on David’s throne—you, your household, your people, and those entering these gates.
  Jeremiah 17:20 — close summons to Judah's ruler and gate-entering people: Both call Judah's ruler or rulers and the gate-entering people to hear the Lord's word. Chapter 22 places the royal summons within an instruction on justice, whereas chapter 17 proceeds to Sabbath observance.

Jeremiah 22:3
Thus says the Lord: Do justice and righteousness; rescue the plundered from the hand of the one wronging him. Do not oppress the newcomer, orphan, or widow, and do not act impiously or shed innocent blood in this place.
  Exodus 22:20-23 — close protection of the newcomer, widow and orphan: Jeremiah commands the royal audience not to oppress the newcomer, orphan or widow. Exodus explicitly forbids mistreating the resident foreigner and widow or orphan, warning that God hears their distress.

Jeremiah 22:4
Because if you truly carry out this word, kings sitting upon David’s throne and riding chariots and horses will enter this house’s gates—they, their servants, and their people.
  1 Kings 9:4-5 — parallel conditional continuance of the Davidic throne: Jeremiah promises kings seated on David's throne entering the royal house if the preceding word is carried out. God's address to Solomon likewise conditions the establishment of his royal throne upon faithful conduct and obedience.

Jeremiah 22:5
But if you do not carry out these words, I swear by Myself, says the Lord, that this house will become a desolation.
  1 Kings 9:6-8 — parallel warning that disobedience brings desolation upon a house: Jeremiah warns that refusal of God's words will make the royal house desolate. The warning to Solomon says turning away will lead to the consecrated temple's rejection and public astonishment; the royal house and sanctuary must not be conflated.

Jeremiah 22:6
Because thus says the Lord against the house of Judah’s king: You are Gilead to Me, the head of Lebanon; surely I will make you a wilderness, cities that will not be inhabited.
  Ezekiel 17:3-4 — related Lebanon-and-cedar imagery for Judah's royal establishment: Jeremiah addresses Judah's royal house as the head of Lebanon before announcing devastation. Ezekiel pictures an eagle taking Lebanon's cedar growth, then explicitly interprets the action as Babylon's removal of Jerusalem's king and rulers in verse 12.

Jeremiah 22:7
And I will bring against you a destroying man and his axe, and they will cut down your chosen cedars and cast them into the fire.
  Isaiah 10:33-34 — related downfall of lofty power through Lebanon imagery: Jeremiah announces a destroyer cutting chosen cedars and casting them into fire. Isaiah joins the humbling of lofty figures with Lebanon's fall; it names a sword rather than Jeremiah's axe and does not supply the same burning scene.

Jeremiah 22:8
And nations will pass through this city, and each will say to his neighbor: Why did the Lord act this way toward this great city?
  Deuteronomy 29:23 — close question by nations about the reason for devastation: Jeremiah pictures nations passing through the city and asking why the Lord treated it this way. Deuteronomy similarly pictures all nations asking why the Lord acted against the land with great anger.

Jeremiah 22:9
And they will say: Because they abandoned the covenant of the Lord their God, bowed down to foreign gods, and served them.
  Deuteronomy 29:24-25 — close explanation of calamity through covenant abandonment and foreign worship: Both answer the preceding nations' question by naming abandonment of the Lord's covenant and service and worship of other gods. The correspondence includes both the question-and-answer setting and the specific explanation.

Jeremiah 22:10
Do not weep for the dead or lament him; weep bitterly for the one going out, because he will never return or see his homeland.
  Jeremiah 22:11-12 — same-chapter explanation of mourning for the deported king: The command to weep for the one going out because he will not return is immediately grounded in the oracle about Shallum. The following verses specify his departure, death abroad and permanent loss of his homeland.

Jeremiah 22:11
Because thus says the Lord concerning Shallum son of Josiah, who reigned in place of his father Josiah and went out from this place: He will not return there again,
  1 Chronicles 3:15 — genealogical counterpart for Shallum son of Josiah: Jeremiah names Shallum as Josiah's son. Chronicles lists Salloum among Josiah's sons, providing the corresponding filial identification under the supplied spelling without independently describing his deportation.

Jeremiah 22:12
but in the place where I deported him, there he will die, and he will never see this land again.
  2 Kings 23:34 — related account of a deposed son of Josiah dying abroad: Jeremiah predicts that Shallum will die where he was deported and never see the land again. Kings records the removal to Egypt and death there of Joachas, identified in its context as a son of Josiah. This comparison concerns the royal death abroad without assuming that the supplied names are identical.

Jeremiah 22:13
O you who build your house without righteousness and your upper rooms without judgment! He works through his neighbor without payment and will not give him his wages.
  Leviticus 19:13 — legal counterpart to the condemnation of withheld wages: Jeremiah condemns a builder who makes his neighbor work without payment. Leviticus forbids wronging or robbing the neighbor and withholding a hired worker's wages overnight.
  James 5:4 — close denunciation of laborers' withheld payment: Jeremiah denounces unpaid work used to build the ruler's house. James denounces withheld harvest wages and says the workers' cries reach the Lord; the work differs, but the unjust retention of payment is explicit in both.

Jeremiah 22:14
You built yourself a well-proportioned house, airy upper rooms set apart by windows, paneled with cedar and painted with red ocher.
  Haggai 1:4 — related criticism surrounding elaborately finished private houses: Jeremiah describes airy upper rooms, cedar paneling and red ocher in the ruler's house, immediately after condemning its unjust construction. Haggai questions dwelling in houses with recessed ceilings while God's house lies waste; the criticized priorities and architectural details differ.

Jeremiah 22:15
Will you reign because you are provoked in Ahaz your father? They will not eat or drink. It was better for you to do judgment and good righteousness.
  Proverbs 29:4 — related royal responsibility for righteousness: Jeremiah's concluding judgment says doing judgment and good righteousness was better for the royal addressee. Proverbs says a righteous king raises a country while a lawless man tears it down; this comparison does not resolve Jeremiah's unusual opening about being provoked in Ahaz.

Jeremiah 22:16
They did not know; they did not judge a case for the lowly or a case for the poor. Is this not your failing to know Me? says the Lord.
  Proverbs 29:14 — contrast concerning royal judgment for the poor: Jeremiah condemns failure to judge the cases of the lowly and poor as failure to know God. Proverbs presents the positive counterpart: a king who judges the poor in truth has an established throne.

Jeremiah 22:17
Behold, neither your eyes nor your heart are good; instead they are directed toward your greed, innocent blood to shed it, wrongdoing, and murder to commit it.
  Micah 3:9-11 — close accusation against leaders joining greed with bloodshed: Jeremiah charges the royal addressee with greed, innocent blood and wrongdoing. Micah accuses leaders of distorted justice, building Zion with bloodshed and judging for gifts; both tie governing corruption to violent injustice.

Jeremiah 22:18
Therefore thus says the Lord concerning Jehoiakim son of Josiah, king of Judah: Woe upon this man! They will certainly not mourn him, O brother! Nor will they weep for him, Alas, lord!
  Jeremiah 41:5 — contrast between denied and promised royal mourning: Jehoiakim is denied mourning with the cry Alas, lord. The later oracle promises Zedekiah mourning and the cry O lord, creating an explicit contrast between the announced funerary treatment of two named kings.

Jeremiah 22:19
He will be buried with a donkey’s burial; swept up, he will be thrown beyond Jerusalem’s gate.
  Jeremiah 43:30 — close prediction of Jehoiakim's dishonored discarded corpse: Jeremiah 22 predicts a donkey's burial and the corpse thrown beyond Jerusalem's gate. The later oracle about Jehoiakim likewise says his carcass will be thrown out to the day's heat and night's frost.

Jeremiah 22:20
Go up to Lebanon and cry out; raise your voice in Bashan and shout to the far side of the sea, because all your lovers have been shattered.
  Lamentations 1:2 — related lament over shattered or faithless lovers: Jeremiah's address calls for lament because all the lovers have been shattered.
