Lamentations describes Jerusalem without comfort among her lovers and with faithless friends turned enemies; it does not say those lovers themselves were shattered.

Jeremiah 22:21
I spoke to you in your falling away, and you said, I will not listen. This is your way from your youth: you did not hear My voice.
  Jeremiah 7:23-26 — close longstanding refusal to hear God's voice: Jeremiah 22 describes refusal to listen as the addressee's way from youth. Chapter 7 recalls the command to hear God's voice and a continuing refusal across generations, without making the two chronological descriptions identical.

Jeremiah 22:22
Wind will shepherd all your shepherds, and your lovers will go out in captivity. Then you will be ashamed and dishonored because of all who love you.
  Jeremiah 10:21 — related judgment upon failed shepherds and their people: Jeremiah 22 announces that wind will shepherd the shepherds and that the lovers will be captive. Chapter 10 associates foolish shepherds who did not seek the Lord with scattering; it does not contain the same wind-as-shepherd figure.

Jeremiah 22:23
You who dwell in Lebanon and nest among the cedars will groan when pangs come upon you like those of a woman giving birth.
  Micah 4:9-10 — close birth-pang imagery for impending calamity: Jeremiah compares the coming pangs of the cedar-nesting addressee to a woman giving birth. Micah addresses Zion with the same birth-pang image and expressly connects it with leaving the city for Babylon.

Jeremiah 22:24
As I live, says the Lord, even if Jeconiah son of Jehoiakim, king of Judah, becomes a signet on My right hand, I will pull you off from there,
  Haggai 2:23 — contrast between removal and choice under the signet image: God says He would remove Jeconiah even if he were a signet on His right hand. Haggai promises to make Zerubbabel like a signet because God has chosen him; the named recipients and directions of the image are opposite.

Jeremiah 22:25
and hand you into the hands of those seeking your life, those before whom you are afraid, into the hands of the Chaldeans.
  Jeremiah 24:1 — close threatened handover and recorded deportation of the same named king: The oracle identifies Jeconiah son of Jehoiakim and announces his delivery into feared Chaldean hands. The later vision is dated after Nebuchadnezzar deported the same explicitly named Jeconiah son of Jehoiakim to Babylon, giving the royal handover a specific narrative counterpart.

Jeremiah 22:26
And I will cast you and your mother who bore you into a land where you were not born, and there you both will die.
  Jeremiah 22:12 — same-chapter parallel deaths abroad for differently named exiled kings: The oracle predicts that Jeconiah and his mother will die in the foreign land to which God casts them. The earlier oracle about Shallum likewise predicts death in the place of deportation. The kings are distinct, and the mother's fate is an additional feature of the Jeconiah oracle.

Jeremiah 22:27
But to the land for which they pray in their souls, they will certainly not return.
  Jeremiah 22:11-12 — same-chapter parallel denial of return to an exiled king: The denial of return in the Jeconiah oracle parallels the earlier denial that Shallum will ever return or see the land again. These are separate royal oracles within the chapter, not an assumption that the kings are the same person.

Jeremiah 22:28
Jeconiah was dishonored like a vessel for which there is no use, because he was cast out and thrown into a land he did not know.
  Hosea 8:8 — close dishonor among foreign lands pictured as a useless vessel: Jeremiah compares Jeconiah, cast into an unfamiliar land, to a vessel with no use. Hosea compares Israel among the nations to a useless vessel, applying the same dishonoring image to the people rather than one king.

Jeremiah 22:29
Land, land, hear the Lord’s word:
  Deuteronomy 32:1 — related direct summons to the earth to hear: Jeremiah addresses the land twice and commands it to hear the Lord's word. Moses' song likewise addresses the earth as a hearer of his words, together with heaven; the scope and repeated wording differ.

Jeremiah 22:30
Record this man as an outcast, because no man from his offspring will grow great sitting on David’s throne and ruling again in Judah.
  Matthew 1:11-12 — genealogical context for Jeconiah's offspring under the royal restriction: Jeremiah bars the named king's offspring from prospering on David's throne and ruling again in Judah. Matthew names Jechoniah at the Babylonian deportation and lists his offspring afterward; the genealogy does not itself say that those descendants resumed rule in Judah or resolve every implication of the oracle.

Jeremiah 23:1
O shepherds who scatter and destroy the sheep of My pasture!
  Ezekiel 34:2-6 — close condemnation of shepherds whose sheep are scattered: Jeremiah addresses shepherds who scatter and destroy God's sheep. Ezekiel accuses Israel's shepherds of failing to feed, heal and seek the sheep, then describes their scattering and exposure to beasts.

Jeremiah 23:2
Therefore thus says the Lord to those shepherding My people: You scattered My sheep and drove them out without visiting them. Behold, I am exacting justice from you according to your evil practices.
  Ezekiel 34:10 — close divine judgment against negligent shepherds: Jeremiah says God will exact justice from shepherds who drove His sheep out and did not visit them. Ezekiel likewise declares God against the shepherds, requiring His sheep from their hands and removing their charge.

Jeremiah 23:3
And I will receive the remnant of My people from all the land where I drove them out, and establish them in their pasture; they will increase and multiply.
  Ezekiel 34:13-14 — close gathering of scattered people into pasture: Jeremiah promises to receive the remnant from the lands of dispersion and establish them in pasture. Ezekiel likewise promises gathering from countries into Israel's land and good pasture, using the same restored-flock imagery.

Jeremiah 23:4
And I will raise shepherds for them who will tend them, and they will no longer fear or be frightened, says the Lord.
  Jeremiah 3:15 — close promise of newly appointed faithful shepherds: God promises shepherds who will tend the restored people so they no longer fear. The earlier restoration oracle promises shepherds according to God's heart who shepherd with knowledge; its emphasis is their character and understanding.

Jeremiah 23:5
Behold, days are coming, says the Lord, and I will raise up a righteous rising for David. A king will reign and understand, doing judgment and righteousness upon the earth.
  Isaiah 9:6 — close Davidic rule characterized by righteousness and judgment: Jeremiah promises a righteous rising for David and a discerning king doing judgment and righteousness. Isaiah describes rule upon David's throne established and upheld in righteousness and judgment, with enduring peace.
  Zechariah 6:12-13 — related Rising imagery associated with royal rule: Jeremiah announces a righteous rising for David who reigns as king. Zechariah names a man Rising who builds the Lord's house and rules on his throne; the target adds temple building and a priest at his right hand, rather than simply repeating Jeremiah's oracle.

Jeremiah 23:6
In his days Judah will be saved and Israel will dwell confidently. This is his name, which the Lord will call him: Josedek.
  Ezekiel 34:28 — related restoration marked by safety for Israel: Jeremiah promises Judah's salvation and Israel's confident dwelling during the announced reign. Ezekiel promises that the restored flock will no longer be plunder and will dwell in hope without anyone terrifying them; it does not explain or replace the name Josedek.

Jeremiah 23:7
Therefore behold, days are coming, says the Lord, and they will no longer say, As the Lord lives, who brought Israel’s house up from Egypt’s land,
  Jeremiah 16:14 — close repeated replacement of the exodus oath formula: Both announce coming days when people will no longer invoke the Lord's life by identifying Him as the one who brought Israel out of Egypt. Their following verses replace that wording with the gathering from dispersion.

Jeremiah 23:8
but, As the Lord lives, who gathered all Israel’s offspring from the northern land and all the countries where He drove them out, and restored them to their land.
  Jeremiah 16:15 — close repeated oath celebrating return from the north and other lands: Both describe the new invocation of the living Lord through His gathering Israel from the northern land and all the regions of dispersion and restoring them to their land.

Jeremiah 23:9
Among the prophets my heart was shattered; within me all my bones were shaken. I became like a shattered man, like a person gripped by wine, before the Lord and the splendor of His glory.
  Habakkuk 3:16 — related bodily trembling in a prophetic response to divine majesty: Jeremiah describes a shattered heart and shaken bones before the Lord and His glory. Habakkuk's prayer describes terror and trembling entering the bones; it does not reproduce Jeremiah's comparison to a person gripped by wine.

Jeremiah 23:10
Because of these things the land mourned and the wilderness pastures dried up. Their course became evil, and their strength was not so.
  Jeremiah 12:4 — close mourning land and dried vegetation associated with human evil: Jeremiah 23 joins the land's mourning and dried pastures with an evil course. Jeremiah 12 explicitly asks how long the earth and field's grass will suffer because of the inhabitants' evil.

Jeremiah 23:11
Because priest and prophet were defiled, and in My house I saw their evils.
  Micah 3:11 — related corruption of both priestly and prophetic offices: Jeremiah identifies priest and prophet as defiled and says God saw their evils in His house. Micah accuses priests of answering for wages and prophets of divining for silver; its specified misconduct does not exhaust Jeremiah's broader accusation.

Jeremiah 23:12
Therefore let their way become a slippery place for them in darkness, and they will be tripped and fall in it, because I will bring evils upon them in their year of visitation, says the Lord.
  Psalms 34:6 — close judgment pictured as a dark and slippery way: Jeremiah announces a slippery place in darkness where the condemned will trip and fall. The psalm similarly asks that the enemies' way become darkness and a slippery path, adding pursuit by the Lord's angel.

Jeremiah 23:13
And among Samaria’s prophets I saw lawless acts: they prophesied through Baal and led My people Israel astray.
  1 Kings 18:21-22 — historical counterpart to prophets serving Baal in Israel: Jeremiah condemns Samaria's prophets for prophesying through Baal and leading Israel astray. Elijah's confrontation explicitly contrasts following the Lord with following Baal and names Baal's many prophets; it does not identify every prophet in Jeremiah's accusation individually.

Jeremiah 23:14
And among Jerusalem’s prophets I have seen horrifying things: committing adultery, walking in lies, and supporting evil hands so that no one turns from his evil way. They all became like Sodom to Me, and her inhabitants like Gomorrah.
  Ezekiel 13:22 — close accusation of strengthening evildoers against repentance: Jeremiah says Jerusalem's prophets support evil hands so no one turns from his evil way. Ezekiel likewise accuses the prophetic women of strengthening the lawless person's hands so he does not turn from evil and live.
  Isaiah 1:9-10 — related application of Sodom and Gomorrah to Judah's corrupt people: Jeremiah compares the prophets and Jerusalem's inhabitants to Sodom and Gomorrah. Isaiah's address against Judah and Jerusalem likewise invokes those cities and directly addresses rulers of Sodom and people of Gomorrah; the immediate groups and surrounding accusations differ.

Jeremiah 23:15
Therefore thus says the Lord: Behold, I will feed them pain and give them bitter water to drink, because from Jerusalem’s prophets defilement went out into all the land.
  Jeremiah 9:14 — close judgment pictured as painful food and bitter drink: Jeremiah 23 threatens the prophets with pain to eat and bitter water to drink. The earlier oracle threatens the law-abandoning people with distresses as food and gall-water as drink, repeating the paired punitive nourishment image.

Jeremiah 23:16
Thus says the Lord Almighty: Do not listen to the prophets’ words, because they make a vision futile for themselves. They speak from their own heart and not from the Lord’s mouth.
  Ezekiel 13:2-3 — close rejection of prophecy originating in the speaker's own heart: Jeremiah warns against prophetic words from the prophets' own hearts rather than the Lord's mouth. Ezekiel likewise condemns prophets speaking from their own heart while seeing nothing at all.

Jeremiah 23:17
They say to those rejecting the Lord’s word, You will have peace. To all who walk according to their own wishes, to everyone walking in the wandering of his heart, they said, No evils will come upon you.
  Ezekiel 13:10 — close deceptive assurance of peace: Jeremiah condemns prophets promising peace and freedom from evils to those rejecting the Lord and following their own wishes. Ezekiel similarly says prophets misled the people by declaring peace where there was no peace.

Jeremiah 23:18
Because who stood in the Lord’s standing place and saw His word? Who gave ear and heard?
  1 Kings 22:19-22 — related access to the Lord's heavenly deliberation: Jeremiah asks who has stood in the Lord's standing place and seen or heard His word. Micaiah reports a vision of the Lord enthroned with heaven's army around Him and recounts the deliberation he heard; the narrative supplies an instance of claimed access without being quoted here verbatim.

Jeremiah 23:19
Behold, a shaking comes from the Lord, and anger goes out into a convulsion; swirling, it will come upon the ungodly.
  Jeremiah 37:23 — close repeated announcement of swirling wrath upon the ungodly: Both announce anger proceeding from the Lord, describe its swirling movement and identify the ungodly as its destination. Chapter 23 also uses shaking and convulsion language.

Jeremiah 23:20
And the Lord’s fury will no longer turn back until He does it and raises it up from His heart’s undertaking. At the end of the days they will understand these things.
  Jeremiah 37:24 — close repeated completion of the Lord's heart's undertaking: Both say the Lord's fury will not turn back until He acts on His heart's undertaking, followed by understanding at the end of the days. The supplied pronoun changes from they in chapter 23 to you in chapter 37.

Jeremiah 23:21
I was not sending the prophets, yet they ran; I did not speak to them, yet they prophesied.
  Ezekiel 13:6 — close denial that the prophets were sent by God: Jeremiah says the prophets ran though God did not send them and prophesied though He did not speak. Ezekiel similarly condemns those saying The Lord says although the Lord has not sent them.

Jeremiah 23:22
And if they had stood in My standing presence and listened to My words, they would have turned My people from their evil practices.
  Malachi 2:6 — related faithful ministry turning people from injustice: Jeremiah says prophets who truly stood before God and heard Him would have turned the people from evil practices. Malachi presents truthful priestly speech and the turning of many from injustice as the positive counterpart.

Jeremiah 23:23
I am a God drawing near, says the Lord, and not a God far away.
  Acts 17:27-28 — close affirmation that God is not far away: Jeremiah declares God drawing near and not far away. Paul's speech likewise says God is not far from each person and that in Him people live, move and exist; it does not turn Jeremiah's declaration into the question found in some other renderings.

Jeremiah 23:24
If a person hides in hidden places, will I not see him? Do I not fill the heaven and the earth? says the Lord.
  Psalms 138:7-12 — related impossibility of escaping God's presence through place or darkness: Jeremiah asks whether a hidden person could escape God's sight and declares His filling heaven and earth. The psalm traces heaven, Hades, the far sea and darkness as places or conditions that cannot remove the speaker from God's presence.
  Hebrews 4:13 — close affirmation that nothing is hidden from divine sight: Jeremiah denies that someone hiding in concealed places can remain unseen by God. Hebrews says no creature is hidden and everything lies exposed to His eyes, directly matching the visibility clause rather than merely the spatial imagery.

Jeremiah 23:25
I heard what the prophets speak, the false things they prophesy in My name, saying, I dreamed a dream.
  Jeremiah 36:8-9 — close warning about dreams and false prophecy in God's name: Jeremiah reports prophets claiming dreams while speaking falsehood in His name. The later address to deportees warns against false prophets and dreams because the prophets speak unjust things in God's name without being sent.

Jeremiah 23:26
How long will it be in the heart of the prophets prophesying false things, and in their prophesying the wishes of their own heart?
  Ezekiel 13:17 — close prophetic claims proceeding from one's own heart: Jeremiah identifies the wishes of the prophets' own hearts as the source of their false prophesying. Ezekiel directs an oracle against women prophesying from their own heart, specifying a group the source does not separately name.

Jeremiah 23:27
They plan to forget My law through their dreams, which each told his neighbor, just as their fathers forgot My name through Baal.
  Deuteronomy 13:2-5 — related dreams threatening fidelity to God's commandments: Jeremiah accuses dream-telling prophets of planning forgetfulness of God's law, likening it to the fathers' forgetfulness through Baal. Deuteronomy warns against listening to a dreamer who urges service of other gods and instead commands adherence to the Lord's voice.

Jeremiah 23:28
Let the prophet in whom there is a dream tell his dream, and the one to whom My word comes tell My word in truth. What has chaff to do with wheat? So are My words, says the Lord.
  Matthew 3:12 — related distinction between wheat and chaff with different referents: Jeremiah distinguishes the truthful divine word from dreams through the question of chaff and wheat. John's announcement pictures wheat gathered and chaff burned in coming judgment; the shared separation image does not identify Jeremiah's kinds of speech with the target's judged people.

Jeremiah 23:29
Are not My words like blazing fire, says the Lord, and like an axe cutting rock?
  Jeremiah 5:14 — close comparison of God's words to consuming fire: Jeremiah 23 calls God's words blazing fire. The earlier oracle makes His words fire in the prophet's mouth and the people wood to be consumed, supplying an explicit application of the same image.
  Hebrews 4:12 — related force of God's word pictured through cutting: Jeremiah pairs blazing fire with an axe cutting rock. Hebrews describes God's word as sharper than a sword and penetrating inward divisions; the instruments and objects differ, and Jeremiah's axe is not replaced with a hammer.

Jeremiah 23:32
Behold, I am against the prophets who prophesy false dreams, who told them and led My people astray by their lies and wanderings. I did not send or command them, and they will bring this people no benefit at all.
  Jeremiah 14:14 — close condemnation of false prophecy without divine commission: Both deny that God sent or commanded prophets who claim to speak for Him. Chapter 23 stresses false dreams leading the people astray, while chapter 14 lists false visions, divinations, omens and choices of the heart.

Jeremiah 23:34
And the prophet, priest, or people who say, The Lord’s burden—I will exact justice from that person and his household.
  Matthew 12:36-37 — related accountability for spoken words: Jeremiah announces judgment on a person and household for using the forbidden Lord's burden expression. Jesus warns of accounting for idle words and condemnation by one's words; the target broadens the principle and does not prescribe Jeremiah's specific phrase prohibition.

Jeremiah 23:35
For this is what you will say, each to his neighbor and his brother: What did the Lord answer? What did the Lord speak?
  1 Samuel 3:17-18 — related request for a truthful report of the divine word: Jeremiah prescribes asking what the Lord answered and spoke. Eli likewise asks Samuel what word was spoken to him and demands that none be concealed, followed by Samuel's complete report; the narrative does not contain the burden controversy.

Jeremiah 23:36
And do not name the Lord’s burden again, because a person’s own word will be his burden.
  Matthew 12:37 — related one's own words becoming the basis of adverse judgment: Jeremiah says a person's own word will become his burden after forbidding the disputed expression. Jesus likewise says people may be condemned by their own words; the comparison concerns responsibility for speech, not an identical use of the burden metaphor.

Jeremiah 23:39
therefore behold, I seize you and dash you down, along with the city I gave to you and your fathers,
  Jeremiah 7:14-15 — related rejection of people together with their inherited place: Jeremiah announces casting down the people along with the city God gave them and their fathers. The temple oracle likewise threatens the place given to them and their fathers and the people's removal from God's presence, denying that inherited location guarantees safety.

Jeremiah 23:40
and I will put eternal reproach and eternal dishonor upon you, which will not be forgotten.
  Jeremiah 20:11 — close enduring disgrace that will not be forgotten: Both describe dishonor whose memory will not disappear. Chapter 20 applies this to the prophet's pursuers, while chapter 23 announces it upon those implicated in the burden controversy.

Jeremiah 24:1
The Lord showed me two baskets of figs lying before the Lord’s sanctuary, after Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon had deported Jeconiah son of Jehoiakim, king of Judah, the rulers, craftsmen, prisoners, and rich people from Jerusalem and brought them to Babylon.
  Jeremiah 36:1-2 — close shared setting after Jeconiah and craftsmen left Jerusalem for exile: The fig vision is dated after Jeconiah and other groups, including craftsmen and prisoners, were deported from Jerusalem to Babylon. Jeremiah's letter to the Babylonian deportees is likewise situated after the departure from Jerusalem of the explicitly named King Jeconiah and groups including prisoners and craftsmen. The passages introduce different prophetic messages within this shared named setting.

Jeremiah 24:2
One basket had very good figs, like early figs; the other basket had very bad figs that could not be eaten because of their badness.
  Matthew 7:17-20 — related good and bad fruit imagery distinguishing human groups: Jeremiah's good and inedible figs become signs for the groups interpreted in the following oracle. Jesus contrasts good and evil fruit in a warning about recognizing false prophets; the signs serve different arguments and the target does not describe the fig baskets.

Jeremiah 24:3
And the Lord said to me: What do you see, Jeremiah? I said: Figs. The good are exceedingly good, and the bad exceedingly bad; they cannot be eaten because of their badness.
  Jeremiah 1:11-12 — close visionary question asking Jeremiah what he sees: Both visions contain God's direct question What do you see, Jeremiah and the prophet's report of a visible object before its meaning is explained. The earlier object is a nut-wood staff, not the figs of this vision.

Jeremiah 24:5
Thus says the Lord, Israel’s God: Like these good figs, so I will acknowledge Judah’s deportees, whom I have sent from this place to the Chaldeans’ land, for good things.
  Ezekiel 11:15-17 — related divine favor toward people already dispersed from Jerusalem: Jeremiah identifies the deportees as the good figs whom God will acknowledge for good. Ezekiel counters Jerusalem's inhabitants' claim against the dispersed and promises to gather the scattered back to Israel's land; the fig symbolism is distinctive to Jeremiah.

Jeremiah 24:6
And I will fix My eyes upon them for good things and restore them to this land for good things. I will rebuild them and certainly not tear them down, plant them and certainly not pluck them out.
  Jeremiah 38:28 — close reversal from tearing down to building and planting: Jeremiah promises to rebuild and plant the returned deportees rather than tear down or pluck them out. The later oracle similarly contrasts God's prior watching to tear down and harm with His watching to build and plant.

Jeremiah 24:7
And I will give them a heart to know Me, that I am the Lord. They will be My people and I will be their God, because they will return to Me from their whole heart.
  Ezekiel 36:26-28 — close God-given heart and restored covenant relationship: Jeremiah promises a heart to know the Lord, return from the whole heart and the bond of My people and their God. Ezekiel promises a new heart and spirit enabling obedience and repeats the same people-and-God relationship.

Jeremiah 24:8
And like the bad figs that cannot be eaten because of their badness—thus says the Lord—so I will hand over Zedekiah king of Judah, his nobles, Jerusalem’s remnant left in this land, and those dwelling in Egypt.
  Jeremiah 21:7 — close handing over of Zedekiah and Jerusalem's remaining people: Jeremiah's bad-fig interpretation names Zedekiah, his nobles and the remnant in Jerusalem for handing over. The earlier siege oracle likewise names Zedekiah and the city's survivors being delivered to enemies; the bad-fig oracle additionally includes people dwelling in Egypt.

Jeremiah 24:9
And I will give them to scattering into all the earth’s kingdoms, and they will become a reproach, a parable, an object of hatred, and a curse in every place where I drove them out.
  Deuteronomy 28:36-37 — close dispersion accompanied by proverbial disgrace among nations: Jeremiah announces scattering into kingdoms and becoming a reproach, parable and curse wherever driven. Deuteronomy likewise predicts removal with rulers to an unknown nation and becoming a riddle, parable and tale among the nations.

Jeremiah 24:10
And I will send famine, death, and sword against them until they disappear from the land I gave them.
  Ezekiel 14:21 — close divine judgments of famine, death and sword: Jeremiah names famine, death and sword against the condemned group until it disappears from the land. Ezekiel names those same three among four judgments against Jerusalem, adding harmful beasts and explicitly mentioning both people and livestock.

Jeremiah 25:1
The word that came to Jeremiah concerning all Judah’s people in the fourth year of Jehoiakim son of Josiah, king of Judah,
  Jeremiah 43:1 — parallel dating to Jehoiakim's fourth year: Both introductions date a divine word to the fourth year of Jehoiakim son of Josiah, king of Judah. The matching royal name, parentage and regnal year connect the settings without making the two speeches identical.

Jeremiah 25:3
In the thirteenth year of Josiah son of Amos, king of Judah, and until this day, twenty-three years, I spoke to you, rising early and saying,
  Jeremiah 1:2 — repeated dating of Jeremiah's prophetic beginning: The twenty-three-year retrospective begins in Josiah's thirteenth year. The book's opening likewise dates God's word to Jeremiah to the thirteenth year of Josiah son of Amos, confirming the stated starting point.

Jeremiah 25:4
and I was sending My servants the prophets to you, sending from early morning, and you did not listen or attend with your ears,
  2 Chronicles 36:15-16 — parallel rejection of repeatedly sent prophets: Both describe God rising early and sending prophetic messengers to His people. Jeremiah stresses failure to listen, while Chronicles specifies their mockery and rejection of the messengers and words.

Jeremiah 25:5
saying: Turn, each from his evil way and from your evil practices, and you will dwell upon the land I gave to you and your fathers from age to age.
  Jeremiah 7:3-7 — parallel repentance linked with continued dwelling in the given land: Both call for corrected ways and practices and connect that response with dwelling in the land given to the fathers. Jeremiah 7 expands the required conduct into justice, protection of vulnerable people and rejection of foreign gods.

Jeremiah 25:6
Do not follow foreign gods to serve them and bow down to them, so that you do not provoke Me by your hands’ works into harming you.
  Deuteronomy 6:14-15 — parallel prohibition of foreign gods with a warning of divine anger: Jeremiah forbids following, serving and bowing to foreign gods, lest the people's works provoke harm. Deuteronomy likewise forbids following other gods and warns that the Lord's anger may bring destruction.

Jeremiah 25:8
Therefore thus says the Lord: Since you did not believe My words,
  Hebrews 3:16-19 — parallel unbelief leading to divine judgment: Jeremiah identifies refusal to believe God's words as the reason for the judgment announced in the following unit. Hebrews similarly explains the wilderness generation's exclusion from rest through unbelief, while describing a different generation and penalty.

Jeremiah 25:9
behold, I am sending and will take the clan from the north and bring them against this land, its inhabitants, and all the surrounding nations. I will lay them waste and make them a ruin, a hissing, and eternal reproach.
  Jeremiah 6:22-23 — parallel northern invasion as judgment: Jeremiah 25 announces a clan brought from the north against the land and surrounding nations. Jeremiah 6 likewise announces a northern people and nations arrayed for war against Zion, supplying a particular northern-invasion counterpart.

Jeremiah 25:10
And I will remove from them the sound of joy and delight, the sound of bridegroom and bride, the fragrance of ointment and the light of a lamp.
  Jeremiah 7:34 — repeated removal of gladness and wedding voices: Both judgments remove sounds of joy and the voices of bridegroom and bride from the land. Jeremiah 25 additionally names ointment's fragrance and a lamp's light; these details are not supplied by the target.

Jeremiah 25:11
And all the land will become a ruin, and they will serve among the nations for seventy years.
  Daniel 9:2 — explicit later consultation of Jeremiah's seventy-year period: Jeremiah announces seventy years of desolation and service among nations. Daniel explicitly considers books concerning Jeremiah and a seventy-year term for Jerusalem's reproach, connecting his calculation with Jeremiah's prophetic period.
  2 Chronicles 36:20-21 — historical account connecting deportation and seventy years with Jeremiah: Chronicles describes deported survivors serving Babylon's rulers and identifies seventy years of the land's desolation with the Lord's word through Jeremiah. It adds the land's Sabbath observance to the service and desolation announced here.

Jeremiah 25:12
And when the seventy years are fulfilled, I will exact justice from that nation, says the Lord, and make them an everlasting ruin.
  Jeremiah 36:10 — complementary outcomes associated with Babylon's seventy years: Jeremiah 25 announces judgment upon the nation when seventy years are fulfilled. Jeremiah 36 promises a visit and return for the exiles when Babylon's seventy years are about to be fulfilled, describing the people's restoration alongside the judgment announcement.

Jeremiah 25:13; 32:13
And I will bring upon that land all My words that I spoke against it, everything written in this book. What Jeremiah prophesied against all the nations.
  Jeremiah 43:2 — parallel recording of Jeremiah's words concerning nations: The composite unit announces execution of the words written in the book and identifies Jeremiah's prophecies against the nations. Jeremiah 43 commands a writing roll containing the delivered oracles concerning Jerusalem, Judah and all nations.

Jeremiah 25:15
Thus says the Lord: Let Elam’s bow be shattered, the beginning of their dominion.
  Hosea 1:4-5 — parallel breaking of a nation's bow: Elam's bow, associated with its dominion, is to be shattered. Hosea likewise announces the shattering of Israel's bow in Jezreel alongside an end to its kingdom; the nations and locations remain distinct.

Jeremiah 25:16
I will bring upon Elam four winds from heaven’s four ends and scatter them among all these winds. There will be no nation where Elam’s outcasts do not come.
  Ezekiel 5:10-12 — parallel scattering of survivors to every wind: Jeremiah threatens Elam with four winds from heaven's ends and dispersal among them. Ezekiel likewise announces scattering Jerusalem's survivors to every wind, with sword judgment accompanying the dispersal.

Jeremiah 25:17
I will terrify them before their enemies who seek their lives and bring calamities upon them according to the anger of My wrath. I will send My sword after them until they are consumed.
  Jeremiah 9:15 — repeated sword pursuit until consumption: After Elam's scattering, Jeremiah 25 threatens a sword sent after them until they are consumed. Jeremiah 9 uses the same combination of scattering among nations and a sword sent until consumption, there addressing the people who abandoned God's law.

Jeremiah 25:18
I will set My throne in Elam and send king and nobles away from there.
  Psalms 46:9 — related divine throne and rule over nations: God promises to set His throne in Elam while removing its king and nobles. The psalm likewise joins God's rule over nations with His sitting upon a holy throne, clarifying the international sovereignty expressed by the image.

Jeremiah 25:19
In the last days I will turn back Elam’s captivity, says the Lord.
  Ezekiel 29:13-15 — parallel restoration of a foreign nation's captivity after judgment: Jeremiah promises to turn back Elam's captivity in the last days. Ezekiel similarly promises to gather scattered Egyptians and turn back their captivity, but gives a forty-year period and a lowly restored dominion rather than Elam's formulation.

Jeremiah 26:2
For Egypt, concerning the force of Pharaoh Neco king of Egypt, who was beside the Euphrates River at Carchemish and whom Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon struck in the fourth year of Jehoiakim king of Judah:
  2 Kings 24:7 — related account of Babylon's seizure of Egyptian territory: Jeremiah names Babylon's defeat of Pharaoh Neco's force at Carchemish beside the Euphrates. Kings reports that Babylon took Egypt's territory up to the Euphrates, supplying a related geopolitical account without naming this battle or Neco.

Jeremiah 26:3
Take up weapons and shields, and advance to war!
  Ezekiel 38:4-7 — parallel military preparation with weapons and shields: Jeremiah commands taking weapons and shields and advancing to war. Ezekiel describes Gog's assembled force with shields and swords and commands its preparation; the shared equipment and mobilization do not identify the two armies.

Jeremiah 26:4
Saddle the horses; mount, riders! Take your positions in your helmets. Hold your spears forward; put on your breastplates!
  Ezekiel 38:4 — parallel equipment of mounted forces: Jeremiah's preparation names horses, riders, helmets and breastplates. Ezekiel's assembled force likewise includes horses and horsemen clothed in breastplates, with helmets among its equipment.

Jeremiah 26:5
Why are they terrified and withdrawing backward? Because their strong men will be cut down. They fled in flight and did not turn back, surrounded on every side, says the Lord.
  Isaiah 19:16-17 — parallel fear in judgment upon Egypt: Jeremiah asks why Egypt's fighters are terrified and retreating as their strong men are cut down. Isaiah likewise portrays Egyptians trembling before the Lord's hand and fearing when the Judeans' country is named.

Jeremiah 26:6
Let the swift not flee, nor the strong man escape. Northward, in the places beside the Euphrates, they grew weak and have fallen.
  Amos 2:14-15 — parallel failure of speed and strength to secure escape: Jeremiah denies escape to the swift and strong in the defeat beside the Euphrates. Amos similarly says flight will fail the runner, strength will fail the mighty, and the swift will not be saved.

Jeremiah 26:7
Who is this who will rise like a river, and like rivers churn water?
  Jeremiah 26:8 — same-chapter question and answer identifying the rising river: The question asks who rises like a river and churns water. The following unit explicitly answers with Egypt's rising waters and its boast of covering the land, giving the particular identification of the image.

Jeremiah 26:8
Egypt’s waters will rise like a river, and it said, I will rise and cover the land and destroy those living in it.
  Isaiah 8:7-8 — parallel imperial advance pictured as rising river water: Egypt boasts of rising like a river and covering the land. Isaiah identifies strong river water with Assyria's king and describes his encampment filling the country's breadth; the invading powers differ while the military water image corresponds.

Jeremiah 26:9
Mount the horses; prepare the chariots! Go out, warriors of the Ethiopians and Libyans equipped with weapons! And Lydians, go up and draw the bow!
  Ezekiel 38:5 — parallel Ethiopian and Libyan military contingents: Jeremiah summons Ethiopian and Libyan warriors equipped with weapons, alongside Lydian bowmen. Ezekiel likewise names Ethiopians and Libyans among an armed force, with helmets and shields, without repeating the Lydian contingent.

Jeremiah 26:10
That day belongs to the Lord our God, a day of vengeance, to take vengeance on His enemies. The Lord’s sword will devour, be filled, and become drunk with their blood, for the Lord Sabaoth has a sacrifice from the northern land beside the Euphrates River.
  Isaiah 34:5-8 — parallel divine sword, blood and sacrificial judgment: Jeremiah describes the Lord's day of vengeance, a sword filled and drunk with blood, and His sacrifice beside the Euphrates. Isaiah similarly joins the Lord's blood-filled sword, sacrifice in Idumea and a day of judgment and repayment.

Jeremiah 26:11
Go up, Gilead, and take resin for the virgin daughter Egypt. In vain you multiplied your remedies; there is no benefit for you.
  Jeremiah 8:22 — parallel Gilead resin and failed national healing: Jeremiah calls on Gilead to take resin for Egypt, whose multiplied remedies are futile. Jeremiah 8 likewise names Gilead's resin and asks why healing has not risen for the people's daughter, connecting medicinal imagery with an afflicted community.

Jeremiah 26:12
Nations heard your voice, and the land was filled with your cry, for warrior grew weak against warrior; both fell together.
  Isaiah 31:3 — related collective collapse associated with Egyptian strength: Jeremiah describes warrior weakening against warrior and both falling together. Isaiah calls Egyptian help human and fleshly, then says the helpers grow weary and all perish together; both passages depict collective failure of relied-upon strength.

Jeremiah 26:13
What the Lord spoke through Jeremiah concerning Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon coming to cut down Egypt’s land:
  Ezekiel 30:10-11 — parallel announcement of Nebuchadnezzar's attack on Egypt: Both explicitly name Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon as the agent of Egypt's destruction. Ezekiel expands the announcement with his people drawing swords and the land filling with wounded men.

Jeremiah 26:14
Announce to Migdol and proclaim to Memphis. Say, Take your position and prepare, for the sword has devoured your smilax growth.
  Ezekiel 30:13-15 — related judgment announcements naming Memphis: Jeremiah commands warnings to Migdol and Memphis as the sword devours. Ezekiel likewise names Memphis in announcements of the removal of its magnates and destruction of its multitude; the target does not repeat the smilax image.

Jeremiah 26:15
Why did Apis flee? Your choice calf did not remain, for the Lord disabled him.
  Isaiah 19:1 — related humiliation of Egypt's named calf and handmade objects: Jeremiah asks why Apis fled and says the Lord disabled the choice calf. Isaiah likewise announces the Lord's arrival in Egypt and the shaking of Egypt's handmade objects before Him, without naming Apis individually.

Jeremiah 26:16
Your multitude grew weak and fell, and each spoke to his neighbor, Let us rise and return to our people, to our homeland, away from the Greek sword.
  Jeremiah 27:16 — repeated return to one's homeland before the Greek sword: Both passages describe people returning to their own people and land before the Greek sword. Jeremiah 26 places the speech among Egypt's weakened multitude, whereas Jeremiah 27 places the flight in the judgment upon Babylon.

Jeremiah 26:19
Prepare belongings for deportation, resident daughter Egypt, for Memphis will become a ruin and will be called Woe because no inhabitants remain in her.
  Ezekiel 12:3-4 — parallel preparation of belongings for deportation: The resident daughter Egypt is commanded to prepare belongings for deportation. Ezekiel receives the same concrete instruction concerning belongings for captivity and must enact departure before his audience; the addressees and settings differ.

Jeremiah 26:20
Egypt is a beautified heifer; a detachment from the north came against her.
  Hosea 10:11 — parallel heifer imagery for a people facing divine action: Egypt is pictured as a beautified heifer facing a northern detachment. Hosea likewise pictures Ephraim as a heifer and speaks of God coming upon the finest part of her neck, using bovine imagery for a people subject to His intervention.

Jeremiah 26:21
Her hired men within her are like fattened calves being fed within her. They too turned and fled together; they did not stand, for a day of destruction came upon them, the time of their vengeance.
  Psalms 21:13 — shared cattle imagery for threatening human forces: Egypt's hired men are likened to fed, fattened calves before their flight. The psalm likewise pictures surrounding adversaries as calves and fat bulls; it conveys their threatening presence rather than Jeremiah's subsequent collapse.

Jeremiah 26:22
A sound like a hissing snake, for they will travel through sand; with axes they will come against her like woodcutters.
  Psalms 73:5-7 — parallel attackers with axes likened to forest cutters: Jeremiah describes attackers approaching Egypt with axes like woodcutters. The psalm describes enemies cutting doors with axes as in a forest of trees before burning the sanctuary, a particular counterpart to the cutting image in another setting.

Jeremiah 26:23
They will cut down her forest, says the Lord God, for it certainly cannot be estimated, for it multiplies beyond a locust swarm, and they are beyond numbering.
  Judges 6:5 — shared locust comparison and innumerability in accounts of invasion: Jeremiah's account of the forest being cut down combines a locust-swarm comparison with inability to number the multitude. Judges similarly describes Midian's destructive arrivals as locusts in multitude, with people and camels beyond numbering.

Jeremiah 26:24
Daughter Egypt was put to shame, handed over to a people from the north.
  Isaiah 20:4 — parallel captivity portrayed as Egypt's shame: Jeremiah's daughter Egypt is ashamed and handed to a northern people. Isaiah likewise describes Egypt's captivity as its shame, explicitly naming Assyria's king rather than the Babylonian setting of Jeremiah's oracle.

Jeremiah 26:25
I am taking vengeance for Amon her son upon Pharaoh and those who trust him.
  Isaiah 30:2-3 — related judgment upon confidence in Pharaoh: Jeremiah announces vengeance upon Pharaoh and those trusting him. Isaiah condemns seeking Pharaoh's help and says his shelter will become shame, supplying a particular counterpart to reliance on Egypt's ruler.

Jeremiah 26:27
But do not fear, My slave Jacob, nor be terrified, Israel, for I am saving you from afar and your offspring from their captivity. Jacob will return, be quiet, and sleep, and no one will trouble him.
  Jeremiah 38:8-11 — parallel rescue and gathering of Jacob from distant captivity: Jeremiah 26 promises Jacob rescue from afar, return and security. Jeremiah 38 likewise promises gathering from the north and earth's end and says the Lord ransomed Jacob from stronger hands, connecting return with divine rescue.

Jeremiah 26:28
Do not fear, My servant Jacob, says the Lord, for I am with you. I will make an end of every nation where I thrust you out, but I certainly will not make you perish. I will discipline you for judgment and certainly will not acquit you.
  Amos 9:8-10 — parallel refusal to destroy Jacob completely amid judgment: Jeremiah promises not to make Jacob perish while still disciplining him and refusing acquittal. Amos similarly distinguishes removal of a sinful kingdom from complete removal of Jacob's house, while retaining sword judgment upon its sinners.

Jeremiah 27:2
Announce among the nations and make it heard; do not hide it. Say, Babylon has been captured! Bel was disgraced, the fearless one; the delicate one was handed over, Marodach.
  Isaiah 46:1-2 — parallel humiliation of Bel and powerless images: Jeremiah announces Babylon's capture and Bel's disgrace alongside Marodach's surrender. Isaiah likewise names Bel's fall and depicts carved images as burdens carried into captivity; its other named figure is Dagon, not Marodach.

Jeremiah 27:3
A nation came up against her from the north; it will make her land a ruin, with no one living in it, from human to livestock.
  Isaiah 13:17-20 — parallel conquest leaving Babylon uninhabited: Jeremiah announces a northern nation making Babylon's land a ruin without inhabitants. Isaiah names the Medes and likewise predicts Babylon's overthrow and enduring lack of habitation, without specifying the northern direction in this passage.

Jeremiah 27:4
In those days and at that time, Israel’s sons and Judah’s sons will come together; walking and weeping, they will go seeking the Lord their God.
  Jeremiah 3:18 — parallel joint return of Israel and Judah: Jeremiah 27 pictures Israel's and Judah's sons coming together to seek God. Jeremiah 3 likewise promises that Judah will join Israel and together return to the ancestral land, a particular reunion of the two houses.

Jeremiah 27:5
They will ask the way as far as Zion, for they will turn their faces here. They will come and take refuge with the Lord God, for an everlasting covenant will not be forgotten.
  Isaiah 55:3-5 — parallel everlasting covenant and language of refuge: The returning people turn toward Zion and take refuge with God under an everlasting covenant. Isaiah likewise promises an everlasting covenant, specifies David's sure blessings and describes peoples fleeing to the addressee for refuge because of Israel's God.

Jeremiah 27:6
My people became lost sheep; their shepherds thrust them away and led them astray upon the mountains. They went from mountain to hill and forgot their resting place.
  Ezekiel 34:4-6 — parallel lost sheep scattered through failed shepherding: Jeremiah's people are lost sheep driven astray by shepherds across mountains and hills. Ezekiel likewise charges shepherds with failing to recover the lost and describes God's sheep scattered over mountains and high hills.

Jeremiah 27:7
All who found them consumed them. Their enemies said, Let us not release them, because they sinned against the Lord, the pasture of righteousness, who gathered their fathers.
  Jeremiah 47:2-3 — parallel captors attributing calamity to disobedience against the Lord: Jeremiah 27 reports enemies holding the people because they sinned against the Lord. The chief guard in Jeremiah 47 similarly attributes the calamities to sin and refusal to obey God; neither comparison requires the enemies' conduct to be vindicated.

Jeremiah 27:8
Separate yourselves from Babylon’s midst and the Chaldeans’ land; come out and become like dragons before sheep.
  Isaiah 48:20 — parallel command to leave Babylon and the Chaldeans: Both explicitly command departure from Babylon and the Chaldeans. Isaiah adds public proclamation of Jacob's rescue; it does not repeat Jeremiah's unusual comparison with dragons before sheep.

Jeremiah 27:9
I am rousing assemblies of nations against Babylon from the north. They will deploy against her; from there she will be captured. Like a discerning warrior’s missile, it will not return empty.
  Isaiah 13:4-5 — parallel divine mustering of nations against Babylon: Jeremiah announces God rousing assembled nations against Babylon. Isaiah's Babylon vision likewise describes kings and gathered nations with the Lord's armed fighters coming from afar; Jeremiah additionally specifies the north and a successful missile.

Jeremiah 27:10
Chaldea will become plunder; all who plunder her will be filled.
  Habakkuk 2:7-8 — parallel reversal in which plunderers become plunder: Chaldea becomes plunder, with its plunderers filled, following its attacks upon God's inheritance. Habakkuk likewise says the one who despoiled many nations will become plunder for the remaining peoples.

Jeremiah 27:11
because you rejoiced and boasted while plundering My inheritance, because you leaped like little cattle in pasture and butted like bulls,
  Obadiah 1:11-13 — parallel condemnation of rejoicing over Judah's devastation: Jeremiah condemns Babylon's rejoicing and boasting while plundering God's inheritance. Obadiah likewise forbids rejoicing and boasting over Judah's destruction and joining the attack, addressing a different hostile neighbor.

Jeremiah 27:12
your mother was deeply ashamed—a mother for good things, last of nations, a wilderness.
  Isaiah 47:1-5 — parallel shame and lowered status for personified Babylon: Jeremiah depicts Babylon's mother deeply ashamed and last among nations. Isaiah similarly addresses daughter Babylon, commands her descent and announces exposed shame and loss of her title as a kingdom's strength.

Jeremiah 27:13
Because of the Lord’s anger she will not be inhabited and will entirely disappear. Everyone passing through Babylon will look gloomy, and they will hiss at every wound of hers.
  Isaiah 13:19-22 — parallel lasting desolation of Babylon: Both explicitly name Babylon and deny her continuing habitation after divine judgment. Isaiah develops the replacement of human habitation with wild and uncanny creatures, whereas Jeremiah emphasizes passersby's reaction to her wounds.

Jeremiah 27:14
Deploy against Babylon all around, all who stretch the bow. Shoot at her; do not spare your arrows.
  Jeremiah 28:3 — parallel commands to deploy archers against Babylon: Both passages command drawing bows against Babylon. Jeremiah 27 says not to spare arrows, while Jeremiah 28 says not to spare her young men and to obliterate her force; the objects of those commands remain distinct.

Jeremiah 27:15
Clap against her! Her hands were disabled, her battlements fell, and her wall was demolished. For this is vengeance from God; take vengeance upon her. Do to her as she did.
  Psalms 136:8 — parallel repayment of Babylon according to her own acts: Jeremiah calls for vengeance upon Babylon, doing to her what she did. The psalm likewise addresses daughter Babylon and describes repayment of the repayment she gave the speakers, expressing the same retaliatory measure.

Jeremiah 27:16
Destroy seed from Babylon, the one holding a sickle at harvest. Before the Greek sword, each will return to his people and flee to his land.
  Jeremiah 26:16 — repeated return to one's people before the Greek sword: Jeremiah 27 joins the destruction of Babylon's agriculture with people returning to their own land before the Greek sword. Jeremiah 26 gives the same homeland-return language in the speech of Egypt's weakened multitude.

Jeremiah 27:17
Israel is a wandering sheep driven away by lions. First Assyria’s king ate him; later this one, Babylon’s king, ate his bones.
  2 Kings 17:5-6 — historical counterpart to the Assyrian attack pictured as devouring: Jeremiah names Assyria's king as the first lion to consume wandering Israel. Kings records the Assyrian siege and capture of Samaria and Israel's deportation, giving the historical action corresponding to that first named attacker.
  2 Kings 24:10-16 — historical counterpart to the later Babylonian attack: Jeremiah names Babylon's king as the later devourer after Assyria. Kings records Babylon's siege of Jerusalem and deportation of its rulers and powerful people, corresponding to the second imperial attacker without reproducing the bone-eating image.

Jeremiah 27:18
Therefore thus says the Lord: I am taking vengeance upon Babylon’s king and his land, as I took vengeance upon Assyria’s king.
  Isaiah 10:12 — related announcement of punishment upon Assyria's king: Jeremiah promises punishment upon Babylon's king like the punishment upon Assyria's king. Isaiah supplies a particular prophetic announcement of judgment upon Assyria's ruler after the Lord's work in Zion and Jerusalem.

Jeremiah 27:19
I will restore Israel to his pasture. He will graze on Carmel, on Ephraim’s mountain, and in Gilead, and his soul will be filled.
  Micah 7:14 — parallel restoration of Israel's pasture in named regions: Jeremiah promises Israel restored grazing on Carmel and in Gilead, also naming Ephraim. Micah likewise describes the sheep of God's inheritance in Carmel and grazing in Gilead, while additionally naming Bashan.

Jeremiah 27:20
In those days and at that time they will seek Israel’s injustice, and it will not exist, and Judah’s sins, and they certainly will not be found, for I will be merciful to those left upon the land, says the Lord.
  Micah 7:18-19 — parallel mercy removing the remnant's sins: Jeremiah says Israel's and Judah's sins will not be found because God will show mercy to those remaining. Micah likewise joins the remaining inheritance, God's desire for mercy and the removal of injustices, picturing sins cast into the sea.

Jeremiah 27:21
Go fiercely against her and her inhabitants. Take vengeance, sword, and make her disappear, says the Lord; do everything I command you.
  Jeremiah 29:6-7 — parallel sword judgment carried out by divine command: Jeremiah commands the sword to take vengeance and do what the Lord orders. Jeremiah 29 likewise explains that the Lord's sword cannot be quiet because He commanded its action against coastal places; the threatened territories differ.

Jeremiah 27:22
A sound of war and great shattering in the Chaldeans’ land!
  Jeremiah 28:54 — repeated sound of great shattering in the Chaldeans' land: Both passages locate a sound and great shattering in the Chaldeans' land. Jeremiah 27 names the sound of war, while Jeremiah 28 names crying in Babylon, closely corresponding descriptions of the city's calamity.

Jeremiah 27:23
How the hammer of the whole earth was snapped and shattered! How Babylon became a ruin among nations!
  Isaiah 14:4-5 — parallel breaking of Babylon's instrument of domination: Jeremiah calls Babylon the shattered hammer of the whole earth. Isaiah's lament against Babylon's king says God shattered the rulers' yoke; the tools differ while both images portray broken imperial domination.

Jeremiah 27:24
They will attack you and you will be captured, Babylon, without knowing it. You were found and taken because you resisted the Lord.
  Isaiah 47:10-11 — parallel destruction overtaking Babylon without her knowing: Jeremiah announces Babylon's capture without her knowledge because she resisted the Lord. Isaiah likewise confronts confident daughter Babylon and repeats that sudden destruction will come without her knowing it.

Jeremiah 27:25
The Lord opened His treasury and brought out the instruments of His anger, for the Lord God has work in the Chaldeans’ land,
  Isaiah 13:3-5 — parallel divine forces deployed in the Babylon judgment: Jeremiah depicts God bringing instruments of His anger from a treasury for work in Chaldea. Isaiah's Babylon vision likewise describes forces led to fulfill divine wrath and the Lord's armed fighters coming for destruction, without the treasury detail.

Jeremiah 27:26
for her times have arrived. Open her storehouses; search her like a cave and destroy her. Let no remnant of her remain.
  Isaiah 14:22-23 — parallel removal of Babylon's remnant: The order to open Babylon's storehouses and destroy her concludes that no remnant should remain. Isaiah likewise announces the destruction of her name, remnant and offspring and the desolation of Babylonia; it does not repeat the cave-search image.

Jeremiah 27:28
A sound of those fleeing and being rescued from Babylon’s land, announcing in Zion vengeance from the Lord our God!
  Isaiah 48:20 — parallel departure from Babylon accompanied by proclamation: Jeremiah pictures escapees from Babylon announcing the Lord's vengeance in Zion. Isaiah likewise joins departure from Babylon with public announcement of the Lord's rescue of Jacob, presenting the complementary rescue side of the judgment.

Jeremiah 27:29
Give orders against Babylon to many, to everyone drawing a bow. Encamp against her all around; let none of her people escape. Repay her according to her works; do to her all she did, for she resisted the Lord, Israel’s holy God.
  Obadiah 1:15 — parallel recompense according to prior conduct: Jeremiah orders repayment of Babylon according to her works, doing to her what she did. Obadiah similarly says that as the addressed enemy acted, so it will happen to him, with recompense returned upon his own head.

Jeremiah 27:30
Therefore her young men will fall in her squares, and all her warriors will be thrown down, said the Lord.
  Jeremiah 30:32 — repeated fall of young men in squares and warriors: Both judgments announce young men falling in public squares and the fall of warriors. Jeremiah 27 addresses Babylon, while Jeremiah 30 places the corresponding announcement in the Damascus oracle.

Jeremiah 27:31
I am against you, insolent woman, says the Lord, for your day has come, the time of your vengeance.
  Isaiah 47:7-11 — parallel judgment upon confident personified Babylon: Jeremiah addresses Babylon as an insolent woman whose day of vengeance has arrived. Isaiah likewise addresses her confident claim to lasting rule and announces sudden loss and destruction, exposing the failure of that confidence.

Jeremiah 27:32
Your insolence will grow weak and fall, with no one to raise it. I will kindle fire in her forest, and it will devour everything around her.
  Proverbs 16:18 — parallel insolence preceding ruin and a fall: Jeremiah says Babylon's insolence will weaken and fall without anyone raising it. Proverbs states the corresponding moral pattern: insolence goes before ruin and evil-mindedness before a fall; it does not supply the forest-fire detail.

Jeremiah 27:33
Thus says the Lord: Israel’s sons and Judah’s sons have been oppressed together. All who captured them oppressed them, because they did not want to release them.
  Exodus 5:1-9 — parallel oppression joined with refusal to release Israel: Jeremiah's captors oppress Israel and Judah and refuse their release. Pharaoh likewise refuses to let Israel go and increases the people's labor, supplying a particular earlier pattern of captivity, oppression and rejected release.

Jeremiah 27:34
The One redeeming them is strong; the Lord Almighty is His name. He will judge a case against His adversaries, to remove the land, and He will provoke Babylon’s inhabitants.
  Proverbs 23:10-11 — parallel mighty redeemer judging a case: Jeremiah calls the people's redeemer strong and says He will judge a case against adversaries. Proverbs likewise calls the orphans' redeeming Lord mighty and says He will judge their case, applying the same protective legal image to a different vulnerable group.

Jeremiah 27:35
A sword against the Chaldeans, Babylon’s inhabitants, her nobles, and her discerning men!
  Jeremiah 28:57 — parallel judgment reaching Babylon's leaders and wise men: Jeremiah 27 directs sword judgment against Babylon's inhabitants, nobles and discerning men. Jeremiah 28 likewise names leaders, wise men and generals as recipients of divine judgment, there expressed as thorough intoxication.

Jeremiah 27:36
A sword against her warriors, and they will be disabled!
  Jeremiah 28:30 — parallel disabling of Babylon's warriors: Jeremiah announces a sword against the warriors and their disabling. Jeremiah 28 describes Babylon's warrior ceasing to fight and the crushing of their power, a concrete counterpart to the loss of military ability.

Jeremiah 27:37
A sword against their horses and chariots! A sword against their warriors and the mixed people within her, and they will become like women! A sword against her treasures, and they will be scattered!
  Nahum 3:13 — shared image of a city's defenders becoming like women: Jeremiah describes Babylon's warriors and mixed population becoming like women amid sword judgment. Nahum uses the same gendered image for the people within the threatened city as its gates open to enemies; the comparison concerns the texts' portrayal of failed defense.

Jeremiah 27:38
She trusted in her water, and they will be ashamed, for it is a land of carved images, and in the islands where they boasted.
  Jeremiah 28:36 — related contrast between confidence in water and its threatened drying: Babylon trusted in her water but will be ashamed. Jeremiah 28 announces that God will lay her sea waste and dry her spring, providing a particular judgment against the water associated with that confidence.

Jeremiah 27:39
Therefore apparitions will live on the islands, and daughters of sirens will live in her. She certainly will never again be inhabited.
  Isaiah 13:20-22 — parallel uncanny inhabitants replacing human habitation in Babylon: Jeremiah places apparitions on the islands and daughters of sirens in Babylon, while denying future habitation. Isaiah likewise denies Babylon human habitation and places sirens and other uncanny creatures there, without reproducing Jeremiah's island clause.

Jeremiah 27:40
As God overturned Sodom, Gomorrah, and their bordering places, said the Lord, no person will live there, and no human being will reside there.
  Genesis 19:24-25 — explicit comparison with the overthrow of Sodom and Gomorrah: Jeremiah expressly compares Babylon's desolation with God's overthrow of Sodom, Gomorrah and bordering places. Genesis narrates that overthrow of the cities and surrounding country, supplying the named event used in the comparison.

Jeremiah 27:41
A people is coming from the north; a great nation and many kings will be roused from the earth’s farthest part,
  Jeremiah 6:22 — repeated northern people and nations from the earth's extremity: Both passages announce a people coming from the north and a force roused from the earth's farthest part. Jeremiah 27 applies the pattern to Babylon and additionally names many kings; Jeremiah 6 introduces the attack on Zion.

Jeremiah 27:42
holding bow and dagger. It is insolent and certainly will not show mercy. Their voice will sound like the sea. They will ride horses, prepared like fire for war against you, daughter Babylon.
  Jeremiah 6:23 — parallel merciless mounted force with a sea-like voice: Both describe armed riders who show no mercy, whose voice resembles the sea and who prepare like fire for war. Jeremiah 27 addresses daughter Babylon, whereas Jeremiah 6 addresses daughter Zion and additionally names chariots.

Jeremiah 27:43
Babylon’s king heard the report about them, and his hands were disabled. Distress seized him, birth pains like a woman giving birth.
  Jeremiah 6:24 — repeated report, disabled hands and birth-pain distress: Babylon's king hears the report, loses strength in his hands and is seized by birth-pain distress. Jeremiah 6 uses the same sequence in the people's first-person report of the force threatening Zion.

Jeremiah 27:44
Like a lion he will come up from the Jordan into Aitham’s place, for I will quickly drive them away from her and appoint every young man over her. For who is like Me? Who will resist Me, and what shepherd can stand before Me?
  Jeremiah 30:13 — repeated lion, Jordan and incomparable divine power imagery: Both passages describe a lion coming from the Jordan into Aitham's place and ask who can resist God or what shepherd can stand before Him. The corresponding images occur in Babylon's and Idumea's distinct judgment oracles.

Jeremiah 27:45
Therefore hear the Lord’s counsel that He has planned against Babylon and His purposes against the resident Chaldeans: Surely the lambs of their sheep will be destroyed; surely pasture will disappear from them.
  Jeremiah 30:14 — parallel divine counsel expressed through sheep and pasture: Jeremiah announces God's counsel against Babylon and consequences for lambs and pasture. The Idumea oracle likewise announces God's counsel and purposes, followed by small sheep and a dwelling made untrodden; the locations and exact outcomes differ.

Jeremiah 27:46
For at the sound of Babylon’s capture the earth will shake, and a cry will be heard among the nations.
  Jeremiah 30:15 — parallel earth-shaking fall and widely heard cry: Babylon's capture shakes the earth and produces a cry among nations. The Idumea oracle similarly describes earth shaking at a fall and a cry heard at the sea, using the same coupled effects in another judgment.

Jeremiah 28:1
Thus says the Lord: I am rousing a destroying hot wind against Babylon and against the Chaldeans who live there.
  Hosea 13:15 — parallel destructive hot wind brought by the Lord: Jeremiah announces a destroying hot wind roused against Babylon. Hosea likewise says the Lord brings a scorching wilderness wind that dries channels, springs and land, a particular weather image of destructive divine action.

Jeremiah 28:2
I will send insolent men to Babylon; they will abuse her and ravage her land. Woe upon Babylon all around on the day of her affliction!
  Isaiah 13:3 — parallel insolent forces directed against Babylon: Jeremiah says God will send insolent men to ravage Babylon. Isaiah's Babylon vision likewise describes forces led by God and giants acting insolently together to fulfill His wrath, without identifying the two groups.

Jeremiah 28:3
Against her let the archer stretch his bow, and let the man who has armor put it on. Do not spare her young men; obliterate her whole force.
  Jeremiah 27:14 — parallel orders to draw bows against Babylon: Jeremiah 28 commands the archer to draw his bow against Babylon and denies mercy to her force. Jeremiah 27 likewise commands archers to deploy and shoot at her, specifying that arrows should not be spared.

Jeremiah 28:4
Wounded men will fall in the Chaldeans’ land, and pierced men outside her.
  Jeremiah 27:30 — parallel fallen fighters in Babylon's judgment: Jeremiah 28 describes wounded and pierced men falling in and outside the Chaldeans' land. Jeremiah 27 likewise announces fallen young men and warriors in Babylon's judgment, locating the young men's fall in her squares.

Jeremiah 28:5
For Israel and Judah have not been widowed of their God, of the Lord Almighty, because their land was filled with injustice from Israel’s holy ones.
  Isaiah 54:4-8 — related denial of lasting abandonment through widowhood imagery: Jeremiah says Israel and Judah have not been widowed of their God. Isaiah similarly promises the end of widowhood's reproach and speaks of God's mercy toward one compared with a wife, while retaining its own description of brief abandonment.

Jeremiah 28:6
Flee from Babylon’s midst; each of you save your life. Do not be cast away in her injustice, for her time of vengeance is from the Lord; He is repaying her recompense.
  Isaiah 48:20 — parallel command to escape Babylon: Jeremiah commands each person to flee Babylon and save life at the time of recompense. Isaiah likewise commands leaving Babylon and fleeing the Chaldeans, joining departure with the proclamation that the Lord rescued Jacob.

Jeremiah 28:7
Babylon was a golden cup in the Lord’s hand, making the whole earth drunk. Nations drank her wine; therefore they were shaken.
  Psalms 74:9 — parallel cup in the Lord's hand affecting the earth: Babylon is a golden cup in the Lord's hand whose wine shakes the nations. The psalm likewise places a wine cup in the Lord's hand and says all the earth's sinners will drink, while not identifying its cup with Babylon.

Jeremiah 28:8
Suddenly Babylon fell and was shattered. Lament her! Take resin for her corruption, if somehow she may be healed.
  Jeremiah 28:9 — same-chapter proposed healing and its unsuccessful outcome: After Babylon's sudden fall, the text calls for resin in case she can be healed. The next unit reports that Babylon was treated but not healed, directly answering the proposed medicinal response.

Jeremiah 28:9
We treated Babylon, but she was not healed. Let us leave her; let each of us go to our own land, for her judgment reached heaven and rose to the stars.
  Jeremiah 26:11 — parallel futile treatment of a judged nation: Jeremiah 28 reports that Babylon was treated without healing. The Egypt oracle similarly calls for Gilead's resin yet says multiplied remedies bring no benefit, extending the failed-healing image to a different nation.

Jeremiah 28:10
The Lord has brought forth His judgment. Come, let us announce in Zion the works of the Lord our God.
  Jeremiah 27:28 — parallel announcement of the Lord's action in Zion: Jeremiah 28 calls for announcing the Lord's works in Zion after His judgment is brought forth. Jeremiah 27 likewise pictures rescued people from Babylon announcing the Lord's vengeance in Zion.

Jeremiah 28:11
Prepare the arrows; fill the quivers. The Lord roused the spirit of the Medes’ king, for His anger against Babylon is to destroy her. This is the Lord’s vengeance, vengeance for His people.
  Isaiah 13:17-18 — parallel divine rousing of the Medes against Babylon: Jeremiah says the Lord roused the Medes' king for Babylon's destruction. Isaiah's Babylon oracle likewise explicitly announces God rousing the Medes, adding their disregard for wealth and lack of pity.

Jeremiah 28:12
Raise a signal on Babylon’s walls; set up quivers, raise guards, prepare weapons, for the Lord has undertaken and will do what He spoke against Babylon’s inhabitants,
  Jeremiah 27:14-15 — parallel preparation against Babylon's fortifications: Jeremiah 28 commands a signal, guards and weapons at Babylon's walls as the Lord carries out His word. Jeremiah 27 likewise orders deployment around the city and announces fallen battlements and a demolished wall.

Jeremiah 28:13
who dwell beside many waters and her abundance of treasures. Your end has truly come to your inward parts.
  Jeremiah 27:38 — related threat to Babylon beside the waters she trusted: Jeremiah 28 addresses Babylon beside many waters and announces her end. Jeremiah 27 says she trusted in her water and will be ashamed, connecting the watery setting with failed confidence without interpreting the inward-parts phrase.

Jeremiah 28:14
For the Lord swore by His arm: I will fill you with people like locusts, and those coming down will speak out against you.
  Isaiah 62:8 — parallel divine oath by the arm: Jeremiah announces that the Lord swore by His arm before threatening Babylon. Isaiah likewise records an oath by the Lord's right hand and strong arm, there promising protection of Jerusalem's produce rather than an enemy's defeat.

Jeremiah 28:15
He makes the earth by His strength, prepares the inhabited world by His wisdom, and stretched out heaven by His understanding.
  Jeremiah 10:12 — repeated creation by divine strength, wisdom and understanding: Both passages describe the earth made by God's strength, the inhabited world established through wisdom, and heaven stretched out by understanding. The closely corresponding clauses affirm the Creator in settings that also expose powerless images.

Jeremiah 28:16
At His voice He set a sound of water in heaven and brought clouds up from the earth’s end. He made lightning for rain and brought light out of His treasuries.
  Jeremiah 10:13 — repeated heavenly waters, clouds, lightning and light: Both describe heavenly water, clouds brought from the earth's extremity, lightning for rain and light brought from divine treasuries. Jeremiah 28 additionally introduces the sound of water through God's voice; neither supplied unit says wind from the treasuries.

Jeremiah 28:17
Every person became foolish, without knowledge; every goldsmith was disgraced by his carvings, because they cast false things, and there is no spirit in them.
  Jeremiah 10:14 — repeated shame of makers of lifeless images: Both say people become foolish without knowledge and goldsmiths are disgraced by their carved things. They explain that the cast things are false and contain no spirit, giving a close verbal counterpart to the critique.

Jeremiah 28:18
They are vain, mocked works; at the time of their visitation they will perish.
  Jeremiah 10:15 — repeated futility and destruction of manufactured images: Both call the objects vain or futile, mocked works, and predict their destruction at the time of visitation. The correspondence concerns the same verdict upon manufactured objects in the surrounding idol critiques.

Jeremiah 28:19
Jacob’s portion is not like this, for the One who formed all things is Himself his inheritance. The Lord is His name.
  Jeremiah 10:16 — repeated contrast between Jacob's inheritance and manufactured objects: Both deny that Jacob's portion resembles the futile images and identify the One who formed everything as his inheritance. Both conclude by naming Him Lord, distinguishing Creator from manufacture.

Jeremiah 28:20
You scatter instruments of war for Me. In you I will scatter nations, and from you I will remove kings.
  Isaiah 10:5-7 — related military instruments within divine judgment: Jeremiah links scattering instruments of war for God with nations scattered in the addressee and kings removed from the addressee. Isaiah's Assyrian oracle likewise joins military force, the rod of divine anger and commanded plunder, without identifying Jeremiah's unnamed addressee as Assyria.

Jeremiah 28:21
In you I will scatter horse and rider; in you I will scatter chariots and their riders.
  Haggai 2:22 — parallel overthrow of horses, riders and chariots: Jeremiah announces scattering of horse and rider and of chariots and their riders. Haggai similarly describes God overturning chariots and riders and horses with their riders falling, within judgment upon royal power.

Jeremiah 28:22
In you I will scatter young man and virgin; in you I will scatter man and woman.
  Jeremiah 6:11 — parallel judgment reaching young people, men and women: Jeremiah 28 enumerates young man, virgin, man and woman in the scattering. Jeremiah 6 likewise extends judgment across young people and the seizure of man and woman, while also naming children and elders.

Jeremiah 28:24
I will repay Babylon and all the Chaldeans who live there for all the evils they did against Zion before your eyes, says the Lord.
  Psalms 136:7-8 — parallel repayment of Babylon for harm to Zion: Jeremiah promises repayment to Babylon for the evils done against Zion. The psalm's remembrance of Jerusalem's destruction is followed by an address to daughter Babylon concerning repayment of what she gave the speakers.

Jeremiah 28:25
I am against you, ruined mountain that ruins the whole earth. I will stretch My hand against you, roll you down from the rocks, and make you like a burned mountain.
  Isaiah 14:12-17 — parallel downfall of Babylon portrayed as exalted and destructive: Jeremiah's mountain that ruins the earth is rolled down from the rocks. Isaiah's lament against Babylon's king likewise combines lofty ambition, descent and devastation of the inhabited world; its heavenly and underworld images differ from the burning mountain.

Jeremiah 28:26
They will certainly not take from you a cornerstone or a foundation stone, for you will be a ruin for ever, says the Lord.
  Isaiah 28:16 — contrast between Babylon's unusable stones and Zion's foundation: Jeremiah denies taking a cornerstone or foundation stone from ruined Babylon. Isaiah promises a precious cornerstone in Zion's foundations, placing the same building vocabulary in a contrasting promise for a different city.

Jeremiah 28:27
Raise a signal on the earth; sound a trumpet among the nations. Consecrate nations against her; give orders against her to Ararat’s kingdoms from Me and to the Ashkenazites. Set up arrow batteries against her; bring up horses against her like a multitude of locusts.
  Judges 6:5 — parallel locust-like multitude in a destructive advance: Jeremiah orders horses brought against Babylon like a multitude of locusts. Judges likewise pictures destructive invaders as locusts in multitude and emphasizes their uncountable numbers, while naming camels rather than horses.

Jeremiah 28:28
Consecrate nations against her: the king of the Medes and of all the earth, his leaders and all his generals.
  Isaiah 13:17 — parallel mustering of the Medes against Babylon: Jeremiah summons nations against Babylon and names the Medes' king among the mobilized power. Isaiah's Babylon oracle likewise explicitly says God is rousing the Medes against its addressee.

Jeremiah 28:29
The earth was shaken and suffered, for the Lord’s purpose rose against Babylon, to make Babylon’s land a ruin, no longer inhabited.
  Isaiah 14:22-23 — parallel divine purpose to desolate Babylon: Jeremiah connects the shaken earth with the Lord's purpose to make Babylon uninhabited. Isaiah likewise announces the Lord rising against her and making Babylonia deserted, with the loss of her remnant and offspring.

Jeremiah 28:30
Babylon’s warrior has ceased fighting; they will sit there in confinement. Their power was crushed; they became like women. Her dwellings were burned; her bars were shattered.
  Nahum 3:13 — parallel failed defenses, women imagery and burning barriers: Jeremiah describes crushed military power, defenders becoming like women, burned dwellings and shattered bars. Nahum likewise compares a city's people with women as its gates open to enemies and fire consumes its bars.

Jeremiah 28:33
For thus says the Lord: The houses of Babylon’s king will be threshed like a threshing floor ready for use; a little longer, and her harvest will come.
  Micah 4:12-13 — parallel threshing imagery for judgment upon hostile power: Babylon's royal houses are to be threshed like a prepared floor as harvest approaches. Micah likewise pictures hostile nations gathered like sheaves on a threshing floor and commands Zion to thresh them, applying agricultural processing to defeated powers.

Jeremiah 28:34
Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon devoured me, divided me, seized me, a thin vessel. He swallowed me like a dragon, filled his belly, and drove me from my luxury.
  Ezekiel 29:2-5 — parallel dragon imagery for an imperial ruler: Jeremiah likens Nebuchadnezzar's devouring to a dragon swallowing and filling its belly. Ezekiel explicitly calls Pharaoh a great dragon among his rivers; both depict imperial rulers through the creature, though Ezekiel emphasizes capture rather than swallowing.

Jeremiah 28:35
My toils and miseries be upon Babylon, the woman dwelling in Zion will say; my blood be upon the Chaldeans who live there, Jerusalem will say.
  Psalms 136:7-8 — parallel complaint seeking repayment for Jerusalem's suffering: Zion and Jerusalem call for their miseries and blood to fall upon Babylon and the Chaldeans.
