The psalm likewise remembers Jerusalem's destruction and anticipates Babylon receiving the repayment she gave the speakers.

Jeremiah 28:36
Therefore thus says the Lord: I will judge your adversary and avenge you; I will lay her sea waste and dry her spring.
  Isaiah 44:27 — parallel divine drying of waters: Jeremiah promises to lay Babylon's sea waste and dry her spring as God judges Zion's adversary. Isaiah likewise depicts God declaring the abyss desolate and drying its rivers; the named bodies of water and surrounding setting differ.

Jeremiah 28:37
Babylon will become a ruin and will not be inhabited.
  Isaiah 13:19-20 — parallel enduring lack of habitation in Babylon: Jeremiah says Babylon will become a ruin without inhabitants. Isaiah likewise names Babylon and denies habitation through generations, expanding the same city's announced desolation.

Jeremiah 28:38
Together they were roused like lions and like lions’ cubs.
  Jeremiah 28:40 — same-chapter contrast between lions and slaughtered flock animals: The group is first pictured as roused lions and lions' cubs. Two units later God brings them down like lambs, rams and young goats for slaughter, reversing the animals' threatening posture into vulnerability.

Jeremiah 28:39
In their heat I will give them a drink and make them drunk, so that they are stupefied and sleep an everlasting sleep and certainly do not rise, says the Lord.
  Isaiah 51:17-22 — related drink and sleep imagery for divine judgment: Jeremiah portrays Babylon's people receiving a stupefying drink and everlasting sleep. Isaiah likewise joins the Lord's cup of anger, drunkenness and helpless sleeping, but calls Jerusalem to rise and promises removal of her cup rather than everlasting sleep.

Jeremiah 28:40
I will bring them down like lambs to slaughter and like rams with young goats.
  Isaiah 34:6-7 — parallel flock animals as imagery of divine slaughter: Jeremiah pictures people brought down like lambs, rams and young goats for slaughter. Isaiah's judgment upon Idumea similarly uses lambs, male goats and rams in the Lord's sacrifice, applying animal-slaughter imagery to another threatened people.

Jeremiah 28:41
How the boast of the whole earth was captured and hunted! How Babylon became a ruin among the nations!
  Jeremiah 27:23 — repeated lament over Babylon's fallen worldwide prominence: Both ask how Babylon became a ruin among nations. Jeremiah 28 calls her the whole earth's boast captured, while Jeremiah 27 calls her the whole earth's hammer shattered, distinct images of the same reversal.

Jeremiah 28:42
The sea rose over Babylon with the sound of its waves, and she was covered.
  Jeremiah 29:2 — parallel waters covering a threatened land and city: Jeremiah 28 pictures the sea covering Babylon with its waves. Jeremiah 29 likewise describes rising waters becoming a flood over land, city and inhabitants in a different oracle; the comparison concerns the covering-water image.

Jeremiah 28:43
Her cities became a waterless and untrodden land; not one person will live there, nor will any human lodge there.
  Jeremiah 30:28 — repeated exclusion of human habitation from devastated places: Babylon's cities become waterless and untrodden, without human habitation. Jeremiah 30 similarly describes the courtyard as perpetually untrodden, with no person settling or living there, while adding ostriches to its description.

Jeremiah 28:44
I will exact vengeance on Babylon and bring what she swallowed out of her mouth. Nations will certainly no longer gather to her.
  Ezekiel 34:10 — parallel rescue from a devouring oppressor's mouth: God promises to bring what Babylon swallowed out of her mouth. Ezekiel likewise promises to rescue His sheep from the shepherds' mouth so they are no longer food, using the same devouring-and-release image for oppressive power.

Jeremiah 28:49
And in Babylon wounded men from all the earth will fall.
  Jeremiah 32:33 — related worldwide scope of fallen wounded people: Jeremiah 28 places wounded men from all the earth in Babylon. Jeremiah 32 describes the Lord's wounded lying from one part of the earth to another; the international scope corresponds while the distribution of the wounded differs.

Jeremiah 28:50
You who escape from the land, go on; do not stand still. You who are far away, remember the Lord, and let Jerusalem rise in your hearts.
  Psalms 136:1-6 — parallel remembrance of Jerusalem while away from her: Those escaping far away must remember the Lord and hold Jerusalem in their hearts. The psalm likewise depicts people in Babylon remembering Zion and insisting that Jerusalem not be forgotten, a particular counterpart in displacement.

Jeremiah 28:51
We were disgraced, for we heard our reproach; dishonor covered our face. Foreigners entered our holy places, the Lord’s house.
  Psalms 78:1-4 — parallel shame over foreigners entering holy places: Jeremiah's speakers report dishonor because foreigners entered the Lord's holy places. The psalm likewise says nations entered God's inheritance and defiled His holy temple, then describes the community's reproach and ridicule.

Jeremiah 28:52
Therefore days are coming, says the Lord, when I will exact vengeance on her carvings, and wounded men will fall throughout her land.
  Isaiah 46:1-2 — related judgment upon Babylon's carved images: Jeremiah promises vengeance upon Babylon's carvings. Isaiah likewise describes fallen Bel and shattered Dagon, with carved images carried as burdens into captivity, giving a concrete depiction of defeated manufactured objects.

Jeremiah 28:53
Even if Babylon rises as high as heaven and fortifies the height of her strength, those who destroy her will come from Me, says the Lord.
  Isaiah 14:13-15 — parallel heavenly-height ambition brought down: Even Babylon's ascent to heaven and fortified height will not stop God-sent destroyers. Isaiah's lament against Babylon's king likewise describes ambition to ascend above stars and clouds, followed by descent to Hades.

Jeremiah 28:54
A sound of crying in Babylon, a great shattering in the Chaldeans’ land!
  Jeremiah 27:22 — repeated great shattering in the Chaldeans' land: The cry in Babylon and great shattering in Chaldea closely correspond to Jeremiah 27's sound of war and great shattering in the same land. The two descriptions locate the heard calamity explicitly in Babylon's domain.

Jeremiah 28:55
For the Lord destroyed Babylon and silenced her loud voice sounding like many waters; He gave her voice over to destruction.
  Isaiah 17:12-14 — related water-like national noise brought to an end: Jeremiah likens Babylon's loud voice to many waters before its silencing. Isaiah likewise compares nations' roaring with abundant rushing water and describes displacement and a swiftly ended threat; its later pronouns are not used to identify Babylon.

Jeremiah 28:56
For misery came upon Babylon. Her warriors were captured; their bow is terrified, because God repays them; the Lord repays her recompense.
  Jeremiah 27:29 — parallel divine repayment upon Babylon: Jeremiah 28 explains Babylon's military misery through God's repayment of her recompense. Jeremiah 27 likewise commands repayment according to her works, doing to her what she did, a shared measure of judgment upon the named city.

Jeremiah 28:57
He will make her leaders and wise men and generals thoroughly drunk, says the King, whose name is the Lord Almighty.
  Jeremiah 28:39 — same-chapter repetition of judgment through divine intoxication: Jeremiah 28:57 names leaders, wise men and generals who will be made thoroughly drunk. The earlier announcement likewise says God will give a stupefying drink, adding the image of everlasting sleep.

Jeremiah 28:58
Thus says the Lord: Babylon’s wall was broadened; it will be utterly demolished, and her high gates burned. Peoples will not labor in vain, and nations will perish at the beginning.
  Jeremiah 27:15 — parallel demolition of Babylon's wall: Jeremiah 28 announces the utter demolition of Babylon's broadened wall and burning of its high gates. Jeremiah 27 likewise describes her fallen battlements and demolished wall; this counterpart does not reinterpret the later statement that peoples will not labor in vain.

Jeremiah 28:59
The word the Lord commanded Jeremiah the prophet to tell Seraiah son of Neriah son of Maaseiah when he went from Zedekiah king of Judah to Babylon in the fourth year of his reign. Seraiah was an official over gifts.
  Jeremiah 35:1 — parallel dating to Zedekiah's fourth year: The instructions associated with Seraiah's journey to Babylon are dated to Zedekiah's fourth year. Jeremiah 35 dates Hananiah's distinct public speech to that same regnal year and its fifth month, connecting chronology without conflating the events.

Jeremiah 28:60
Jeremiah wrote in one scroll all the evils that would come upon Babylon, all these words written against Babylon.
  Jeremiah 43:2-4 — parallel written preservation of Jeremiah's judgment words: Jeremiah writes Babylon's announced evils in one scroll. Jeremiah 43 likewise commands a roll containing the delivered oracles and describes Baruch writing from Jeremiah's mouth, there with a broader scope including Judah and all nations.

Jeremiah 28:61
Jeremiah said to Seraiah: When you arrive in Babylon, you will see and read all these words,
  Jeremiah 43:5-8 — parallel delegated reading of written prophetic words: Jeremiah instructs Seraiah to read the written words when he reaches Babylon. Jeremiah 43 likewise records his instruction to Baruch to read a roll to the people, while specifying the Lord's house and a fast day rather than Babylon.

Jeremiah 28:62
and say, Lord, Lord, Thou spakest against this place to destroy it and leave no inhabitants in it, from human to livestock, for it will be a ruin for ever.
  Jeremiah 27:3 — repeated desolation of Babylon without human or animal inhabitants: The instructed prayer recalls God's word to make the place a perpetual ruin without people or livestock. Jeremiah 27 announces Babylon's ruin with the corresponding exclusion of human and animal inhabitants.

Jeremiah 28:63
When you finish reading this scroll, bind a stone to it and throw it into the Euphrates,
  Jeremiah 19:10-11 — parallel enacted signs of a city's irreversible destruction: The stone-bound scroll thrown into the Euphrates is explained in 28:64 as Babylon's sinking without rising. Jeremiah 19 similarly uses the breaking of a jar to portray a people and city smashed beyond healing, with a different object and city.

Jeremiah 28:64
and say, Thus Babylon will sink and certainly not rise because of the evils I am bringing upon her.
  Exodus 15:4-5 — shared sinking imagery for the defeat of an imperial power: Babylon's announced sinking is illustrated by the scroll cast into the river in the preceding unit. Exodus celebrates Pharaoh's force covered by the sea and sinking like a stone, a corresponding defeat image rather than the same event.

Jeremiah 29:2
Thus says the Lord: Waters are rising from the north and will become a flooding torrent. It will flood the land and its fullness, the city and those living in it. People will cry out, and all the land’s inhabitants will shout aloud.
  Isaiah 8:7-8 — parallel overflowing waters threatening land and inhabitants: Jeremiah describes northern waters becoming a torrent over the land and city. Isaiah likewise depicts the Assyrian king's advance as strong river water and an encampment filling the country's breadth, an explicit military application of the water image.

Jeremiah 29:3
At the sound of his onrush, of the hooves of his feet, the shaking of his chariots, and the noise of his wheels, fathers did not turn back to their sons, because their hands failed,
  Nahum 3:2-3 — parallel sounds of horses, wheels and chariots in calamity: Jeremiah emphasizes the sound of the onrush, hooves, chariots and wheels before describing failed parental response. Nahum likewise lists shaking wheels, a pursuing horse and a jolting chariot amid slaughter; it does not repeat the fathers-and-sons detail.

Jeremiah 29:4
on the day coming to destroy all the people of another tribe. I will obliterate Tyre and Sidon and all the remainder of their assistance, for the Lord will destroy the survivors of the islands.
  Joel 4:4-8 — parallel judgment naming Tyre and Sidon: Jeremiah names Tyre and Sidon among the coastal powers facing destruction. Joel likewise addresses both cities and announces repayment for their treatment of Judah and Jerusalem, giving a particular counterpart for the named coastal pair.

Jeremiah 29:5
Baldness has come upon Gaza; Ashkelon and the survivors of the Anakim have been cast away. How long will you strike,
  Zephaniah 2:4 — parallel judgment naming Gaza and Ashkelon: Jeremiah describes baldness upon Gaza and the casting away of Ashkelon. Zephaniah likewise announces Gaza's plundering and Ashkelon's disappearance; the target does not supply the baldness or Anakim detail.

Jeremiah 29:6
sword of the Lord? How long will you not be quiet? Return to your sheath; rest and be lifted away!
  Jeremiah 29:7 — same-chapter question and answer about the Lord's sword: The speaker asks how long the Lord's sword will remain unquiet and urges it to return to its sheath. The following unit answers that it cannot be quiet because the Lord commanded its action against Ashkelon and the coast.

Jeremiah 29:7
How will it be quiet? The Lord commanded it to rise against Ashkelon, against the coastal places, against those remaining.
  Jeremiah 27:21 — parallel sword action governed by divine command: Jeremiah 29 explains the sword's continuing action through the Lord's command against the coast. Jeremiah 27 likewise orders the sword to avenge and carry out everything God commands, there in Babylon's judgment.

Jeremiah 30:1
For Idumea, thus says the Lord: There is no longer wisdom in Teman; counsel perished from the discerning; their wisdom went away.
  Obadiah 1:8-9 — parallel loss of wisdom in Idumea and Teman: Jeremiah announces the loss of wisdom and discerning counsel in Teman within the Idumea oracle. Obadiah likewise predicts the destruction of Idumea's wise people and understanding, then names Teman's terrified warriors.

Jeremiah 30:2
Their place was deceived. Go deep to dwell, inhabitants of Dedan, for he did difficult things; I brought them upon him at the time I visited him.
  Jeremiah 30:25 — same-chapter command to go deep to dwell: Both units instruct the addressed inhabitants to go deep to dwell. Jeremiah 30:2 addresses Dedan amid visitation, while 30:25 addresses those in the courtyard before Babylon's planned attack; the repeated instruction connects distinct local warnings.

Jeremiah 30:3
For grape gatherers came to you; they will leave you no remnants. Like thieves by night they will lay their hand upon you.
  Obadiah 1:5 — parallel grape-gatherer and night-thief comparisons with differing wording: Both Idumea oracles compare loss with grape gathering and thieves at night. Jeremiah says the gatherers leave no remnants, whereas Obadiah asks whether gatherers would not leave a gleaning; the shared comparisons should not erase that verbal distinction.

Jeremiah 30:4
For I dragged Esau away and uncovered their secrets; they certainly cannot hide. They perished by the hand of his brother and his neighbor, and it is not possible
  Obadiah 1:6-7 — parallel exposure of Esau's secrets and hostility from close associates: Jeremiah describes Esau dragged out, secrets uncovered and destruction through brother and neighbor. Obadiah likewise speaks of Esau searched out and hidden things seized, followed by betrayal from covenant partners and men at peace with him.

Jeremiah 30:6
For thus said the Lord: Those for whom there was no law to drink the cup drank it. And you, though acquitted, certainly will not be acquitted, for you will certainly drink.
  Jeremiah 32:28-29 — parallel insistence that judgment's cup cannot be refused: Jeremiah 30 addresses one as acquitted yet denies acquittal and insists upon drinking. Jeremiah 32 likewise says unwilling recipients certainly will drink and argues that judgment beginning with God's named city does not leave others exempt.

Jeremiah 30:7
For I swore by Myself, says the Lord, that you will become an untrodden place, a reproach, and a curse in her midst, and all her cities will be deserted for ever.
  Amos 6:8 — parallel divine oath by Himself announcing judgment: The Lord swears by Himself before announcing lasting desolation in the Idumea oracle. Amos likewise introduces a city's removal through the Lord's oath by Himself, there condemning Jacob's arrogance.

Jeremiah 30:8
I heard a report from the Lord, and He sent messengers to the nations: Gather and come against her; rise for war!
  Obadiah 1:1 — parallel report from the Lord and summons of nations to war: Both Idumea passages report hearing from the Lord and a dispatch or messengers sent among nations. Both then summon an uprising for battle against the addressed land, a close correspondence in the oracle's announced mobilization.

Jeremiah 30:9
I made you small among nations, easily despised among people.
  Obadiah 1:2 — parallel lowering of Idumea among nations: Both passages say God has made the addressed people small or insignificant among nations and despised or dishonored. Their common Idumea setting makes this a particular counterpart to the loss of national standing.

Jeremiah 30:10
Your playfulness assailed you; your heart’s insolence destroyed holes in rocks and seized the strength of a high hill. For he raised his nest like an eagle; from there I will bring you down.
  Obadiah 1:3-4 — parallel pride, eagle-nest imagery and divine abasement: Jeremiah joins the heart's insolence, rocks, a high hill and an eagle-like nest before God's promise to bring the addressee down from there. Obadiah similarly joins heart-pride, rock dwellings and an eagle's nest among stars, also followed by God bringing the addressee down.

Jeremiah 30:11
Idumea will become an untrodden place; everyone passing by her will hiss.
  Isaiah 34:9-10 — parallel enduring desolation in Idumea's judgment: Jeremiah announces Idumea made untrodden, with passersby hissing. Isaiah's Idumea oracle likewise predicts a land deserted for generations, there described through sulfur, pitch and continuing fire rather than the passersby's response.

Jeremiah 30:12
As Sodom, Gomorrah, and her neighboring places were overturned, said the Lord Almighty, no person will settle there, and no human being will live there.
  Genesis 19:24-25 — explicit comparison with Sodom and Gomorrah's overthrow: Jeremiah directly compares Idumea's desolation with the overthrow of Sodom, Gomorrah and neighboring places. Genesis narrates the Lord overthrowing those cities and the surrounding country, supplying the stated comparison's event.

Jeremiah 30:13
Like a lion he will come up from Jordan’s midst into Aitham’s place, for I will quickly drive them away from her. Appoint the young men over her. For who is like Me? Who will resist Me, and what shepherd can stand before Me?
  Jeremiah 27:44 — repeated lion and Jordan imagery with questions about resisting God: Both describe a lion coming from the Jordan into Aitham's place and ask who can resist God or what shepherd can stand before Him. The closely corresponding language appears in the distinct Idumea and Babylon oracles.

Jeremiah 30:14
Therefore hear the Lord’s counsel that He planned against Idumea and His purpose against Teman’s inhabitants: Surely the smallest sheep will be swept together; surely their dwelling will be made untrodden over her.
  Jeremiah 27:45 — parallel divine counsel expressed through threatened sheep and dwelling: The Lord's counsel against Idumea and Teman is followed by small sheep and their dwelling made untrodden. Jeremiah 27 likewise joins counsel against Babylon with lambs and disappearing pasture, retaining different places and exact consequences.

Jeremiah 30:15
For at the sound of their fall the earth shook, and your cry was heard at the sea.
  Jeremiah 27:46 — parallel earth shaking and a far-reaching cry at a fall: The Idumea oracle describes earth shaking at the fall and a cry heard at the sea. The Babylon oracle similarly joins the earth's shaking at capture with a cry heard among nations.

Jeremiah 30:16
Like an eagle he will look and stretch his wings over her fortifications. That day Idumea’s strong men’s hearts will be like the heart of a woman in labor.
  Deuteronomy 28:49-52 — parallel eagle-like assault upon fortifications: Jeremiah depicts an eagle stretching wings over Idumea's fortifications. Deuteronomy likewise compares an approaching nation with an eagle and announces the collapse of trusted fortified walls, while addressing Israel rather than Idumea.

Jeremiah 30:17
For Ammon’s sons, thus said the Lord: Are there no sons in Israel, or have they no one to inherit? Why did Melchom take Gad, and will their people live in their cities?
  Joshua 13:24-28 — territorial background to Gad's challenged inheritance: Jeremiah asks why Melchom took Gad and whether their people will live in their cities. Joshua explicitly records an inheritance for Gad, including territory near Ammon and Rabbah, supplying background to the oracle's challenge about the rightful heirs.

Jeremiah 30:18
Therefore days are coming, says the Lord, when I will make the din of wars heard against Rabbath. They will become an untrodden place and a destruction, her altars will be burned with fire, and Israel will receive his rule.
  Amos 1:13-15 — parallel war and fire against Ammon's Rabbah: Jeremiah announces war against Rabbath and fire upon her altars in the Ammon oracle. Amos likewise announces fire upon Rabbah's walls amid war-shouting and the captivity of her leadership; the burned structures are described differently.

Jeremiah 30:19
Shout aloud, Heshbon, for Gai perished! Cry out, daughters of Rabbath; put on sackcloth, be seized with distress, and beat yourselves over Melchom, for he will go into deportation, his priests and rulers together.
  Amos 1:15 — parallel deportation with priests and rulers in Ammon's judgment: Jeremiah says Melchom will go into deportation with priests and rulers. Amos's Ammon oracle likewise sends kings, priests and rulers into captivity together, preserving its different named subjects rather than equating Melchom with the kings.

Jeremiah 30:20
Why do you exult in the plains of the Anakim, daughter of dishonor, trusting in her treasures and saying, Who will come against me?
  Zephaniah 1:18 — contrast to confidence that treasures provide security: The Ammon oracle questions the dishonored daughter's confidence in treasure and her challenge about who could come against her. Zephaniah explicitly denies that silver and gold can rescue people in the Lord's wrath, countering that kind of confidence.

Jeremiah 30:21
I am bringing fear upon you, said the Lord, from all your surroundings. You will be scattered, each straight ahead, and there will be no one gathering.
  Isaiah 13:14 — parallel dispersal without anyone gathering: Jeremiah announces surrounding fear and people scattered with no gatherer. Isaiah likewise depicts fugitives and a wandering sheep without anyone gathering, with each turning toward his own people and land.

Jeremiah 30:23
For Kedar, queen of the courtyard, whom Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon struck. Thus said the Lord: Rise and go up against Kedar, and fill the sons of Kedem!
  Isaiah 21:16-17 — parallel prophetic judgment specifically naming Kedar: Jeremiah introduces the attack upon Kedar associated with Babylon's king. Isaiah likewise announces the failure of Kedar's glory and few remaining arrows of its strong sons, giving another particular judgment upon the named people without identifying the campaigns.

Jeremiah 30:24
They will take their tents and sheep, their garments, all their equipment, and camels for themselves. Call destruction against them on every side!
  1 Chronicles 5:20-21 — parallel taking of tents, sheep and camels in war: Jeremiah predicts the taking of tents, sheep, equipment and camels from the addressed people. Chronicles similarly records tents handed over and camels and sheep captured from the Hagaraeans and their allies, in a different battle.

Jeremiah 30:25
Flee far away; go deep to dwell, you who stay in the courtyard, for Babylon’s king planned counsel against you and devised a purpose against you.
  Jeremiah 30:2 — same-chapter repeated command to go deep to dwell: Before Babylon's planned attack, the courtyard's inhabitants must flee far and go deep to dwell. The earlier Dedan warning uses the same unusual dwelling instruction amid visitation, without making the two locations identical.

Jeremiah 30:26
Rise and go up against a stable nation living in refreshment, with neither doors nor pins nor bars; they lodge alone.
  Ezekiel 38:10-12 — parallel attack upon a settled people without defensive barriers: Jeremiah describes a stable nation living in refreshment with no doors, pins or bars. Ezekiel likewise records a plan to attack peaceful inhabitants of a land without walls, bars or doors, with plunder as the purpose.

Jeremiah 30:27
Their camels will become plunder and their numerous livestock destruction. I will winnow them to every wind, those shorn before their faces; from every side of them I will bring their rout, said the Lord.
  Jeremiah 25:16 — parallel scattering to every wind: Jeremiah 30 threatens to winnow the addressed people to every wind alongside the plundering of their animals. Jeremiah 25 similarly scatters Elam among winds from heaven's four ends, giving a particular dispersion image applied to another people.

Jeremiah 30:28
The courtyard will become an abode for ostriches, untrodden for ever. No person will settle there; no human being will live there.
  Jeremiah 28:43 — repeated exclusion of human habitation from devastated places: The courtyard becomes perpetually untrodden, without people settling or living there. Jeremiah 28 similarly describes Babylon's cities as waterless and untrodden with no human dwelling; the ostrich detail belongs to Jeremiah 30.

Jeremiah 30:30
Damascus grew feeble and turned to flight; trembling seized her.
  Isaiah 17:1-3 — parallel collapse announced for Damascus: Jeremiah depicts Damascus growing feeble and fleeing in terror. Isaiah likewise explicitly announces Damascus's removal as a city and the loss of its kingdom, a particular counterpart to the city's failing strength.

Jeremiah 30:32
Therefore young men will fall in your squares, and all your warriors will fall, says the Lord.
  Jeremiah 27:30 — repeated fall of young men in squares and warriors: The Damascus oracle announces young men falling in its squares and all its warriors falling. Jeremiah 27 uses the same paired announcement against Babylon, while the cities remain distinct.

Jeremiah 30:33
I will kindle fire in Damascus’s wall, and it will devour the quarters of Ader’s son.
  Amos 1:4-5 — parallel fire against Damascus and the son of Ader: Jeremiah announces fire in Damascus's wall consuming the quarters of Ader's son. Amos likewise sends fire against Hazael's house to consume the foundations of the son of Ader and shatters Damascus's bars.

Jeremiah 31:1
For Moab, thus said the Lord: Woe to Nebo, for she perished! Kiriathaim was taken; Amath was disgraced and defeated.
  Isaiah 15:1-2 — parallel Moab oracle naming Nebo or Nabau in calamity: Jeremiah opens Moab's oracle with Nebo's ruin among other cities. Isaiah likewise opens with Moab's ruin and then calls for wailing over Nabau; the correspondence concerns the Moab oracle and that closely corresponding place-name, not every city Jeremiah lists.

Jeremiah 31:2
There is no longer healing for Moab, a boast in Heshbon. They devised evils against her: We cut her off from a nation, and she will certainly cease. A sword will walk behind you.
  Jeremiah 28:9 — parallel national wound that cannot be healed: Jeremiah 31 says there is no healing for Moab, before announcing evil against her. Jeremiah 28 likewise says Babylon was treated but not healed, applying the unhealed-nation image to another land.

Jeremiah 31:3
For a sound of people crying out comes from Horonaim: destruction and great shattering!
  Isaiah 15:5 — parallel Moabite cry of destruction associated with Horonaim or Aroniim: Jeremiah locates a cry of destruction and great shattering at Horonaim. Isaiah's Moab lament similarly locates a cry of destruction and shaking on Aroniim's road; the native place-name forms are preserved in each passage.

Jeremiah 31:4
Moab was shattered; announce it in Zogora.
  Isaiah 15:5 — parallel Moabite cry associated with Zogora or Segor: Jeremiah announces Moab's shattering and commands an announcement in Zogora. Isaiah likewise joins Moab's cry with Segor, within its detailed account of the land's distress; neither wording needs to be replaced with another version's account of children crying.

Jeremiah 31:5
For Alaoth was filled with weeping; he will go up weeping on Horonaim’s road. You heard a cry of shattering:
  Isaiah 15:5 — parallel weeping and cry of shattering on a Moabite road: Jeremiah says Alaoth was filled with weeping, then describes going up weeping on Horonaim's road and a heard cry of shattering. Isaiah likewise joins a weeping ascent, named Louith, with a cry of destruction along Aroniim's road; it does not make Alaoth an explicitly named ascent in Jeremiah.

Jeremiah 31:6
Flee and save your lives, and you will be like a wild donkey in the wilderness!
  Job 39:5-8 — shared wild-donkey image associated with wilderness dwelling: Jeremiah urges fugitives to save their lives and says they will be like a wild donkey in the wilderness. Job explicitly describes the free wild donkey whose dwelling God made in the wilderness, supplying the animal-and-habitat comparison without making Job's animal a fugitive nation.

Jeremiah 31:7
Because you trusted in your fortifications, you too will be seized. Chemosh will go into deportation, his priests and rulers together.
  Jeremiah 30:19 — parallel deportation with priests and rulers: Jeremiah 31 sends Chemosh into deportation with priests and rulers. Jeremiah 30 similarly sends Melchom with priests and rulers, a close formulation applied to the distinct Moab and Ammon oracles.

Jeremiah 31:8
Destruction will come upon every city; no city will be saved. The valley will perish and the plain be destroyed, as the Lord said.
  Jeremiah 31:24 — same-chapter comprehensive judgment upon Moab's cities: Jeremiah 31:8 says destruction comes to every city and no city escapes. The concluding city-list unit extends judgment to all Moab's cities far and near, making explicit the same comprehensive geographic scope.

Jeremiah 31:10
Accursed is one who does the Lord’s works carelessly, removing his sword from blood.
  Judges 5:23 — parallel curse for failure to participate in the Lord's warfare: Jeremiah curses one who does the Lord's works negligently and withholds his sword from blood. Deborah and Barak's song likewise curses Meroz because its inhabitants did not come to the Lord's help, then calls the Lord their helper, mighty among warriors; both concern their particular warfare settings.

Jeremiah 31:11
Moab rested from childhood and trusted in his glory. He did not pour from vessel to vessel or go into deportation. Therefore his taste remained in him and his scent did not fade.
  Jeremiah 31:12 — same-chapter contrast between undisturbed vessels and coming tilters: Moab rested from childhood and did not pour from vessel to vessel; his taste remained and scent did not fade. The next unit answers that condition by promising tilters who will tilt him and thin his vessels; its final clause concerns horns rather than shattered jars.

Jeremiah 31:12
Therefore days are coming, says the Lord, when I will send him tilters who will tip him, thin out his vessels, and cut up his horns.
  Jeremiah 31:11 — same-chapter vessel judgment answering Moab's prior ease: The promised tilters and treatment of vessels answer the preceding description of Moab resting and not pouring from vessel to vessel. The connection is the text's explicit vessel imagery, not an imported account of jars being smashed.

Jeremiah 31:13
Moab will be ashamed of Chemosh, as Israel’s house was ashamed of Bethel, their hope, having trusted in them.
  1 Kings 12:28-30 — historical background to Israel's confidence in Bethel: Jeremiah compares Moab's shame concerning Chemosh with Israel's shame over Bethel, their hope. Kings records Jeroboam's two golden heifers, one placed in Bethel, and calls the resulting practice sin, supplying the named sanctuary's background.

Jeremiah 31:14
How will you say, We are strong, and a man strong for warfare?
  Jeremiah 9:22-23 — related warning against boasting in strength: Jeremiah 31 challenges the claim to be strong and mighty in warfare. Jeremiah 9 explicitly forbids the strong person to boast in strength and directs boasting toward understanding the Lord, addressing the same claimed basis of confidence.

Jeremiah 31:15
Moab perished, his city, and his choice young men went down to slaughter.
  Jeremiah 27:30 — parallel destruction of a nation's young men: Jeremiah 31 announces Moab's destruction and its chosen young men going to slaughter. Jeremiah 27 likewise announces Babylon's young men falling in its squares and all its warriors being thrown down; the nations remain distinct.

Jeremiah 31:16
Moab’s day is near to coming, and his calamity is very swift.
  Zephaniah 1:14 — parallel insistence that the day of calamity is near and swift: Jeremiah says Moab's day is near and its evil very swift. Zephaniah likewise describes the Lord's great day as near and coming very swiftly, a close temporal warning without identifying every event in the two oracles.

Jeremiah 31:17
Move in lament for him, all who surround him, all who know his name. Say, How the renowned staff is shattered, the rod of grandeur!
  Ezekiel 19:11-14 — parallel shattered staff or destroyed rod in a national lament: Jeremiah calls observers to lament Moab's shattered renowned staff, the rod of grandeur. Ezekiel's lament about Israel's ruler likewise describes a rod of strength and its destruction, ending with no such rod left; its vine-and-fire account differs from Moab's wording.

Jeremiah 31:18
Come down from glory and sit in dampness, resident Dibon. Be crushed, for Moab perished; he came up against you, ruining your fortification.
  Isaiah 47:1 — parallel command for a city's honored figure to descend and sit: Jeremiah commands resident Dibon to come down from glory and sit in dampness. Isaiah similarly commands Babylon's virgin daughter to come down and sit on the ground, contrasting former honor with abasement while preserving dampness versus ground.

Jeremiah 31:19
Stand on the road and look, you who live in Aroer. Ask the one fleeing and the one being saved, and say, What happened?
  1 Samuel 4:12-17 — parallel roadside questioning of a fugitive about a disaster: Jeremiah tells Aroer's inhabitants to stand on the road and ask the fugitive what happened. Samuel narrates Eli sitting by the road and asking the escaped battle messenger what happened, providing a concrete scene of receiving disaster news rather than the same event.

Jeremiah 31:20
Moab was ashamed, for he was shattered. Wail and cry out; announce at Arnon that Moab perished.
  Isaiah 15:1-4 — parallel Moabite ruin answered by public lament: Jeremiah announces Moab's shame and shattering and commands lament and an announcement of its ruin. Isaiah likewise describes Moab's overnight ruin and people mourning in public places, with named cities raising their voices.

Jeremiah 31:21
Judgment is coming into the land of Misor: upon Chailon, Jahzah, and Mophath,
  Joshua 13:16-18 — territorial background to the Misor, Jahzah and Mophath list: Jeremiah places judgment upon the Misor and names Jahzah and Mophath among its places. Joshua's account of Reuben's inheritance likewise names the Misor, Jahaz and Mephaath, supplying earlier territorial context while retaining the different native spellings.

Jeremiah 31:22
upon Dibon, Nebo, and the house of Deblathaim,
  Isaiah 15:2 — parallel Moab oracle naming Dibon and Nebo with corresponding forms: Jeremiah names Dibon and Nebo among places under judgment. Isaiah's Moab oracle likewise names Debon and Nabau in its announcement of ruin and wailing; the connection is the particular city pair, with each native spelling preserved.

Jeremiah 31:23
upon Kiriathaim, the house of Gamol, and the house of Maon,
  Numbers 32:37 — earlier settlement background for Kiriathaim: Jeremiah includes Kiriathaim among the places facing judgment. Numbers records Reuben's sons building Kiriathaim with Heshbon and Elealeh, giving earlier settlement background for this one city rather than for every place in Jeremiah's unit.

Jeremiah 31:24
upon Kerioth, Bosor, and all Moab’s cities, far and near.
  Jeremiah 31:8 — same-chapter judgment extending to every Moabite city: The city list concludes by including all cities of Moab far and near. Jeremiah 31:8 had already said destruction would reach every city and none would escape, supplying the earlier comprehensive claim developed by this list.

Jeremiah 31:25
Moab’s horn was broken and his effort shattered.
  Psalms 74:11 — parallel breaking of horns as loss of wicked strength: Jeremiah announces Moab's horn broken and effort shattered. The psalm likewise promises to break sinners' horns while raising the righteous person's horns, a particular shared image of strength brought down without repeating the statement about effort.

Jeremiah 31:26
Make him drunk, for he magnified himself against the Lord. Moab will strike with his hand and become a laughingstock himself.
  Jeremiah 32:21-27 — parallel Moab included among nations made to drink judgment: Jeremiah 31 commands that Moab be made drunk because it magnified itself against the Lord. Jeremiah 32 names Moab among the nations receiving the cup and commands its recipients to drink and become drunk; Jeremiah 31's hand-striking clause is not replaced with the target's vomiting.

Jeremiah 31:27
Was not Israel a laughingstock to you? Was he found in your thefts, that you made war on him?
  Zephaniah 2:8-10 — parallel charge that Moab reproached God's people: Jeremiah asks whether Israel was an object of laughter to Moab and exposes its response against him. Zephaniah explicitly condemns Moab's reproaches against God's people and its arrogant self-exaltation, supplying the same particular national offense.

Jeremiah 31:28
Moab’s inhabitants left the cities and lived among rocks. They became like doves nesting among rocks at a pit’s mouth.
  Song of Songs 2:14 — shared image of a dove sheltered at a rock: Jeremiah reports that Moab's inhabitants left the cities, lived among rocks and became like doves nesting among rocks at a pit's mouth. The Song likewise addresses a dove in the rock's shelter; its affectionate invitation differs from this report of displaced inhabitants.

Jeremiah 31:29
I heard Moab’s insolence; he acted very insolently—his insolence and arrogance—and his heart was lifted up.
  Isaiah 16:6 — parallel report of Moab's arrogance and insolence: Both passages introduce what has been heard of Moab's insolence and then describe its arrogance. Jeremiah adds the heart being lifted up, while Isaiah adds a statement about divination.

Jeremiah 31:31
Therefore wail over Moab from every side; cry out over the men of Kiradas of drought.
  Isaiah 16:7 — parallel lament extending throughout Moab: Jeremiah commands lament for Moab on every side and calls for mourning at Kiradas of drought. Isaiah likewise says Moab will wail and all will wail in Moab; its further reference to Deseth is preserved rather than equated with Jeremiah's locality.

Jeremiah 31:32
Like the weeping for Jazer, I will weep for you, vine of Sibmah. Your branches crossed the sea and reached Jazer. Destruction fell upon your summer fruit and your grape gatherers.
  Isaiah 16:9 — parallel weeping for the Sibmah or Sebama vine like Jazer or Iazer: The Jeremiah passage compares weeping for Sibmah's vine with Jazer's weeping and describes destruction falling upon summer fruit and grape gatherers. The Isaiah passage similarly describes weeping like Iazer for Sebama's vine and harvest and vintage trampled down, retaining the differing objects and name forms.

Jeremiah 31:33
Joy and gladness were boiled away from Moab’s land, and wine was upon your winepresses. They did not tread in the morning or in the evening; they did not make an aidad shout.
  Isaiah 16:10 — parallel loss of Moab's vineyard gladness and winepress celebration: Jeremiah describes joy boiled away from Moab, wine upon the presses and the absence of treading and the aidad shout. Isaiah likewise removes gladness from vineyards and says people will not tread wine in the vats; it does not supply Jeremiah's morning-and-evening wording.

Jeremiah 31:34
From Heshbon’s cry as far as Elealeh, their cities raised their voice; from Zogor to Horonaim and Aglath-salisia, for even Nebrim’s water will become a burning.
  Isaiah 15:4-6 — parallel named Moabite cries and ruined waters: Jeremiah joins Heshbon and Elealeh's cry with other Moabite places and says Nebrim's water will be burning. Isaiah likewise joins Hesebon and Eleale's voices with the country's distress and Nemrim's water, there described as deserted rather than burning.

Jeremiah 31:35
I will destroy Moab, says the Lord, as he goes up to an altar and burns incense to his gods.
  Isaiah 16:12 — related failure of Moabite worship in calamity: Jeremiah announces that the Lord will destroy Moab as he ascends to an altar and burns incense to his gods. Isaiah likewise describes Moab's labor at altars and prayer to handmade objects as unable to rescue, connecting the named nation's worship with its failure in judgment.

Jeremiah 31:36
Therefore my heart, Moab, will hum like flutes; my heart will hum like a flute for Kiradas’s people. Therefore what he acquired perished from a person.
  Isaiah 16:11 — parallel bodily lament for Moab compared with an instrument: The Jeremiah passage compares the heart's sound for Moab with flutes. The Isaiah passage likewise compares the belly for Moab with a lyre; the bodily location and instrument differ, but both turn internal grief for the same nation into a musical simile.

Jeremiah 31:37
They will shave every head in every place; every beard will be shaved, all hands will beat themselves, and on every waist is sackcloth.
  Isaiah 15:2-3 — parallel baldness and sackcloth in Moabite mourning: Both Moab oracles mention bald heads and sackcloth. Jeremiah also describes shaved beards and hands beating themselves, whereas Isaiah speaks of cut arms; the shared mourning scene does not make those additional actions identical.

Jeremiah 31:38
And on all Moab’s rooftops and in her squares—for I shattered Moab, says the Lord, like a vessel for which there is no use.
  Jeremiah 19:10-11 — parallel nation or city pictured through a broken vessel: The Lord compares His shattering of Moab with a vessel that has no use. The enacted sign in Jeremiah 19 breaks a jar and explains the Lord's breaking of the people and city through that earthenware vessel, there emphasizing that it cannot be healed again.

Jeremiah 31:39
How he has changed! How Moab turned his back! He was ashamed, and Moab became a laughingstock and an object of resentment to all around her.
  Psalms 78:4 — parallel defeated people becoming neighbors' object of derision: Jeremiah describes Moab turning its back in shame and becoming an object of laughter and anger to surrounding people. The psalm likewise laments becoming neighbors' reproach and mockery to those around, speaking there for God's people rather than Moab.

Jeremiah 31:41
Akkarioth was taken and the fortifications seized.
  Jeremiah 31:7 — same-chapter seized fortresses answering misplaced confidence: Jeremiah 31:7 identifies confidence in fortifications before announcing seizure. Jeremiah 31:41 says Akkarioth was taken and fortifications seized, developing that announced collapse of security without requiring a separate historical identification.

Jeremiah 31:42
Moab will perish from being a multitude, for he magnified himself against the Lord.
  Jeremiah 31:26 — same-chapter repeated charge of self-exaltation against the Lord: Both units explain Moab's humiliation through the explicit charge that he magnified himself against the Lord. Jeremiah 31:26 orders drunkenness, while 31:42 says Moab will perish from being a multitude, preserving the differing consequences.

Jeremiah 31:43
Trap, fear, and pit are upon you, resident Moab.
  Isaiah 24:17 — shared triad of trap, fear and pit: Jeremiah places trap, fear and pit upon resident Moab. Isaiah announces the same three dangers in the order fear, pit and trap upon the earth's inhabitants, giving a close verbal counterpart with a different order and broader audience.

Jeremiah 31:44
One who flees from fear will fall into the pit, and one who climbs from the pit will be caught in the trap, for I will bring these things upon Moab in the year of her visitation.
  Isaiah 24:18 — repeated inescapable progression from fear to pit to trap: Both passages say one fleeing fear falls into a pit and one climbing from the pit is caught by a trap. Jeremiah applies the sequence to Moab's year of visitation, while Isaiah continues with opened heavenly windows and shaken foundations.

Jeremiah 32:15
Thus said the Lord, Israel’s God: Take this cup of unmixed wine from My hand, and give it to all the nations to whom I send you to drink,
  Psalms 74:9 — parallel cup of unmixed wine in the Lord's hand: Jeremiah is told to take the Lord's cup of unmixed wine for the nations. The psalm likewise describes a cup in the Lord's hand containing unmixed wine, then speaks of its mixture and the earth's sinners drinking, preserving the supplied wording's combination.

Jeremiah 32:16
and they will drink, vomit, and go mad before the sword I am sending among them.
  Jeremiah 28:7 — parallel wine causing disorder among nations: Jeremiah says the cup's recipients will drink, vomit and become mad before the sword. Jeremiah 28 depicts Babylon as a golden cup whose wine the nations drank and were shaken; the source of the wine and exact effects are worded differently.

Jeremiah 32:17
And I took the cup from the Lord’s hand and gave it to the nations to whom the Lord sent me to drink:
  Jeremiah 32:15 — same-chapter execution of the command to distribute the cup: Jeremiah says he took the cup from the Lord and gave it to the nations to which he was sent. This explicitly carries out the earlier instruction to take that cup and make those nations drink it.

Jeremiah 32:18
Jerusalem, Judah’s cities, Judah’s kings, and its rulers, to make them a desolation, an untrodden place, and a hissing;
  Jeremiah 25:9-11 — related desolation and hissing announced against Judah: The cup list names Jerusalem and Judah's cities, kings and rulers, destined for desolation and hissing. The earlier oracle to Judah likewise announces devastation and hissing from the northern force, followed by service among the nations.

Jeremiah 32:19
and Pharaoh king of Egypt, his servants, his nobles, and all his people;
  Jeremiah 26:13-15 — particular Egypt oracle for a named recipient of the cup: The cup list includes Pharaoh, his servants, nobles and people. Jeremiah 26 expressly introduces Babylon's king coming to strike Egypt and describes the defeat that the Lord brings, supplying a particular judgment oracle for this listed nation.

Jeremiah 32:20
and all his mixed population, all the Philistine kings, Ashkelon, Gaza, Ekron, and Ashdod’s remnant;
  Zephaniah 2:4 — parallel judgment naming the four Philistine cities: Jeremiah lists Ashkelon, Gaza, Ekron and Ashdod among recipients of judgment's cup. Zephaniah likewise names Gaza, Ashkelon, Ashdod and Ekron and assigns each its downfall, a particularly close city grouping.

Jeremiah 32:21
and Idumea, the Moabite land, and Ammon’s sons;
  Zephaniah 2:8-10 — particular judgment upon Moab and Ammon within the cup's list: Jeremiah lists Idumea, Moab and Ammon among the cup's recipients. Zephaniah supplies an explicit judgment upon two of these, Moab and Ammon, because of their reproach and arrogance; it does not give the Idumea portion of this unit.

Jeremiah 32:22
and all Tyre’s kings, Sidon’s kings, and the kings across the sea;
  Joel 4:4-8 — particular judgment upon Tyre and Sidon within the cup's list: Jeremiah names Tyre and Sidon's kings among recipients of the cup. Joel likewise directly addresses Tyre and Sidon and announces recompense for their actions against Judah and Jerusalem, giving a particular counterpart for the named coastal cities.

Jeremiah 32:23
and Dedan, Teman, Ros, and everyone shorn about his face;
  Jeremiah 30:1-2 — particular Teman and Dedan warnings within the cup's list: Jeremiah 32 lists Dedan and Teman among those receiving the cup. Jeremiah 30 explicitly describes Teman's lost wisdom and commands Dedan to go deep to dwell amid visitation, supplying particular warnings for those two names without explaining every other listed group.

Jeremiah 32:24
and all the mixed populations lodging in the wilderness;
  Jeremiah 9:24-25 — related judgment lists including wilderness dwellers: The cup list includes mixed populations dwelling in the wilderness. Jeremiah 9's visitation list likewise ends with wilderness dwellers and also names several surrounding peoples found in Jeremiah 32; its face-clipping description is distinct from the mixed-populations wording.

Jeremiah 32:25
and all Elam’s kings and all the Persian kings;
  Jeremiah 25:15-19 — particular Elam oracle within the cup's list: Jeremiah 32 includes Elam's kings among those receiving the cup. Jeremiah 25 announces the breaking of Elam's bow, dispersion and the sending away of its king and nobles, giving a particular counterpart for Elam without extending those details to the separate Persian kings.

Jeremiah 32:26
and all the kings from the east, far and near, each with his brother, and all the kingdoms upon the face of the earth.
  Zephaniah 3:8 — parallel judgment encompassing assembled nations and kings: Jeremiah expands the cup list to kings far and near and kingdoms across the earth. Zephaniah likewise describes judgment for assemblies of nations and kings under God's wrath, with all the earth affected.

Jeremiah 32:27
And say to them: Thus said the Lord Almighty: Drink, get drunk, and vomit; you will fall and certainly not rise before the sword I am sending among you.
  Habakkuk 2:16 — parallel command to drink the Lord's cup in judgment: Jeremiah commands drinking and drunkenness and predicts falling before the coming sword. Habakkuk likewise commands drinking and shaking because the cup of the Lord's right hand has come around, there contrasting dishonor with former glory.

Jeremiah 32:28
And when they are unwilling to receive the cup from your hand to drink, say: Thus said the Lord: You will certainly drink.
  Jeremiah 30:6 — parallel refusal of exemption from drinking judgment: Jeremiah 32 says those refusing the cup certainly must drink. Jeremiah 30 similarly says the addressed one will not be cleared and certainly will drink, preserving the explicit denial of escape from the cup.

Jeremiah 32:29
Because I am beginning to bring harm in the city upon which My name was named, and you will certainly not be cleansed, because I am summoning a sword against those dwelling upon the earth.
  1 Peter 4:17 — analogous argument from judgment beginning with God's own people: Jeremiah argues that judgment beginning with the city bearing God's name does not leave the other nations unpunished. Peter likewise asks what awaits disobedient people if judgment begins with God's house; the city and the Christian community are distinct settings.

Jeremiah 32:30
And prophesy these words against them and say: The Lord will give an oracle from on high; from His holy place He will raise His voice. He will give an oracular word over His place, and they will answer with Aidad, like grape-gatherers. And upon those dwelling upon the earth,
  Joel 4:16 — parallel divine voice from a sacred location within judgment of nations: Jeremiah announces the Lord giving His voice from His holy place on high. Joel likewise describes the Lord crying from Zion and giving His voice from Jerusalem as heaven and earth shake; the stated locations and consequences remain distinct.

Jeremiah 32:31
destruction comes upon a part of the earth, because the Lord has a judgment among the nations. He enters judgment with all flesh, and the ungodly were given to the sword, says the Lord.
  Isaiah 66:16 — parallel judgment of all flesh by the Lord's sword: Jeremiah announces the Lord's judgment against all flesh and ungodly people given to the sword. Isaiah likewise says all flesh is judged by His sword and many are wounded by the Lord, giving a close combination of agent, scope and weapon.

Jeremiah 32:32
Thus said the Lord: Behold, evils come from nation to nation, and a great whirlwind goes out from the earth’s farthest end.
  Isaiah 66:15 — parallel storm imagery accompanying divine wrath: Jeremiah announces calamities passing between nations and a great whirlwind from the earth's extremity. Isaiah compares the Lord's chariots with a storm when He renders vengeance in wrath; the shared storm image does not make the whirlwind and chariots identical.

Jeremiah 32:33
And those wounded by the Lord on the Lord’s day will lie from one part of the earth to another. They will certainly not be buried; they will be dung upon the earth’s face.
  Jeremiah 16:4 — parallel unburied dead upon the earth under judgment: Jeremiah 32 describes those wounded by the Lord lying unburied across the earth. Jeremiah 16 likewise announces deaths without burial, also denying mourning and describing bodies upon the earth's surface exposed to animals; it does not use the source's dung description.

Jeremiah 32:34
Raise a shout, shepherds, and cry out; beat yourselves in mourning, rams of the sheep, because your days for slaughter have been fulfilled, and you will fall like choice rams.
  Jeremiah 28:40 — parallel slaughter pictured through selected sheep or rams: Jeremiah addresses shepherds and rams, announces the fulfillment of their slaughter days and predicts their falling like choice rams. Jeremiah 28 likewise describes bringing the condemned down like lambs to slaughter and like rams with young goats, applying the flock image to another judgment scene.

Jeremiah 32:35
And flight will perish from the shepherds, and salvation from the rams of the sheep.
  Amos 2:14-15 — parallel denial of flight and survival: Jeremiah denies flight to shepherds and salvation to the rams of the sheep. Amos likewise says flight will perish and the swift, armed and mounted will not escape, giving a parallel collapse of presumed means of survival with different addressed figures.

Jeremiah 32:36
A sound of shepherds crying and of sheep and rams shouting, because the Lord destroyed their grazing places,
  Zechariah 11:3 — parallel sound of shepherds lamenting a devastating loss: Jeremiah announces a sound of shepherds crying because the Lord destroyed their grazing places. Zechariah likewise announces shepherds lamenting because their greatness suffered; the target names their lost greatness rather than repeating the grazing-place clause.

Jeremiah 32:37
and the remnants of peace will cease before the anger of My fury.
  Jeremiah 12:12 — parallel removal of peace under the Lord's judgment: Jeremiah says the remnants of peace cease before the Lord's fierce wrath. Jeremiah 12 likewise denies peace to any flesh when the Lord's sword devours from one end of the earth to the other, placing lost peace within sweeping judgment.

Jeremiah 32:38
Like a lion He abandoned His lodging, because their land became an untrodden place before the great sword.
  Hosea 5:14-15 — parallel divine lion imagery and departure: Jeremiah describes the Lord leaving His lodging like a lion as the land becomes untrodden. Hosea likewise likens Him to a lion that seizes and goes away, then speaks of returning to His place; the differing directions do not identify Jeremiah's lodging with a particular building.

Jeremiah 33:1
At the beginning of the reign of King Jehoiakim son of Josiah, this word came from the Lord:
  2 Kings 23:34-36 — historical setting of Jehoiakim's reign: Jeremiah dates this oracle to the beginning of Jehoiakim son of Josiah's reign. Kings describes Pharaoh installing Josiah's son Eliakim, renaming him Joakim, and gives his reign's length. The father and accession setting connect the accounts while their name forms remain unchanged.

Jeremiah 33:2
Thus said the Lord: Stand in the courtyard of the Lord’s house and deliver an oracle to all the Judeans and all who come to bow down in the Lord’s house—all the words I commanded you to deliver to them. Do not take away a word.
  Deuteronomy 4:2 — preserving the commanded word: Jeremiah must deliver every commanded word without taking one away. Deuteronomy likewise forbids taking anything from the commanded word. The shared restriction concerns faithful transmission and observance of divine instruction, with Jeremiah applying it to his public oracle.

Jeremiah 33:3
Perhaps they will hear and turn, each from his evil way, and I will cease from the evils I plan to do to them because of their evil practices.
  Jeremiah 18:7-8 — repentance and the withdrawal of threatened calamity: The possibility that listeners turn from evil explains why the threatened calamity might cease. Jeremiah 18 states the same conditional pattern for a nation that turns from its evils: the Lord changes His announced intention to harm it.

Jeremiah 33:4
And say: Thus said the Lord: If you do not hear Me, walking in My ordinances that I set before your face,
  Jeremiah 7:23 — hearing God and walking in His commands: The temple warning makes hearing the Lord and walking in His ordinances decisive. Jeremiah 7 similarly joins hearing His voice with walking in all His commanded ways. Both measure the people's response by obedience rather than their presence at the temple.

Jeremiah 33:5
listening to the words of My servants the prophets whom I send to you from early morning, and have sent—though you did not listen to Me—
  Jeremiah 7:25-26 — rejected prophets sent persistently: Jeremiah recalls the Lord sending His servants the prophets from early morning without being heard. Jeremiah 7 likewise describes prophets sent by day and at dawn, followed by the people's refusal to hear. The repeated sending and refusal closely correspond.

Jeremiah 33:6
then I will make this house like Shiloh and make the city a curse to all the nations of all the earth.
  Jeremiah 7:12-15 — Shiloh as a precedent for temple judgment: The threatened fate of the house is explicitly compared with Shiloh. Jeremiah 7 explains that precedent by directing hearers to what happened at Shiloh and announcing the same treatment for the house bearing the Lord's name.

Jeremiah 33:8
And when Jeremiah stopped speaking everything the Lord commanded him to speak to all the people, the priests, false prophets, and all the people seized him, saying: You must die!
  Acts 6:12-14 — narrative parallel of seizure amid temple accusations: Jeremiah is seized after delivering the temple oracle and threatened with death. Stephen is also seized, with witnesses accusing him of speaking against the holy place and its destruction. The accounts share seizure amid temple controversy; Acts explicitly calls Stephen's witnesses false.

Jeremiah 33:9
Because you prophesied in the Lord’s name, saying, This house will be like Shiloh and this city will be emptied of inhabitants. And all the people assembled against Jeremiah in the Lord’s house.
  Jeremiah 7:12-14 — the disputed Shiloh warning repeated: The accusation against Jeremiah repeats his warning that the house will become like Shiloh. Jeremiah 7 contains the corresponding warning and its stated reason in the people's deeds and refusal to hear. It supplies the content contested by Jeremiah's opponents.

Jeremiah 33:11
And the priests and false prophets said to the rulers and all the people: A sentence of death for this man, because he prophesied against this city, as you heard with your own ears!
  Matthew 26:65-66 — judicial parallel of a religious death demand: The priests and false prophets demand a death sentence because of Jeremiah's prophecy against the city. Matthew records a high priest's charge of blasphemy and the answer that Jesus is liable to death. The parallel concerns religious accusation and a death demand, with different charges.

Jeremiah 33:12
And Jeremiah said to the rulers and all the people: The Lord sent me to prophesy against this house and this city all these words you heard.
  Jeremiah 1:7-9 — prophetic commission invoked in defense: Jeremiah answers the accusation by saying the Lord sent him to speak these words. His commissioning account likewise joins being sent with speaking everything commanded and receiving the Lord's words in his mouth. His defense appeals to that assigned responsibility.

Jeremiah 33:13
And now make your ways and works better and hear the Lord’s voice, and the Lord will cease from the evils He spoke against you.
  Jeremiah 18:11 — reform of ways and practices: The appeal to improve ways and works repeats the instruction to Judah in Jeremiah 18 to turn from evil and improve practices. Both place a concrete call to reform within a warning of calamity, rather than treating the warning as unrelated to the people's response.

Jeremiah 33:14
And behold, I am in your hands. Do to me what is advantageous and better for you.
  Jeremiah 45:5 — Jeremiah placed in others' power: Jeremiah declares that he is in his hearers' hands and leaves his treatment to them. In Jeremiah 45 the king declares him in the officials' hands because he cannot oppose them. The shared expression marks the prophet's vulnerability, although the speakers and occasions differ.

Jeremiah 33:15
But know for certain that if you kill me, you place innocent blood upon yourselves, this city, and its inhabitants, because the Lord truly sent me to you to speak all these words in your ears.
  Matthew 27:24-25 — responsibility for a condemned man's blood: Jeremiah warns that killing him would place innocent blood on his hearers and the city. Matthew's trial scene also explicitly contests responsibility for blood: Pilate disclaims it and the crowd accepts it. The comparison concerns the narrated responsibility, not any extension of guilt beyond those scenes.

Jeremiah 33:16
And the rulers and all the people said to the priests and false prophets: There is no sentence of death for this man, because he spoke to us in the name of the Lord our God.
  Acts 23:29 — finding no charge deserving death: The rulers and people reject a death sentence for Jeremiah because he spoke in God's name. The official report concerning Paul likewise finds no charge deserving death or imprisonment. Both counter a lethal accusation with a stated finding, while giving different reasons.

Jeremiah 33:18
Micah the Morathite lived in the days of Hezekiah king of Judah and said to all Judah’s people: Thus said the Lord: Zion will be plowed as a field, Jerusalem will become an untrodden place, and the house’s mountain a grove of woodland.
  Micah 3:12 — explicit quotation of Micah's judgment oracle: Jeremiah's elders name Micah and repeat the judgment of Zion plowed as a field and the temple mountain becoming woodland. Micah contains that oracle. The supplied Jerusalem descriptions differ: Jeremiah says an untrodden place, while Micah says a fruit watcher's hut.

Jeremiah 33:19
Did Hezekiah and all Judah kill him? Was it not that they feared the Lord and entreated the Lord’s face, and the Lord ceased from the evils He spoke against them? And we have done great evils against our own lives!
  Jonah 3:10 — judgment relented after a changed response: The elders recall fearing and entreating the Lord, followed by His ceasing from threatened evils in Hezekiah's time. Jonah narrates God seeing Nineveh turn from evil and withholding the announced calamity. These are distinct events sharing a responsive change in the announced judgment.

Jeremiah 33:21
And King Jehoiakim and all the rulers heard all his words and sought to kill him. Uriah heard and entered Egypt.
  1 Kings 19:1-3 — prophet fleeing a royal death threat: Uriah hears that the king and rulers seek his death and enters Egypt. Elijah also leaves to preserve his life after a royal death threat, reaching Beer-sheba. Both narratives connect a prophet's flight with royal hostility, without identifying the destinations or outcomes.

Jeremiah 33:23
and they brought him out from there and brought him to the king. He struck him with the sword and threw him into the tomb of the sons of his people.
  2 Chronicles 24:20-22 — royal killing of a prophetic speaker: Jehoiakim has Uriah brought back and kills him with the sword. Chronicles describes Azarias son of Jehoiada speaking by God's Spirit and being stoned at King Joash's command. Both depict royal responsibility for killing a prophetic critic; the names and methods differ.

Jeremiah 33:24
Nevertheless, Ahikam son of Shaphan’s hand was with Jeremiah, so that he was not handed into the people’s hands and they did not kill him.
  2 Kings 22:12-14 — Ahikam's earlier role among royal envoys: Ahikam son of Shaphan protects Jeremiah. Kings names Achikam son of Sapphan among the men commissioned to inquire of the Lord and sent to the prophetess Olda. The corresponding personal and paternal names connect his protective role with earlier royal service, preserving the supplied spellings.

Jeremiah 34:2
Thus said the Lord: Make bonds and neck collars and put them around your neck,
  Jeremiah 35:10-13 — prophetic neck collars contested in the next episode: The Lord commands Jeremiah to put bonds and collars around his neck. The next chapter identifies the collars on Jeremiah's neck when Hananiah breaks them, followed by the announcement of iron replacements. The material sign in the command is the object contested in that episode.

Jeremiah 34:3
and send them to Idumea’s king, Moab’s king, the king of Ammon’s sons, Tyre’s king, and Sidon’s king by their messengers coming to meet them in Jerusalem, to Zedekiah king of Judah.
  Jeremiah 32:21-22 — the same surrounding nations addressed in judgment: The collar message is sent to the rulers of Idumea, Moab, Ammon, Tyre and Sidon. These same peoples and rulers appear among those made to drink the judgment cup. The common recipients connect two prophetic signs without making their messages or symbols identical.

Jeremiah 34:5
I made the earth by My great strength and My lofty undertaking, and I will give it to whoever seems right in My eyes.
  Daniel 4:17 — divine authority over earthly rule: Jeremiah grounds the Lord's assignment of the earth in His creative power and will. Daniel's dream declares the Lord of heaven's authority over heaven and earth to do what He wills. Both subordinate earthly power to divine authority; only Jeremiah's unit explicitly mentions creation.

Jeremiah 34:6
I gave the earth to Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon to serve him, and the field’s beasts to work for him.
  Daniel 2:37-38 — Babylonian dominion granted by God: Jeremiah says the earth and its beasts were given to Nebuchadnezzar for service. Daniel tells the Babylonian king that heaven gave him rule and handed humans and beasts into his hands. The distinctive combination of royal dominion and animals closely corresponds.

Jeremiah 34:8
And the nation and kingdom, whoever will not put their neck under Babylon’s king’s yoke—I will visit them with sword and famine, said the Lord, until they disappear by his hand.
  Deuteronomy 28:48-51 — foreign domination pictured as a destructive neck collar: Jeremiah warns nations refusing Babylon's yoke of sword, famine and destruction. Deuteronomy describes serving an enemy in deprivation with an iron collar until destruction. The shared image is imposed foreign rule; the immediate command being resisted differs between the passages.

Jeremiah 34:9
And do not listen to your false prophets, those divining for you, those dreaming for you, your omens, or your enchantments, who say: You will certainly not work for Babylon’s king.
  Deuteronomy 18:10-14 — rejecting divination and enchantment: Jeremiah warns against diviners, omens and enchantments supporting the prediction that Babylon need not be served. Deuteronomy prohibits these practices and contrasts the nations' reliance on them with Israel's instruction. The overlap concerns named practices, not a claim that every dream is condemned.

Jeremiah 34:10
Because they prophesy false things to you, to remove you far from your land.
  Lamentations 2:14 — false prophecy associated with displacement: Jeremiah says the false predictions will remove their hearers far from their land. Lamentations links futile prophetic visions with failure to turn back captivity and with expulsions. Both connect misleading prophecy to the people's displacement rather than to their promised security.

Jeremiah 34:11
And the nation that brings its neck under Babylon’s king’s yoke and works for him, I will leave it in its own land, and it works for him and will dwell in it.
  Jeremiah 47:9-10 — serving Babylon while remaining in the land: The promise allows a nation serving Babylon to remain in its land. Gedaliah later urges the remnant to live in the land, work for Babylon's king and gather its produce. His practical counsel follows the same combination of submission and continued residence.

Jeremiah 34:14
and work for Babylon’s king, because they prophesy unjust things to you.
  Jeremiah 47:9-10 — counsel to work for Babylon's king: Jeremiah tells Zedekiah to work for Babylon's king despite unjust predictions. Gedaliah likewise tells the surviving community to work for that king and remain in the land. The political counsel corresponds, while the audiences and narrative settings differ.

Jeremiah 34:15
Because I did not send them, says the Lord, and they prophesy unjustly in My name to destroy you. You will perish, you and your prophets who prophesy false things unjustly to you.
  Jeremiah 36:31-32 — unsent prophets and harm to their hearers: The Lord denies sending the prophets and announces that they and their hearers will perish. The judgment on Shemaiah likewise joins an unsent prophet, trust in unjust things and exclusion from the coming good. Both deny that the claimed divine commission protects the deceivers.

Jeremiah 34:16
I spoke to you, all this people, and the priests, saying: Thus said the Lord: Do not listen to the prophets’ words prophesying to you, Behold, the vessels of the Lord’s house will return from Babylon, because they prophesy unjust things to you. I did not send them.
  Jeremiah 35:3-4 — a condemned return prediction voiced by Hananiah: Jeremiah warns that predictions of the temple vessels' return from Babylon are unjust and unsent. Hananiah then publicly predicts the return of those vessels and deportees. The later speech supplies a concrete instance of the disputed message rather than confirming it.

Jeremiah 34:20
which Babylon’s king did not take when he deported Jeconiah from Jerusalem,
  Jeremiah 24:1 — Jeconiah's deportation as a shared chronological marker: The remaining vessels are identified relative to Babylon's earlier deportation of Jeconiah from Jerusalem. The fig vision is likewise dated after Nebuchadnezzar deported Jeconiah and other Jerusalem residents to Babylon. The same named deportation anchors both passages; the fig vision does not inventory the vessels.

Jeremiah 34:22
they will go into Babylon, says the Lord.
  2 Kings 25:13-17 — temple vessels carried to Babylon: The oracle states that the remaining vessels will go to Babylon. Kings narrates the Chaldeans removing temple bronze and other utensils, explicitly carrying the bronze to Babylon. The narrative provides a corresponding removal of temple property in contrast to the false assurances of its return.

Jeremiah 35:1
And in the fourth year of Zedekiah king of Judah, in the fifth month, Hananiah son of Azzur, the false prophet from Gibeon, said to me in the Lord’s house before the priests and all the people:
  Jeremiah 34:16 — Hananiah introduced within the false-return controversy: The narrative introduces Hananiah as a false prophet speaking before priests and people in the Lord's house. Jeremiah 34 had warned the priests and people against unsent predictions of the vessels' return, the message Hananiah gives in the following units. The audience and disputed claim connect the episodes.

Jeremiah 35:2
Thus said the Lord: I smashed Babylon’s king’s yoke.
  Jeremiah 37:8 — Hananiah's liberation claim contrasted with promised restoration: Hananiah claims that the Babylonian king's yoke has been smashed. Jeremiah 37 also promises a broken yoke and ended foreign service, within a restoration oracle. The shared liberation image does not authenticate Hananiah's timetable or his claim to have been sent.

Jeremiah 35:3
In two full years I will return to this place the vessels of the Lord’s house,
  Jeremiah 34:16 — the specific vessel-return claim previously rejected: Hananiah predicts the temple vessels' return in two full years. Jeremiah's preceding warning explicitly rejects predictions that the vessels will return from Babylon. This connects the concrete public claim with the warning against its purported divine authority.
  Jeremiah 36:10 — contrasting announced periods for return: Hananiah gives a period of two full years for the vessels' return. Jeremiah's letter promises a return for the deportees as Babylon's seventy years approach fulfillment. The sharply different periods help distinguish the competing expectations; the promises concern overlapping but not identical objects.

Jeremiah 35:4
and Jeconiah and Judah’s deportees, because I will smash Babylon’s king’s yoke.
  Jeremiah 24:1 — the named king and deportees in the return claim: Hananiah promises Jeconiah and Judah's deportees will return. The fig vision names Jeconiah among those Nebuchadnezzar deported from Jerusalem to Babylon. This establishes the earlier displacement presupposed by the claim without validating Hananiah's prediction.

Jeremiah 35:6
and Jeremiah said: Truly! May the Lord do so. May He establish your word that you prophesy, returning the vessels of the Lord’s house and all the deportees from Babylon to this place.
  Ezra 1:7-11 — desired return of temple vessels and its later narrative: Jeremiah expresses the wish that the Lord establish a return of vessels and deportees. Ezra narrates Cyrus releasing the vessels that Nebuchadnezzar had taken, and their movement from Babylon to Jerusalem. The later event corresponds to the wished-for return, without confirming Hananiah's two-year claim.

Jeremiah 35:8
The prophets who were before me and before you from of old prophesied over much land and against great kingdoms concerning war.
  Isaiah 13:1-5 — an example of the earlier prophetic war tradition: Jeremiah recalls earlier prophets speaking war against great kingdoms. Isaiah's named vision against Babylon describes gathered nations and the Lord's armed fighters. It supplies a concrete example of such an oracle, rather than making every earlier prophecy a prediction of war.

Jeremiah 35:9
The prophet who prophesied peace—when the word comes, they will know the prophet whom the Lord sent to them in faithfulness.
  Deuteronomy 18:21-22 — fulfilled speech as a test of prophetic authority: Jeremiah says the arrival of a peace prophet's word establishes that the Lord sent him faithfully. Deuteronomy states the complementary test: a word not fulfilled was not spoken by the Lord. Both direct attention to the outcome of the claimed prophecy.

Jeremiah 35:10
And Hananiah took the collars from Jeremiah’s neck before all the people and smashed them.
  Jeremiah 34:2 — the commanded sign physically broken: Hananiah removes and smashes the collars on Jeremiah's neck. Jeremiah 34 records the Lord commanding Jeremiah to make and wear them. The action therefore directly attacks an earlier prophetic sign; the connection does not grant Hananiah's interpretation divine approval.

Jeremiah 35:11
And Hananiah said before all the people: Thus said the Lord: This is how I will smash Babylon’s king’s yoke from all the nations’ necks. And Jeremiah went his way.
  Jeremiah 34:6-8 — Hananiah's claim contradicts the submission oracle: Hananiah interprets his action as breaking Babylon's yoke from all nations. Jeremiah 34 instead announces Babylon's God-given dominion and punishment for refusing its yoke. The two messages conflict over the nations' relation to Babylon, rather than describing the same fulfilled event.

Jeremiah 35:13
Go and tell Hananiah: Thus said the Lord: You smashed wooden collars, and I will make iron collars in their place.
  Deuteronomy 28:48 — an iron collar as a stronger image of subjection: The response to broken wooden collars announces iron replacements. Deuteronomy depicts enemy service under an iron collar until destruction. The shared material image conveys severe subjection; Deuteronomy does not narrate Hananiah's change from wood to iron.

Jeremiah 35:14
Because thus said the Lord: I placed an iron yoke upon all the nations’ necks to work for Babylon’s king.
  Jeremiah 34:6-8 — Babylon's imposed rule reaffirmed: The iron yoke is explicitly imposed on the nations to serve Babylon's king. Jeremiah 34 had already declared his dominion and warned nations refusing his yoke. The response to Hananiah reinforces that earlier message rather than withdrawing it.

Jeremiah 35:15
And Jeremiah said to Hananiah: The Lord has not sent you, and you made this people trust in injustice.
  Jeremiah 36:31 — unsent prophets inducing trust in injustice: Jeremiah tells Hananiah the Lord did not send him and that he made the people trust injustice. The announcement against Shemaiah uses the same combination of an unsent speaker and trust in unjust things. The parallel concerns the accusation, not the identity of the prophets.

Jeremiah 35:16
Therefore thus said the Lord: Behold, I am sending you away from the earth’s face. This year you will die.
  Deuteronomy 18:20 — death announced for an unauthorized prophet: The Lord announces that Hananiah will die that year, after Jeremiah denies his divine commission. Deuteronomy states that a prophet speaking an uncommanded word in God's name shall die. The narrative judgment corresponds to the legal warning, with a specific time added for Hananiah.

Jeremiah 35:17
And he died in the seventh month.
  Jeremiah 35:16 — same-chapter prediction and narrated fulfillment: Hananiah's death in the seventh month follows the announcement that he will die that year. The narrative presents the death as the outcome corresponding to Jeremiah's specific prediction. This is a same-chapter prediction-and-fulfillment connection, not an independent chronological reconstruction.

Jeremiah 36:1
And these are the words of the document Jeremiah sent from Jerusalem to the elders of the deportation, the priests, and the false prophets—a letter to Babylon for the deportees and all the people—
  Jeremiah 36:28 — same-chapter citation of the letter to Babylon: The chapter introduces Jeremiah's written message sent to the Babylonian deportees.
