Later in the same chapter its instruction to build houses and plant gardens is explicitly recalled as what he sent to Babylon. The later citation identifies content of the introduced document.

Jeremiah 36:2
after King Jeconiah, the queen, the eunuchs, and every free person, prisoner, and craftsman left Jerusalem,
  Jeremiah 24:1 — Jeconiah and the deported Jerusalem community: The letter is situated after Jeconiah and groups of Jerusalem residents departed. The fig vision likewise follows Jeconiah's deportation to Babylon and names rulers and craftsmen among those taken. The shared king, origin and deported community connect the settings without harmonizing every listed category.

Jeremiah 36:4
Thus said the Lord, Israel’s God, concerning the deportees whom I deported from Jerusalem:
  Jeremiah 24:5 — the deportees addressed as people sent by the Lord: The letter addresses those the Lord says He deported from Jerusalem. The good-fig interpretation similarly identifies Judah's deportees as those He sent to the Chaldeans' land for good. Both frame the displaced community as the recipient of His continuing address and intention.

Jeremiah 36:5
Build houses and dwell in them; plant gardens and eat their fruits,
  Isaiah 65:21-23 — houses inhabited and planted produce enjoyed: Jeremiah instructs deportees to build houses, dwell in them and eat what they plant. Isaiah promises people will inhabit their houses and eat their own planted produce. The activities closely correspond, although Jeremiah commands settled life in exile and Isaiah describes a promised condition.

Jeremiah 36:6
and take wives and beget sons and daughters. Take wives for your sons and give your daughters to husbands. Multiply and do not become fewer,
  Exodus 1:7 — Israel increasing while living outside its homeland: The deportees are told to form families, multiply and not become fewer. Exodus narrates Israel's sons multiplying and becoming numerous in Egypt. Both associate Israel's life in a foreign land with numerical increase; one is counsel and the other a narrative report.

Jeremiah 36:7
and seek the peace of the land where I deported you, and pray to the Lord for them, because in its peace you will have peace.
  1 Timothy 2:1-2 — prayer for society's peace and the worshippers' welfare: Jeremiah joins prayer for the receiving land with the deportees' own peace within it. First Timothy urges prayers for rulers and all people so that believers may live quietly. Both connect intercession for the wider society with the community's peaceful life.

Jeremiah 36:8
Because thus said the Lord: Do not let the false prophets among you persuade you, and do not let your diviners persuade you. Do not listen to your dreams that you dream,
  Jeremiah 34:9 — refusing deceptive prophetic and divinatory assurances: The letter warns deportees against false prophets, diviners and dreams. Jeremiah 34 warns against the same kinds of speakers and practices when they promise freedom from Babylonian service. The repeated warning addresses deceptive guidance about the exile's course.

Jeremiah 36:9
because they prophesy unjust things to you in My name, and I did not send them.
  Jeremiah 23:21 — speaking in God's name without being sent: The letter denies that the Lord sent those prophesying unjust things in His name. Jeremiah 23 likewise distinguishes the prophets' running and speaking from any sending or speech by the Lord. Claimed prophetic activity is not treated as proof of commission.

Jeremiah 36:10
Because thus said the Lord: When seventy years are about to be fulfilled for Babylon, I will visit you and establish My words for you, to return your people to this place.
  2 Chronicles 36:20-23 — seventy years and the announced return: Jeremiah connects Babylon's seventy years with divine visitation and return. Chronicles explicitly links the exile's seventy years and Cyrus's proclamation with fulfillment of the Lord's word through Jeremiah. This is an explicit narrative reception of the promised period and restoration.
  Daniel 9:2 — explicit consultation of Jeremiah's seventy years: Daniel names Jeremiah while considering the books and a seventy-year term for Jerusalem's reproach. Jeremiah's letter also names a seventy-year period associated with Babylon and return. The explicit attribution links Daniel's reading with Jeremiah's temporal promise, without claiming he consulted this letter alone.

Jeremiah 36:11
And I will plan for you a plan of peace and not evils, to give you these things.
  Jeremiah 24:5-7 — good intentions for the deportees: The letter announces a plan of peace rather than evils. The good-fig interpretation likewise promises the deportees good, restoration to their land and a heart to know the Lord. Both address the exiled community with a favorable divine intention beyond its displacement.

Jeremiah 36:12
And pray to Me, and I will listen to you.
  Isaiah 65:24 — prayer answered by divine listening: The Lord invites prayer and promises to listen. Isaiah similarly promises listening before the people cry out and a response while they speak. The connection is the stated responsiveness to prayer; Isaiah intensifies its immediacy.

Jeremiah 36:13
And seek Me earnestly, and you will find Me, because you will seek Me with your whole heart,
  Deuteronomy 4:29-31 — wholehearted seeking after displacement: Jeremiah promises finding the Lord when sought with the whole heart. Deuteronomy likewise promises finding Him through wholehearted seeking in distress, followed by return and covenant mercy. The shared wording fits the exile setting of Jeremiah's letter.

Jeremiah 36:21
Thus said the Lord concerning Ahab and Zedekiah: Behold, I am giving them into Babylon’s king’s hands, and he will strike them before your eyes.
  Deuteronomy 18:20 — judgment upon speakers of uncommanded oracles: Ahab and Zedekiah are handed to Babylon's king to be struck, and the following units explain their unauthorized oracles. Deuteronomy announces death for a prophet speaking in God's name without His command. The connection concerns this stated offense and judgment, not other kings bearing the same names.

Jeremiah 36:22
And all Judah’s deportees in Babylon will take a curse from them, saying: May the Lord make you as He made Zedekiah and Ahab, whom Babylon’s king fried in fire,
  Jeremiah 24:9 — judged people becoming a curse expression: Judah's deportees will use the fate of Ahab and Zedekiah in a curse formula. Jeremiah's bad-fig oracle likewise announces people becoming a curse and reproach where they are scattered. The named pair provides a specific narrated form of that public disgrace.

Jeremiah 36:23
because of the lawlessness they committed in Israel: they committed adultery with their fellow citizens’ wives and delivered an oracle in My name that I did not command them. I am a witness, says the Lord.
  Jeremiah 23:14 — adultery and lying within prophetic wrongdoing: The charge against Ahab and Zedekiah combines adultery with unauthorized speech in the Lord's name. Jeremiah 23 also joins adultery and walking in lies among Jerusalem's prophets. The shared offenses are explicit, while the locations and named offenders differ.

Jeremiah 36:25
I did not send you in My name. And to Zephaniah son of Maaseiah the priest say:
  Jeremiah 21:1 — Zephaniah son of Maaseiah identified as priest: This address names Zephaniah son of Maaseiah the priest. Jeremiah 21 names the same priest as one of Zedekiah's messengers to Jeremiah. The matching personal name, father and office provide the connection without rewriting the unusual wording of the letter passage.

Jeremiah 36:26
The Lord appointed you priest in place of Jehoiada the priest, to become an overseer in the Lord’s house over every person prophesying and every person raging, and you will put him into confinement and into the trap.
  Jeremiah 20:1-2 — priestly confinement of a prophetic speaker: The letter describes priestly oversight placing a person prophesying into confinement and a trap. Jeremiah 20 narrates Pashhur, a temple leader, striking Jeremiah and putting him into a trap. The narrative supplies a concrete parallel to the described coercive practice, without identifying the two priests.

Jeremiah 36:27
And now why did you join in reviling Jeremiah from Anathoth, who prophesied to you?
  Jeremiah 1:1 — Jeremiah's association with Anathoth: The question names Jeremiah from Anathoth as the prophet being reviled. The book's opening identifies Jeremiah son of Hilkiah among priests living in Anathoth in Benjamin. That personal and local identification supports the link; the question is preserved as joining in reviling him.

Jeremiah 36:28
Was it not for this that he sent to you in Babylon, saying: It is far off. Build houses and dwell in them; plant gardens, and you will eat their fruit?
  Jeremiah 36:5 — same-chapter repetition of the letter's settlement instruction: The later question quotes Jeremiah's instruction to build houses, dwell in them, plant gardens and eat their fruit. Those are the instructions given earlier in the letter. This is a same-chapter literary repetition showing the specific advice recalled by the later question.

Jeremiah 36:31
Send to the deportees, saying: Thus said the Lord concerning Shemaiah the Nelamite: Since Shemaiah prophesied to you, though I did not send him, and made you trust in unjust things,
  Jeremiah 35:15 — Shemaiah and Hananiah charged with the same deception: The Lord denies sending Shemaiah and says he made hearers trust unjust things. Jeremiah's charge against Hananiah likewise denies his commission and names trust in injustice. The wording connects two distinct false prophets, without merging their messages or fates.

Jeremiah 36:32
therefore thus said the Lord: Behold, I will visit Shemaiah and his family, and none of them will be among you to see the good things I will do for you. They will not see.
  Deuteronomy 1:34-35 — exclusion from seeing promised good: Shemaiah and his family will not see the good the Lord will do for the deportees. Deuteronomy likewise excludes the judged men from seeing the good land promised to their fathers. Both use exclusion from a promised good as judgment, with different groups and circumstances.

Jeremiah 37:2
Thus said the Lord, Israel’s God: Write in a book all the words I delivered to you as oracles,
  Isaiah 30:8 — prophetic words preserved in writing: Jeremiah is commanded to write all the delivered oracles in a book. Isaiah likewise receives a command to write on a tablet and in a book for enduring times. Both make a lasting written record part of the prophetic task.

Jeremiah 37:3
because behold, days are coming, says the Lord, and I will return the deportees of My people Israel and Judah, said the Lord. I will return them to the land I gave their fathers, and they will rule over it.
  Ezekiel 37:21-22 — Israel and Judah gathered into their land: Jeremiah promises the return of Israel's and Judah's deportees to the ancestral land. Ezekiel describes gathering Israel from the nations into that land and ending its division into two kingdoms. Both place restoration in a gathered people inhabiting the land again.

Jeremiah 37:5
Thus said the Lord: You will hear a sound of fear—fear, and there is no peace.
  Jeremiah 8:15 — peace absent amid approaching calamity: Jeremiah announces a sound of fear and no peace. The earlier lament reports gathering for peace but finding no good, and looking for healing but finding haste. Both describe the absence of expected security; the target's final word is preserved as haste, not changed to terror.

Jeremiah 37:6
Ask and see whether a male gave birth, and about fear in which they will hold loins and salvation. For I have seen every person with his hands upon his loins; faces turned toward jaundice—
  Isaiah 13:7-8 — fear pictured through childbirth pains and altered faces: Jeremiah portrays people holding their loins and faces turning toward jaundice, while asking whether a male gives birth. Isaiah describes labor pains like a woman giving birth and faces changing toward flame. The shared physical imagery expresses fear, while the facial descriptions differ.

Jeremiah 37:7
so it became. For that day is great, and there is none like it; it is a narrow time for Jacob, and he will be saved from it.
  Daniel 12:1 — unprecedented affliction with a favorable announced outcome: Jeremiah calls Jacob's narrow time a great day without equal and promises he will be saved from it. Daniel also describes an unparalleled day of affliction and an outcome for the people inscribed in the book. Daniel's supplied verb is exalted; the passages are not identified as the same dated event.

Jeremiah 37:8
On that day, said the Lord, I will smash the yoke from their neck and tear apart their bonds, and they will no longer work for foreigners.
  Isaiah 10:27 — the removal of an oppressive yoke: Jeremiah promises that the yoke and bonds will be broken and foreign service cease. Isaiah likewise announces removal and destruction of the yoke from the people's shoulders. Both portray relief from oppression through a removed yoke, with distinct immediate enemy settings.

Jeremiah 37:9
And they will work for the Lord their God, and I will raise up David their king for them.
  Ezekiel 34:23-24 — David raised as ruler with the Lord as God: Jeremiah joins service to the Lord with David their king being raised for them. Ezekiel likewise promises to raise David as shepherd and calls him ruler while the Lord is their God. The corresponding combination of divine allegiance and Davidic rule is explicit.
  Hosea 3:5 — return to the Lord and David their king: Jeremiah announces service to the Lord and a raised Davidic king. Hosea similarly says Israel's sons will return and seek the Lord their God and David their king. Both associate Israel's renewed allegiance with David, without specifying how the royal promise is to be realized.

Jeremiah 37:12
Thus said the Lord: I raised up a fracture; your wound is painful.
  Jeremiah 10:19 — a painful wound as an image of communal crushing: The Lord describes a raised fracture and painful wound. Jeremiah 10 likewise laments crushing and a painful wound that has seized the speaker. The repeated wound language connects the descriptions of suffering; the grammatical speakers and statements about its cause differ.

Jeremiah 37:13
There is no one judging your case; you were treated into pain. There is no benefit for you.
  Jeremiah 8:22 — communal injury without effective healing: Jeremiah says treatment has brought pain and no benefit. The earlier lament asks whether Gilead lacks resin or a physician because healing has not arisen for the people. Both present the community's injury as remaining without effective remedy.

Jeremiah 37:14
All your friends forgot you; they will certainly not inquire. For I struck you with an enemy’s blow, a firm discipline; upon all your injustice your sins multiplied.
  Lamentations 1:2 — abandonment by former friends amid suffering: Jeremiah says all the friends have forgotten and will not inquire after the stricken people. Lamentations describes Jerusalem without comfort among those who love her, with friends becoming enemies. Both add failed human support to the people's affliction.

Jeremiah 37:16
Therefore all who eat you will be eaten; as for all your enemies, they will eat all their flesh. Upon the multitude of your injustices your sins multiplied; they did these things to you. Those plundering you will become plunder, and I will give all who despoil you as spoil.
  Isaiah 49:26 — enemies consuming their own flesh: Jeremiah's reversal includes enemies eating their flesh and plunderers becoming plunder. Isaiah likewise says the afflicters will eat their own flesh and says all flesh will recognize the Lord as rescuer. The unusual flesh-consuming image gives this connection a specific basis beyond a general reversal of fortunes.

Jeremiah 37:17
Because I will bring up your cure and heal you from a painful wound, says the Lord, because you were called Scattered: She is your prey, because no one seeks her.
  Jeremiah 40:6 — promised healing after a painful communal wound: The Lord promises a cure and healing for the painful wound of the one called Scattered. Jeremiah 40 likewise promises the closing of wounds, remedy and healing for the city. The later oracle repeats this image of restoration through healing.

Jeremiah 37:18
Thus said the Lord: Behold, I will return Jacob’s deportees and have mercy on his captivity. A city will be built upon its height, and the sanctuary will sit according to its judgment.
  Ezekiel 36:10-11 — resettlement and rebuilding after devastation: Jeremiah links returned deportees with a city rebuilt on its height and the sanctuary's restoration. Ezekiel promises inhabited cities, rebuilt devastation and settlement as at the beginning. Both connect the people's return with the recovery of their built environment.

Jeremiah 37:19
And singers and the sound of people playing will come out from them. I will increase them, and they will certainly not be diminished.
  Psalms 125:1-3 — joyful voices accompanying returned captivity: Jeremiah promises singers and people playing as the community increases. The psalm describes captivity reversed and mouths filled with joy and exultation. Both portray audible celebration as a response to restored communal life, though the psalm does not state a population increase.

Jeremiah 37:20
And their sons will enter as formerly, and their testimonies will be set upright before Me. I will visit those afflicting them.
  Isaiah 49:25 — children's restoration and judgment concerning their afflicters: Jeremiah promises the sons' return as formerly and visitation upon those afflicting them. Isaiah joins the Lord's judgment of the people's case with rescuing their sons. Both place the children's welfare alongside divine action against their adversity.

Jeremiah 37:21
And stronger ones of his will be over them, and his ruler will come out from him. I will gather them, and they will return to Me. For who is this who gave his heart to return to Me? says the Lord.
  Deuteronomy 17:15 — a ruler drawn from the people themselves: Jeremiah says the ruler will come from the people being restored. Deuteronomy requires a ruler from among the people's brothers and excludes a foreigner. The shared feature is the ruler's origin within the community; Jeremiah's gathering and return language is not changed into a ruler approaching God.

Jeremiah 37:23
Because the Lord’s anger went out furious; swirling anger went out and will come upon the ungodly.
  Jeremiah 23:19 — recurring imagery of swirling divine anger: Jeremiah again describes the Lord's anger going out in a swirling movement upon the ungodly. Jeremiah 23 similarly describes anger emerging in a convulsion and swirling upon them. The distinctive movement and recipients connect the two formulations.

Jeremiah 37:24
The anger of the Lord’s fury will certainly not turn back until He acts and establishes His heart’s undertaking. At the end of the days you will know these things.
  Jeremiah 23:20 — anger continuing until the divine undertaking is accomplished: Both passages say the Lord's fury will not turn back before His heart's undertaking is carried out, followed by understanding at the end of the days. The closely repeated sequence links the judgment declarations without supplying an external timetable.

Jeremiah 38:1
At that time, said the Lord, I will be God to Israel’s family, and they will be My people.
  Jeremiah 24:7 — the reciprocal God-and-people promise: The Lord promises to be God to Israel's family and that they will be His people. The good-fig oracle gives the same reciprocal promise to the returning deportees, together with a heart to know Him. Both describe restoration as a renewed relationship.

Jeremiah 38:3
The Lord appeared to him from afar: I loved you with an eternal love; therefore I drew you into compassion.
  Isaiah 54:8 — enduring divine love expressed in renewed compassion: Jeremiah joins eternal love with being drawn into compassion. Isaiah promises everlasting mercy after a short period of anger. Both emphasize enduring divine favor in restoration; Isaiah explicitly frames it against a preceding withdrawal.

Jeremiah 38:4
Again I will build you, and you will be built, virgin Israel. Again you will take your tambourine and go out with a gathering of people playing.
  Jeremiah 37:18-19 — rebuilding accompanied by renewed celebration: Virgin Israel will be built again and go out with tambourine and people playing. The preceding restoration oracle likewise combines a rebuilt city with singers and the sound of people playing. The paired features of rebuilding and celebration recur together.

Jeremiah 38:5
Again plant vineyards on Samaria’s mountains; plant and praise.
  Amos 9:13-15 — vineyards planted after captivity is reversed: Jeremiah calls for renewed vineyard planting on Samaria's mountains. Amos describes Israel's reversed captivity, planted vineyards and enjoyment of their wine. Both make restored cultivation a concrete feature of the people's return, while identifying different geographical details.

Jeremiah 38:6
Because there is a day of calling by those making their defense in Ephraim’s mountains: Rise and go up to Zion, to the Lord our God.
  Isaiah 2:3 — a communal call to ascend to the Lord at Zion: The call from Ephraim's mountains is to rise and go to Zion, to the Lord. Isaiah similarly presents people saying to one another that they should go up to the Lord's mountain and Jacob's God's house. Jeremiah's callers are associated with Ephraim; Isaiah's are many nations.

Jeremiah 38:7
Because thus said the Lord to Jacob: Rejoice and neigh aloud toward the head of the nations; make it heard and praise. Say: The Lord saved His people, Israel’s remnant!
  Isaiah 44:23 — praise because Jacob has been redeemed: Jeremiah calls for rejoicing and proclamation that the Lord saved Israel's remnant. Isaiah likewise calls heaven, earth and mountains to rejoice because God redeemed Jacob and had mercy on Israel. The announced reason for the praise is the people's divine rescue.

Jeremiah 38:8
Behold, I am bringing them from the north and will gather them from the earth’s farthest end at the Passover festival. You will bear a great crowd, and they will return here.
  Jeremiah 23:8 — the gathering of Israel from the north: Jeremiah promises a great returning crowd from the north and earth's farthest end. The earlier oracle also describes Israel's offspring gathered from the northern land and the countries of dispersion. The supplied Passover detail belongs to this source unit and is not imported into the target.

Jeremiah 38:9
They went out with weeping, and with encouragement I will bring them up, lodging them beside water channels on a straight road where they will certainly not wander, because I became Israel’s father, and Ephraim is My firstborn.
  Exodus 4:22 — Israel described as God's firstborn: Jeremiah explains the guided return by the Lord becoming Israel's father and Ephraim His firstborn. Exodus commands Moses to tell Pharaoh that Israel is the Lord's firstborn son. The shared family designation links the return promise with the earlier address concerning Israel.
  Isaiah 49:8-12 — returnees comforted and guided beside water: Jeremiah describes encouragement, water channels and a straight road for the returning people. Isaiah likewise promises comfort, guidance through water springs and roads for people coming from afar. Both depict divine care for the return journey, while Jeremiah adds the father-and-firstborn explanation.

Jeremiah 38:10
Hear the Lord’s word, nations, and announce it to the farther islands. Say: He who winnowed Israel will gather him and guard him like one tending his flock.
  Ezekiel 34:12-13 — gathering dispersed Israel through shepherd imagery: The one who winnowed Israel will gather and guard him like a shepherd. Ezekiel likewise depicts the Lord seeking scattered sheep, bringing them from the nations and feeding them in their land. The gathering of dispersed people is interpreted through the same shepherding image.

Jeremiah 38:11
Because the Lord ransomed Jacob and rescued him from the hand of those stronger than he.
  Isaiah 49:24-26 — rescue from a stronger captor: Jeremiah says the Lord ransomed Jacob from those stronger than he. Isaiah's questions about taking from a giant and a strong man lead to the Lord's promise to rescue the people's sons. Both connect divine rescue with an otherwise formidable captor, without normalizing Isaiah's unusual questions.

Jeremiah 38:12
They will come and rejoice on Mount Zion; they will come for the Lord’s good things, to a land of grain, wine, fruits, livestock, and sheep. Their life will be like a fruitful tree, and they will hunger no more.
  Joel 2:19 — the restored people satisfied with agricultural abundance: Jeremiah describes the Lord's good things as grain, wine, fruits and livestock, ending the people's hunger. Joel likewise promises grain, wine and oil with which the people will be filled. The named foods and promised satisfaction connect the restoration pictures.

Jeremiah 38:13
Then virgins will rejoice in a gathering of young men, and elders will rejoice. I will turn their mourning into gladness and make them joyful.
  Psalms 29:12-13 — mourning turned into gladness and celebration: Jeremiah promises to turn mourning into gladness among the restored generations. The psalm similarly thanks God for turning lament into dancing and clothing the speaker with gladness. The emotional reversal is explicit, though one promise is communal and the psalm's voice is individual.

Jeremiah 38:14
I will magnify and fill the priests’ souls to abundance, the sons of Levi, and My people will be filled with My good things.
  Isaiah 61:6 — priestly service accompanied by abundant provision: Jeremiah promises abundance for the priests, sons of Levi, and satisfaction for the people. Isaiah addresses those called the Lord's priests and God's ministers who will consume the nations' resources. Both associate priestly identity with provision; the recipients' descriptions and sources of abundance differ.

Jeremiah 38:15
Thus said the Lord: A voice was heard in Ramah, lamentation, weeping, and wailing. Rachel, weeping aloud, would not stop over her sons, because they are no more.
  Matthew 2:17-18 — explicit Gospel citation of Rachel's lament: Matthew explicitly attributes to Jeremiah the voice in Ramah and Rachel's weeping for children who are no more. The supplied Jeremiah says she would not stop, while Matthew says she would not be comforted. The shared place, mother and loss establish the citation despite that wording difference.

Jeremiah 38:16
Thus said the Lord: Let your voice cease from weeping and your eyes from your tears, because your works have a reward, and they will return from enemies’ land,
  Psalms 125:5-6 — tears followed by a rewarded return: Jeremiah tells the mourner to cease weeping because her works have a reward and the children will return. The psalm similarly moves from tearful sowing and departure to joyful return carrying sheaves. The imagery differs, but both connect tears, work and a favorable returning outcome.

Jeremiah 38:17
a lasting stay for your children.
  Amos 9:15 — lasting settlement after return: Jeremiah's brief promise gives the children a lasting stay in the context of return from the enemy's land. Amos promises planting on the given land without future uprooting. The permanence of restored residence supplies the connection; Amos does not separately address the children.

Jeremiah 38:18
I surely heard Ephraim wailing: Thou didst discipline me, and I was disciplined; like a calf I was not taught. Turn me back, and I will return, because Thou art the Lord my God.
  Lamentations 5:21 — a plea that God enable the people's return: Ephraim asks the Lord to turn him back so that he will return. Lamentations likewise asks the Lord to turn the speakers back to Him, promising that they will be turned back. Both make divine action central to the desired return.

Jeremiah 38:19
Because after my captivity I repented, and after I came to know, I groaned over days of shame and showed Thee that I received reproach from my youth.
  Ezekiel 36:31 — repentance and shame when former wrongdoing is recognized: Ephraim links repentance after captivity with recognizing shame and youthful reproach. Ezekiel describes restored people remembering their evil ways and feeling disgust at their lawless deeds. Both make recognition of the past part of the restoration response, without equating captivity itself with repentance.

Jeremiah 38:20
Ephraim is a beloved son to Me, a child taking delight, because My words are in him; I will surely remember him. Therefore I hastened toward him; I will surely have mercy on him, says the Lord.
  Hosea 11:8-9 — compassion toward Ephraim despite threatened judgment: Jeremiah calls Ephraim a beloved son remembered with mercy. Hosea likewise names Ephraim when describing the Lord's inward change and refusal to erase him in furious anger. The shared named recipient and compassionate reversal make the connection particular.

Jeremiah 38:22
How long will you turn away, dishonored daughter? Because the Lord created salvation for a new planting; people will go about in salvation.
  Isaiah 60:21 — restoration pictured as a divinely established planting: Jeremiah speaks of the Lord creating salvation for a new planting. Isaiah describes the restored people inheriting the land and guarding the planting, the work of His hands. Both associate the restored community with planting and divine work; neither supplied passage is replaced with another edition's wording.

Jeremiah 38:23
Thus said the Lord: They will again say this word in Judah’s land and its cities when I return its captivity: Blessed is the Lord over the righteous mountain, His holy mountain,
  Zechariah 8:3 — the restored city's holy mountain: Jeremiah foresees returned captivity and an acclamation blessing the Lord over His righteous, holy mountain. Zechariah likewise describes the Lord returning to Zion and the mountain being called holy. Both link renewed presence in Jerusalem's setting with the mountain's holiness.

Jeremiah 38:24
and those dwelling in Judah’s cities and all its land together with a farmer; and he will be lifted up with a flock.
  Jeremiah 40:12-13 — farmers and flocks in the resettled land: Jeremiah's restored Judah includes a farmer and a flock alongside the city's inhabitants. Jeremiah 40 expands the pastoral picture with lodgings for shepherds and counted sheep throughout Judah and Benjamin. The recovery of rural livelihood accompanies the renewed towns in both passages.

Jeremiah 38:25
Because I filled every thirsty soul to abundance and filled every hungry soul.
  Psalms 106:9 — hungry souls filled with good: Jeremiah describes the Lord filling thirsty and hungry souls. The psalm similarly says the Lord filled an empty soul and a hungry soul with good things. The explicit filling of the hungry provides the link; the psalm's first description is empty rather than thirsty.

Jeremiah 38:27
Therefore behold, days are coming, says the Lord, and I will sow Israel and Judah with human seed and livestock seed.
  Ezekiel 36:9-11 — people and livestock multiplied on cultivated land: Jeremiah depicts Israel and Judah sown with human and livestock seed. Ezekiel likewise combines cultivated and sown land with the multiplication of humans and livestock. The pairing of both populations within an agricultural restoration picture is distinctive.

Jeremiah 38:28
And just as I watched over them to tear down and harm, so I will watch over them to build and plant, says the Lord.
  Jeremiah 1:10 — the building and planting side of Jeremiah's commission: The Lord contrasts His watching to tear down and harm with watching to build and plant. Jeremiah's original commission also includes uprooting and demolishing alongside building and planting. The restoration oracle brings the constructive terms of that commission into focus.

Jeremiah 38:29
In those days they will certainly not say: The fathers ate an unripe grape, and the children’s teeth were set on edge.
  Ezekiel 18:2-4 — the unripe-grape proverb disallowed: Jeremiah says the proverb about fathers eating an unripe grape and children's teeth reacting will no longer be spoken. Ezekiel quotes the same proverb and forbids its continued use, grounding responsibility in the soul that sins. The shared proverb and its repudiation are explicit.

Jeremiah 38:30
Instead, each will die in his own sin, and the one eating the unripe grape will have his own teeth set on edge.
  Ezekiel 18:20 — individual responsibility for wrongdoing: Jeremiah says each will die in his own sin and bear the effect of his own eaten grape. Ezekiel likewise says the sinning soul dies and neither father nor son receives the other's injustice. Both oppose transferring the other's wrongdoing to an innocent family member.

Jeremiah 38:31
Behold, days are coming, says the Lord, and I will make a new covenant with the house of Israel and with the house of Judah,
  Hebrews 8:8-9 — explicit quotation of the new-covenant promise: Hebrews quotes the coming days and new covenant with Israel's house and Judah's house. Those are the named parties and promise in Jeremiah. Hebrews places the quotation within its discussion of priestly ministry and covenant, providing an explicit later use rather than an inferred verbal resemblance.
  Luke 22:20 — later Gospel use of new-covenant language: Jeremiah announces a new covenant with Israel and Judah. Luke records Jesus calling the supper's cup the new covenant in His blood. The Gospel applies new-covenant language to that act; the connection does not erase Jeremiah's explicitly named covenant parties.

Jeremiah 38:32
not according to the covenant I made with their fathers on the day I took their hand to bring them out of Egypt’s land, because they did not remain in My covenant, and I disregarded them, says the Lord.
  Hebrews 8:9 — quotation of the broken Exodus covenant contrast: Hebrews repeats the contrast with the covenant made when the fathers were led from Egypt, their failure to remain in it and God's disregard of them. These corresponding clauses make the connection a direct quotation of Jeremiah's covenant comparison.

Jeremiah 38:33
Because this is the covenant I will make with Israel’s house after those days, says the Lord: I will surely put My laws into their understanding and write them upon their hearts. I will be their God, and they will be My people.
  Hebrews 8:10 — quotation of laws inscribed within the people: Hebrews repeats laws put into the mind and inscribed on hearts, followed by the God-and-people promise. Jeremiah contains the same sequence, using understanding for the inner faculty. The inward inscription and reciprocal relationship are both preserved in the later quotation.
  2 Corinthians 3:3 — divine inscription on hearts in new-covenant ministry: Jeremiah promises laws written upon hearts. Paul describes the community as Christ's letter inscribed by God's Spirit on hearts rather than stone, and the surrounding passage names new-covenant ministry. The shared heart-inscription image connects the passages without treating Paul's letter metaphor as a verbatim quotation.

Jeremiah 38:34
And they will certainly not teach each his fellow citizen or his brother, saying, Know the Lord, because they will all know Me, from their least to their greatest. I will be merciful toward their injustices and certainly remember their sins no more.
  Hebrews 8:11-12 — quotation of knowledge of God and sins remembered no more: Hebrews repeats the absence of neighbor-to-neighbor teaching to know the Lord, knowledge from least to greatest and forgiveness with sins no longer remembered. The full sequence follows Jeremiah's promise, making this an explicit citation rather than a generic forgiveness theme.
  Hebrews 10:16-17 — the forgiveness clause reused in a later argument: Hebrews again cites covenant words and the promise that sins and lawless deeds will no longer be remembered. Jeremiah likewise joins knowledge of God with mercy and sins remembered no more. Hebrews uses this clause in its subsequent argument about forgiveness and offerings.

Jeremiah 38:35
If the heaven is raised to the height, says the Lord, and the earth’s ground lowered beneath, even then I do not reject Israel’s family, says the Lord, for all they did.
  Romans 11:1-2 — God's refusal to reject Israel: Jeremiah states that even the described extremes of heaven and earth do not cause rejection of Israel's family for its deeds. Romans explicitly denies that God rejected His people and identifies Paul as an Israelite. Both affirm non-rejection, with different supporting arguments and historical settings.

Jeremiah 38:36
Thus said the Lord, who gave the sun for daylight, moon and stars for night light, and a cry in the sea so its waves boomed—the Lord Almighty is His name:
  Genesis 1:14-18 — the heavenly lights assigned to day and night: Jeremiah names the Lord as giver of the sun for day and moon and stars for night. Genesis narrates the lights being made and placed to govern day and night. The reference to their ordered illumination grounds Jeremiah's description in the creation narrative.
  Isaiah 51:15 — the Lord named through the sounding sea: Jeremiah describes the Lord who makes the sea's waves boom and names Him Almighty. Isaiah likewise names the Lord Sabaoth as the one stirring the sea and making its waves resound. The combination of divine naming and audible waves is closely parallel.

Jeremiah 38:37
If these laws cease from before Me, says the Lord, then Israel’s family will cease to be a nation before Me for all days.
  Psalms 88:36-38 — enduring covenant identity compared with cosmic stability: Jeremiah links Israel's continued national existence to the continuance of the divine laws just described through the lights. The psalm compares David's enduring seed and throne with sun and moon. Both use cosmic stability to describe enduring commitment, while national Israel and David's dynasty remain distinct scopes.

Jeremiah 38:40
And all Asaremoth as far as the Kidron wadi, to the corner of the eastern Horse Gate, will be a sanctuary for the Lord. It will certainly never disappear or be torn down for ever.
  Zechariah 14:20-21 — holiness extending across the restored city: Jeremiah describes a defined area reaching the Kidron and Horse Gate as a lasting sanctuary for the Lord. Zechariah extends holiness to ordinary vessels throughout Jerusalem and Judah. Both broaden the described sphere of holiness, though one names territory and the other objects within it.

Jeremiah 39:1
The word that came from the Lord to Jeremiah in King Zedekiah’s tenth year, which was the eighteenth year of King Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon.
  Jeremiah 52:4-5 — chronological placement within Jerusalem's siege: The oracle is dated to Zedekiah's tenth year. Jeremiah 52 dates the siege from his ninth year until his eleventh. These stated dates place the oracle within that siege interval, without equating the oracle's date with either the siege's beginning or the city's capture.

Jeremiah 39:2
The king of Babylon’s force had built siegeworks against Jerusalem, and Jeremiah was kept in the prison court at the king’s house.
  Jeremiah 44:20-21 — Jeremiah's confinement in the prison court: Jeremiah is described as confined in the prison court during the siege. The later narrative describes the king ordering him into custody with a daily loaf and concludes that he remained in the prison court. Both identify this specific place of confinement, without assuming their literary order is chronological.

Jeremiah 39:3
King Zedekiah had shut him in there, saying, Why do you prophesy, saying, Thus said the Lord: I am handing this city over to Babylon’s king, and he will take it?
  Jeremiah 21:10 — the city-delivery oracle cited as the reason for confinement: Zedekiah's complaint quotes Jeremiah's prediction that Babylon's king will receive the city. Jeremiah 21 also announces that Jerusalem will be handed to Babylon's king and burned. The shared prediction explains the stated content of the royal objection.

Jeremiah 39:4
Zedekiah will certainly not be saved from the Chaldeans’ hand, but will certainly be handed over to Babylon’s king. His mouth will speak to his mouth, and his eyes will see his eyes.
  Jeremiah 41:2-3 — Zedekiah's capture and face-to-face encounter repeated: The prediction says Zedekiah will be handed to Babylon's king, see his eyes and speak mouth to mouth. Jeremiah 41 repeats these particulars directly to Zedekiah. The same named king and encounter make this a close parallel prophecy.

Jeremiah 39:5
Zedekiah will enter Babylon and remain there.
  Jeremiah 52:11 — Zedekiah's Babylonian confinement: The oracle says Zedekiah will enter Babylon and remain there. Jeremiah 52 narrates his being brought to Babylon and kept in a mill house until his death. The narrated destination and continued confinement correspond to the prediction, preserving the unusual mill-house description.

Jeremiah 39:7
Hanamel son of Shallum, your father’s brother, is coming to you, saying, Acquire my field at Anathoth for yourself, because you have the right to receive it as a possession.
  Leviticus 25:25 — a relative's right concerning family land: Hanamel's offer grounds Jeremiah's acquisition in his right to receive the field as a possession. Leviticus provides for a nearest kinsman to redeem a poor brother's sold property. The legal family interest supplies a parallel, without adding a prior sale or poverty claim absent from Jeremiah's unit.

Jeremiah 39:8
Hanamel son of Shallum, my father’s brother, came to me in the prison court and said, Acquire my field in Benjamin’s land at Anathoth, because you have the right to acquire it, and you are the elder. Then I knew that this was the Lord’s word.
  Jeremiah 39:7 — same-chapter arrival confirming a predicted offer: Hanamel arrives and offers the Anathoth field, leading Jeremiah to recognize the Lord's word. The preceding oracle had announced that arrival and offer. This is an explicit same-chapter prediction and recognition, preserving the source's familial descriptions without harmonizing them.

Jeremiah 39:9
I acquired the field of Hanamel, my father’s brother’s son, and weighed out to him seventeen shekels of silver.
  Genesis 23:15-18 — field acquisition for an expressly stated silver price: Jeremiah acquires the field for seventeen shekels of silver. Genesis describes Abraham paying the named silver price and a field becoming his possession before witnesses. Both record land transferred for a stated silver amount; Genesis's verb here is paid, not a claim that it mentions weighing.

Jeremiah 39:10
I wrote a document, sealed it, called witnesses, and weighed the silver on a balance.
  Ruth 4:9-11 — witnesses to a property acquisition: Jeremiah calls witnesses while documenting and paying for the field. Boaz likewise calls the elders and people witnesses to his acquisition, and they affirm their witness. Both make public attestation part of the transaction; Ruth does not supply Jeremiah's sealed-document procedure.

Jeremiah 39:12
I gave it to Baruch son of Neriah son of Maaseiah, before Hanamel my father’s brother’s son, before those standing there and writing in the purchase document, and before the Judeans in the prison court.
  Jeremiah 43:4 — Baruch son of Neriah entrusted with Jeremiah's written records: Jeremiah hands the purchase document to Baruch son of Neriah. In the scroll episode Jeremiah calls the same named Baruch to write the oracles from his mouth. The personal identification and handling of Jeremiah's documents connect the passages, while the documents have different purposes.

Jeremiah 39:14
Thus said the Lord Almighty: Take this purchase document and the document that has been read, and put it in an earthenware vessel so that it may last many days.
  Jeremiah 39:43-44 — same-chapter deed preservation as a sign of future transactions: The purchase documents are to remain in an earthenware vessel for many days. The chapter later promises renewed field purchases with written, sealed and witnessed documents. That future explains the deed's significance as an enduring sign of restored landholding, without changing the read document into an unsealed copy.

Jeremiah 39:15
For thus said the Lord: Fields, houses, and vineyards will again be acquired in this land.
  Ezekiel 28:25-26 — returned exiles living in houses and vineyards: Jeremiah announces that fields, houses and vineyards will again be acquired in the land. Ezekiel promises gathered Israel will dwell on its land, build houses and plant vineyards. Both give restoration a concrete residential and agricultural form, though only Jeremiah's unit speaks of acquisition.

Jeremiah 39:17
O Lord, Thou madest heaven and earth by Thy great strength and Thy high, uplifted arm. Nothing can be hidden from Thee.
  Jeremiah 23:24 — nothing hidden from the Lord of heaven and earth: Jeremiah's prayer joins the making of heaven and earth with the assertion that nothing can be hidden from the Lord. Jeremiah 23 likewise asks whether anyone can hide from the One filling heaven and earth. The connection is divine knowledge and cosmic scope, not a substituted statement about impossibility.

Jeremiah 39:18
Thou showest mercy to thousands and repayest fathers’ sins into their children’s laps after them, the great and strong God,
  Exodus 20:5-6 — mercy to thousands and intergenerational consequences: The prayer describes mercy to thousands and repayment of fathers' sins to children. Exodus similarly joins repayment across generations with mercy to thousands. The paired descriptions closely correspond; Exodus adds qualifications concerning hatred, love and keeping the commandments.

Jeremiah 39:19
Lord of great counsel and powerful in works, the great God, the Almighty and renowned Lord. Thine eyes are upon the ways of human beings, to give each according to his way.
  Jeremiah 17:10 — divine examination and recompense according to ways: Jeremiah describes God's eyes on human ways and His giving each according to that way. Jeremiah 17 likewise joins examination of hearts and kidneys with recompense according to ways and practices. Both connect divine discernment with individually described recompense.

Jeremiah 39:20
Thou didst perform signs and wonders in Egypt down to this day, in Israel and among earthborn people, and madest a name for Thyself as it is this day.
  Deuteronomy 4:34-35 — Egyptian signs and wonders as evidence of God's action: The prayer recalls signs and wonders performed in Egypt and the resulting divine renown. Deuteronomy likewise recalls Egypt's signs and wonders so that Israel knows the Lord alone is God. Both use the remembered wonders as evidence of God's distinctive action, while Jeremiah also speaks of later works.

Jeremiah 39:21
Thou broughtest Thy people Israel out of Egypt with signs and wonders, with a mighty hand, a high arm, and great sights.
  Deuteronomy 26:8 — the Exodus recalled through hand, arm and wonders: Jeremiah describes Israel brought from Egypt with signs, wonders, a mighty hand and high arm. The firstfruits confession in Deuteronomy repeats that combination of deliverance, strength, hand, uplifted arm and wonders. The close formula connects two retellings of the Exodus.

Jeremiah 39:22
Thou gavest them this land, which Thou hadst sworn to their fathers, a land flowing with milk and honey.
  Deuteronomy 26:9 — the gift of land flowing with milk and honey: Jeremiah recalls the land given according to the ancestral oath and describes it as flowing with milk and honey. Deuteronomy's confession likewise thanks the Lord for bringing the people to and giving them that land. The repeated description identifies the shared gift being remembered.

Jeremiah 39:23
They entered and received it, but did not obey Thy voice or walk in Thine ordinances. They did none of all that Thou didst command them, and Thou didst cause all these calamities to befall them.
  Daniel 9:10-14 — confession of disobedience explaining national calamity: Jeremiah's prayer says the people received the land but failed to hear and obey, and that God caused the calamities to befall them. Daniel likewise confesses refusal to hear the law and describes the announced calamities falling upon Jerusalem. Both interpret disaster within a prayer acknowledging the people's disobedience.

Jeremiah 39:24
A multitude has reached this city to seize it. The city was handed over to the Chaldeans fighting it because of sword and famine. As Thou spakest, so it happened.
  Jeremiah 52:4-6 — siege and famine in the account of Jerusalem's fall: Jeremiah describes forces reaching the city and its delivery to the Chaldeans amid sword and famine. Jeremiah 52 narrates Babylon's enclosing siege and severe famine. The same city, attacking power and conditions connect the prayer's account with the later siege narrative.

Jeremiah 39:25
Yet Thou sayest to me, Acquire a field with silver. And I wrote a document, sealed it, and called witnesses—even though the city was handed over to the Chaldeans.
  Jeremiah 39:9-10 — same-chapter transaction recalled amid the siege: Jeremiah's prayer repeats the field purchase, silver, written document, sealing and witnesses while noting its tension with the city's surrender. Those actions were narrated earlier in the chapter. The repetition frames the transaction's apparent tension with the siege rather than adding another purchase.

Jeremiah 39:27
I am the Lord, the God of all flesh. Can anything be hidden from Me?
  Jeremiah 23:23-24 — the impossibility of hiding from God: The Lord asks whether anything can be hidden from Him as God of all flesh. Jeremiah 23 similarly asks whether a person hidden in secret places escapes His sight and speaks of His filling heaven and earth. Both concern concealment from God; neither is paraphrased as a statement about an impossible task.

Jeremiah 39:28
Therefore thus said the Lord, Israel’s God: This city will certainly be handed over to Babylon’s king, and he will take it.
  Jeremiah 21:10 — Jerusalem handed to Babylon's king: The Lord again declares that Babylon's king will receive and take the city. Jeremiah 21 had announced the same transfer of Jerusalem to that king. The repeated recipient and city connect the declarations, rather than making the field purchase a denial of the announced capture.

Jeremiah 39:29
The Chaldeans fighting this city will come and burn this city with fire. They will burn down the houses on whose roofs they burned incense to Baal and poured libations to other gods to provoke Me bitterly.
  Jeremiah 19:13 — rooftop worship provoking judgment upon houses: Jeremiah identifies houses whose roofs were used for incense and libations to other gods. Jeremiah 19 likewise singles out Jerusalem's houses and royal houses where rooftop incense and libations were offered. Both make these specific domestic cult practices part of the city's indictment.
  2 Kings 25:9-10 — the announced burning and the narrated destruction of houses: Jeremiah predicts that the Chaldeans will burn Jerusalem and its houses. Kings narrates fire set to the temple, royal house and Jerusalem's houses by the chief cook. The physical destruction corresponds, while Jeremiah supplies the religious accusation behind its announcement.

Jeremiah 39:30
The children of Israel and Judah alone were doing evil in My sight from their youth.
  Jeremiah 22:21 — persistent wrongdoing traced back to youth: The Lord describes Israel's and Judah's evil as beginning in their youth. Jeremiah 22 similarly says refusal to hear His voice has been the addressee's way from youth. The common time span presents the rebellion as longstanding rather than a single recent failure.

Jeremiah 39:31
This city has aroused My anger and wrath from the day they built it until this day, so that I would remove it from My presence,
  2 Kings 23:27 — Jerusalem and Judah removed from God's presence: Jeremiah describes the city's sustained provocation leading to removal from the Lord's presence. Kings likewise announces Judah's removal and rejection of Jerusalem and the house bearing His name. The shared city, divine presence and rejection make the correspondence specific.

Jeremiah 39:32
because of all the wicked deeds the children of Israel and Judah committed to embitter Me—they and their kings, their rulers, their priests, their prophets, the men of Judah and Jerusalem’s inhabitants.
  Jeremiah 2:26 — the people and their governing and religious leaders implicated together: Jeremiah lists kings, rulers, priests and prophets among the people whose deeds provoked the Lord. Jeremiah 2 similarly names those groups alongside Israel's sons in their coming shame. Both make the indictment communal and explicitly include these leadership offices.

Jeremiah 39:33
They turned their backs to Me, not their faces. I taught them from early morning and kept teaching, but they did not listen and take hold of discipline.
  Jeremiah 2:27 — turning the back instead of the face to God: Jeremiah describes the people turning their backs to the Lord and refusing instruction. Jeremiah 2 uses the same back-versus-face image in its account of calling wood and stone their parent. The distinctive bodily image connects their refusal of God in both accusations.

Jeremiah 39:34
They set their pollutions in the house over which My name is invoked, amid their impurities.
  Jeremiah 7:30 — defilement within the house bearing God's name: The people set pollutions within the house over which the Lord's name is invoked. Jeremiah 7 likewise says Judah placed abominations in that named house to defile it. The shared place and act make this a close recurrence of the temple indictment.

Jeremiah 39:35
They built the altars to Baal in the Valley of the Son of Hinnom, to offer their sons and daughters to Molech the king. I had not instructed them, nor had it entered My heart, to do this abomination and cause Judah to sin.
  Jeremiah 7:31 — offspring offered in Hinnom contrary to divine command: Jeremiah names the Valley of the Son of Hinnom and offering sons and daughters in an abomination the Lord never instructed or conceived. Jeremiah 7 similarly describes the Hinnom altar and burning sons and daughters, explicitly denying divine command or conception. The particular place, victims and denial correspond.

Jeremiah 39:36
And now, thus said the Lord, Israel’s God, concerning the city of which you say, It will be handed over to Babylon’s king by sword, famine, and a sent affliction:
  Jeremiah 21:7 — enemy capture amid sword and famine: The description of the city's threatened transfer names sword, famine and a sent affliction. Jeremiah 21 likewise announces survivors of death, famine and sword being handed to their enemies. Both connect multiple afflictions with enemy capture; the source's sent affliction is not silently renamed death.

Jeremiah 39:37
I am gathering them from all the land where I scattered them in My anger, wrath, and great indignation. I will bring them back to this place and settle them in confidence.
  Ezekiel 37:21 — gathering from dispersed lands into Israel again: Jeremiah promises gathering from all the lands of angry dispersion and return to this place. Ezekiel likewise promises taking Israel from the surrounding nations and bringing them into Israel's land. Both describe a geographically gathered community after dispersion.

Jeremiah 39:38
They will be My people, and I will be their God.
  Ezekiel 37:27 — the restored people's reciprocal covenant relationship: Jeremiah promises that they will be His people and He their God. Ezekiel repeats the same reciprocal formula alongside the Lord's dwelling among them. The shared words define the relationship of the restored community in both oracles.

Jeremiah 39:39
I will give them another way and another heart, to fear Me always, for their good and their children’s good after them.
  Ezekiel 11:19-20 — another heart given for renewed fidelity: Jeremiah promises another way and another heart so the people fear the Lord continually. Ezekiel likewise promises another heart and new spirit so they walk in His commands. Both attribute renewed fidelity to divine transformation, without replacing Jeremiah's another way with a different reading.

Jeremiah 39:40
I will establish an eternal covenant with them, which I will certainly not turn back from behind them. I will put My fear in their heart so that they do not depart from Me.
  Ezekiel 37:26 — an everlasting covenant of continuing divine commitment: Jeremiah promises an eternal covenant and a heart that will not depart from the Lord. Ezekiel likewise promises an everlasting covenant of peace with holy things among the people for ever. Both express enduring commitment, while describing its effects differently.

Jeremiah 39:41
I will attend to them to do them good and will plant them in this land in faithfulness, with all heart and all soul.
  Jeremiah 24:6 — doing good and planting the returned people: The Lord promises attentive good and faithful planting in the land. The good-fig oracle likewise fixes His eyes upon the deportees for good and promises return, building and planting. The shared combination of favorable attention and secure reestablishment is explicit.

Jeremiah 39:42
For thus said the Lord: Just as I brought all these great calamities upon this people, so I will bring upon them all the good things I spoke concerning them.
  Jeremiah 38:28 — restoration corresponding to earlier destructive action: Jeremiah promises to bring the announced good just as He brought the great calamities. Jeremiah 38 similarly contrasts watching to tear down and harm with watching to build and plant. Both describe restoration through a deliberate reversal of previous divine action.

Jeremiah 39:43
Fields will again be acquired in the land of which you say, It is untrodden by people and animals, and they were handed over to the Chaldeans.
  Jeremiah 39:15 — same-chapter reaffirmation of renewed property acquisition: The promise that fields will again be acquired in the desolate, surrendered land reiterates the earlier assurance that fields, houses and vineyards will be acquired again. The recurrence reinforces the interpretation of Jeremiah's purchase as a sign of restoration despite present loss.

Jeremiah 39:44
They will acquire fields with silver, and you will write a document, seal it, and call witnesses in Benjamin’s land, around Jerusalem, in Judah’s cities, the hill-country cities, the cities of the Shephelah, and the cities of the Negeb, because I will bring back their exiles.
  Jeremiah 39:9-12 — same-chapter transaction becoming the pattern of future purchases: The future promise names silver, writing, sealing and witnesses for purchases throughout the land. Jeremiah's earlier field purchase already included those specific actions. His particular transaction provides the narrative pattern for the broader restoration promise, without counting the later prediction as another completed purchase.

Jeremiah 40:1
The Lord’s word came to Jeremiah a second time, while he was still bound in the prison court:
  Jeremiah 39:2 — continued prophetic address during prison-court confinement: The second word reaches Jeremiah while he is still bound in the prison court. The preceding chapter had explicitly located him there during the siege. The stated continuing custody connects the settings of the two prophetic addresses.

Jeremiah 40:2
Thus said the Lord, who makes the earth and forms it to set it upright—the Lord is His name:
  Isaiah 45:18 — the Lord identified as maker and establisher of the earth: Jeremiah introduces the speaker as the one making, forming and establishing the earth, named the Lord. Isaiah likewise identifies the Lord by making and marking out the earth to be inhabited. Both ground the ensuing speech in this particular creative identity.

Jeremiah 40:3
Cry out to Me and I will answer you. I will announce to you great, mighty things you have not known.
  Amos 3:7 — divine disclosure to a prophet before the announced acts: Jeremiah is invited to call and receive an announcement of great things he has not known, followed by promises concerning the city's future. Amos says the Lord reveals His discipline to His servants the prophets before acting. Both depict prophetic disclosure, while Amos's object is specifically discipline.

Jeremiah 40:4
For thus said the Lord, Israel’s God, about this city’s houses and the houses of Judah’s king, torn down for siegeworks and defenses,
  Isaiah 22:10 — Jerusalem's houses demolished for defense: Jeremiah describes city and royal houses torn down for siegeworks and defenses. Isaiah likewise says Jerusalem's houses were pulled down to fortify the wall. The specific defensive use of demolished urban houses supplies the connection, without requiring that the passages describe the same siege.

Jeremiah 40:5
to fight the Chaldeans and fill the city with the dead bodies of people I struck in My anger and wrath. I turned My face from them because of all their wicked deeds.
  Jeremiah 14:16 — Jerusalem filled with the dead under judgment: Jeremiah describes people struck in divine anger whose bodies fill the city during the Chaldean conflict. Jeremiah 14 likewise predicts people cast into Jerusalem's streets because of sword and famine, without burial. Both depict the city's dead as a consequence of the announced judgment.

Jeremiah 40:6
I am bringing her the closing of wounds and a remedy. I will reveal to them that I listen, heal her, and make peace and faithfulness for them.
  Jeremiah 37:17 — the restoration promise expressed as a cure: The Lord promises closure of wounds, remedy and healing for the city. Jeremiah 37 had likewise promised a cure and healing of the people's painful wound. The repeated healing metaphor connects the two restoration oracles and their reversal of communal suffering.

Jeremiah 40:7
I will bring back Judah’s exiles and Israel’s exiles and build them as before.
  Jeremiah 37:3 — both Judah's and Israel's deportees returned: Jeremiah promises to bring back Judah's and Israel's exiles and rebuild them. Jeremiah 37 likewise names both people's deportees and promises return to their ancestral land. The same two named communities and return promise closely correspond.

Jeremiah 40:8
I will cleanse them from all the injustices by which they sinned against Me. I will certainly not remember the sins they committed against Me when they departed from Me.
  Ezekiel 36:25-28 — cleansing accompanies the people's restoration: Jeremiah promises cleansing from injustices and sins remembered no more. Ezekiel likewise promises cleansing from uncleannesses and idols, followed by a new heart and restored dwelling in the land. Both join restoration with cleansing; Ezekiel's passage does not repeat the remembered-no-more clause.

Jeremiah 40:9
It will be for joy, praise, and greatness to all the people of the earth. They will hear all the good things I will do, and they will fear and be distressed over all the good and peace I will bring them.
  Psalms 125:1-3 — the nations recognize the restored people's good: Jeremiah says the earth's peoples will hear of the good and peace bestowed on the city. The psalm similarly places the nations' declaration of the Lord's great deeds alongside Zion's reversed captivity and joy. Both make restoration publicly recognizable; Jeremiah additionally describes fear and distress among observers.

Jeremiah 40:10
Thus said the Lord: Again in this place, which you call deserted of people and animals, in Judah’s cities and outside Jerusalem, desolate because there are no people or animals, there will be heard
  Jeremiah 7:34 — renewed sounds promised where desolation had silenced them: Jeremiah describes the deserted cities as the place where sounds will again be heard, completed in the following unit by joy and wedding voices. Jeremiah 7 had announced those voices would cease as the land became desolate. The restoration reverses that specific earlier silencing.

Jeremiah 40:11
the sound of joy and rejoicing, the bridegroom’s voice and the bride’s voice, the voices of those saying, Give thanks to the Lord Almighty, for the Lord is good; His mercy is for ever. They will bring gifts into the Lord’s house, for I will bring back all the exiles of that land as before, said the Lord.
  Ezra 3:10-11 — returning worship with thanksgiving for enduring mercy: Jeremiah promises voices thanking the Lord for His goodness and everlasting mercy and gifts brought to His house. Ezra narrates temple-foundation praise after return, using goodness and enduring mercy language. The shared worship setting and thanksgiving formula connect promise and later narrative without equating every circumstance.
  Jeremiah 7:34 — wedding and rejoicing voices restored after their announced cessation: Jeremiah now promises the voices of joy, bridegroom and bride. Jeremiah 7 had explicitly announced the cessation of those same voices in Judah's cities and Jerusalem. The repeated list makes the restoration a precise reversal of the earlier judgment image.

Jeremiah 40:12
Thus said the Lord of forces: Again in this place, deserted because there are no people or animals, and in all its cities, there will be lodgings for shepherds settling sheep down.
  Isaiah 65:10 — flocks finding lodging in restored land: Jeremiah promises lodgings for shepherds settling sheep where the land had been deserted. Isaiah likewise promises shelters for flocks and a resting place for cattle for the people who sought the Lord. Both make renewed pastoral resting places part of the restoration picture.

Jeremiah 40:13
In the hill-country cities, the cities of the Shephelah, the cities of the Negeb, in Benjamin’s land, in the places around Jerusalem, and in Judah’s cities, sheep will again pass under the hand of the one counting, said the Lord.
  Leviticus 27:32 — the practice of counting passing livestock: Jeremiah depicts sheep passing under the counter's hand throughout the restored regions. Leviticus describes cattle and sheep passing under the staff in a count for the tithe. The common practice is counted passing livestock; Jeremiah mentions neither a staff nor a tithe obligation.

Jeremiah 41:1
The word that came to Jeremiah from the Lord while Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon, his whole army, and all the land under his rule were fighting Jerusalem and all Judah’s cities:
  Jeremiah 52:4-5 — close narrative counterpart to Babylon's siege setting: The oracle is situated while Nebuchadnezzar and his forces fight Jerusalem and Judah's cities. The closing account dates Nebuchadnezzar's investment of Jerusalem and describes the siege lasting into Zedekiah's eleventh year; it supplies a particular counterpart for the Jerusalem part of that setting.

Jeremiah 41:2
Thus said the Lord: Go to Zedekiah king of Judah and tell him, Thus said the Lord: This city will certainly be handed over to Babylon’s king. He will seize it and burn it with fire.
  Jeremiah 52:12-13 — close threatened burning and subsequent account of Jerusalem's burning: The message to Zedekiah predicts that Babylon's king will seize and burn the city. The closing narrative records Nebuzaradan's arrival and the burning of the Lord's house, the royal house and the city's houses.

Jeremiah 41:3
You will certainly not be saved from his hand. You will certainly be captured and handed over to him. Your eyes will see his eyes, and his mouth will speak with your mouth. You will enter Babylon.
  Jeremiah 39:4-5 — close repeated prediction of Zedekiah's encounter with Babylon's king: Both oracles deny Zedekiah's escape, describe his mouth and eyes meeting those of Babylon's king, and announce his entry into Babylon. The uncommon paired mouth-and-eye language makes the correspondence particularly close.

Jeremiah 41:5
You will die in peace. As they wept for your fathers who reigned before you, they will weep for you and lament you, O lord! For I have spoken the word, said the Lord.
  Jeremiah 22:18 — contrast between promised and denied royal lament: Zedekiah is promised mourning with the lament O lord. The oracle naming Jehoiakim denies mourning and the corresponding Alas, lord lament. The distinct kings receive opposite predictions about this public expression of royal honor.

Jeremiah 41:7
while Babylon’s king’s force fought Jerusalem and Judah’s cities, Lachish and Azekah; these were the fortified cities left among Judah’s cities.
  2 Chronicles 11:9-11 — related named fortified cities of Judah: Jeremiah identifies Lachish and Azekah among the fortified cities still resisting. Chronicles lists Lachis and Azeka among Rehoboam's walled and supplied cities. The passages give related fortification settings under different kings; their supplied spellings are preserved.

Jeremiah 41:8
The word that came to Jeremiah from the Lord after King Zedekiah concluded a covenant with the people to proclaim release:
  Nehemiah 5:12-13 — parallel solemn undertaking to end oppression within the community: Zedekiah concludes a covenant to proclaim release. Nehemiah's assembly promises restitution, swears before priests and carries out its undertaking. The comparison concerns a publicly pledged remedy for oppression; the later reversal in Jeremiah contrasts with Nehemiah's reported compliance.

Jeremiah 41:9
each was to send his Hebrew male slave and his Hebrew female slave away free, so that a man from Judah would not be enslaved.
  Deuteronomy 15:12 — close legal counterpart for freeing Hebrew male and female slaves: Jeremiah specifies release of both Hebrew male and female slaves. Deuteronomy explicitly provides for a Hebrew man or woman to serve six years and then be sent free; its stated service term is supplied by the law, rather than asserted in this individual source unit.

Jeremiah 41:10
All the magnates and all the people who had entered the covenant to send away their male and female slaves turned back,
  Nehemiah 5:12-13 — contrast between reversing and fulfilling an undertaking of restitution: The covenant participants turn back from their undertaking to release their slaves. Nehemiah records an oath of restitution and says the people carried out the word. The neighboring source unit explains the reversal as renewed enslavement.

Jeremiah 41:11
and thrust them into the position of male and female slaves.
  Nehemiah 5:8 — parallel condemnation of enslaving fellow Judeans after release: The people thrust the released servants back into slavery. Nehemiah condemns selling Jewish brothers while he and his companions have been buying brothers out of foreign bondage. Both expose conduct that reverses liberation within the community.

Jeremiah 41:13
Thus said the Lord, Israel’s God: I established a covenant with your fathers on the day I rescued them from Egypt, from the house of slavery, saying:
  Deuteronomy 15:15 — close grounding of emancipation obligations in rescue from Egypt: The Lord recalls establishing the covenant when He rescued the fathers from Egypt's house of slavery. The emancipation law likewise grounds its command in Israel's former slavery and the Lord's redemption from Egypt.

Jeremiah 41:14
When six years are complete, you must send away your Hebrew brother who is sold to you. He will work for you six years, and you must send him away free. But they did not obey Me or incline their ears.
  Deuteronomy 15:12 — close repetition of the six-year Hebrew-servant release law: The oracle states that the Hebrew brother serves six years and must be sent free when those years are complete. Deuteronomy gives the same service term and expressly names the seventh year as the time of release; Jeremiah adds the charge that the fathers did not obey.

Jeremiah 41:15
Today they turned to do what was right before My eyes, each proclaiming his neighbor’s release. They concluded a covenant before Me in the house over which My name is invoked.
  2 Kings 23:2-3 — parallel public covenant before the Lord at His house: Jeremiah recalls a covenant made before God in the house bearing His name. Kings describes a king and people at the Lord's house covenanting to uphold the written commandments. The commitments have different immediate contents, but share the public sanctuary setting and obligation before God.

Jeremiah 41:16
Then you reversed yourselves and profaned My name by bringing back your male and female slaves, whom you had sent away free in their own persons, to be your slaves.
  Leviticus 19:12 — related profanation of God's name through faithless solemn commitment: Reversing the promised release is explicitly called profaning God's name. Leviticus joins unjust swearing by that name with its profanation. Jeremiah concerns the covenant just made before God; the law addresses unjust use of His name in an oath.

Jeremiah 41:17
Therefore thus said the Lord: You did not obey Me by proclaiming release, each to his neighbor. So I proclaim release for you to sword, death, and famine! I will give you over to scattering among all the earth’s kingdoms.
  Leviticus 26:25-26 — close covenant judgment through sword, death and bread scarcity: The ironic proclamation of release hands the covenant breakers over to sword, death and famine. Leviticus describes the sword exacting the covenant's penalty, death among the people, surrender to enemies and scarcity of bread.
  Deuteronomy 28:25 — close covenant-curse language of scattering among earthly kingdoms: Jeremiah announces scattering among all the earth's kingdoms. Deuteronomy uses the same worldwide-kingdom scattering language in its curse of defeat before enemies, giving a specific counterpart to this part of Jeremiah's judgment.

Jeremiah 41:20
I will hand them over to their enemies, and their carcasses will become food for the birds of heaven and the wild beasts of the earth.
  Deuteronomy 28:26 — close covenant curse of corpses feeding birds and beasts: Both passages consign the dead to the birds of heaven and beasts of the earth. Deuteronomy additionally says no one will drive the creatures away; Jeremiah applies the threat to the covenant breakers.

Jeremiah 41:21
I will hand Zedekiah king of Judea and their rulers over to their enemies. And the force of Babylon’s king is for those running away from them.
  Jeremiah 52:8-11 — close threatened surrender and narrated capture of Zedekiah: The first clause promises to hand Zedekiah and the rulers to their enemies. The closing account describes Zedekiah's capture, judgment and removal to Babylon. This connection concerns that named royal surrender and does not resolve the source's unusual subsequent clause about the fleeing force.

Jeremiah 41:22
I am giving orders, says the Lord, and will bring them back to this land. They will fight against it, take it, and burn it with fire, along with Judah’s cities; I will make them deserted of inhabitants.
  Jeremiah 44:5-8 — close explanation of the returning force that will burn Jerusalem: The Lord promises to bring the force back to seize and burn the land's cities. The later oracle places the Chaldeans' withdrawal in the setting of Pharaoh's advance and then predicts their return to seize and burn Jerusalem.

Jeremiah 42:6
They said, We certainly will not drink wine, because Jonadab son of Rechab, our father, commanded us: Neither you nor your sons must ever drink wine.
  2 Kings 10:15-16 — related identification of Jonadab son of Rechab: The family names Jonadab son of Rechab as the father who commanded its abstinence. Kings independently names Jonadab son of Rechab meeting Jehu and joining his chariot. The target identifies the named figure without supplying the wine command itself.
  Numbers 6:2-4 — related wine abstinence under different binding commitments: The Rechabites refuse wine because of their father's enduring command. Numbers requires abstinence from wine and vine products during a consecration vow. The comparison concerns commanded abstinence; it does not classify the Rechabites as the vow-takers described in Numbers.

Jeremiah 42:7
You must not build a house or sow seed, and you must not have a vineyard. You must live in tents all your days, so that you may live many days on the land where you spend your time.
  Hebrews 11:9 — parallel tent dwelling as sojourners in a land: The ancestral command requires tent dwelling rather than settled houses and cultivation in the land where the family spends its time. Hebrews describes Abraham living as a foreigner in the promised land, dwelling in tents with Isaac and Jacob. It does not impose the Rechabites' particular prohibitions on those patriarchs.

Jeremiah 42:8
We have obeyed the voice of Jonadab our father by not drinking wine all our days, we, our wives, our sons, and our daughters,
  Colossians 3:20 — related obedience to a parental command: The descendants report that their entire households have kept Jonadab's command against wine. Colossians calls children to obey their parents in all things. Jeremiah gives an ancestral family practice; the letter states the household exhortation rather than that particular abstinence rule.

Jeremiah 42:10
We lived in tents, obeying and doing everything Jonadab our father commanded us.
  Ephesians 6:1-3 — related parental obedience with a promise of life in the land: The family says it has obeyed and done everything its father commanded, including the tent dwelling previously connected with long life. Ephesians joins obedience and honoring parents with the promise of living long upon the earth.

Jeremiah 42:11
When Nebuchadnezzar invaded the land, we said, Come in, let us enter Jerusalem to escape the Chaldean force and the Assyrian force. So we were living there.
  Jeremiah 4:5-6 — related flight to Jerusalem before an invading force: The Rechabites explain their move into Jerusalem as escape from the Chaldean and Assyrian forces. Jeremiah's earlier warning calls people to gather into walled cities and flee to Zion before the northern calamity. The target supplies a comparable refuge response, without naming both forces in this source.

Jeremiah 42:13
Thus says the Lord: Go and say to the people of Judah and Jerusalem’s inhabitants, Will you really not accept discipline and hear My words?
  Jeremiah 7:27–28 — close refusal to hear and accept instruction: The question asks whether Judah and Jerusalem will accept discipline and hear God's words. The composite target describes the nation as neither hearing the Lord's voice nor accepting instruction, stating the same two failures directly.

Jeremiah 42:14
The sons of Jonadab son of Rechab upheld the word he commanded his children, not to drink wine, and they drank none. Yet I spoke to you from early morning and kept speaking, and you did not listen.
  Malachi 1:6 — parallel appeal from honor of a human father to honor due God: Jonadab's descendants keep his word while Judah refuses God's repeated speech. Malachi argues from a son's honor for his father to the honor God should receive as Father. Both use ordinary filial allegiance to expose failure toward God.

Jeremiah 42:15
I sent you My servants the prophets, saying, Each of you, turn from his evil way and improve your practices. Do not follow other gods to serve them, and you will live on the land I gave you and your fathers. But you did not incline your ears or listen.
