  Jeremiah 25:4-6 — close repeated appeal to turn from evil and foreign gods: Both passages describe God sending His servants the prophets, calling each person to turn from evil practices, promising dwelling in the ancestral land and forbidding service to other gods. Their specific cluster of commands and assurances closely corresponds.

Jeremiah 42:16
The sons of Jonadab son of Rechab upheld their father’s command, but this people did not obey Me.
  Matthew 21:28-31 — related contrast between obeying and failing a father's command: Jeremiah contrasts the Rechabites' obedience to their father with the people's failure to obey God. Jesus' two-child parable distinguishes the child who actually does the father's will from the one who does not. The target emphasizes action rather than an identical family or historical episode.

Jeremiah 42:17
Therefore thus said the Lord: I am bringing upon Judah and Jerusalem’s inhabitants all the calamities I pronounced against them.
  Jeremiah 19:15 — close judgment on Jerusalem after rejection of God's words: Both declarations bring upon Jerusalem the calamities previously spoken against it. The earlier courtyard oracle explicitly grounds the judgment in refusal to hear; the Rechabite comparison immediately supplies that refusal in the source's setting.

Jeremiah 42:18
Therefore thus said the Lord: Because the sons of Jonadab son of Rechab obeyed their father’s command, doing as their father commanded them,
  Proverbs 1:8-9 — related honoring of obedience to parental instruction: The source grounds the following favorable promise in the descendants' obedience to their father's command. Proverbs urges attention to parental discipline and presents it as a crown and collar of honor. The rewards have different forms, while parental instruction is honored in both.

Jeremiah 42:19
there will certainly never fail a man from the sons of Jonadab son of Rechab standing before Me all the earth’s days.
  1 Kings 8:25 — close promise that a family's man will not fail before God: The Rechabites receive a promise that a man of their family will not fail to stand before God. Solomon recalls the promise that a man of David's family will not be removed from before God on Israel's throne, subject to the stated obedience condition. The families and the royal office are distinct.

Jeremiah 43:1
In the fourth year of Jehoiakim son of Josiah, king of Judah, the Lord’s word came to me:
  Jeremiah 25:1 — close shared fourth-year dating under Jehoiakim: Both introductions date a word to Jeremiah to the fourth year of Jehoiakim son of Josiah, king of Judah. This is a precise chronological correspondence, without asserting that every word of the two literary settings was delivered on the same occasion.

Jeremiah 43:2
Take a writing roll and write on it all the words I have delivered to you as oracles concerning Jerusalem, Judah, and all the nations, from the day I spoke to you, from the days of Josiah king of Judah until today.
  Isaiah 30:8 — parallel divine command to preserve prophetic words in writing: Jeremiah is told to put the delivered oracles on a writing roll. Isaiah is told to write on a tablet and in a book for enduring witness. The commanded written preservation is shared, while the specified media and scope differ.

Jeremiah 43:3
Perhaps Judah’s house will hear all the calamities I intend to bring upon them, so that they turn from their evil way and I may be merciful toward their injustices and sins.
  Jeremiah 33:3 — close hope that hearing warning will bring repentance and mercy: Both passages use perhaps they will hear and turn from an evil way as the hoped-for response to a warning. The source speaks of mercy toward injustices and sins; the target speaks of ceasing from the planned calamities.

Jeremiah 43:4
Jeremiah called Baruch son of Neriah, and he wrote on a writing roll, from Jeremiah’s mouth, all the Lord’s words which He had delivered to him as oracles.
  Jeremiah 51:31 — close corroborating notice of Baruch writing from Jeremiah's mouth: The source names Baruch son of Neriah writing the Lord's words from Jeremiah's mouth. The later notice again identifies Baruch writing words in the scroll from Jeremiah's mouth and dates the setting to Jehoiakim's fourth year.

Jeremiah 43:5
Jeremiah instructed Baruch, I am under guard and cannot enter the Lord’s house.
  Jeremiah 39:2-3 — related restriction of Jeremiah's movement by royal confinement: Jeremiah explains that being under guard prevents his entry into the Lord's house. The target describes Zedekiah shutting him in the royal prison court. These are distinct dated settings of the same prophet's confinement; the target does not identify the source's unspecified place of custody.

Jeremiah 43:6
Read from this roll in the people’s hearing at the Lord’s house on a fast day, and read it to all Judah’s people coming from their city.
  Nehemiah 9:1-3 — parallel public reading of sacred words in an assembly fasting before God: Baruch must read the roll publicly at the Lord's house on a fast day. Nehemiah describes an assembly fasting, confessing and reading God's law. The source is prophetic warning and the target is the law book, rather than the same document.

Jeremiah 43:7
Perhaps their appeal for mercy will fall before the Lord, and they will turn from their evil way, for great are the Lord’s wrath and anger which He has pronounced against this people.
  2 Chronicles 7:14 — parallel prayer and turning from evil in hope of mercy: Jeremiah hopes an appeal for mercy and turning from evil will follow the reading amid threatened wrath. God's answer concerning the temple in Chronicles joins prayer, seeking Him and turning from wicked ways with forgiveness and healing of the land.

Jeremiah 43:8
Baruch did everything Jeremiah instructed him, reading the Lord’s words from the document in the Lord’s house.
  Nehemiah 8:3 — parallel reading a sacred document in the people's hearing: Baruch performs the instructed public reading of the Lord's words from the document. Nehemiah describes Esdras reading the law while the people's ears attend to it. Both identify the reader, written text and listening assembly.

Jeremiah 43:9
In King Jehoiakim’s eighth year, in the ninth month, all the people in Jerusalem and Judah’s house assembled for a fast before the Lord.
  Joel 2:15-16 — parallel assembly of the people for a solemn fast: Jeremiah dates a gathering of Jerusalem and Judah for a fast before the Lord to Jehoiakim's eighth year and ninth month. Joel calls for a consecrated fast and gathered congregation. The target supplies no matching year or month.

Jeremiah 43:10
Baruch read Jeremiah’s words from the document in the Lord’s house, in the house of Gemariah son of Shaphan the scribe, in the upper court at the entrance of the New Gate of the Lord’s house, in all the people’s hearing.
  2 Kings 23:2 — close public reading of divine words at the Lord's house: Baruch reads Jeremiah's words in the Lord's house in all the people's hearing. Kings describes the king reading the covenant book to Judah, Jerusalem, priests, prophets and people at that house. The settings closely correspond while readers and documents differ.

Jeremiah 43:13
Micaiah reported to them all the words he had heard when Baruch read in the people’s hearing.
  2 Kings 22:9-10 — parallel official report bringing a sacred document to royal attention: Micaiah reports to the rulers the words he heard Baruch read. Kings describes Sapphan bringing the book to Josiah, reporting its discovery and reading it before him. These are distinct stages of official transmission, not an assertion that the named messengers are the same person.

Jeremiah 43:15
They said to him, Read it again to us. Baruch read it.
  Jeremiah 43:23 — same-chapter contrast in reception of the prophetic roll: The rulers request that Baruch read the roll again to them. The king later cuts and burns it as Jehudi reads its columns. The two audiences' responses to the same written prophecy form a concrete literary contrast.

Jeremiah 43:18
Baruch said, Jeremiah reported all these words to me from his mouth, and I wrote them in a document.
  Romans 16:22 — related named writers transmitting another speaker's message: Baruch identifies Jeremiah's oral report as what he wrote down. Tertius identifies himself as the writer of Romans, whose opening names Paul. The source expressly describes dictation; the target identifies the letter's writer without specifying that writing process.

Jeremiah 43:19
They said to Baruch, Go and hide, you and Jeremiah. Let no one know where you are.
  1 Kings 18:4 — parallel concealment of prophets threatened by royalty: The rulers tell Jeremiah and Baruch to hide and keep their whereabouts unknown. Kings describes Obadiah hiding the Lord's prophets when Jezebel was killing them. The particular common action is protective concealment under a royal threat.

Jeremiah 43:21
The king sent Jehudi to fetch the roll. He took it from Elishama’s house, and Jehudi read it to the king and all the rulers standing around him.
  2 Kings 22:10 — parallel reading a recovered religious document before a king: Jehudi fetches the deposited roll and reads it to the king and the surrounding rulers. Sapphan reports the discovered law book and reads it before Josiah. Their different royal responses become explicit in the surrounding narratives.

Jeremiah 43:23
When Jehudi read three or four columns, the king cut them off with the scribe’s knife and threw them into the fire on the brazier, until the whole roll was consumed in the brazier’s fire.
  Jeremiah 43:28-32 — same-chapter destruction of a scroll followed by its replacement: Jehoiakim cuts and burns the roll until it is consumed. The later command and fulfillment restore all its words in another roll, with additional words. The connection shows that destruction of the physical document does not end the announced message.

Jeremiah 43:24
Neither the king nor his servants who heard all these words sought anything or tore their garments.
  2 Kings 22:11-13 — contrast between unresponsive and repentant royal hearing: Jehoiakim and his servants neither seek anything nor tear their garments upon hearing the words. Josiah tears his garments and orders inquiry of the Lord after hearing the law book. The comparison preserves the source's stated actions without substituting an unstated description of fear.

Jeremiah 43:25
Elnathan, Gedaliah, and Gemariah advised the king not to burn the roll.
  2 Chronicles 25:16 — related royal rejection of warning counsel: The named rulers advise Jehoiakim not to burn the roll, against the burning already described. Amaziah rejects a prophet's counsel and is told that his refusal brings destruction. The advisers and the matters of counsel differ; the connection concerns disregarded royal warning.

Jeremiah 43:26
The king ordered Jerahmeel the king’s son and Seraiah son of Azriel to arrest Baruch and Jeremiah. But they were hidden.
  1 Kings 18:4 — parallel hidden prophetic servants escaping a royal threat: Jehoiakim orders Jeremiah and Baruch arrested, but they are hidden. Obadiah hides prophets during Jezebel's attacks. Jeremiah's unit does not name the agent of concealment, whereas Kings expressly names Obadiah.

Jeremiah 43:28
Take another roll again and write all the words that were on the roll which King Jehoiakim burned.
  Exodus 34:1 — parallel command to replace destroyed writing with its former words: Jeremiah is commanded to take another roll and write the words of the burned roll. Moses is commanded to prepare replacement tablets on which God will write the words of the shattered tablets. The media, writers and circumstances of destruction differ.

Jeremiah 43:29
Say, Thus said the Lord: You burned this roll, saying, Why did you write in it that Babylon’s king will certainly enter and destroy this land, and people and animals will cease from it?
  Jeremiah 21:6-7 — related prophecy of destruction affecting people and livestock: The king objects to the prediction that Babylon's king will destroy the land and that people and animals will cease from it. The target threatens death to the city's people and livestock, followed by the surrender of Zedekiah and the survivors. Both announcements concern Judah's judgment, with different immediate addressees and details.

Jeremiah 43:30
Therefore thus said the Lord concerning Jehoiakim king of Judah: He will have no one sitting on David’s throne. His carcass will be thrown out in the day’s heat and the night’s frost.
  Jeremiah 22:18-19 — close repeated judgment upon Jehoiakim's exposed corpse: Both named oracles against Jehoiakim predict his body being cast out dishonorably. Jeremiah 22 describes a donkey's burial beyond Jerusalem's gate; this source specifies exposure to daytime heat and nighttime frost.

Jeremiah 43:31
I will visit him, his family, and his servants, and bring upon them, Jerusalem’s inhabitants, and Judah’s land all the calamities I pronounced to them, but they did not listen.
  Jeremiah 19:15 — close announced calamities following refusal to hear: The Lord will bring the previously pronounced calamities upon the king's circle, Jerusalem and Judah because they did not listen. The courtyard oracle likewise announces the spoken calamities upon Jerusalem and its surrounding settlements because they refuse God's words.

Jeremiah 43:32
Baruch took another roll and wrote on it, from Jeremiah’s mouth, all the words of the document Jehoiakim burned. Still more words like these were added to it.
  Exodus 34:27-28 — parallel writing after the loss of an earlier sacred document: Baruch writes the destroyed document's words again on another roll, with more words added. In the setting of replacement tablets, Exodus records writing the covenant's words. The comparison concerns renewed inscription; only Jeremiah explicitly mentions added words.

Jeremiah 44:1
Zedekiah son of Josiah reigned in place of Jehoiakim; Nebuchadnezzar had made him king over Judah.
  2 Chronicles 36:10 — close appointment of Zedekiah by Nebuchadnezzar with differing succession notices: Both passages name Nebuchadnezzar as the one who made Zedekiah king over Judah. Jeremiah says he reigned in place of Jehoiakim, while the preceding Chronicles unit names Jechonias; the connection concerns the explicit appointment and does not harmonize that succession difference.

Jeremiah 44:2
Neither he nor his servants nor the people of the land obeyed the Lord’s words, which He spoke through Jeremiah.
  2 Chronicles 36:12-14 — close refusal of Jeremiah's divine message under Zedekiah: Jeremiah says king, servants and people did not obey the Lord's words through the prophet. Chronicles says Zedekiah felt no shame before Jeremiah and God's word, then describes the officials', priests' and people's breaches of faith. Both connect the reign with rejection of the prophetic message.

Jeremiah 44:3
King Zedekiah sent Jehucal son of Shelemiah and Zephaniah son of Maaseiah the priest to Jeremiah, saying, Please pray for us to the Lord.
  Jeremiah 21:1-2 — close royal delegation seeking Jeremiah's intercession: Zedekiah sends men, including Zephaniah son of Maaseiah, asking Jeremiah to pray. The earlier delegation from the same king also includes that named priest and asks Jeremiah to inquire of the Lord concerning Babylon's attack. The other delegate's name differs.

Jeremiah 44:5
Pharaoh’s force came out of Egypt. The Chaldeans heard the report about them and withdrew from Jerusalem.
  Ezekiel 17:15-17 — related Egyptian military intervention and its inability to secure Judah: Pharaoh's advance causes a temporary Chaldean withdrawal in Jeremiah. Ezekiel describes the appeal to Egypt for military aid and denies a successful outcome for the covenant-breaking ruler. The target does not narrate this particular withdrawal, and the source does not claim lasting Egyptian deliverance.

Jeremiah 44:7
Thus said the Lord: Tell Judah’s king, who sent to you to seek Me, Pharaoh’s force, which came out to help you, will return to Egypt’s land.
  Isaiah 31:1-3 — parallel warning that Egyptian military help will fail: Jeremiah says the Egyptian force that came to help will return home. Isaiah rebukes reliance on Egypt's horses and chariots and says the helpers will grow weary. The common point is the failure of military confidence in Egypt, expressed through different predictions.

Jeremiah 44:8
The Chaldeans themselves will return, fight this city, seize it, and burn it with fire.
  Jeremiah 41:22 — close repeated prediction of return, capture and burning: Both oracles say the force will return, take the city or land's cities and burn them. The source identifies the returning force as the Chaldeans; the earlier warning announces God's command bringing it back.

Jeremiah 44:9
For thus said the Lord: Do not suppose within yourselves, The Chaldeans will flee and leave us. They certainly will not leave.
  Jeremiah 7:4 — related rejection of false assurance about Jerusalem's safety: Jeremiah forbids assuming that the Chaldeans will depart for good. The temple sermon similarly warns against confidence in words promising benefit from the temple. The false assurances differ, but both concern unfounded confidence in Jerusalem's preservation.

Jeremiah 44:10
Even if you struck the whole Chaldean force fighting you and some were left pierced through, each in his place, these would rise and burn this city with fire.
  Deuteronomy 32:30 — related defeat by a seemingly insufficient force under divine judgment: Even pierced survivors of the Chaldean force are said to be capable of burning Jerusalem. Deuteronomy asks how one or two can rout multitudes unless God has delivered His people up. Neither target supplies the other's particular military numbers or injuries.

Jeremiah 44:12
Jeremiah left Jerusalem to go to Benjamin’s land, to buy from there among the people.
  Jeremiah 39:7-9 — related purchase in Benjamin's land by Jeremiah: The source says Jeremiah leaves to buy from Benjamin's land. The field narrative names Anathoth in Benjamin and records his acquisition from Hanamel. The comparison identifies another explicit purchase involving the same prophet and district, without collapsing the distinct narrative settings into one event.

Jeremiah 44:13
At Benjamin’s Gate there was a man with whom he lodged, Sarouiah son of Shelemiah son of Hananiah. He arrested Jeremiah, saying, You are fleeing to the Chaldeans!
  Nehemiah 6:6-8 — related accusation of political disloyalty met by denial: Sarouiah, the man with whom Jeremiah lodged, arrests him with the accusation of flight to the Chaldeans; Jeremiah denies it in the following unit. Nehemiah is accused of planning revolt and answers that the charge is fabricated. The allegations and accusers are different.

Jeremiah 44:14
He said, A lie! I am not fleeing to the Chaldeans. But Sarouiah would not listen to him; he arrested Jeremiah and brought him to the rulers.
  Nehemiah 6:8 — parallel explicit denial of an accusation of political disloyalty: Jeremiah calls the defection charge a lie and denies fleeing to the Chaldeans. Nehemiah denies the reported rebellion and says it was devised from the accuser's heart. Jeremiah additionally reports that his denial did not prevent arrest.

Jeremiah 44:15
The rulers were embittered against Jeremiah. They struck him and sent him into the house of Jonathan the scribe, which they had made into a prison house.
  Jeremiah 20:1-2 — related episodes of Jeremiah being struck and confined: The rulers strike Jeremiah and put him into Jonathan's house as a prison. The earlier account describes Pashhur striking him and placing him in a trap at the Lord's house. The responsible authorities and forms of confinement remain distinct.

Jeremiah 44:17
Zedekiah sent and summoned him. The king secretly asked him whether there was a word from the Lord. He said, There is. You will be handed over to Babylon’s king.
  Jeremiah 39:3-5 — close repeated royal confrontation over the prediction of captivity: Zedekiah secretly asks whether there is a word from the Lord and receives the prediction of surrender to Babylon's king. The prison-court account likewise presents Zedekiah confronting Jeremiah over the oracle that city and king will be handed over and the king will enter Babylon.

Jeremiah 44:18
Jeremiah said to the king, How have I wronged you, your servants, or this people, that you are putting me in prison?
  Genesis 40:15 — parallel protest of confinement despite no wrong done: Jeremiah asks what wrong he has committed to warrant imprisonment. Joseph says he has done nothing yet has been cast into the pit. The source is a question to the king and the target an assertion to the cupbearer, not the same judicial episode.

Jeremiah 44:19
Where are your prophets who prophesied to you, saying, Babylon’s king certainly will not come against this land?
  Jeremiah 35:1-4 — related exposure of optimistic prophecy about Babylon: Jeremiah challenges the prophets who said Babylon's king would not come against the land. The target identifies Hananiah as a false prophet and records his promise that Babylon's yoke is smashed and deportees and vessels will return. These are distinct optimistic claims, not identical quoted predictions.

Jeremiah 44:20
Now, lord king, let my appeal for mercy fall before you. Why send me back to Jonathan the scribe’s house—and will I not die there?
  Jeremiah 45:26 — close later repetition of Jeremiah's prison appeal: Jeremiah asks not to be returned to Jonathan's house to die. In the later secret-interview narrative the king instructs him to report precisely that appeal. The target records the proposed report, while the source gives the appeal itself.

Jeremiah 44:21
The king gave orders, and they put him into the prison house, giving him one loaf a day from outside where they bake, until the city’s loaves ran out. Jeremiah remained in the prison court.
  1 Kings 18:4 — related provision of bread to prophets under threat: Jeremiah receives a daily loaf during confinement until the city's supply fails. Obadiah supplies hidden prophets with bread and water during Jezebel's attacks. The protectors and confinement settings differ, while food sustains endangered prophetic servants in both.

Jeremiah 45:2
Thus said the Lord: Whoever remains in this city will die by sword and famine. Whoever goes out to the Chaldeans will live; his life will be his gain, and he will live.
  Jeremiah 21:9 — close repeated choice between remaining to die and surrendering to live: Both oracles say that remaining in the city brings death by sword and famine, while going out to the Chaldeans preserves life. The source calls life one's gain, while the target calls it spoil; both frame survival as what the surrendering person obtains.

Jeremiah 45:3
For thus said the Lord: This city will certainly be handed over to the force of Babylon’s king, and he will seize it.
  Jeremiah 21:10 — close repeated prediction of the city's surrender to Babylon: Both declarations say Jerusalem will be handed over to Babylon's king. The target additionally specifies burning by fire and God's adverse purpose; those details are not added to this shorter source unit.

Jeremiah 45:4
They said to the king, Let that man be killed! By speaking these words to them he weakens the hands of the fighters left in the city and of all the people. This man delivers oracles of evil to this people, not peace.
  Amos 7:10-11 — parallel accusation that a prophet's adverse words endanger the nation: Jeremiah's opponents tell the king that his words weaken fighters and people and deliver evil rather than peace. Amaziah tells Jeroboam that Amos conspires against him and that the land cannot endure his words of death and captivity. Both accusations portray unwelcome prophecy as a political danger.

Jeremiah 45:5
The king said, He is in your hands—for the king was unable to oppose them.
  Luke 23:23-25 — parallel ruler yielding a prisoner's fate to hostile demand: Zedekiah puts Jeremiah in the accusers' hands; the account says the king was unable to oppose them. Luke describes the accusers' voices prevailing and Pilate handing Jesus over to their will. The rulers' stated motives and the resulting punishments differ.

Jeremiah 45:6
They threw him into the pit of Malchiah the king’s son in the prison court and lowered him into the pit. There was no water in it, only mire, and he was in the mire.
  Genesis 37:23-24 — close narrative parallel of being cast into a waterless pit: Jeremiah is lowered into a pit without water, where there is mire. Joseph is stripped and cast into an empty pit explicitly having no water. The shared waterless-pit confinement is specific; Genesis does not supply Jeremiah's mire.

Jeremiah 45:7
Ebed-melech the Ethiopian, who was in the king’s house, heard that they had put Jeremiah in the pit. The king was at Benjamin’s Gate.
  Jeremiah 46:16-18 — close later divine address to the named Ethiopian rescuer: The source introduces Ebed-melech the Ethiopian as hearing of Jeremiah's confinement. The later oracle names the same Ethiopian and promises preservation amid the city's calamity because he trusted God. The intervening source narrative supplies his act of intervention.

Jeremiah 45:9
You have acted wickedly in what you did, killing this man through hunger, for there are no more loaves in the city.
  Proverbs 31:9 — related advocacy for a vulnerable person's just treatment: Ebed-melech confronts the king over the treatment that threatens Jeremiah with death from hunger. Proverbs calls for speaking, judging justly and deciding the case of the poor and weak. The comparison concerns intervention for a vulnerable person, without assigning Jeremiah a lifelong economic status.

Jeremiah 45:10
The king instructed Ebed-melech, Take thirty men from here under your command and bring him up from the pit so that he does not die.
  Proverbs 24:11 — parallel command to rescue someone facing death: The king orders Ebed-melech to bring Jeremiah out of the pit so that he does not die. Proverbs commands rescuing those led to death and ransoming those being killed. The source gives a particular intervention, including thirty men, rather than those details being supplied by the proverb.

Jeremiah 45:13
They pulled him with the ropes and brought him up from the pit. Jeremiah remained in the prison court.
  Psalms 39:3 — close imagery of rescue from a pit and muddy confinement: Jeremiah is pulled out of the pit whose mire was described earlier. The psalm praises God for bringing the speaker out of a pit of misery and muddy slime. The source names a physical rescue by ropes; the comparison does not identify the psalmist with Jeremiah or the two passages as one event.

Jeremiah 45:14
The king sent and summoned him to himself, to the aselisi house in the Lord’s house. The king said to him, I will ask you something. Please hide no word from me.
  1 Samuel 3:17-18 — parallel demand that the recipient of a divine word conceal nothing: Zedekiah tells Jeremiah to hide no word from him. Eli similarly asks Samuel what was spoken and insists that no word be hidden; Samuel reports it all. The addressees and settings differ, while the demand for complete prophetic disclosure closely corresponds.

Jeremiah 45:15
Jeremiah said to the king, If I tell you, will you not certainly put me to death? And if I advise you, you certainly will not listen to me.
  1 Kings 22:17-18 — related danger of giving a king unwelcome prophecy: Jeremiah fears death if he answers and says the king will not heed counsel. Micaiah's adverse vision prompts the king's complaint that he prophesies only bad things. The surrounding target account later imprisons Micaiah; it does not say that the king kills him.

Jeremiah 45:16
The king swore to him, As the Lord lives, who made this life for us, I will not kill you or hand you over to these men.
  1 Samuel 19:6 — close royal oath by the living Lord not to kill a threatened servant: Zedekiah swears by the living Lord that he will not kill Jeremiah or hand him to the men. Saul likewise swears, As the Lord lives, that David will not die. The source additionally describes the Lord as the maker of their life.

Jeremiah 45:17
Jeremiah said to him, Thus said the Lord: If you go out to Babylon’s king’s commanders, your life will be spared and this city will certainly not be burned with fire. You and your household will live.
  Jeremiah 21:9 — close application of the surrender-and-life promise to the king: The source tells Zedekiah that going out to Babylon's commanders will preserve his life and household and prevent the city's burning. The earlier oracle offers life to anyone going out to the Chaldeans. Jeremiah here applies the same surrender condition to the king with further consequences for household and city.

Jeremiah 45:18
But if you do not go out, this city will be handed over to the Chaldeans and they will burn it with fire. You certainly will not be saved.
  Jeremiah 21:10 — close threatened surrender and burning of Jerusalem: The source warns that refusal to go out will leave the city to Chaldean capture and fire. The target likewise announces its handover to Babylon's king and burning. The source expressly frames that outcome against the preceding offered alternative.

Jeremiah 45:19
The king said to Jeremiah, I am concerned about the Judeans who have fled to the Chaldeans, lest they hand me over to them and mock me.
  Proverbs 29:25 — related fear of people contrasted with trust in God: Zedekiah fears being handed to Judean deserters and mocked. Proverbs describes people being tripped up by fear and shame before others, contrasting them with the person who trusts in the Lord. The following source answer offers divine reassurance; the proverb does not name these deserters.

Jeremiah 45:20
Jeremiah said, They certainly will not hand you over. Hear the Lord’s word which I speak to you, and it will be better for you; your life will be spared.
  Isaiah 1:19-20 — parallel contrast between hearing God for good and refusing Him unto harm: Jeremiah urges the king to hear the Lord's word so that it will be better for him and his life spared. Isaiah joins willing hearing with the land's good things and unwilling refusal with the sword. The immediate promised benefits differ, but the contrast of responses is shared.

Jeremiah 45:22
All the women left in Judah’s king’s house were being brought out to Babylon’s king’s rulers. They said, Your peaceful friends deceived you and will overpower you. They will bring your feet to rest in slippery places; they turned away from you.
  Obadiah 1:7 — close betrayal by those formerly described as peaceful allies: The women say the king's peaceful friends deceived him and will overpower him; they also describe those friends turning away. Obadiah says covenant allies and men at peace oppose and prevail over the addressee and set ambushes. Both expose trusted peaceful relationships becoming hostile; Obadiah does not use the source's slippery-feet wording.

Jeremiah 45:23
They will bring your wives and children out to the Chaldeans. You certainly will not be saved, for Babylon’s king will seize you, and this city will be burned.
  Jeremiah 52:8-13 — close prediction and narrative counterpart of royal capture and city burning: The oracle predicts the king's seizure and Jerusalem's burning, alongside the removal of his household. The closing narrative records his capture and the subsequent burning of the houses. It gives additional details of his sons' deaths and his blinding rather than repeating the source's whole household prediction.

Jeremiah 45:26
tell them, I am casting my appeal for mercy before the king’s eyes, not to send me back to Jonathan’s house to die there.
  Jeremiah 44:20 — close prescribed report of an earlier prison appeal: The king tells Jeremiah to report his plea not to return to Jonathan's house to die. The earlier account records Jeremiah making that appeal. The source supplies what is to be told to the rulers, while the target contains the direct petition to the king.

Jeremiah 45:28
Jeremiah remained in the prison court until the time Jerusalem was captured.
  Jeremiah 46:14 — close end of confinement followed by removal from the prison court: Jeremiah remains in the prison court until Jerusalem is taken. The subsequent account records his removal from that court and delivery to Gedaliah, after which he lives among the people. The connection traces the stated endpoint of his confinement without supplying an unstated exact release date.

Jeremiah 46:1
In the ninth year of Zedekiah king of Judah, in the tenth month, Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon and his whole force arrived against Jerusalem and besieged it.
  Jeremiah 52:4 — close parallel dating of Nebuchadnezzar's siege: Both accounts place Nebuchadnezzar's arrival with his force against Jerusalem in Zedekiah's ninth year and tenth month. The closing account additionally supplies the tenth day and describes the enclosing works.

Jeremiah 46:2
In Zedekiah’s eleventh year, on the ninth day of the fourth month, the city was breached.
  Jeremiah 52:5-7 — close parallel breach of Jerusalem at the siege's end: The source dates the breach to Zedekiah's eleventh year and the ninth day of the fourth month. The closing account gives the eleventh-year end of the siege, the ninth day and the breach, but does not repeat the fourth-month designation in those units.

Jeremiah 46:3
All Babylon’s king’s commanders entered and sat at the Middle Gate: Nargalasar, Samagoth, Nabousachar, Nabousaris, Nagargasnaser Rabamag, and the remaining commanders of Babylon’s king.
  Jeremiah 1:15 — related foreign rulers occupying Jerusalem's gates: The Babylonian commanders enter and sit at the Middle Gate. The opening oracle predicts northern kingdoms setting their thrones at Jerusalem's gate forecourts. The narrative supplies a particular gate occupation, without expressly calling the commanders' seats thrones.

Jeremiah 46:14
They sent and took Jeremiah from the prison court and handed him over to Gedaliah son of Ahikam son of Shaphan. They brought him out, and he lived among the people.
  Jeremiah 47:4-6 — close linked accounts of Jeremiah's release and residence with Gedaliah: The source records Jeremiah being removed from the prison court, handed to Gedaliah and living among the people. The next account describes release from handcuffs, permission to return to Gedaliah and residence with him at Mizpah. The distinct custody details are preserved rather than collapsed.

Jeremiah 46:16
Go and tell Ebed-melech the Ethiopian, Thus said the Lord, Israel’s God: I am bringing My words upon this city for calamity, not good.
  Jeremiah 45:7-13 — close identification of the Ethiopian addressed after rescuing Jeremiah: The oracle addresses Ebed-melech the Ethiopian in the setting of the city's coming calamity. The preceding chapter identifies him by the same name and origin and narrates his intervention to bring Jeremiah out of the pit. This supplies the recipient's particular narrative background.

Jeremiah 46:17
I will save you that day and certainly will not hand you over to the men you fear.
  Psalms 90:14-15 — related divine assurance of rescue to one who trusts: Ebed-melech is promised deliverance from the men he fears; the following source unit grounds the promise in his trust. The psalm promises rescue and presence in affliction to one who hopes in God. It is not an identification of the psalm's beneficiary with Ebed-melech.

Jeremiah 46:18
I will certainly save you. You certainly will not fall by the sword; your life will be your gain because you trusted in Me, says the Lord.
  Jeremiah 51:35 — close personal promises that preserved life will be one's gain: Both assurances promise life as gain amid announced calamity. This source addresses Ebed-melech and explicitly grounds rescue in trust; the later oracle addresses Baruch while warning him against seeking great things for himself.

Jeremiah 47:1
The word that came from the Lord to Jeremiah afterward, after Nebuzaradan, the chief guard from Daman, sent him away, when he took him in handcuffs among Judah’s exiles who were being led to Babylon.
  Jeremiah 46:14 — related notices of Jeremiah being released from custody: The source introduces the chief guard's release of Jeremiah from handcuffs among the deportees. The preceding narrative records his removal from the prison court and placement with Gedaliah. The comparison concerns the named prophet's liberation and does not replace the native Daman wording with another place-name.

Jeremiah 47:2
The chief guard took him and said to him, The Lord your God spoke these calamities against this place.
  Jeremiah 21:10 — related acknowledgment of calamity previously announced against the city: The chief guard acknowledges that the Lord spoke the calamities against the place. Jeremiah's earlier oracle explicitly announces the city's surrender to Babylon's king and burning. The target supplies a specific earlier announcement rather than words quoted verbatim by the guard.

Jeremiah 47:3
And the Lord acted, because you sinned against Him and did not obey His voice.
  Deuteronomy 29:24-27 — close explanation of national calamity as covenant disobedience: The guard says the Lord acted because the people sinned and did not obey His voice. Deuteronomy explains judgment on the land and removal to another land by abandonment of God's covenant and service to other gods. Both attribute the disaster to disobedience, with Deuteronomy specifying its idolatrous form.

Jeremiah 47:5
But if not, go off and return to Gedaliah son of Ahikam son of Shaphan, whom Babylon’s king appointed in Judah’s land, and live with him among the people in Judah’s land. Go wherever it seems good in your eyes to go. The chief guard gave him gifts and sent him away.
  2 Kings 25:22 — close parallel appointment of a governor over Judah's remnant: Jeremiah's account names Gedaliah son of Ahikam son of Shaphan as Babylon's king's appointee in Judah. Kings names Godolias son of Achikam son of Saphan in the same governing role over the people left in the land. The source additionally gives Jeremiah permission to live with him; the supplied name forms remain distinct in the quotations.

Jeremiah 47:6
He came to Gedaliah at Mizpah and lived among the people left in the land.
  2 Kings 25:23 — related residence and assembly at the governor's settlement: Jeremiah comes to Gedaliah at Mizpah and lives among those remaining. Kings describes the military commanders coming to Godolias at Massephath. The target supplies another gathering around the governor; it does not itself mention Jeremiah's residence.

Jeremiah 47:7
All the commanders of the force in the countryside, they and their men, heard that Babylon’s king had appointed Gedaliah in the land and had entrusted to him men and their wives whom he had not deported to Babylon.
  2 Kings 25:22-23 — close parallel report of the governor's appointment reaching the commanders: Both accounts describe Babylon's king appointing a governor over the remaining population and the commanders hearing of the appointment. Jeremiah additionally specifies the entrusted men and wives who had not been deported.

Jeremiah 47:8
Ishmael son of Nethaniah, Johanan son of Kareah, Seraiah son of Tanhumeth, the sons of Ophe the Netophathite, and Jezaniah son of the Maacathite came to Gedaliah at Mizpah, they and their men.
  2 Kings 25:23 — close parallel lists of commanders coming to the appointed governor: Both accounts list Ishmael, Johanan or Joanan, Seraiah or Saraias, and Jezaniah or Jezonias among those coming with their men to the governor. The supplied patronymics and the placement of the Netophathite description differ; the comparison does not make the lists identical.

Jeremiah 47:9
Gedaliah swore to them and their men, saying, Do not be afraid of the Chaldeans’ servants. Live in the land and work for Babylon’s king, and it will be better for you.
  2 Kings 25:24 — close parallel oath urging settlement and service to Babylon's king: Both speeches reassure the commanders and their men concerning the Chaldeans and urge remaining in the land and serving Babylon's king for their good. Jeremiah says the Chaldeans' servants, whereas Kings says the passing of the Chaldeans; that wording difference is preserved.

Jeremiah 47:10
Behold, I am staying before you at Mizpah to stand before the Chaldeans who come against you. Gather wine and summer fruit, gather oil, put them in your containers, and live in the cities you have taken over.
  Jeremiah 36:5-7 — related counsel to sustain settled life under Babylonian rule: Gedaliah tells the remnant to gather produce, store it and inhabit their towns while he stays at Mizpah. The letter to the deportees instructs building houses, planting gardens, eating their produce and seeking the land's peace. The source concerns Judah's remnant and the target the deportees, not one location.

Jeremiah 47:12
They came to Gedaliah in Judah’s land at Mizpah and gathered a very great amount of wine and summer fruit, and oil.
  Jeremiah 36:5 — related agricultural provision after displacement: Those returning to Gedaliah gather much wine, summer fruit and oil in Judah. The exiles' letter commands planting gardens and eating their fruit. The shared feature is settled provision from produce amid displacement; the target does not report this harvest or its abundance.

Jeremiah 47:14
They said to him, Do you really know that Belisa king of Ammon’s people has sent Ishmael to you to take your life? But Gedaliah did not believe them.
  Jeremiah 48:1-3 — close warning of Ishmael's murderous intent followed by the attack: The commanders warn that Ishmael intends to take Gedaliah's life, although Gedaliah disbelieves them. The next chapter records Ishmael and his men attacking Gedaliah and those with him. It confirms the threatened attack without independently establishing the alleged commission from Ammon's king.

Jeremiah 47:15
Johanan spoke secretly to Gedaliah at Mizpah: Let me go and strike down Ishmael, and let no one know, lest he take your life and all Judah gathered to you be scattered, and Judah’s survivors perish.
  Jeremiah 48:10 — related anticipated danger to the gathered remnant and its later seizure: Johanan warns that Gedaliah's death would scatter the people gathered to him. The later narrative records Ishmael taking away the remaining people at Mizpah. This supports the particular danger to the settlement without endorsing Johanan's proposed preemptive killing or claiming it occurred.

Jeremiah 47:16
Gedaliah said to Johanan, Do not do this thing, for you are speaking falsehoods about Ishmael.
  Jeremiah 48:1-2 — close disbelief of a warning contradicted by the ensuing assault: Gedaliah calls Johanan's report about Ishmael false and forbids action against him. The ensuing account names Ishmael as the one who rises with his men and strikes Gedaliah. The narrative outcome directly bears on the disputed warning.

Jeremiah 48:1
In the seventh month, Ishmael son of Nethaniah son of Elasa, of the king’s family, came with ten men to Gedaliah at Mizpah. They ate bread together there.
  2 Kings 25:25 — close parallel arrival of Ishmael with ten men in the seventh month: Both accounts place Ishmael's arrival with ten men and royal-family affiliation in the seventh month. Jeremiah names the grandfather Elasa and Kings Elisama; Jeremiah additionally records eating bread together. Those details are not silently harmonized.
  Psalms 40:10 — related treachery in a relationship of shared bread: Ishmael eats bread with Gedaliah before the attack recorded next. The psalm laments treachery by a trusted man of peace who ate the speaker's loaves. The connection concerns betrayed table fellowship, not an identification of the psalmist or its unnamed companion.

Jeremiah 48:2
Ishmael and the ten men with him rose and struck Gedaliah, whom Babylon’s king had appointed over the land,
  2 Kings 25:25 — close parallel attack by Ishmael and his ten companions on the governor: Both accounts describe Ishmael and ten men striking Babylon's appointed governor, named Gedaliah in Jeremiah and Godolias in Kings. Kings expressly adds that the governor died; the surrounding Jeremiah narrative likewise treats the attack as his killing.

Jeremiah 48:3
and all the Judeans with him at Mizpah, and all the Chaldeans found there.
  2 Kings 25:25 — close parallel inclusion of Judean and Chaldean victims: Jeremiah includes the Judeans with the governor at Mizpah and the Chaldeans found there among the victims. Kings likewise names Judeans and Chaldeans with the governor at Massephath. The shared victim groups are more specific than a general account of violence.

Jeremiah 48:5
eighty men came from Shechem, Salem, and Samaria, with shaved beards and torn clothes, beating themselves, carrying grain offerings and frankincense to bring to the Lord’s house.
  Leviticus 2:1-2 — related grain offering accompanied by frankincense: The mourners carry grain offerings and frankincense to the Lord's house. Leviticus prescribes a flour offering with oil and frankincense, presented to the priests. The target explains the particular offering combination; it does not supply the source's eighty men or their mourning actions.

Jeremiah 48:6
Ishmael went out to meet them. They were walking and weeping, and he said to them, Come in to Gedaliah.
  2 Samuel 3:26-27 — related invitation or private approach preceding concealed murderous intent: Ishmael invites the approaching mourners to Gedaliah although Gedaliah is already dead; the next unit records their slaughter. Joab has Abner brought back and draws him aside to speak while lying in wait, then strikes him. The invitations and victims differ, but deceptive approach precedes killing in both.

Jeremiah 48:8
Ten men found there said to Ishmael, Do not kill us, for we have stores in the countryside: wheat, barley, honey, and oil. He passed them by and did not kill them among their brothers.
  Proverbs 13:8 — close illustration of wealth serving as ransom for life: Ten men disclose stores of grain, honey and oil, and Ishmael spares them. Proverbs says a man's own wealth is the ransom of his life. The narrative supplies a particular use of valuable provisions to avert death without approving the killer's conduct.

Jeremiah 48:9
The cistern into which Ishmael threw all those he had struck down is a large cistern that King Asa made because of Baasha king of Israel. Ishmael filled it with the slain.
  2 Chronicles 16:1-6 — related Asa-Baasha conflict and Asa's construction at the remnant's settlement: Jeremiah attributes the large cistern to Asa because of Baasha. Chronicles narrates their conflict and Asa's subsequent building of Gabae and Maspha from Rama's materials. It supplies the named royal conflict and construction background but does not mention this cistern or establish its particular dimensions.

Jeremiah 48:10
Ishmael turned back all the people left at Mizpah and the king’s daughters whom the chief guard had entrusted to Gedaliah son of Ahikam, and he went away to the far side of Ammon’s people.
  Jeremiah 47:15 — close realization of the warned danger to Gedaliah's gathered people: Ishmael takes away the people left at Mizpah, including the king's daughters. Johanan's earlier warning feared the scattering of the people gathered to Gedaliah after his death. The target anticipates the settlement's vulnerability rather than describing every detail of the later abduction.

Jeremiah 48:11
Johanan son of Kareah and all the commanders of the force with him heard of all the evils Ishmael had done.
  Jeremiah 47:14-15 — related earlier warning by the commanders who later hear of the crimes: Johanan and his fellow commanders hear what Ishmael has done. The preceding account records their warning to Gedaliah and Johanan's attempt to prevent the threatened killing. The correspondence identifies their particular prior involvement, not merely another report of violence.

Jeremiah 48:12
They led their entire army and went away to fight him. They found him beside abundant water at Gibeon.
  2 Samuel 2:12-13 — related military encounters beside Gibeon's water: Johanan's force finds Ishmael beside abundant water at Gibeon. Samuel locates the meeting of Abner's and Joab's forces at Gibeon's spring. These are distinct military encounters; Jeremiah does not specify that its abundant water is that same spring.

Jeremiah 48:14
they returned to Johanan.
  1 Samuel 30:18-19 — parallel recovery of people taken captive: The people taken with Ishmael return to Johanan. Samuel describes David recovering the people and possessions taken by the Amalekites. The common result is recovery from captivity; the source does not narrate David's method of battle or its spoils.

Jeremiah 48:15
Ishmael escaped with eight men and went away to Ammon’s people.
  Jeremiah 47:14 — related flight to Ammon after an alleged Ammonite connection: Ishmael escapes to Ammon's people with eight men. The earlier warning alleges that Ammon's king sent him against Gedaliah. His destination is relevant to that allegation but is not independent proof of the king's commission.

Jeremiah 48:16
Johanan and all the commanders of the force with him took all the people left whom he had brought back from Ishmael: men mighty in war, the women, the rest, and the eunuchs whom he brought back from Gibeon.
  1 Samuel 30:18-19 — parallel restoration of an abducted group including women: Johanan takes charge of the recovered people, including women, men and eunuchs. David recovers the captives, with his wives, sons and daughters expressly mentioned. The recovered groups are differently described; their lists and numbers are not equated.

Jeremiah 48:17
They went away and stayed at Gaberoth-chamaam near Bethlehem, intending to go into Egypt,
  Jeremiah 50:5-7 — close intended migration to Egypt followed by its narrated execution: The survivors stay near Bethlehem intending to enter Egypt. The later account records Johanan taking Judah's survivors into Egypt and reaching Taphnas. The target supplies the stated journey's outcome, not a different name for the source's stopping place.

Jeremiah 48:18
away from the Chaldeans, for they feared them because Ishmael had struck Gedaliah, whom Babylon’s king had appointed in the land.
  2 Kings 25:25-26 — close explanation of flight to Egypt after the governor's murder: Jeremiah says the survivors fear the Chaldeans because Ishmael killed Babylon's appointed governor. Kings places the governor's killing immediately before the people and commanders enter Egypt for fear of the Chaldeans. The same sequence of murder, fear and migration is explicit.

Jeremiah 49:2
Jeremiah the prophet and said to him, Please let our appeal for mercy come before you. Pray to the Lord your God for these survivors, for few of us remain out of many, as your eyes see.
  1 Samuel 12:19 — close communal request for a prophet's intercession amid danger: Judah's few survivors ask Jeremiah to pray to the Lord his God for them. Samuel's people similarly ask him to pray to the Lord his God so that they do not die. The requests share prophetic intercession for an endangered community, without identical circumstances or numbers.

Jeremiah 49:3
Let the Lord your God tell us the way we should go and the matter we should carry out.
  Psalms 24:4-5 — close request that God disclose the way to follow: The people ask God to tell them the way they should go and what they should do. The psalm asks the Lord to make His ways known, teach His paths and guide the speaker in truth. One request concerns a community's immediate decision; the other is a personal prayer for guidance.

Jeremiah 49:4
Jeremiah said to them, I have heard. I will pray to the Lord our God as you have said. Whatever word the Lord answers, I will tell you; I certainly will not hide a word from you.
  1 Samuel 3:18 — parallel commitment to report the whole divine word without concealment: Jeremiah promises to tell whatever the Lord answers and hide no word. Samuel reports all the words and hides nothing from Eli. The source is a prospective commitment; the target narrates its performance in a different prophetic setting.
  1 Samuel 12:23 — parallel prophetic undertaking to pray for the people: Jeremiah agrees to pray to the Lord for those requesting intercession. Samuel declares that he must not cease praying for the people and will show them the good way. This target specifically supports the intercessory undertaking, distinct from the source's promise of complete disclosure.

Jeremiah 49:5
They said to Jeremiah, May the Lord be a righteous and faithful witness among us if we do not act according to every word with which the Lord sends you to us.
  Genesis 31:44 — parallel invocation of God as witness to a solemn undertaking: The people invoke the Lord as a righteous and faithful witness to their promise to act on His message. The covenant between Jacob and Laban explicitly invokes God as witness between them. Their obligations differ, but both place an undertaking before God's witness.

Jeremiah 49:6
Whether good or bad, we will obey the voice of the Lord our God, to whom we are sending you, so that it may be better for us, because we will obey the voice of the Lord our God.
  Exodus 24:7 — close public pledge to do and obey everything God speaks: The survivors promise to obey whether the answer is good or bad. At the covenant-book reading the people likewise promise to do and obey everything the Lord has spoken. The source makes the unconditional character of its pledged response especially explicit.
  Jeremiah 50:2-4 — close contrast between pledged obedience and subsequent refusal: The people promise obedience to whatever divine answer comes. The ensuing narrative records named leaders rejecting Jeremiah's answer and the people refusing to remain in Judah. This gives the later outcome of the particular undertaking rather than a generic warning about disobedience.

Jeremiah 49:10
If you remain in this land, I will build you up and certainly not tear you down; I will plant you and certainly not pluck you up. For I have rested from the calamities I brought upon you.
  Jeremiah 24:6 — close assurance of building and planting rather than destroying: Both promises use rebuilding without tearing down and planting without plucking out. The source conditions this assurance on the survivors remaining in the land; the earlier fig oracle concerns restoration of the deportees. The source's statement that God has rested from the calamities is not replaced with another expression.

Jeremiah 49:11
Do not fear Babylon’s king, whom you fear. Do not fear, says the Lord, for I am with you to rescue you and save you from his hand.
  Jeremiah 1:8 — close divine reassurance of presence and rescue against feared people: The survivors are told not to fear Babylon's king because God is with them to rescue and save them. Jeremiah's commission likewise says not to fear those addressed because God is with him to rescue him. The beneficiary and immediate threat differ.

Jeremiah 49:12
I will grant you mercy, have mercy on you, and return you to your land.
  Psalms 105:45-47 — related divine compassion and restoration: God promises mercy and return to the land. The psalm recalls His covenant mercy and granted compassion before captors, then asks for gathering from the nations. The source does not expressly make Babylon's king the agent of its promised mercy.

Jeremiah 49:13
But if you say, We certainly will not remain in this land, so as not to obey the Lord’s voice,
  Jeremiah 50:4 — close warned refusal to remain followed by recorded disobedience: The oracle frames refusal to remain in the land as refusal to obey the Lord. The later narrative explicitly says Johanan, the commanders and the people did not obey by remaining in Judah. The target records the conduct anticipated in the warning.

Jeremiah 49:14
because we will enter Egypt’s land, certainly not see war or hear the trumpet’s sound or hunger for bread, and there we will live—
  Isaiah 30:2-3 — parallel misplaced hope for security in Egypt: The survivors imagine Egypt as a place without war, trumpets or hunger. Isaiah warns that seeking Pharaoh's help and Egyptian shelter will bring shame and reproach. Isaiah says its people did not ask God; Jeremiah's people asked and then reject the answer, so their procedures are not equated.

Jeremiah 49:16
the sword you fear will find you in Egypt’s land, and the famine you are concerned about will catch up with you from behind in Egypt, and there you will die.
  Amos 5:19 — parallel failure to escape danger by fleeing elsewhere: Sword and famine will meet the survivors in the Egypt to which they flee for safety. Amos pictures flight from a lion leading to a bear and apparent safety in a house ending in a snakebite. The source is a concrete destination warning, while the target expresses the futility of escape through images.

Jeremiah 49:17
All the people and all the foreigners who set their faces toward Egypt’s land to live there will perish by sword and famine. None of them will be saved from the calamities I am bringing upon them.
  Jeremiah 51:12-13 — close repeated judgment upon those settling in Egypt: The source threatens those who set their faces toward Egypt with sword and famine. The later oracle likewise announces destruction of the Egyptian remnant through sword and famine, adding death in its comparison with Jerusalem. The source expressly includes foreigners as well as the people.

Jeremiah 49:18
For thus said the Lord: As My wrath dripped upon Jerusalem’s inhabitants, so My wrath will drip upon you when you enter Egypt. You will become an untrodden place, subject to others, a curse, and a reproach, and you certainly will never see this place again.
  Jeremiah 51:13 — close comparison of Egypt's coming judgment with Jerusalem's: Both announcements apply to the emigrants in Egypt a judgment corresponding to what already struck Jerusalem. The source describes wrath and a resulting curse and reproach; the target names sword, famine and death.

Jeremiah 49:19
These are the things the Lord spoke concerning you, Judah’s survivors: Do not enter Egypt. And now know for certain
  Jeremiah 50:7 — close prohibition of entering Egypt and its subsequent violation: The Lord explicitly says not to enter Egypt. The later narrative says they entered Egypt because they had not obeyed His voice and identifies Taphnas as their destination. The target supplies the specific disobedience to this command.

Jeremiah 49:20
that you acted wickedly in your souls when you sent me, saying, Pray for us to the Lord, and we will do everything the Lord tells you.
  Ezekiel 33:30-32 — related professed desire for God's word without doing it: Jeremiah condemns the people's request for prayer and promise to do the answer in light of their actual refusal. Ezekiel describes people eagerly gathering to hear a word from the Lord yet refusing to do it. Both distinguish an appearance of receptive listening from the response in action.

Jeremiah 49:21
And you did not obey the Lord’s voice, with which He sent me to you.
  Jeremiah 50:4 — close charge of disobedience and its narrated form: Jeremiah says the people have not obeyed the voice with which the Lord sent him. The ensuing account identifies their disobedience as refusal to remain in Judah. The target specifies the action at issue in the source's accusation.

Jeremiah 49:22
And now you will perish by sword and famine in the place where you want to go and live.
  Jeremiah 51:12 — close repeated threat of death by sword and famine in Egypt: The source says the people will die by sword and famine in the place where they want to settle, identified as Egypt in the preceding warning. The later oracle explicitly names the remnant in Egypt and the same two instruments of death.

Jeremiah 50:2
Azariah son of Maaseiah, Johanan son of Kareah, and all the men who spoke said to Jeremiah, You are lying! The Lord did not send you to us to say, Do not enter Egypt to live there.
  1 Kings 22:24 — related challenge to an unwelcome prophet's divine authority: The leaders call Jeremiah's message a lie and deny that the Lord sent it. Zedekiah son of Chenaanah challenges what sort of the Lord's spirit spoke in Micaiah and strikes him. The forms of opposition differ, while both dispute the claimed divine source of adverse prophecy.

Jeremiah 50:3
Rather, Baruch son of Neriah is setting you against us, so that you will hand us over to the Chaldeans, to put us to death and have us deported to Babylon.
  Jeremiah 43:4 — contrast between alleged influence and Baruch's recorded writing role: The leaders accuse Baruch of setting Jeremiah against them. The earlier account explicitly describes Baruch writing from Jeremiah's mouth the words God had delivered to the prophet. That recorded scribal role is relevant to the allegation without independently establishing every motive attributed to Baruch here.

Jeremiah 50:4
Johanan, all the commanders of the force, and all the people did not obey the Lord’s voice by remaining in Judah’s land.
  Jeremiah 49:19 — close refusal of the specific command against entering Egypt: The narrative says the commanders and people did not obey by remaining in Judah. The preceding oracle expressly forbids the survivors to enter Egypt. This is the particular divine instruction against which the conduct is measured.

Jeremiah 50:5
Johanan and all the commanders of the force took all Judah’s survivors who had returned to live in the land:
  Jeremiah 47:11-12 — related movement of the survivors who had returned to Judah: Johanan takes the survivors who had returned to live in the land. The earlier account describes Judeans returning from neighboring countries to Gedaliah in Judah. It supplies the preceding return movement without asserting that its list identifies every individual now departing.

Jeremiah 50:6
the mighty men, the women, the little children, the king’s daughters, the people Nebuzaradan had left with Gedaliah son of Ahikam, and Jeremiah the prophet and Baruch son of Neriah.
  Jeremiah 48:10 — close identification of the population entrusted to Gedaliah: Both passages name the king's daughters and the people entrusted by the chief guard to Gedaliah. The source also explicitly includes Jeremiah and Baruch; the target does not name those two among Ishmael's captives.

Jeremiah 50:7
They entered Egypt, for they had not obeyed the Lord’s voice. They entered Taphnas.
  2 Kings 25:26 — close parallel account of the survivors' entry into Egypt: Both narratives describe the people and military commanders entering Egypt after the governor's murder. Kings expressly gives fear of the Chaldeans; Jeremiah stresses disobedience to God's voice and names Taphnas. The stated explanations are preserved rather than substituted for each other.

Jeremiah 50:8
The Lord’s word came to Jeremiah at Taphnas:
  Ezekiel 30:18 — related Egyptian oracle settings at Taphnas and Tahpanhes: Jeremiah receives the word at Taphnas that introduces the following sign of Babylonian domination. Ezekiel names Tahpanhes in an oracle of Egypt's shattered power and captivity. The distinct supplied place-name forms are preserved, and the source introduction is not made to contain Ezekiel's darkness imagery.

Jeremiah 50:9
Take large stones for yourself and hide them in the forecourt at the gate of Pharaoh’s house at Taphnas, before the eyes of Judah’s men.
  Jeremiah 19:10-11 — parallel public material sign interpreted as coming judgment: Jeremiah is told to hide stones before the Judeans' eyes, with their meaning stated in the next unit. The earlier command breaks a jar before accompanying men and explains it as the people's and city's breaking. The prophetic method is shared; the objects and predicted actions differ.

Jeremiah 50:10
Say, Thus said the Lord: I am sending and will bring Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon. He will place his throne above these stones that you hid and raise his weapons over them.
  Ezekiel 29:19-20 — close prophecy that Nebuchadnezzar will receive Egypt: Jeremiah announces Nebuchadnezzar's arrival and throne above the hidden stones in Egypt. Ezekiel likewise names Nebuchadnezzar as the recipient of Egypt from God, there as wages for his force. The target does not mention Jeremiah's stones or throne-sign.

Jeremiah 50:11
He will enter and strike Egypt’s land: those for death, to death; those for deportation, to deportation; and those for the sword, to the sword.
  Jeremiah 15:2 — close formula assigning people to death, sword or captivity: Both declarations distribute people to their appointed forms of judgment using the repeated construction those for a fate, to that fate. Jeremiah 15 includes famine as well; the source names death, deportation and sword in its Egyptian setting.

Jeremiah 50:12
He will kindle fire in the houses of their gods, burn the houses, and deport the gods. He will delouse Egypt’s land as a shepherd delouses his garment, and he will go out in peace.
  Exodus 12:12 — related judgment upon Egypt's gods in different historical settings: The source predicts burning the gods' houses and deporting the gods during the Babylonian attack. Exodus announces the Lord's vengeance upon Egypt's gods in the Passover setting. The agents, actions and occasions differ; both specifically include Egyptian deities within judgment.
  Isaiah 46:1-2 — related humiliation of deities carried into captivity: Jeremiah's oracle includes the deportation of Egypt's gods. Isaiah depicts the images of Bel and Dagon as burdens and says they themselves were led captive. This connection concerns divine images' captivity and does not identify those named images with the source's Egyptian gods or reinterpret its delousing simile.

Jeremiah 50:13
He will shatter the pillars of the City of the Sun, those in On, and burn their houses with fire.
  Exodus 34:13 — related destruction of cultic pillars and shrines: Jeremiah predicts the shattering of the City of the Sun's pillars and burning of the houses. Exodus commands the breaking of pillars and destruction of other cultic objects in the land Israel is entering. This is a comparison of destructive treatment of rival cults, not an assertion that the commands describe the same place or army.

Jeremiah 51:1
The word that came to Jeremiah for all the Judeans living in Egypt’s land and those staying at Migdol, Taphnas, and in Pathoures’s land:
  Jeremiah 50:7 — related setting of Judean survivors in Egyptian Taphnas: The oracle addresses Judean communities in Egypt, including Taphnas. The preceding narrative records Johanan's party entering Egypt and reaching Taphnas. The target accounts for one stated destination without placing all the source's other communities there.

Jeremiah 51:2
Thus said the Lord, Israel’s God: You have seen all the calamities I brought upon Jerusalem and Judah’s cities. Behold, they are deserted of inhabitants,
  Lamentations 1:1 — close recollection of Jerusalem's desolation after captivity: Jeremiah points to the devastation and emptied cities already visible in Judah. Lamentations opens with Israel taken captive, Jerusalem laid waste and the once populous city sitting alone. The lament specifically supports the Jerusalem portion of the source's wider judgment on Judah's cities.

Jeremiah 51:3
because of the wickedness they committed to embitter Me, going to burn incense to other gods whom you did not know.
  Deuteronomy 32:16-17 — close provocation through worship of previously unknown gods: The source attributes the calamity to provoking God by incense to other gods whom the people did not know. Deuteronomy speaks of provocation by foreign gods and sacrifice to gods they did not know. The forms of ritual action differ, while the unknown foreign recipients and divine provocation correspond.

Jeremiah 51:4
I sent you My servants the prophets at dawn, and I sent them saying, Do not commit this act of defilement that I hate.
  Jeremiah 7:25 — close repeated sending of God's prophetic servants from dawn: Both passages describe God sending His servants the prophets at dawn. This source adds the particular prohibition of the hated act of defilement; the target places the repeated sending across the period since the exodus.

Jeremiah 51:5
But they did not listen to Me or incline their ear to turn from their evils and stop burning incense to other gods.
  2 Chronicles 36:15-16 — parallel rejection of repeatedly sent divine messengers: Jeremiah says the people did not listen or turn from incense to other gods. Chronicles describes God's repeated sending of prophets and the people's disregard of His words and mockery of His messengers. The source specifies the ritual practice from which they refused to turn.

Jeremiah 51:6
My anger and wrath dripped out and blazed in Judah’s cities and outside Jerusalem, and they became a desolation and an untrodden place, as on this day.
  Lamentations 4:11 — related divine wrath and destructive fire: Jeremiah says wrath dripped out and blazed in Judah's cities and outside Jerusalem. Lamentations describes poured-out fury and fire kindled in Zion consuming her foundations. The shared wrath-and-fire imagery connects the city's destruction with divine judgment.

Jeremiah 51:7
Now thus said the Lord Almighty: Why are you doing great evils against yourselves, cutting off from yourselves man and woman, little child and nursing infant from Judah, so that none of you remains,
  Jeremiah 7:19 — close rhetorical charge that idolatrous provocation harms the people themselves: The source asks why the people do great evils against themselves, cutting off their households. The earlier oracle asks whether their provocative worship harms God or themselves to their shame. Both turn attention from the purported benefit of the cult to the worshipers' own harm.

Jeremiah 51:8
embittering Me with the work of your hands by burning incense to other gods in Egypt’s land, which you entered to live there, so that you are cut off and become a curse and a reproach among all the earth’s nations?
  Jeremiah 25:6 — close warning against provoking God through service to foreign gods: The source connects the works of their hands and incense to other gods with their own cutting off and reproach. Jeremiah 25 forbids service and worship of foreign gods so as not to provoke God through their hands' works into harming them. Neither comparison requires treating every mention of hands' works as an expressly described manufactured statue.

Jeremiah 51:9
Have you forgotten your fathers’ evils, the evils of Judah’s kings, your rulers’ evils, and your wives’ evils, which they committed in Judah’s land and outside Jerusalem?
  Zechariah 1:2-4 — related warning not to repeat the fathers' evil: Jeremiah asks whether the earlier generations' and rulers' evils have been forgotten. Zechariah recalls God's anger with the fathers and forbids becoming like those who ignored calls to turn from evil. The source includes wives and rulers in addition to the fathers.

Jeremiah 51:10
They have not stopped to this day and have not held fast to My ordinances, which I set before their fathers.
  Jeremiah 7:23-24 — close failure to heed the ordinances given to the fathers: The source says the people have not stopped their practices or held fast to the ordinances set before the fathers. The earlier passage recalls the command to hear and walk in God's ways and the fathers' refusal. Both describe persistence in the opposite course to the received command.

Jeremiah 51:11
Therefore thus said the Lord: I am setting My face
  Leviticus 20:3-5 — related image of God setting His face in destructive judgment: The source states only that God is setting His face; its following unit supplies destruction of the Egyptian remnant. Leviticus explicitly sets God's face against a man and his kin in judgment. The connection concerns this image of divine opposition, not an identification of the different offenses.

Jeremiah 51:12
to destroy all the survivors in Egypt, and they will fall. By sword and famine they will perish, from the least to the greatest, and become a reproach, a destruction, and a curse.
  Jeremiah 49:16-17 — close repetition of sword and famine against the Egyptian remnant: Both oracles locate the threatened people in Egypt and announce death by sword and famine. The source emphasizes the least and greatest and the resulting reproach and curse, while the earlier warning emphasizes the failure of the hoped-for escape.

Jeremiah 51:13
I will visit those staying in Egypt’s land as I visited Jerusalem, by sword, famine, and death.
  Jeremiah 49:18 — close parallel between judgment in Egypt and prior judgment in Jerusalem: The source expressly says Egypt's residents will be visited as Jerusalem was. The earlier warning likewise compares wrath on those entering Egypt with wrath already falling on Jerusalem. Their lists of the judgment's effects differ.

Jeremiah 51:14
None of Judah’s remainder residing in Egypt’s land will be saved to return to Judah’s land, where they hope in their souls to return. They certainly will not return except those rescued.
  Jeremiah 51:28 — same-chapter clarification of the exception for rescued returnees: The source denies return to Judah except for those rescued. The later statement identifies sword survivors who return, few in number. This clarifies the expressly stated exception rather than changing the warning into a promise of universal return.

Jeremiah 51:15
All the men who knew that their wives burned incense to other gods, all the women, a great assembly, and all the people staying in Egypt’s land at Pathoures answered Jeremiah:
  Jeremiah 51:19 — same-chapter link between men's knowledge and the women's appeal to their husbands: The source identifies men who knew of their wives' incense alongside the answering women. The women's later question asks whether they made cakes and poured offerings without their husbands. These statements connect the household participants without making mere knowledge identical to each person's performance of every ritual.

Jeremiah 51:16
As for the word you spoke to us in the Lord’s name, we will not listen to you.
  Jeremiah 6:17 — close explicit refusal to hear the divine warning: The people directly refuse to listen to Jeremiah's word in the Lord's name. In the earlier oracle they likewise answer the call to hear the watchers' trumpet by saying they will not hear. Both record an open verbal refusal rather than inadvertent failure to understand.

Jeremiah 51:17
We will certainly do everything that will come from our mouths: burn incense to the queen of heaven and pour out drink offerings to her, as we, our fathers, our kings, and our rulers did in Judah’s cities and outside Jerusalem. We were filled with bread, were well off, and saw no calamities.
  Hosea 2:7-10 — related false attribution of material well-being to rival objects of devotion: The speakers credit devotion to the queen of heaven with bread and prosperity. Hosea's woman credits her lovers with provisions, while God says He gave the grain, wine and oil that she failed to recognize. The rival recipients differ; the comparison does not identify the queen of heaven with Baal or the lovers.

Jeremiah 51:18
But when we stopped burning incense to the queen of heaven, we were all diminished and perished by sword and famine.
  Hosea 2:10-11 — contrast between rival explanations of lost provision: The people claim that stopping incense to the queen caused scarcity, sword and famine. Hosea identifies God as the provider who takes back His grain and wine. The target supplies a contrasting account of deprivation, while Jeremiah's subsequent reply likewise rejects the speakers' defense of their cult.

Jeremiah 51:19
And when we burn incense to the queen of heaven and poured out drink offerings to her, did we make cakes for her and pour out drink offerings to her without our husbands?
  Jeremiah 7:18 — close household involvement in cakes and drink offerings to heavenly recipients: The women mention making cakes and pouring drink offerings and question whether this was without their husbands. The earlier oracle describes children, fathers and women preparing cakes for heaven's host and pouring offerings to foreign gods. The source says queen of heaven, whereas the target says heaven's host; the recipients' terms are not made identical.

Jeremiah 51:21
Was it not the incense you burned in Judah’s cities and outside Jerusalem—you, your fathers, your kings, your rulers, and the people of the land—that the Lord remembered, and did it not come into His heart?
  Hosea 8:13 — related divine remembrance of cultic wrongdoing for judgment: Jeremiah says the people's earlier incense came into God's heart and remembrance. Hosea says God does not accept the sacrifices and will remember injustices and punish sins. Both oppose a favorable reading of ritual conduct by invoking God's adverse remembrance.

Jeremiah 51:22
The Lord could no longer bear the wickedness of your actions and the abominations you had committed. Your land became a desolation, an untrodden place, and a curse, as on this day,
  Leviticus 26:31-33 — close correspondence between covenant curses and the desolate land: Jeremiah explains the land's desolation and curse by unbearable wickedness and abominations. Leviticus threatens desolate cities and land, ruined holy places and scattering among nations for covenant disobedience. The source describes the resulting condition; the target states the threatened consequences.

Jeremiah 51:23
because you burned incense, sinned against the Lord, did not obey the Lord’s voice, and did not walk in His ordinances, law, and testimonies. These calamities have seized you.
  Deuteronomy 28:15 — close causal link between refusing God's voice and overtaking calamities: The source attributes the calamities to failing to obey God's voice and walk in His ordinances, law and testimonies. Deuteronomy says curses will come and overtake those who do not hear the Lord's voice and keep His commandments. The source also specifies the incense that occasioned the charge.

Jeremiah 51:25
Thus said the Lord, Israel’s God: You women spoke with your mouths and fulfilled it with your hands, saying, We will certainly carry out our pledges that we pledged, to burn incense to the queen of heaven and pour out drink offerings to her. You firmly persisted in your pledges and indeed carried them out.
  Ecclesiastes 5:3-4 — contrast between fulfillment of idolatrous pledges and vows made to God: The source says the women carried out their pledges to the queen of heaven. Ecclesiastes requires paying vows made to God and warns against vowing without paying. The comparison concerns the seriousness of enacted pledges while distinguishing their recipients; it does not commend the women's idolatrous vows.

Jeremiah 51:26
Therefore hear the Lord’s word, all Judah staying in Egypt’s land: I have sworn by My great name, said the Lord, that My name will no longer be in any Judean’s mouth to say, As the Lord, the Lord, lives, throughout Egypt’s land.
  Amos 6:10 — related withholding of the Lord's name amid judgment: The source announces that Judeans in Egypt will no longer invoke the living Lord's name. In Amos, amid deaths in a house, one person tells another to be silent so as not to name the Lord. The source is God's sworn declaration; the target records a human warning, not that identical oath.
