  Hebrews 6:13 — related divine oath by God's own authority: God swears by His great name in the source. Hebrews says God swore by Himself because there was no greater by whom to swear. The oath in Hebrews confirms blessing to Abraham, while Jeremiah's oath announces judgment.

Jeremiah 51:27
For I am watching over them to harm them, not do them good. All Judah living in Egypt’s land will perish by sword and famine until they perish.
  Amos 9:4 — close divine attention directed toward harm rather than good: Jeremiah says God watches the people for harm, not good, and announces sword and famine. Amos says God will fix His eyes on the captives for evils, not good things, and command the sword against them. The correspondence concerns purposeful divine attention in judgment.

Jeremiah 51:28
Those saved from the sword will return to Judah’s land, few in number. Judah’s survivors who settled in Egypt’s land to live there will know whose word will endure.
  Deuteronomy 18:21-22 — related testing of disputed words by their eventual outcome: The few returnees will learn whose word endures, after rival claims about safety and prosperity. Deuteronomy judges a claimed divine word by whether it comes to pass. The source's disputed speakers are not all described as prophets, whereas the law addresses prophetic claims explicitly.

Jeremiah 51:30
Thus said the Lord: I am handing Ouaphre king of Egypt over to his enemy and those seeking his life, as I handed Zedekiah king of Judah over to Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon, his enemy who sought his life.
  Jeremiah 52:8-11 — close narrative counterpart to the stated Zedekiah comparison: The threat against Egypt's Ouaphre is compared with God's handover of Zedekiah to Nebuchadnezzar. The closing narrative records Zedekiah's capture and removal to Babylon. It supports the named comparison, without supplying a narrated fulfillment of Ouaphre's fate.

Jeremiah 51:31
The word Jeremiah the prophet spoke to Baruch son of Neriah when he wrote these words in the scroll from Jeremiah’s mouth, in the fourth year of Jehoiakim son of Josiah king of Judah:
  Jeremiah 43:1-4 — close corroborating notice of Baruch's writing in Jehoiakim's fourth year: Both accounts place Baruch's writing from Jeremiah's mouth in the fourth year of Jehoiakim son of Josiah. The earlier narrative describes the commission and act; this source introduces the personal word addressed to the writer.

Jeremiah 51:33
For you said, Woe to me, woe to me! The Lord has added toil to my pain. I lay down amid groanings and found no rest.
  Psalms 6:7 — close lament of weary groaning at one's resting place: Baruch says he lay amid groanings and found no rest after toil was added to pain. The psalm speaks of weariness with groaning and tears wetting the bed and resting place. It supplies a comparable bodily lament, not Baruch's identity or the same occasion.

Jeremiah 51:34
Tell him, Thus said the Lord: Those I have built up I am tearing down, and those I have planted I am plucking up.
  Jeremiah 1:10 — close opposing images of uprooting or demolishing and building or planting: God announces tearing down what He built and plucking up what He planted. Jeremiah's commission similarly includes uprooting and demolishing alongside building and planting. The source describes reversal of prior constructive work, while the commission lists the prophet's appointed scope.

Jeremiah 51:35
Are you seeking great things for yourself? Do not seek them, for I am bringing calamities upon all flesh, says the Lord. I will give your life as gain wherever you walk.
  Jeremiah 46:18 — close personal assurance that one's life will be gain amid calamity: Baruch is promised his life as gain wherever he goes while calamity comes upon all flesh. Ebed-melech likewise receives a promise that his life will be his gain. Their immediate grounds differ: Baruch is warned against seeking great things, while Ebed-melech is commended for trust.
  Psalms 130:1 — parallel refusal to pursue great things for oneself: The source forbids Baruch to seek great things for himself amid general calamity. The psalm says the speaker's heart and eyes are not exalted and that he has not walked among great things beyond him. This target supports the restraint of ambition, distinct from the source's promise of preserved life.

Jeremiah 52:1
Zedekiah was twenty-one years old when he became king, and he reigned eleven years in Jerusalem. His mother’s name was Amitaal daughter of Jeremiah, from Lobena.
  2 Chronicles 36:11 — close parallel age and length of Zedekiah's reign: Both accounts state that Zedekiah began to reign at twenty-one and reigned eleven years in Jerusalem. The source additionally names his mother and adds Lobena, details which this target does not provide.
  2 Kings 24:18 — close parallel regnal notice including the king's mother: Both notices give the age twenty-one and eleven years in Jerusalem and name the mother as a daughter of Jeremiah. The source says Zedekiah and Amitaal and adds Lobena; Kings says Sedekias and Amital and does not name Lobena in this unit.

Jeremiah 52:4
In the ninth year of his reign, in the tenth month, on the tenth of the month, Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon and all his force came against Jerusalem. They enclosed her with a palisade and built around her with squared stones.
  2 Kings 25:1 — close parallel commencement of the Babylonian siege: Both place the Babylonian king's arrival with his force in the ninth regnal year and tenth month and describe surrounding siegeworks. Jeremiah adds the tenth day and specifies a palisade and squared stones; Kings describes an enclosing wall.

Jeremiah 52:5
The city was under siege until the eleventh year of King Zedekiah.
  2 Kings 25:2 — close parallel endpoint of the siege: Both accounts say the city remained besieged until the king's eleventh year, naming him Zedekiah in Jeremiah and Sedekias in Kings. Neither comparison changes the supplied chronology or name forms.

Jeremiah 52:6
On the ninth of the month, the famine grew severe in the city, and there were no loaves for the people of the land.
  2 Kings 25:3 — close parallel report of famine and absence of bread: Both accounts place severe famine on the ninth of the month and say there were no loaves for the people of the land. The individual units do not themselves supply the month's number.

Jeremiah 52:7
The city was breached, and all the warriors went out at night by the gate between the wall and the outer wall beside the king’s garden, while the Chaldeans surrounded the city. They went by the road to Arabah,
  2 Kings 25:4 — close parallel escape route after Jerusalem's breach: Both describe the breach, warriors going out at night by the gate between walls beside the king's garden, the surrounding Chaldeans and the route toward Arabah or Araba. The detailed cluster supports a close narrative comparison without normalizing the place-name forms.

Jeremiah 52:8
and the Chaldean force pursued the king and overtook him beyond Jericho. All his servants scattered away from him.
  2 Kings 25:5 — close parallel pursuit and capture near Jericho: Both describe the Chaldean pursuit overtaking the king near Jericho and the dispersal of those with him. Jeremiah calls them his servants; Kings calls them his force. The corresponding movement is shared without making those designations identical.

Jeremiah 52:9
They captured the king and brought him to the king of Babylon at Deblatha, and he spoke to him in judgment.
  2 Kings 25:6 — close parallel royal judgment at Deblatha: Both accounts bring the captured king to Babylon's king at Deblatha and describe judgment being spoken upon him. The named place and judicial encounter provide a specific counterpart.

Jeremiah 52:10
The king of Babylon slaughtered Zedekiah’s sons before his eyes, and he slaughtered all Judah’s rulers at Deblatha.
  2 Kings 25:7 — close parallel killing of the king's sons before his eyes: Both accounts explicitly put the sons' slaughter before the father's eyes. Jeremiah additionally reports the killing of Judah's rulers at Deblatha; Kings proceeds to the king's blinding and fetters.

Jeremiah 52:11
He blinded Zedekiah’s eyes and bound him in fetters. The king of Babylon brought him to Babylon and put him in a mill house until the day he died.
  2 Kings 25:7 — close parallel blinding, fetters and deportation of the king: Both accounts describe the king being blinded, bound with fetters and brought to Babylon. Jeremiah additionally places him in a mill house until death; that detail is not replaced with a conventional prison description or attributed to Kings.
  Ezekiel 12:13 — related prediction of arrival in Babylon without seeing it: Jeremiah's blinded king is brought to Babylon. Ezekiel predicts that Jerusalem's ruler will be caught, brought to Babylon and not see it, and die there. The narrative supplies a particular counterpart to arrival without sight; Ezekiel does not name Zedekiah in the target unit.

Jeremiah 52:12
In the fifth month, on the tenth of the month, Nebuzaradan, the chief guard who stood before the king of Babylon, came to Jerusalem.
  2 Kings 25:8 — close parallel arrival of the Babylonian officer with a differing day: Both accounts place the officer's arrival at Jerusalem in the fifth month. Jeremiah gives the tenth day and calls him chief guard; Kings gives the seventh day, the nineteenth year of the Babylonian king, and the title chief cook. The differing details remain explicit.

Jeremiah 52:13
He burned the Lord’s house, the king’s house, and all the city’s houses; every great house he burned with fire.
  2 Kings 25:9-10 — close parallel burning of the sanctuary, palace and city houses: Both accounts name the Lord's house, the king's house and Jerusalem's houses as burned. The target's final agent phrase continues into verse10, so both actual units are retained together as one passage.

Jeremiah 52:14
The Chaldean force with the chief guard pulled down the whole wall of Jerusalem all around.
  2 Chronicles 36:19 — close parallel demolition of Jerusalem's wall: Jeremiah records the Chaldean force pulling down Jerusalem's wall all around. Chronicles likewise says the wall was demolished amid the burning of the sanctuary and citadels. This target expressly contains wall demolition, unlike the supplied 2 Kings 25:10 wording.

Jeremiah 52:16
The chief guard left the remaining people as vinedressers and farmers.
  2 Kings 25:12 — close parallel retention of people as vinedressers: Both accounts say the Babylonian officer left people as vinedressers. Jeremiah adds farmers; Kings calls the remaining people poor and adds the native term gabin. The comparison does not silently translate that latter term as the source's farmers.

Jeremiah 52:17
The Chaldeans shattered the bronze pillars in the Lord’s house, the bases, and the bronze Sea in the Lord’s house. They took their bronze and carried it to Babylon.
  2 Kings 25:13 — close parallel removal of bronze temple fixtures to Babylon: Both accounts describe Chaldeans shattering the bronze pillars and Sea and carrying their bronze to Babylon. Jeremiah names bases, whereas Kings retains mechonoth. The shared named fixtures and destination are explicit without replacing that native term.

Jeremiah 52:18
And the rim, the bowls, the meat forks, and all the bronze utensils with which they performed service,
  2 Kings 25:14 — parallel inventories of bronze service utensils with differing item lists: Both passages enumerate bronze objects used in service, including bowls. Jeremiah also names the rim and meat forks; Kings names cauldrons, iamin and incense dishes. The inventories are related without treating all the supplied item names as interchangeable.

Jeremiah 52:19
and the saphphoth, the masmaroth, the pouring vessels, the lampstands, the incense bowls, and the ladles—those of gold, gold, and those of silver, silver—the chief guard took.
  2 Kings 25:15 — close parallel taking of gold and silver temple vessels: Jeremiah distinguishes the gold and silver objects taken by the chief guard within a broader utensil list. Kings records the chief cook taking firepans and gold and silver bowls. This supports the precious-metal spoliation without supplying glosses for saphphoth or masmaroth.

Jeremiah 52:20
The two pillars, the one Sea, and the twelve bronze calves beneath the Sea, which King Solomon made for the Lord’s house—their bronze was beyond weighing.
  2 Kings 25:16 — close parallel unweighed bronze of Solomon's temple furnishings: Both accounts name Solomon, two pillars, one Sea and bronze beyond a reckoned weight. Jeremiah includes twelve calves beneath the Sea, while Kings includes mechonoth. Their lists are not made identical.
  1 Kings 7:13 — close counterpart for twelve animals supporting the Sea: Jeremiah names twelve bronze calves beneath the Sea. Kings describes twelve cattle beneath it, grouped three in each of four directions, with the Sea above them. This target specifically supports the supporting-animal arrangement rather than the separate unweighed-bronze statement.

Jeremiah 52:21
As for the pillars, one pillar was thirty-five cubits high, and a cord of twelve cubits encircled it. Its thickness was four fingerbreadths all around,
  2 Kings 25:17 — parallel descriptions of a temple pillar with differing heights: Jeremiah assigns one pillar a height of thirty-five cubits; Kings gives one pillar eighteen cubits. Both describe the same class of temple furnishing, but the measurements are expressly different and are not reconciled by the comparison.
  2 Chronicles 3:15 — related thirty-five-cubit pillar description with different grammatical scope: Jeremiah describes one pillar as thirty-five cubits high. Chronicles describes two pillars before the house as thirty-five cubits in height. The shared measurement language is noted without deciding whether the latter expression measures each pillar or combines them.

Jeremiah 52:22
and a bronze cornice was on them. The projection of one cornice was five cubits in length, and a net and pomegranates surrounded the cornice, all of bronze. The second pillar had the same: eight pomegranates per cubit over the twelve cubits.
  1 Kings 7:27-28 — close correspondence of pillar ornaments, nets and pomegranates: Jeremiah describes bronze cornices surrounded by netting and pomegranates. Kings lists pillar ornaments covered by nets and rows of pomegranates. The differing ornament terminology and the target's four-hundred enumeration are preserved rather than made identical to Jeremiah's count.

Jeremiah 52:23
There were ninety-six pomegranates on one side; all the pomegranates around the net numbered one hundred.
  2 Chronicles 3:16 — related hundred-pomegranate enumeration with differing descriptions: Jeremiah counts ninety-six on one side and one hundred around the net. Chronicles says a hundred pomegranates were made and placed on hanging ornaments. The passages share a numbered pomegranate decoration but do not explain how their arrangements and counting scopes correspond.

Jeremiah 52:24
The chief guard took the first priest, the second priest, and the three men guarding the road,
  2 Kings 25:18 — parallel seizure of priestly personnel and three guards with different identifications: Both accounts include a first priest and three guards in the seized group. Jeremiah leaves the priests unnamed and calls the guards men guarding the road; Kings names Saraias and Sophonias and calls the three doorpost guards. The comparison does not identify the unnamed second priest with a named person or erase the different guard descriptions.

Jeremiah 52:25
and one eunuch who supervised the warriors, seven prominent men in the king’s presence who were found in the city, the scribe of the forces who kept records for the people of the land, and sixty people from the people of the land found inside the city.
  2 Kings 25:19 — close parallel captive officials with a differing court-party count: Both lists include a eunuch supervising warriors, a military scribe and sixty people found in the city. Jeremiah gives seven prominent men in the king's presence; Kings gives five who saw his face. That numerical difference is retained.

Jeremiah 52:26
Nebuzaradan, the chief guard, took them and brought them to the king of Babylon at Deblatha,
  2 Kings 25:20 — close parallel transfer of captives to Babylon's king at Deblatha: Both accounts describe the Babylonian officer taking the detained group to the king at Deblatha. Their supplied officer titles differ, chief guard in Jeremiah and chief cook in Kings.

Jeremiah 52:27
and the king of Babylon struck them at Deblatha in Aimath’s land.
  2 Kings 25:21 — close parallel royal violence at Deblatha in Aimath: Both accounts say Babylon's king struck the captives at Deblatha in Aimath's land. Kings explicitly adds that he put them to death and that Judah was deported; the source's shorter wording is preserved.

Jeremiah 52:31
In the thirty-seventh year after Ioakim king of Judah had been deported, in the twelfth month, on the twenty-fourth of the month, Oulaimaradach king of Babylon, in the year he became king, took up the head of Ioakim king of Judah and brought him out of the house where he was guarded.
  2 Kings 25:27 — close parallel royal release with differing day and name forms: Both date a Judean king's release to the thirty-seventh year of deportation and the twelfth month. Jeremiah names Ioakim and gives the twenty-fourth day; Kings names Joakim and gives the twenty-seventh. The close sequence is compared without harmonizing those details or supplying a different Judean royal name.

Jeremiah 52:32
He spoke kindly to him and placed his throne above the thrones of the kings with him in Babylon.
  2 Kings 25:28 — close parallel kind speech and elevation above other kings: Both passages describe kind speech to the Judean king and placement of his throne above those of the other kings in Babylon. The distinct supplied names in their preceding units remain as written.

Jeremiah 52:33
He changed his prison robe, and he ate bread continually before him all the days he lived.
  2 Kings 25:29 — close parallel change of prison clothing and continual royal meals: Both accounts describe changing prison garments and eating bread continually before the Babylonian king throughout life. The clothing and daily-table details form a specific verbal and narrative correspondence.

Jeremiah 52:34
His allowance was given to him continually by the king of Babylon, from day to day until the day he died.
  2 Kings 25:30 — close parallel daily royal allowance for life: Both accounts give continual provision from the Babylonian royal establishment, apportioned day by day for the rest of the Judean king's life. Jeremiah calls it an allowance and expressly says until death; Kings describes table provision and each day's portion.
